# Softanza · The Software Crafters Platform of the AI Age

Built 2026-10-14 12:19 UTC+01:00. Library commit 93d7a39bdd040f82eae21d949460d69c32a75c70. Every page below is a draft unless it says otherwise.

# Preface

Source: Preface. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Run: 1 of 1 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:13 UTC+01:00.

## A question you already have

You use a tool that writes code. You ask it for a function, or for a whole feature, and it answers in seconds. Then comes the question that no tool answers for you: should this code enter my system?

You have always had ways to answer. You run the tests. The type checker agrees, or it does not. A colleague reads the change. Those ways were made when people wrote code slowly. Now one sentence can produce a thousand lines, and nobody has time to read a thousand lines for every sentence.

Code has become cheap. Trust has not become cheap. This book keeps coming back to one sentence, the one every chapter rests on: **change arrives faster than trust.**

## The idea, in two moves

First you **declare**. In plain text, you say what a thing is: an application (I call it a *world*), a language, an AI agent, a whole product. Then you **govern**. From your declaration you get a judge, which I call a *court*. A court is a validator built from your declaration. It gives a verdict, *kept* or *refused*, and the reason, in the words of your declaration.

Every change goes to the court before it enters, whoever wrote it: you, a colleague, a script or an AI model. The model can still propose anything. It cannot decide anything. It is also asked last: whatever rules can decide, the rules decide first.

## A declaration, in real code

Here is the smallest case. You add an AI agent to your system, a program that watches stock levels and reorders. You do not start with its code. You start with a short text file that says what it is: its name, how often it runs, what it may do, and whether its actions can be undone. That file is a declaration. You load it, and the court reads it before the agent can ever run. This agent's file leaves something out. What does the court say?

```haro
cPia = "pia: 1
name: stock-watcher
kind: pi
reversibility: compensable
schedule:
  timer: 20
skills:
  - name: restock
    does: learn stock level ordered
"
oD = StzAgentDeclarationQ(cPia)
? oD.IsValid()
#--> 0
? oD.CiteFindings()
#--> [pia-coverage @ coverage] an agent must say WHAT IT COVERS. The engine loop refuses to schedule an agent with no coverage statement (law 18), so a file without one could never run and is refused here instead of at the first tick.
```

> run: kept

Read it in three parts. The text between the quotes is the declaration. The next line loads it. Then `?` prints an answer, and the line after `#-->` is what the block printed when I ran it. The court answers `0`: refused. It names the rule and gives the reason: an agent must say what it covers, because the engine will not schedule an agent that does not, so the file is refused now and not at its first run. A few words of the file: `pia` names the file format, `compensable` means its effects can be undone by a compensating action, and law 18 is the engine's rule that an agent with no coverage is never scheduled.

You could write this check in your own CI. Here nobody had to. The court for agents ships with the library, so the rule is the library's, and the facts are yours, in the declaration. A one-off assert in a start-up script judges one fact. A declaration holds all the facts about the agent in one text, and the same court judges it whether a person, a script or a model wrote it. The rule can be yours too: chapter 2 has a rule you write in a few lines over two facts, that an actor is a language model and that it holds an effect, and the court finds the violation in your own graph of agents. This example is small on purpose, and a schema can check it too. The book does not argue that a court checks more than a schema. It argues that one court, built from your declaration, stands in front of every actor and gives its reason. It does not read a thousand generated lines and call them good: it checks that a change fits what you declared, and chapter 24 shows a change tried out on a copy of your system before anything is committed. For a thing of your own, such as a language you invent, chapter 12 shows how you make the court, and says how far that is built. Chapter 1 tells a second scene, with a mail service, and chapter 2 runs this block again.

You will say that you already have tests, schemas and policy checks in your CI. You do. Today those rules live in many places: tests here, a schema there, a policy file elsewhere, each written by hand after the code. Here you state them once, in the declaration, and the court comes from it. Chapter 22 compares the two honestly, and where the field does better, it says so. A court judges what you declared. It cannot judge what you left out, and each chapter's limit says where that stops.

## What Softanza is

Three things, and for each I say how far it exists.

- A **library** that you call from code. You write a few lines of Haro like the ones above, and you run them with the library loaded. The library holds the declarations and the courts made from them. Its source is at github.com/mayouni/stzlib, and every block of code in this book ran against a named commit of it. If your work is in Python or TypeScript, today you would use the library beside your code, not inside it: through one governed door it can run code in Python, NodeJS (JavaScript), R, Julia, Prolog and C, and chapter 16 shows how.
- An **engine** beneath the library, written in Zig, a systems language. It does the actual computation, on text, lists and tables, once and in one place, so that every language that talks to it gets the same behaviour. It runs today.
- A **language**, Haro, made to hold the declarations. For now it uses the syntax of its first facet, so all the code written so far runs unchanged. Its own executable is in development: it has a charter and a first experiment.

Chapter 4 draws the three in one picture.

## Where it stands

Softanza is not a research project, although it rests on deep research. Real projects shaped it: a restaurant, a bank and an organisation, and their feedback shaped its design. Parts of Softanza are in production. Other parts are in development, as is normal for a platform that is new and this ambitious. Each chapter says which is which.

## What this book is

This is a book of arguments, with running code. For each question about programming, about languages and about intelligence, I do four things. I give the answer the field usually gives. I give the answer Softanza takes. I give the decision I made, and what I refused. And I give the proof: the code ran, and the tests that prove it are named. I also say what the field already does well, so that you can compare.

It has seven parts and thirty-three chapters. You do not need to know Softanza or Zig. You need to have written code, and to have felt the doubt above. The project's website holds its evidence, with the proofs, and its course teaches the same ground by exercises. This book holds the argument.

Each chapter ends with six short doors. A door is a way in for one kind of reader: the programmer, the analyst, the designer, the decision-maker, the educator and the agent. The agent is an AI program, because the book is also published as data that a program can read. Enter by the door that is yours today.

## How to read it

Read nine chapters first. They carry the whole argument and can be read alone: the four of Part I, the last two, and three that each state a rule the book holds everywhere. Chapter 5: the code should read the way a person thinks. Chapter 11: a language is declared and closed. Chapter 17: a program is programmatic by default, which means rules and code decide first, and the model comes last. The reader has a button that shows only these nine. Come back to the others when one of them interests you.

Every chapter has the same nine parts, in the same order. The idea as the field holds it today. The first principle. The form it takes in Softanza, in code. The decision, and what I refused. The court: the named tests that prove the chapter's claim. The field, and what it does better. The limit. The six doors. An exercise, which you answer before you run it. Chapters 5, 11 and 17 open with one more short section, on what the AI age changes.

## What to trust

Every block of code in this book was run inside the library. Under each title the page says when, at which commit, and how many blocks kept their promise: how many printed what the line after `#-->` says they print. If a block prints something else, the build fails, and so does the page. The exercises are yours to run.

When a chapter says what is not finished, it uses three words and no others. *Built*: the code exists, a test exists, and a block ran. *Designed*: a document says so, and nothing runs. *Absent*: I looked and found nothing, and I say how far I looked. The project's website uses five names for the same things, and chapter 3 shows how they map to these three.

One more note. Haro's own executable is still in development, so the blocks marked `haro` run today on the runtime of the language the library was first written for, version 1.27, and each page says so. Appendix H, the project's history, tells how that came about.

## About this draft

The chapters were drafted with AI agents working from the project's own files, and every code block was run. I have not read every page yet, and each chapter has a banner at its top that says so until I do. Where the files held no answer to a question, the page says so in one plain sentence and does not invent one. The French edition is a translation of the English drafts, and each French page says so. This preface was written after the chapters, and it stays unfinished until I write my own. This is the second book of my imprint, AYOUNI. The first, *Refinement-Oriented Programming*, is about one discipline for software in the age of AI agents, told through one bank.


=====

# Change arrives faster than trust

Source: Chapter 1. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the court that admits or refuses a change, for a person and for a machine; absent: a customer platform running on the Softanza engine

Run: 1 of 1 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:13 UTC+01:00.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Ask the industry what changed in programming these last years and you will get one answer, said with excitement or with dread: *machines write the code now.*

The picture is familiar. A model proposes a function, a file, a whole application. A person reviews it, or does not. Tools are added around the model to make it safer: linters that read what it wrote, tests it generates for itself, guardrails that catch the worst of it, a second model that judges the first. The model is at the centre. Everything else is a fence built around it after the fact.

I do not think that picture is wrong about the facts. It is wrong about where to stand.

## 2. The first principle

Here is the sentence I keep coming back to, and it is the oldest sentence in this book: **change arrives faster than trust.**

Code has become cheap. A thousand lines cost a sentence. What has not become cheap, what has in fact become the scarcest thing in software, is the trust that those lines do what their author, human or machine, says they do. Every team I have met in the last two years has more code than it can review and less confidence than it had before. The bottleneck of our age is not writing. It is admission: deciding what may enter a system and stay there.

So the question Softanza asks is not *how do we generate better?* It is *how do we admit?* Who judges a change, by what law, before it runs, and what is written down about the verdict so that someone can read it later and be answerable for it.

When you put admission at the centre, the picture turns around. The court is in the middle. The model is one proposer among others, and it comes last, after the person, after the data, after the formula, after the plain sentence. Nothing it says enters the world until the court has read it. That is not a fence built around a generator. It is a different building.

## 3. The Softanza form

Let me show you rather than tell you, by example, which is how I have always preferred to teach.

A solution declares that it depends on a mail service. While I program, I bind that dependency to a sandbox: an object that captures every message and sends nothing, so my tests can read what *would* have gone out. Free, offline, deterministic. Then I move the solution to production, with the sandbox still bound, the way people do at two in the morning before a demo.

The registry does not call it sound. The first line it prints is a zero, and the second says why: a fake must never ship. The fake is refused by name, and a service that was declared and never bound raises the moment it is asked for, instead of silently doing nothing. I did not remember to check this. The phase decided what comes back, and the check is enforced, not remembered.

That is the whole philosophy in one small scene. Expression is free: bind what you like, while you like. Admission is governed: the gate reads the phase and the binding, and refuses.

Now a second scene, because the first one has no model in it and you are waiting for the model.

Take a restaurant as the illustration: an agent that knows its world proposes to place an order. In Softanza the agent does not place it. It proposes a plan. A workbench rehearses the plan on a twin of the system, a copy that holds no reference to the real one, and records every operation it would perform. A court reads the rehearsal against the rules the restaurant declared: who may order, what may be ordered, what a supplier must confirm. Only then does a governed actor commit, and the commit is recorded with its actor and its outcome. The guard that proves this sequence, `safeworld_narrated`, rehearses in a twin of the files, plans, and lets an actor commit; it holds seventy-six assertions as I write, none of them about a restaurant, and the site shows a recorded run of the same mechanism.

Notice what the model did and did not do. It proposed. It did not decide, did not write, did not send. It was a proposer beside the registers of answer. Three of them, an option chosen, a data structure given, a sentence spoken, are built today and open to the person before the model is called at all; two more, a formula written and an example shown, are not yet built (Chapter 18). I call this *programmatic by default, the model last*. It is the sentence under every chapter of Part IV.

And a third scene, the smallest, because it shows the same law at the level of a single line. The platform is judged on what its code looks like and what it answers:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ "tea", "rice", "tea", "fish", "rice", "tea" ])
? @@( o1.FindAll("tea") )
#--> [ 1, 3, 6 ]
? @@( o1.DuplicatesRemoved() )
#--> [ "tea", "rice", "fish" ]
```

> run: kept

The question is spoken as it is thought: find all, remove duplicates. And the two lines under the two questions are not my commentary. They are promises the library kept on the night this chapter was built. If it stops keeping them, this page fails. The same law, admission before trust, applied to a paragraph.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

Every chapter of this book carries this section, and I promised in the preface that it is the reason the book exists. So let me be concrete about the decisions behind this first chapter, because they were not comfortable.

**I refused to put the model at the centre.** When the models arrived, that was the obvious move, and I had the pieces to make it: a natural layer, a knowledge graph, an agent class. I could have wired a model into each and shipped a Softanza that "talks". I did not, and I wrote the refusal into the architecture as a law: the mind is programmatic by default, and a model may only propose. I gave up some fashion by that choice. I think I kept the platform.

**I retired the language I was born on.** Softanza was written for years on a small, warm scripting language that I love and that I pleaded with, long before the models, to make executable natural language its first concern. It did not, and that is its right. In 2026 I ruled that the language would not be carried forward: the runtime beneath Softanza is being replaced by one we own, written in Zig, a charter and a spike so far, wearing the old surface so that every line I wrote can keep running, under a new name, **Haro**. The old surface stays because the code is the asset; the old runtime goes because sovereignty over the runtime is one of the commitments I made. That decision was not free. It is in Chapter 27 with its numbers, including the one I did not want: our runtime is slower than the one we measured against, by a factor between one and two, on every row. I recorded the loss in the estate memo of 13 September. A book that hides that is not worth your trust, and neither is a platform.

**I refused to let anything into BASE without a court, and I failed.** The three layers of the library, CORE, BASE and MAX, were meant to keep the foundation small and the innovations above it. Under the pressure of time I let everything go into BASE: ninety-three percent of the code sits there today, and the dependency rule is held at the engine and broken at the inheritance. I say this on the first chapter, not the last, because the discipline I am about to preach for thirty-two chapters is one I have broken, and the study of the record caught me. That is what a court is for, and the court for the layers is still only proposed.

**I refused a second embedding.** When someone asks "what about Python, what about JavaScript, what about my language", the answer is one door, to be governed, and never another host runtime sewn into the platform. The record's seven languages were chosen for seven layers of a solution, and Chapter 16 counts them. The door is the answer to "what about language X", and it is one answer, not twenty.

What was refused is as much a part of Softanza as what was built. You will not find the refusals on the site, because the site shows what is. You will find them here, in every chapter, under this heading.

## 5. The court

What proves this chapter? Three things, and I give them to you by name so that you can check them rather than believe me.

The service registry's refusal of a fake in production is a guard, `service_registry_narrated`, in the system plane. The agent's propose, rehearse, judge and commit is `safeworld_narrated`, seventy-six assertions, in the agentic plane, beside `governance_crossing_narrated`, in the system plane, for the crossing itself. The code block above was run by the book's own runner inside the library on the night this page was built, and its output is shown as it came.

Three guards for a chapter of philosophy may seem few. They are the right three, because they are the three places where philosophy meets a verdict.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

I will not pretend the field has nothing. It has, in the last three years, built the most capable code generators in history, and every day I use them. The industry's answer to the trust problem is real work: generated tests, review bots, policy engines, sandboxes for agents, structured outputs, a second model to grade the first. Where the field leads, and it leads clearly, is in the generators themselves. Softanza does not build the large generators and does not compete with them; where it trains, it trains small models bound to one domain, whose every answer will be checked by a grammar. The field also leads in scale: its review tools read volumes of generated code that nothing in Softanza has been run against.

Where I believe Softanza stands alone is in the order of things. The field adds admission to a generator. Softanza adds a generator to a court. In the field, a model writes in your language and tools check it afterwards; here, the model may only write in a language you declared, whose grammar is closed and whose court is meant to be born with it, so that, for the languages whose grammar is compiled today, an invalid sentence cannot be spoken at all. In the field, the dialogue with a system is a prompt; here, the system asks, and the answer can be an example, a formula or a sentence before it is ever a model. In the field, the record of a decision is a log; here, it is a transcript with a speaker and a certainty, answerable forever.

Those are claims, and each has its chapter and its guard. I list them here so that you know where the argument is going, and so that you can hold me to it.

## 7. The limit

The honest limit of this chapter is also the honest limit of the whole platform, and I put it on the first page on purpose.

Softanza has been used. A bank in Niamey runs an assessment platform whose building shaped Softanza, under licence, attested by the installation guide we delivered. A restaurant in Lyon runs a platform for neighbourhood commerce whose first demo its owner made himself, on his own phone, over a messaging app. An organisation in Niger has its hub designed. Their lessons are in the code, by file name, in Part VI.

But the customer platforms run today on ordinary stacks. The bank's is a Python application; the restaurant's is a set of single-file web worlds; the organisation's hub is not built. Their declarations are being written as Softanza's own, so that moving them onto the Softanza engine is a change of engine and not of plan. That move is the next step. The court is built, and the platforms exist; the next milestone is the first of them running on the engine. That is the next line this book will carry, and Chapter 33 is about nothing else.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You will keep your editor and your language. What changes is that every claim you make about your code can carry a promise under it, and a court will read it.

**The analyst.** Your rules become data a person can change, and every change is judged before it lands and recorded after.

**The designer.** The interface is judged too: twenty-two verbs, one hundred and twenty-two rules, and a verifier that refuses what a machine can check.

**The decision-maker.** The question you ask every vendor, "who decides, and what is written down", has a mechanical answer here, and it is the same answer for a person and for a machine.

**The educator.** The tutor asks and never answers first. That is a law of the platform, not a feature of a product.

**The agent.** You are welcome here, and you will be judged. Appendix G is yours.

## 9. Exercise

Take one function a model wrote for you this week. Under it, write the line it must print for one input you care about, in the form you have just met:

```haro exercise
? YourFunction(the_input)
#--> the line it must print
```

> run: exercise

Run it. If the line matches, you have your first promise. If it does not, you have your first finding, and you have learned more about that function in a minute than the model's explanation told you. Do not fix it yet. Chapter 2 is about who you are when you do.


=====

# The software crafter

Source: Chapter 2. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the agent file and its court; the registry that refuses a fake in production; rules as data on a model; a regulator's rules judging an organisation chart; the natural layer in Hausa and Arabic; designed: a guard that fails when a step of a journey page breaks, each step run in order; the door through which a coding agent asks the library to judge its work; absent: the platforms of the three customers running on the Softanza engine; a crafter's own medium, the messaging app on their phone, as a target

Run: 9 of 9 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:13 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take the word "crafter" from the field's craftsmanship and from its two answers that define the person by a tool, low-code and the coding agent. I say what the site means by it. I show the six doors, each with a line or two that run, and the three cases this book is for: a restaurateur in Lyon, a bank in Niamey, an organisation in the field. I say what I kept, the programmer first; what I added, the agent, with one power withheld; and what I refused, "maker" and "builder". The principle is one sentence that joins the site's and the glossary's: a crafter is whoever declares what they know about a world, and owns what they declare.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field already has a word for programmers who care about their work: craftsmanship. A book of 2001 gave the idea its name, and a manifesto of 2009 gave it its slogan. Since then "software craftsman" has meant a professional who holds their code to a standard higher than the one the job requires. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.) It is a good movement. It is also a movement of programmers, about programmers' standards.

The field's two other answers to "who makes software, if not programmers" define the person by a tool. The first is low-code: business people building applications on a vendor's visual platform, the "citizen developer" of the analysts. The second is the coding agent: a model that reads files, edits them and runs commands, steered in plain words, with "vibe coding" as the name for keeping whatever it writes. Both answers share a shape. The person is defined by a tool, and the work lives where the tool lives. When the vendor changes its prices or its model, the person's work changes with it. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.)

The next section says what the site means by the word.

## 2. The first principle

The site gives the definition in two sentences. They say "crafter" today (read on 9 October 2026); until 8 October they said "maker". The glossary says that a crafter is whoever is declaring, at the moment they declare. The site says that a crafter owns what they make. I ruled on 12 October 2026 that the book keeps both, in one sentence: **a crafter is whoever declares what they know about a world, and owns what they declare: the artefact is theirs, in plain text, and it does not expire.** Skill does not enter the definition. The word "craft" suggests it, and I keep the word: the person is defined by the act and by ownership, not by programming skill. I hold this as a view, not a result.

## 3. The Softanza form

I was the first crafter of Softanza, and not for a reason I would have chosen. My wife runs a music club where people learn to sing classical Arabic songs. Every Sunday morning she sends me the list of songs to prepare, and I search for their lyrics. The lyrics found online repeat every phrase as the singer performed it, and she reminds me, every time, to remove the duplicates. I wrote `RemoveDuplicates()` to stop doing it by hand. Is its family still in the library, and what does it say of a list with three teas in it?

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ "tea", "rice", "tea", "fish", "rice", "tea" ])
? o1.ContainsDuplicates()
#--> 1
? o1.NumberOfOccurrence("tea")
#--> 3
```

> run: kept

The family answers: the list holds duplicates (`1`), and `tea` occurs `3` times.

I write about this in an article on what the library was built on: I introduced its features when a real problem asked for them, or when I anticipated they would meet programmers' needs. This one came from a problem: the crafter comes first, and the feature after.

Since 2022, I have presented Softanza not by its anatomy, its modules and classes, but by who you are and what you are trying to do. The 2022 deck had five audiences, each with a recipe of three to five steps. The site today has seven doors (read on 9 October 2026), one per kind of crafter. The seventh, for a team that ships an application, was added that day, and this chapter keeps the six it was drafted with. Each door says what you declare, what you get, and where to start. Here is what each of the six declares, in a line or two that run.

**The programmer** declares intent in a library that reads like a sentence. You have just seen it.

**The analyst** declares the data of a world, and the engine computes the view. Here, three regions of Niger with the population the 2012 census counted:

```haro
o = new stzTable([ [ :region, :population ],
    [ "Agadez", 487620 ], [ "Maradi", 3402094 ], [ "Zinder", 3539764 ] ])
o.Show()
#--> ╭────────┬────────────╮
#    │ Region │ Population │
#    ├────────┼────────────┤
#    │ Agadez │     487620 │
#    │ Maradi │    3402094 │
#    │ Zinder │    3539764 │
#    ╰────────┴────────────╯
```

> run: kept

The engine drew the view from the data alone: `Maradi │ 3402094` is one of its rows.

**The designer** declares a picture as a program, and gets the same picture every time:

```haro
? Q("Softanza").BoxedXT([ :AllCorners = :Round ])
#--> ╭──────────╮
#    │ Softanza │
#    ╰──────────╯
```

> run: kept

The picture is the program's output: a boxed `Softanza` with round corners.

**The decision-maker** declares what each actor in the world may do, a person or an agent, and the court enforces it. An agent is a small text file. What does the court say to one that does not say what it covers? Load it:

```haro
cPia = "pia: 1
name: stock-watcher
kind: pi
reversibility: compensable
schedule:
  timer: 20
skills:
  - name: restock
    does: learn stock level ordered
"
oD = StzAgentDeclarationQ(cPia)
? oD.IsValid()
#--> 0
? oD.CiteFindings()
#--> [pia-coverage @ coverage] an agent must say WHAT IT COVERS. The engine loop refuses to schedule an agent with no coverage statement (law 18), so a file without one could never run and is refused here instead of at the first tick.
```

> run: kept

The court refused it! Its reason is printed: `[pia-coverage @ coverage] an agent must say WHAT IT COVERS`. Here "law 18" is the engine's rule that an agent with no coverage statement is never scheduled, and `pia` names the format of the file, as its first line shows.

**The educator** declares in the learner's own language. A sentence in Hausa runs. Does the engine count the letters of an Arabic word rather than its bytes, and does it know its script?

```haro
? NaturallyIn("ha", "Yi rubutu dauke 'hello' Maida shi").Result()
#--> HELLO
? Q("سلام").NumberOfChars()
#--> 4
? Q("مرحبا بالعالم").Script()
#--> arabic
```

> run: kept

The Hausa sentence came back as `HELLO`. The Arabic word counts `4`, its letters and not its bytes, and its script is `arabic`.

**The agent** is the sixth crafter, the one 2020 could not draw. It declares itself in the same kind of file, and it is welcome, with one thing withheld. An agent whose mind is a language model may propose; it may not hold an effect. What happens to a file that gives it one?

```haro
cLlm = "pia: 1
name: rogue
kind: llm
coverage: proposes a summary
reversibility: reversible
schedule:
  timer: 20
proposes:
  prompt: Summarize {input}
  structure:
    - field: topic
      type: string
skills:
  - name: send
    when: always
    does: propose
    effect: send-email
"
oBad = StzAgentDeclarationQ(cLlm)
? oBad.IsValid()
#--> 0
? oBad.CiteFindings()
#--> [no-llm-effectful @ skills.send.effect] REFUSED: granting 'effectful' to llm actor 'rogue' -- an LLM proposes, only a pi-gate commits (no-llm-effectful, enforced at CONSTRUCTION, not merely audited).
```

> run: kept

The llm actor `rogue` does not get its effect! The court answers `REFUSED`, and says when: `enforced at CONSTRUCTION, not merely audited`. A "pi-gate" is the gate where a governed actor commits.

Now the three cases this book is for. They are not personas. They are people and organisations with documents, and Part VI tells each of them in full. Here, a page each.

**A restaurateur in Lyon.** He owns a couscous restaurant, and the project is a platform for neighbourhood commerce. The project's notes say it plainly: he is not a programmer. The project began with an offer in May 2024 and then two years of rich specification that produced nothing he could use. What came out of that failure is the rule I have worked by since. Deliver small, fast and useful, and let the foundations mature in parallel without ever blocking a delivery.

On 4 July 2026 he answered a scoping report in his own words and reversed the order of the work: customer first, restaurateur next, supplier last. The project's architecture was redrawn as a constellation of worlds, using the library's design, and the first amendment closed on 4 August 2026.

A constellation is a graph of worlds. A world is one application; a bond is a declared relation between two worlds, for one named act, and nothing crosses without one. Chapter 23 shows all three run; here it is only the shape the project took.

The project counts eleven iterations and a campaign of twenty-two simulated tests behind its interface rules. In his own report the speed of ordering is his first, non-negotiable priority. And on 19 August, after a day of tests in the field with two phones and a messaging app, the notes call him the project's co-architect.

Chapter 1 tells the first demonstration as the owner's own, on his own phone, over a messaging app. The record is more exact. Its checklist for the 15 August demonstration plans it for the owner alone, with his own phone and a messaging app as the support line. The measurements he took over a messaging app are those of 19 August.

One of his needs is carried by a law of the library: try payments before paying anyone for an account. What does the registry say to a fake payments service while you rehearse, and what does it say when you go live? Run it:

```haro
oReg = new stzServiceRegistry("restaurant")
oReg.Bind(:payments, new stzPaymentsSandbox())
oReg.SetPhase(:emulated)
? oReg.IsSound()
#--> 1
oReg.SetPhase(:production)
? oReg.IsSound()
#--> 0
? oReg.Findings()[1][:message]
#--> still bound to a SANDBOX in a production phase -- a fake must never ship
```

> run: kept

It answers `1` in the emulated phase and `0` in production, and says why: `still bound to a SANDBOX in a production phase -- a fake must never ship`. A fake in production is refused!

**A bank in Niamey.** A commercial bank under the rules of the central bank of the West African monetary union is the licensee of an organisation-assessment product I built beside the library. It is installed on its own internal network, under that licence. The installation guide delivered to the bank is the document that attests it. The lesson it left in the library is that an organisation can be judged by its own chart, under a regulator's rules. Declare a chart with a chief executive and an internal audit that reports to him. What do the regulator's rules say of it?

```haro
oOrg = new stzOrgChart("bank")
oOrg.AddPositionXTT("ceo", "Chief Executive", [ :department = "executive" ])
oOrg.AddPositionXTT("audit", "Internal Audit", [ :department = "audit", :reportsTo = "ceo" ])
? @@( oOrg.ValidateBCEAOGovernance() )
#--> [ [ "status", "fail" ], [ "domain", "BCEAO_governance" ], [ "issuecount", 3 ], [ "issues", [ "BCEAO-001: No Board of Directors found", "BCEAO-002: Audit reports to non-board position", "BCEAO-003: No dedicated Risk Management function" ] ] ]
```

> run: kept

The chart fails, with `"issuecount", 3`: three findings from a chart of two positions! Each is a rule with a number an auditor can read: `BCEAO-001: No Board of Directors found` is the first. The record shows the bank as the licensee of a product I built; it does not say who at the bank declared anything, and this page does not say it.

**An organisation in the field.** An organisation working in Niger, with a head office in Niamey and bases in the field, asked for a hub. Its own specification preferred a low-code platform built on a large vendor's office suite. My offer of 5 August 2026 was chosen instead. The study lists four gains, with ownership first among them. The organisation owns its vocabulary and its rules, changes them without programming, pays no licence per seat, and can hand everything to a programmer of its own country. The design study was built with its teams between 22 September and 8 October 2026. Two of its principles are the book's in small: the default is to work offline, and the agent proposes while a person decides. That rule is data the organisation attaches to its own model. Does the model judge itself, with an LLM actor in it that holds an effect?

```haro
oRule = new stzGraphRule("no-llm-effectful")
oRule.SetDomainQ("agentic")
oRule.WhenQ("kind", "equals", "llm_actor")
oRule.WhenQ("capabilities", "contains", "effectful")
oRule.ThenViolationQ("an LLM proposes, only a pi-gate commits")
g = new stzGraph("agents")
g.AddNode("writer")
g.SetNodeProperty("writer", "kind", "llm_actor")
g.SetNodeProperty("writer", "capabilities", [ "inference", "effectful" ])
g.AddRule(oRule)
? g.RulesAreSound()
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

`RulesAreSound()` answers `0`: the model finds its own `writer`, an LLM actor holding `effectful`, in violation of the rule.

The three cases are a restaurateur who is not a programmer, a bank under a regulator and an organisation that chose ownership over a vendor. The restaurateur and the organisation are crafters by the record; the bank is the licensee of a product, and the record does not say who there declared anything. The restaurateur and the organisation did not wait for the software industry, and each is offered ownership of what is built for them.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I kept the programmer first.** The vision of the platform, which I ratified, says it in one line: the first audience stays the programmer in a small team; the agentic era did not replace that person, it multiplied them. I held that agents do not make programmers obsolete.

**I added the agent as a crafter, and withheld one power.** The agent is the sixth reader of every Softanza language: both a builder and a user. It gets a workbench and a court. It does not get the capability to commit.

**I refused "maker", and I refused "builder".** "Maker" names the workshop movement, the soldering iron and the 3D printer (general knowledge, not a study of the record). The site's headline used it until 8 October 2026. "Builder" names someone who assembles what a plan says. Neither says what I think programming is. It is handwork, done with care, on something that is yours: it is craft. I call the person who does it a crafter. What this book's cover image carries, the record does not say, and this page does not say it for me.

**I refused to count a customer without a document.** The site counts a project as a reference only when a document proves it, and quotes no number of countries and no number of programmers. It names a customer only on my word. A lesson is listed only when a file of the library carries it.

**I refused to present the library by its anatomy.** Since 2022, the front door is who you are and what you want to do, and the anatomy is placed under each step. The site keeps seven doors (read on 9 October 2026), and the journeys behind them exist as pages, nine on that day, each step carrying its stage word. That every step runs in order under a guard is designed; building it is the next step.

The guards that prove these claims, and the one still in development, are in the next section.

## 5. The court

`agentfile_narrated` proves that an agent is a file judged when it is loaded: one refusal per gate, the missing coverage among them, and the model agent holding an effect refused by the same rule the graph uses. `service_registry_narrated` proves that a sandbox in production is a violation. `graphrule_object_narrated` proves the rule as an object attached to a model. `semantic_resolver_narrated` holds the Hausa path as its last scenario. At this commit it is in development. I ran it on 8 October 2026 and it stops with an error raised from a string-splitting function, after 29 assertions and before it reaches the Hausa ones; finishing that run is the next step. So the page counts its assertions and does not claim its verdict; the Hausa block ran on its own. `00_orgchart_narrated` proves the chart's positions, reporting lines and vacancy tracking; the BCEAO finding printed above comes from a sibling file, `14_bceao_governance.ring`.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

Coding agents lead in hands. They already read, search, edit and run. What they lack, by the site's own account, is a way to know an unfamiliar interface without guessing, to judge their work against rules and promises, and to present a change as an intention a person can review. Softanza's judges are built; the door between them and a coding agent is designed, and building it is the next step.

Low-code leads in installed base and in everything around it. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.) The organisation in the field keeps its vendor's suite for its accounts, its documents and its notifications. The crafter's platform sits beside the field's tools, not in place of them.

The messaging apps lead where the crafters already are. This is my view, not a result. The restaurateur's tool before the project was a spreadsheet; his own report of 4 July 2026 says so. The line that names messaging apps is the label of an option in the scoping form, and not his answer. No page of the site names those apps, and no conversational medium is a target of the platform yet. Chapter 25 is about that direction.

And the educator's door is at an earlier stage than the others. The course's editions in French, Arabic and Hausa are drafts that a reviewer has yet to read, and the first institution to adopt it is still to come.

The next section says what is built, what is designed and what is absent.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the agent file and its court; the registry that refuses a fake in production; rules as data on a model; a regulator's rules judging an organisation chart; the natural layer in Hausa and Arabic.

What is designed: a guard that fails when a step of a journey page breaks, each step run in order; the door through which a coding agent asks the library to judge its work.

What is absent: the platforms of the three customers running on the Softanza engine. Today the restaurant's platform is a set of web worlds, each a single file; the bank's is a Python application; the organisation's hub is a design. Their declarations are being written as Softanza's own. And the crafter's own medium, the messaging app on their phone, is not yet something a world can be declared to.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You declare intent in a library that reads like a sentence, and an engine computes under every call. You stay the first audience.

**The analyst.** You declare your organisation's entities, rules and flows in plain text, and the rules judge the model.

**The designer.** You declare a picture, a map or a screen as a program, and the first rule of an interface can come from a real trade: for a restaurateur, the speed of ordering.

**The decision-maker.** You declare your world, your governance, and what each actor may commit, and you own all of it, in plain text.

**The educator.** You declare a course in your learners' language, and every exercise is checked by running, in the site's words. The editions in French, Arabic and Hausa are drafts a reviewer has yet to read.

**The agent.** You declare yourself in a file: what you cover, how reversible your acts are. You get a workbench and a court. You do not get the capability to commit.

## 9. Exercise

Declare one agent for a world you know: a shop, a class, an office. Write its file: what it covers, how reversible its acts are, when it runs, one skill. Before you run it, write the promise you expect under the question below. Then run it.

```haro exercise
? StzAgentDeclarationQ(cYourAgent).IsValid()
#--> 1
```

> run: exercise

If the court refuses, read the rule it cites and change your file, not the court. Then make your agent a language model and give it an effect, and read why it is refused. You will not find the corrected file in this book.

I left this one in your hands. Take the graph-rule block of section 3, the one for the organisation in the field, and change one line: the writer's capabilities become `[ "inference" ]`, without `"effectful"`. Run it and read the last answer.

```haro exercise

oRule.SetDomainQ("agentic")
oRule.WhenQ("kind", "equals", "llm_actor")
oRule.WhenQ("capabilities", "contains", "effectful")
oRule.ThenViolationQ("an LLM proposes, only a pi-gate commits")
```

Then compare it with the answer printed in section 3, and say what the rule read to give each. Section 3 shows its two conditions. When you can say it, you will know which line of the model the rule reads.

Chapter 3 is the method, from first principles, and the sentence its method returns to is this: you declare a world in a language made for it, and you govern how that world changes.


=====

# The method, from first principles

Source: Chapter 3. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the court behind every block of this chapter but the truth one, and the stage words the site prints on every product; designed: the register of rethought concepts as data, with a guard that fails when a concept has no proof; absent: a page that shows each rethought concept with a run proof; a test file for the paradigm engine of section merging

Run: 5 of 5 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:13 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I show the method every chapter after this one follows. I take "from first principles", the field's slogan, and give it a narrow meaning: a concept, the field's form, a redesign in running code, and a court that judges it. I show the smallest rethink I know, the empty string, and then the two acts, declare and govern, in a few lines each. I say what I refused. First, a feature without a need, and general-purpose code as the meeting ground between people and machines. Then a title that looked back, the grid alone, and a reading called a census. The whole method returns to one sentence: you declare a world in a language made for it, and you govern how that world changes.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

In our industry, "from first principles" is a slogan. It means to reason from the bottom, not by analogy. You will hear it in talks and read it in launch posts, and you will rarely be shown what it produced.

The field does have a method for the decisions behind software, and it is a good one. A team writes a short record for each decision it takes, made up of the context, the decision and the consequences. Many teams call these architecture decision records, from a blog post Michael Nygard wrote in 2011. The field also has a method for proving what a function does: an example written under the function, which the tools can run as a test. Python has offered this since 2001 with its doctest module, which runs when someone invokes it, and Rust runs the examples in its documentation as tests by default. And when the field compares one tool with another, it draws a grid: rows of features, one column per tool, ticks and crosses.

The field has three forms; the method returns to one sentence.

## 2. The first principle

Here is the sentence the whole method returns to: **you declare a world in a language made for it, and you govern how that world changes.**

Two acts. Everything else in this book is one of them, or the two together.

## 3. The Softanza form

"From first principles" can be checked. In this book it has a narrow meaning. Take a concept everyone uses, write down how the field holds it, redesign it, and leave code that carries the redesign and a court that judges it. The site's history page is titled *From first principles*, and the library has been on GitHub since 12 March 2022. The phrase is not decoration here. It is a work order.

Let me show you the smallest rethink I know. Ask Python how many times the empty string occurs in the word "text", and it answers five: one before each letter and one at the end. Ask it whether "text" contains the empty string, and it says yes; JavaScript says the same. (Python 3.13 and Node 22, run on 8 October 2026.) That is a convention of implementers, not a fact anyone would say aloud. Softanza states a rule instead, and the rule has a name in the narration that argues it: emptiness is uncountable, and emptiness is uncontainable. What does Softanza answer to the same two questions? Run it:

```haro
? Q("text").Count("")
#--> 0
? Q("text").Contains("")
#--> FALSE
```

> run: kept

It answers `0` and `FALSE`. Two lines, and a decision you can disagree with. That is what a rethink looks like when it is honest: small enough to read, explicit enough to refuse.

A reading of the record, made in October 2026, counted forty-six such concepts, in seven families. The first two are the paradigms, such as scope-oriented programming, knowledge programming and reactive programming redesigned, and the paradigm engines, such as truth, quantifiers and time. The other five are patterns and reaction; expression and perception; data, time and reasoning; system, delivery and operations; and the library that knows itself. Forty-six is a reading and not a census; the site's own register page, read on 9 October 2026, says forty-seven. Each concept on it has an article or a design document of its own, and that was the test for entering the list.

Now the two acts, each in a few lines.

**Declare.** A kind of value is declared in a sentence, and every verdict against it is computed and explains itself. Why can't -4 be a score?

```haro
ConstrainQ("score").ToBeQ(:Number).AndQ().ToBeQ(:Positive).AndQ().ToBeQ(:Even)
? CanQ(42).BeA("score")
#--> TRUE
? CanQ(-4).BeA("score")
#--> FALSE
? Why()
#--> no: -4 is not positive (constraint 2 of 'score')
```

> run: kept

Notice the last line: `no: -4 is not positive (constraint 2 of 'score')`. The verdict names the rule it applied, by its number! A verdict on a declared kind that cannot say why is not admitted in this house.

**Govern.** A declared constraint, once enforced, refuses a change before it lands. Here the string "hello" must stay lowercase. Does the update to "HELLO" land?

```haro
oName = new stzString("hello")
oName.EnforceConstraint("keep-lowercase", :Lowercase)
bRefused = FALSE
try
	oName.UpdateWith("HELLO")
catch
	bRefused = TRUE
done
? bRefused
#--> TRUE
? oName.Content()
#--> hello
```

> run: kept

The update was refused! The object stands as it was: it still answers `hello`. Declaring a constraint and enforcing it are two acts, and the guard behind this block checks that a constraint you only declared stays passive.

Governing also has a side that faces the person. The system asks, and it does not let you close a world that is not complete. What does it say when you try to conclude anyway?

```haro
oKB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("restaurant")
oKB.Know("margherita", "dish").Know("tiramisu", "dish")
oKB.AddConversationQ("setup").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"))
aQ = oKB.AskInXT("setup")
? aQ[:why]
#--> every dish needs 'contains'
try
	oKB.ConcludeIn("setup", "world")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Can't conclude: 2 gap(s) remain -- the wise-coding loop is not done. Ask the next question.
```

> run: kept

The goal was declared: every dish must say what it contains. The gap between the goal and what is known generates the question, and the question carries its reason. Concluding while two gaps remain is refused! The printed line is `Can't conclude: 2 gap(s) remain -- the wise-coding loop is not done. Ask the next question.`, and its last words tell you what to do next. Chapter 18 is about this conversation. Here it is enough to see that governing is not only refusing; it is asking.

One more rethink, because the paradigm engines are part of the method too. Truth, in the language beneath Softanza, is a matter of empty and non-empty, and a list of words counts as true. Softanza keeps that truth by default and lets you extend it by declaration. Once "wrong" is declared an item that makes a list false, is a list that holds it still true?

```haro
SetItemsMakingAListFalse([ "false", "wrong", "dangerous" ])
? IsTrue([ "hello", "this", "is", "wrong" ])
#--> TRUE
? IsTrueXT([ "hello", "this", "is", "wrong" ])
#--> FALSE
```

> run: kept

The host's answer is unchanged: `TRUE`. The extended answer reads your declaration and says `FALSE`! "Truth becomes a matter of policy", the narration says, and the policy is written where you can read it.

So this is the method, in the form you will meet in every chapter after this one. A concept as the field holds it. The one principle the record returns to. The redesign, in running code, with its promise under each block. The decision, and what was refused. The court that proves it, by name. The field, and where it leads. The limit, in three stage words. The first, **built**, means the code exists, a guard exists, and a block ran. The second, **designed**, covers what the site calls in construction, specification and ratified proposal. The third, **absent**, covers a name with nothing behind it yet, and what nobody has named. Then six doors, one for each kind of reader, and one exercise.

And the book is judged the way the library is. Every block in it ran inside the library on the night the page was built, at the commit printed at the top of the page. A page whose promises are not kept does not build.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

There is no single record of a decision called "the method". The method was decided in pieces, and the pieces are in the record.

**I refused to add a feature without a need.** I wrote it in an article of the library, which carries no date. "I introduce new features only when they solve a tangible problem or when I anticipate they'll meet developers' needs." First principles without that rule produce a museum. That is my view, not a result. With it, a rethink has to earn its place: someone had a problem the field's form did not answer.

**I refused general-purpose code as the meeting ground between people and machines.** The vision of the platform says it in one line, which I ratified. Humans and AIs meet in declared languages, small, closed and judged, and not in general-purpose code. Declaring is the first act because it makes the second possible. A court can only judge a sentence of a language it knows.

**I refused a title that looked back.** When this book was first proposed, its title was *Programming by Heart*, the slogan of the 2020 presentation. I turned it down as outdated: it named a stance, and the book had to name what was rethought. The next proposal was *Declare, Then Govern*. I kept the site's own sentence for the cover instead (on 8 October 2026 I changed one word of it, for *crafters*). The two verbs moved to the back cover, where you will find them. They organise every chapter; they did not need to be its name.

**I refused the grid alone.** The library's documents hold dozens of comparison grids, and in them Softanza's column wins almost every row. A reader who sees only those grids is right to ask for more than a grid. The honesty in the record is elsewhere: in the sections of the field studies titled "what the field has that Softanza lacks, honestly", and in measured numbers. When the runtime beneath Softanza was measured again in September 2026 against the one it was compared with, the earlier headline did not reproduce. The loss was recorded the same day, in an internal memo. That is the rule this book follows: a loss is printed beside the win, in the same section.

**I refused to call a reading a census.** The forty-six concepts are a reading of the record by one session, not a list I ruled row by row. Appendix A prints the register of forty-six rows as the study gives it, with the book's own column, as a working appendix: the rows are not ruled one by one.

Those are the five refusals; now the court that judges the blocks above.

## 5. The court

The three blocks of the two acts stand on two guards. `modality_narrated` proves that a declared kind can be inspected, that a verdict names the constraint that failed, and that an unknown kind never silently allows. It also proves that an enforced constraint refuses a bad update before it lands, while a declared one stays passive. `wisecoding_narrated` proves the conversation. It proves gaps computed from the declared goal, a question that says why it is asked, answers admitted, and a law refusing through the conversation and reaching a person. And it proves that concluding with open gaps is refused. Its sister, `wisecoding_rich_narrated`, carries the richer cases of the same loop.

The rethink of emptiness stands on `612_dealing_in_emptiness_in_softanza.ring`, a library test written as scenarios, each line stating the result it expects. The rethink of truth stands on its narration and on this book's run, and a guard for it is the next step. The library's file for it, `04_truth_in_softanza.ring`, sets the list of false words by assignment and never calls the function, so it does not prove the block.

The court has been named; next, the field, and where it leads.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in adoption. Decision records are a light standard that many teams use, with templates and tools around them. The decision element of this book exists in this book and in one study of the record, and not yet as data in the library. Examples in documentation can be run by the standard tool of Python, and Rust runs them by default. The promises of Softanza are judged by the house's own runners, which no outside tool reads yet. And the field compares by grid, as the record's own pages also do; a grid alone is a start, which is why section 4 asks for the loss beside the win.

Where I think Softanza stands apart is in one place only: the rethink is carried all the way down. I hold this as a view, not a result. The decision is written, the redesign runs, the promise is judged at build, and the stage word says how far each piece has gone. A decision record does not run. An example does not say what was refused. Here they are one object.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the court behind every block of this chapter but the truth one, and the five stage words the site prints on each product.

What is designed: the register of rethought concepts as data, one row per concept with its article, its code, its guard and its stage. Beside it, a guard that fails when a new concept arrives without a row. Both are a specification today.

What is absent: a page that shows each concept with a run proof (the site has a register page, read on 9 October 2026, that lists each with its evidence). A test file for the engine module of one paradigm engine, section merging: it has none of its own, though its merge methods run in `base/test/string/289_content.ring` and `max/test/stzListOfPairsTest.ring`. And a few rethinks where the article goes a step beyond the code of today. The article on quantifiers asks whether all your marks `AreGreaterThan` the average, and that method is spelled differently in the library today. Its friendlier quantifiers, some, most and few, draw at random, so they carry no promise today. The register is a reading, and a reading can miss a concept that lives in only one article.

That is where this chapter stops; six doors follow, one for each kind of reader.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Every claim you make can carry its promise. An enforced constraint refuses a bad update at the object's update point (`Update()` for a string or a number, `_SetContent()` for a list).

**The analyst.** Your rule is a declaration, and its verdict names the rule that failed, by number.

**The designer.** The stage word is part of the interface. A product that does not exist yet says so, in a word you can check.

**The decision-maker.** Ask every vendor for the loss beside the win. Chapter 1 prints one, with its date: the runtime beneath Softanza trails the one it was compared with by 1.04 to 1.88 times, measured on 13 September 2026.

**The educator.** Each rethink has an article that argues it: the concept, the field's form, the redesign. That is a curriculum, ordered by family.

**The agent.** You learn this platform from the platform, and you are corrected by it. A verdict that says why is written for you as much as for a person.

## 9. Exercise

Choose one default of your own language that you never questioned: what the empty string counts to, what counts as true, what "most" means. Before you run anything, write in one sentence the rule you think is right. Then write two promises in the form you have met: the line your language prints today, and the line your rule must print.

```haro exercise
? YourDefault(the_input)
#--> what your language prints today
? YourRule(the_input)
#--> what your declared rule must print
```

> run: exercise

Run both. I will not tell you which rule is right. If your rule and your language disagree, you have found a decision that someone took for you, and you can now take it yourself.

I left this one in your hands, on purpose. Take the block of section 3 that governs a string, change one word, `EnforceConstraint` for `AddConstraint`, and run it as it stands below. I print no output for it.

```haro exercise
oName = new stzString("hello")
oName.AddConstraint("keep-lowercase", :Lowercase)
bRefused = FALSE
try
	oName.UpdateWith("HELLO")
catch
	bRefused = TRUE
done
? bRefused
? oName.Content()
```

> run: exercise

Look at what it prints: whether the update was refused, and what the object holds. Then read section 3, just after the block, and section 5, on the guard that checks it: the page says why. When you have both lines, you will know why a declared constraint is not an enforced one.

Chapter 4 draws the platform in one picture, under one idea at every altitude: nothing is believed, everything is judged.


=====

# The platform in one picture

Source: Chapter 4. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the engine and its libraries; the base layer; worlds, constellations and platforms with their guards; the engine subset a part ships, for the web; designed: the max layer loaded by its entry file; the base layer building on the core one; a loader for one domain of base alone; a court that checks the layers against their contract; absent: a whole program and its engine packed into one file; a customer platform on the engine

Run: 8 of 8 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:13 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I draw the whole platform in one picture, read from the bottom up: the engine, the layers, the areas, the worlds, the crafters, and beside every floor its court. I walk it floor by floor, with one block at each floor that you can run. Then I say what I refused along the way: computation in the faces, a flag to choose a layer, mini-applications. The picture is summed up in one idea at every altitude: nothing is believed, everything is judged.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field draws a platform as a stack of boxes. The kernel at the bottom, a runtime and its libraries in the middle, applications on top, and sometimes a column at the side for security or tools. Inside one codebase the same picture becomes a rule about dependencies: an inner layer knows nothing of the outer ones, and the arrows only point inward. Rust's three standard crates, one for bare metal, one for memory, one for a full operating system, are the cleanest version I know. Three depths of the same language, chosen by what the target offers.

For applications, the field's picture is a host. A large application embeds a runtime and runs small applications inside it, which share its services and its store. In both pictures the judge is outside: a test suite beside the code, a review beside the store. In the pictures I know, neither draws the court as a column standing at every floor.

Softanza draws that column, and one idea holds the whole picture.

## 2. The first principle

The stack of the platform is summed up, in the vision I ratified, in one idea at every altitude: **nothing is believed, everything is judged.**

## 3. The Softanza form

Here is the whole platform in one picture, read from the bottom up. Each floor stands on the one beneath it, and each floor is judged by a court beside it. The numbers in it are not typed by me. The build reads them, when this page is made, from the library and from the site's map of its areas.

{{fig:platform-one-picture|The platform in one picture. Each floor stands on the one beneath it; each is judged by the court beside it; the architect works across all of them. The numbers are read at build from the library's engine, its test tree and the site's Atlas.}}

Let me walk it, floor by floor, with one block at each floor that you can run.

**The engine.** At the bottom is an engine written in Zig: a set of libraries behind a C interface, one per domain, grouped by the layer that loads them. By the engine's design rule, the engine does the computation; the library above it gives it a vocabulary. A small example makes the division visible. Ask the language beneath Softanza for the length of the Arabic word for peace, then ask the engine. What does each one count? Run it:

```haro
? len("سلام")
#--> 8
? Q("سلام").NumberOfChars()
#--> 4
? Q("مرحبا بالعالم").Script()
#--> arabic
```

> run: kept

The language counts bytes and answers `8`. The engine counts letters and answers `4`, and it knows the script: `arabic`. Four letters, not eight!

The engine also has its own door, beneath every class. Numbers too large for the machine's arithmetic are added exactly, by the engine itself:

```haro
a = StzEngineBigIntFromString("99999999999999999999999999999999")
? StzEngineBigIntToString( StzEngineBigIntAdd(a, StzEngineBigIntFromString("1")) )
#--> 100000000000000000000000000000000
```

> run: kept

The engine answers `100000000000000000000000000000000`, the exact sum.

**The layers.** Above the engine, the library is cut in three layers, by depth of capability. Core, with the prefix `stk`, is lean and talks to the engine directly, for small devices and small binaries. Base, `stz`, is the rich layer, where the vocabulary and its forms live, enough for most solutions. Max, `stx`, is meant for the strongest innovations. A layer is chosen by the file a program loads. Does the same verb mean the same act at core and base? Run it:

```haro
o1 = new stkList([ "one", "and", "one", "makes", "two", "ones" ])
? o1.FindFirst("one")
#--> 1
o2 = new stzList([ "one", "and", "one", "makes", "two", "ones" ])
? o2.FindFirst("one")
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Both answer `1`: the same verb, the same act, at two depths.

**The areas.** Across the layers, the library is mapped by the site's Atlas into areas grouped in bands, and each area into lanes. Each lane is rated from emerging to strong, most of them with the line that proves it. The Atlas says what exists, and how well. The engine's design rule is that a verb means the same act on every structure that admits it. Does it hold for one pair, a string and a list? Run it:

```haro
? @@( Q("tea rice tea").FindAll("tea") )
#--> [ 1, 10 ]
? @@( Q([ "tea", "rice", "tea" ]).FindAll("tea") )
#--> [ 1, 3 ]
```

> run: kept

On the string, `FindAll` answers character positions: `[ 1, 10 ]`. On the list, it answers item positions: `[ 1, 3 ]`. The same act, on two structures.

**The worlds.** Above the library, the unit is not a module but a world: an application is a living world of meaning, with its things, its relations and its rules. A world is declared. What does it say of itself when you ask it to explain? Run it:

```haro
oApp = new stzApp("FieldVisits")
oApp {
    AddThingQ(:Account) { Has([ :number, :chapter, :balance ])  IsTrue(:balance, "@ >= 0") }
    AddThingQ(:Client)  { Has([ :code, :name, :city ])  Owns(:Account) }
    AddRelation(:Account, :belongsTo, :Client)
}
oApp.Explain()
#--> WORLD FieldVisits   lives in: memory (not persisted)
#      BEING
#        account (number, chapter, balance)
#            true when balance @ >= 0
#        client (code, name, city)
#      RELATIONS
#        client owns account
#        account belongsto client
oApp.Live()
#--> [FieldVisits] is live -- 2 thing(s), 0 flow(s), 0 reaction(s), 0 goal(s); 0 proposal(s)
```

> run: kept

It answers `WORLD FieldVisits   lives in: memory (not persisted)`, then lists its being and its relations. `Live()` answers `[FieldVisits] is live -- 2 thing(s), 0 flow(s), 0 reaction(s), 0 goal(s); 0 proposal(s)`. Two things, and no flow, no reaction, no goal. The world says what was declared, and no more.

Worlds compose into a constellation. Here a restaurant and its produce supplier are two worlds, and a bond between them declares the one crossing that is allowed. What becomes of a crossing that no bond declares? Run it:

```haro
oResto = StzAppQ("resto")
oResto.AddThingQ(:dish) { Has([ :name, :price ]) }
oSupplier = StzAppQ("supplier")
oSupplier.AddThingQ(:crate) { Has([ :produce, :qty ]) }
oCon = new stzSuperApp("acme-group")
oCon.AddWorld("resto", oResto)
oCon.AddWorld("supplier", oSupplier)
oCon.Bond("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
oCon.GovDeclareRisk("order-produce", 2)
oCon.GovGrant("resto", "order-produce")
oCon.GovSetAuthority("resto", :Delegated)
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 1
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "raid-kitchen")
#--> 0
? oCon.Why()
#--> no bond declares 'raid-kitchen' from 'resto' to 'supplier'.
```

> run: kept

The declared crossing answers `1`. The other answers `0`, refused! The constellation gives its reason in one line: `no bond declares 'raid-kitchen' from 'resto' to 'supplier'.` Every other crossing is refused with its reason.

The commons is the shared ground of a constellation, what every world of it stands on. It holds an identity, a session and a store. It is a world like the others, world zero, and not a node of a special kind. Chapter 23 shows it and says how far the code has come in that direction.

And a platform is the envelope around a solution: its parts, the systems they run on, and the order in which it is built, rehearsed and deployed. You will see it refuse to deploy before it is built in chapter 33. Here is what it does with the layers, for the web: a part of a solution ships only the engine groups it needs, and only those that are Softanza's own. The phone below asks for four capabilities. How many engine groups does it ship? Run it:

```haro
oDelivery = new stzDelivery("my-shop")
oDelivery.AddSuperApp(:phone, :Android)
oDelivery.AddBackend(:api, :LinuxServer)
oDelivery.NeedsIn(:phone, [ :Unicode, :PivotTable, :ConstraintSolver, :Regex ])
oPlan = oDelivery.Plan()
? @@( oPlan.EngineCapsFor(:phone) )
#--> [ "pivottable", "constraintsolver" ]
? @@( StzWasmGroupsFor( oPlan.EngineCapsFor(:phone) ) )
#--> [ "aggregation", "solver" ]
```

> run: kept

Two, not four: `aggregation` and `solver`! The phone's own system handles one of the other two, Unicode, and Softanza ships the other, Regex, as a construct in the target's own language.

**The crafters.** At the top are the six doors of chapter 2: the programmer, the analyst, the designer, the decision-maker, the educator and the agent. And one person works across every floor, from the engine to the organisation: the platform architect. The record names that person in three unrelated places, and no other role crosses every band of the Atlas.

**The court.** Beside every floor stands its judge. The engine has its tests and the contracts between the floors. The layers have the narrated guards. Most lanes in the areas carry a proof line. The worlds have their bonds and norms, the order of build and deploy, and the refusal of a fake in production. The crafters' interfaces have a constitution of verbs and rules that every element must trace to. And above all of it, the platform's threat model lists its guarantees, each tied to the guard that proves it. Its own guard fails when a row names a guard that no longer exists.

Parts II to IV of this book are rethinks, and each of them is a box of this picture. Part V assembles the picture in use.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused computation in the faces.** The engine is the product. The language above it adds beauty, not computation: thousands of methods that read like sentences, and the algorithms beneath them in one place. The rule is that every feature is implemented once, in the engine. The migration is in development. The layer contract lists number, list, object, graph, stats and natural as still in the host language the library was first written for.

**I removed the old framework, with no fallback.** The library sat on a large C++ framework for its text, its dates and its files. When the engine replaced it, every path through the framework was removed: no fallback, no feature flag kept in case.

**I chose sovereignty as a verdict on each dependency, not as "every line ours".** The engine vendors and pins a set of third-party libraries, the figure counts them. The record defines the word in one line: nothing anyone else can withdraw. That is the test. A library whose source is pinned in the tree passes it.

**I refused a flag to choose a layer.** The first way of choosing a layer was a flag. It could never work, because the language beneath loads every file it sees at compile time, so all three layers loaded on every call. The failure of one was the failure of all. The flag was removed rather than kept as a switch that silently does nothing. The reason was written where the next reader would stand: a layer is chosen by the file you load.

**I let everything go into base, and I say so.** Under the pressure of time, the domains landed in base. The engine, the planes such as security and performance, and the customers moved forward meanwhile. That was the agile choice, and the next step is to place each domain in its layer, in plain sight. So the figure draws base widest, and chapter 27 gives the numbers.

**I refused mini-applications.** The field's super-app is a host that runs small applications. Softanza's is a governed graph of worlds: worlds, not mini-apps; bonds and norms, not a container.

Five guards stand beside the floors; the next section names what each proves.

## 5. The court

`00_stzapp_narrated` proves a world declared and made visible. `06_stzsuperapp_narrated` proves the constellation: worlds joining, the commons as world zero, crossings gated by norms with the bond named as the reason, a retired world breaking its bonds. `full_scope_model_narrated`, the architect's common ground, proves the solution modelled first, the scopes that refuse what a target forbids, and the order of build and deploy. `01_platform_narrated` proves the platform's duties. `threat_model_narrated` reads the platform's threat model and fails when a guarantee names a guard that no longer exists.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

Rust's three depths are enforced by its compiler and used every day on bare metal. Softanza's rule is held at the engine and at the entry files, and not yet carried to where base should build on core, as section 7 shows.

The field's text libraries lead on locale: Softanza's own map says that the clear gap of its strings is the international components' locale layer. Its locale area is a deliberate bet against those components: no collation, no plural rules and no time zones.

At the scale of real users, the field's super-app hosts lead today. The record names them as the model it refused and measures nothing against them, so I will not either.

And the Atlas itself prints what is still to come for Softanza. The binary area is a plan for now, and its shipped code is the next step; the plugin, foreign-function and embed plane of extensibility is mostly planned; the system area has few lanes rated strong so far. I hold this as a view, not a result. A map that prints its stages is the only kind worth reading.

## 7. The limit

Now I put the three stage words on the picture, built, designed and absent.

What is built: the engine and its libraries, the base layer, worlds, constellations and platforms with their guards, and, for the web, the engine subset a part ships.

What is designed, or built and not reachable. The max layer's own classes are written and loaded by no entry file. What does the runtime answer when you ask for one? Run it:

```haro
try
	o3 = new stxString("one and one")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Error (R11) : Error in class name, class not found: stxstring
```

> run: kept

The runtime answers: `Error in class name, class not found: stxstring`. The class is written, and no entry file loads it!

The layer contract says the base layer builds on core, by inheritance or delegation. It does neither for the common domains: the two share the engine and implement those domains twice. The core layer itself is in development against the engine today. Its string and character classes call engine names that no engine file registers, as the site study of the book's roots reports. The block in section 3 shows lists only. A loader that brings one domain of base without the others is not built. A court that reads the layer contract against the tree and fails on drift is a specification, proposed in that site study. What is built is two implementations sharing one engine; the contract's line that base builds on core is designed.

What is absent: a whole program and its engine packed into one file with no dependency, and a customer platform running on the engine.

## 8. Six doors

Each of the six doors reads this picture from where it stands.

**The programmer.** You choose your depth by the file you load, and one verb means the same act on a string and on a list.

**The analyst.** The Atlas tells you, lane by lane, where the platform is strong and where it is emerging. Build on the strong lanes.

**The designer.** Your interface has a court of its own, the constitution's verbs and rules, in the same column as the code's guards.

**The decision-maker.** Ask two questions of every floor: what stands beneath it, and who judges it. The engine's answer to the first is a written list of pinned libraries.

**The educator.** The picture is the book's map. Each rethink in Parts II to IV is one box of it.

**The agent.** The picture exists as data you can read, the Atlas as a file, and every floor you write to has a court that will read you.

## 9. Exercise

Draw your own solution the way this chapter draws Softanza: floors, and a court beside each. For each box, write the file that declares it and the guard that judges it, and put a stage word on it: built, designed or absent.

Then declare two parts of it with `stzPlatformProfile`, and before you run anything, write the line you expect when you ask it to deploy before you build it. Run it. A box with no court beside it is your first finding.

I left this one in your hands. Section 7 says the core layer's string classes are still in development against the engine, and the block of section 3 shows you lists only. Take the block of section 7 and change one word: `stxString` becomes `stkString`.

```haro exercise
try
	o3 = new stkString("one and one")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
```

> run: exercise
