Run it, and read what the runtime answers. Then go back to section 7 and find which name is called, and who should have registered it. When you have solved it, you will know why the layer contract's line that base builds on core is called designed, and not built.

The next chapter leaves the picture for the code written inside it, and states the first law: the code adheres to the way a person thinks, and the court judges it whoever wrote it. Its principle is one sentence: what you think is what you write.


=====

# Law: the code adheres to the way a person thinks, and the court judges it whoever wrote it

Source: Chapter 5. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the forms of the vocabulary in thousands of method names; the house rules as a court over the library's own code; the promise read by a machine in the house's own form; designed: a court of style for the code a programmer or an agent writes; the writing grammar declared in Haro, so a model can only write in it; absent: any machine that judges today the style of a program written with the library

Run: 7 of 7 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:13 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take the two things the field calls coding style and ask the question it does not ask: whether a line follows the order in which its writer reasoned. I run the founding example in the nearest form that still runs, then the mental model on a list, the verb's forms, the named parameter, the scene and the history of a chain. A promise stands under every question. I say what I let go, the library's own implementation, and what I refused to let go, the style of the code written with it. I show the court that exists today, over the library's own classes, and the one that is only designed. The motto has not changed since 2020: what you think is what you write.

## What the AI age changes here

For years, most of the code written with Softanza was written by one hand, and the hand was mine. The style of the library was the way I wrote. Then the machines arrived, and today most code, here as everywhere, is written by models. The design of the intelligence plane says it in one sentence: most programming today is done by agents and language models, not humans.

That changes what a style is. A style that lives in one person's habits dies when that person stops writing every line. It has to become something a machine can be held to, and something a machine can check, or it thins away. In this house it did thin, and I will show you where. So this movement of the book, Programming rethought, opens on a law that has two halves. The second half exists because of the age: the code adheres to the way a person thinks, and the court judges it whoever wrote it. By the court I mean the machine that judges code against the house's rules; today it judges only the library's own classes, and section 5 says how.

There is a dependency to say aloud. This book follows the order of its cover, from the familiar to the new: programming, then languages, then intelligence. The record's own logic runs the other way. The first act is to declare a language; programming happens inside a declared language; intelligence is governed by both. The vocabulary's forms are already declared in a spike of Haro (its charter, entry of 3 October 2026). The usage that this chapter describes, the scene, the chain, the named parameter, the promise under each line, is meant to be declared too. So read this law knowing that Part III holds it up.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

In our industry, "coding style" means two things, and neither is about how a person thinks. The first is layout and naming, enforced by machines: Python's style guide since 2001, Go's formatter since 2009, and the formatters that every modern project runs before a commit. They settle arguments about spaces and capital letters, and they are good at it. The second is the fluent interface: methods chained so that a line reads almost like a sentence.

The age of generated code added a third practice: check after. A model writes, the tests and the linters read what it wrote, and the model tries again if they complain. The line itself is judged for its shape and its result. Whether it follows the order in which the writer reasoned is a question I know no tool to ask.

## 2. The first principle

My motto for this has not changed since 2020: **what you think is what you write.**

Section 3 shows the motto at work.

## 3. The Softanza form

The founding slide put the whole idea on one page. On the left, a person's four steps of thought: I take the word "softanza"; I turn it to uppercase; I get its characters; I join them with a space. On the right, the same four steps as one line of code, as if the text on the left had been pasted and run.

That exact line no longer runs. Its third link, a method that turned the characters into a list of strings in one step, is not in the library today. It survives only in archived files and in one scratch sample, as `CharsQRT`. I tell you this first because a law chapter that hides its own founding example would not deserve your trust. What is the nearest form that runs, with the four steps still visible, and what does the library offer as one dedicated verb? Run both:

```haro
? StzListOfStringsQ( StzStringQ("softanza").UppercaseQ().Chars() ).ConcatenatedUsing(" ")
#--> S O F T A N Z A
? StzStringQ("softanza").UppercaseQ().Spacified()
#--> S O F T A N Z A
```

> run: kept

Both forms print `S O F T A N Z A`. The idea is intact; the fluent link was lost. A request goes to the library to restore it.

The mental model is the same idea at the level of a problem. You say the problem in plain words. You select the object that holds the data. Then you ask, in order: does it contain what I care about, how many, where? Then you act on those positions, or you call the verb that does it for you. Take a list with empty strings in it. Does it contain them, how many, and where? Run it:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ "A", "", "B", "", "", "C" ])
? o1.ContainsEmptyStrings()
#--> TRUE
? o1.CountEmptyStrings()
#--> 3
? @@( o1.FindEmptyStrings() )
#--> [ 2, 4, 5 ]
o1.ReplaceEmptyStrings(:With = "~")
? @@( o1.Content() )
#--> [ "A", "~", "B", "~", "~", "C" ]
```

> run: kept

The list answers `TRUE`, then `3`, then `[ 2, 4, 5 ]`, and after the act it reads `[ "A", "~", "B", "~", "~", "C" ]`. Contain, count, find, act. Each line is one step of thought, and the names are the words you would use to say it.

The verb's form says what happens to the object. What does each form do to it? Run them on one string:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("RIxxNxG")
? o1.Removed("x")
#--> RING
? o1.Content()
#--> RIxxNxG
o1.Remove("x")
? o1.Content()
#--> RING
? Q("rixxnxg").RemoveQ("x").UppercaseQ().Spacified()
#--> R I N G
```

> run: kept (touches files)

`Removed` printed `RING`, and the object still read `RIxxNxG`! `Remove` then acted on it, and `Content()` read `RING`. The chain with Q ended on `R I N G`. By the library's convention, the past participle gives you a changed copy and leaves the object alone. The plain verb acts on the object. The verb with Q acts and hands the object back, so the next step can follow.

Do you need to remember which argument comes second? A parameter can be named, and the name reads as English:

```haro
? Q("I love Haro").Replaced("love", :With = "♥")
#--> I ♥ Haro
o2 = new stzList([ "tea", "rice", "fish" ])
o2.SwapItems(:AtPositions = 1, :And = 3)
? @@( o2.Content() )
#--> [ "fish", "rice", "tea" ]
```

> run: kept

The text prints `I ♥ Haro` and the list `[ "fish", "rice", "tea" ]`. The names `:With`, `:AtPositions` and `:And` say which argument is which.

How do several questions about one thing read as a list? They are asked inside its scene, a block of braces around the object. The object is named once:

```haro
Q("haro php HARO ruby Haro") {
	? NumberOfOccurrence(:Of = "haro")
	#--> 1
	? NumberOfOccurrenceCS(:Of = "haro", :CS = FALSE)
	#--> 3
	? @@( FindAllCS("haro", FALSE) )
	#--> [ 1, 10, 20 ]
}
```

> run: kept

The scene answers `1`, then `3`, then `[ 1, 10, 20 ]`. The same word counted `1`, then `3` once `:CS = FALSE` was said! The object was named once, and the three questions read as a list.

Can the steps of thought be read back? An object lifted with history keeps the result of each step of its chain:

```haro
? @@( Qh(12500).AddQ(500).RetrieveQ(1500).DivideByQ(500).MultiplyByQ(2).History() )
#--> [ 12500, 13000, 11500, 23, 46 ]
```

> run: kept

The run printed `[ 12500, 13000, 11500, 23, 46 ]`. Five values, the starting one included, and the chain ends at `46`! The article that first told this promised four values, without the first, and a final value one unit short, 45. The guard behind the block states today's rule, on a string chain: the initial value, then each step's result. The book takes the run's answer, not the article's.

Notice the line under every question. It is not a comment. It is a promise, what the line must print. In this house it has been the form of an example from the first day: a short story, a few questions, and the answers written under them. That is the first half of the law, made visible: the code follows the order of thought, and each step says what it expects.

Now the second half. A rule a person must remember is folklore; I hold this as a view, not a result. The library turns its house rules into validators, so that a person or an agent can check a class before it lands. Five of the fourteen run over the graph of the code. Here a class breaks the house's convention that a verb ending in Q returns the object. This rule needs the return statements, which the graph does not model, so it runs as a text pass. What does the court say of the class? Run it:

```haro
cCode = "class Basket" + nl + "def Add(x)" + nl + "	return x" + nl + "def AddQ(x)" + nl + "	return 42" + nl
? StzCodeIsClean(cCode)
#--> FALSE
? StzCheckCode(cCode)[1][:rule]
#--> q-returns-object
```

> run: kept

The court answers `FALSE`, and names the rule: `q-returns-object`. It did not ask who wrote the class! That is the whole of the second half. And the promise under each question is read by a machine too: the library's runner judges a promise as a claim about what the line printed. The house's oldest form of example is also its newest form of test.

The chapters of this part prove the law, each from one side. Chapter 6 tells the mental model and the four metaphors that replace loops: walk, check, yield, perform. Chapter 7 tells the writing style as fourteen rules of its own, not the fourteen house rules of the court below, and the seven design goals as its architectural half. Chapter 8 tells the forms a function's name can take, and scope named in the verb. Chapter 9 tells patterns of thought: the machine that reacts to what it recognises, reactive programming in plain words, and the timeline, the grid and deep operations. Chapter 10 tells how programming becomes refinement.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I let the library's implementation go.** The code the models wrote inside the library is accepted as good. It does not look like mine. Its local variables carry underscores on both sides, hundreds of times per thousand lines. They are a safeguard. The language beneath Softanza lets a library's local variable overwrite a caller's global, and the convention prevents it. I did not ask for the old style there, and I would refuse a campaign to impose it.

**I refused to let the style of the code written with Softanza go.** Every line a programmer, a person or an agent, writes with the library must take the historic style. That style is the scene, the chain one step per line, the named parameter, the verb's form, and the promise under each question. That is the line I drew when I saw what had happened.

What had happened is measurable. A study of the record counted the promise under each line, per thousand lines. In the short recipes the house keeps, there are nearly a hundred. In my articles, seventy-five. In the classic tests, fifty-two. In the guards the sessions wrote, under one. They assert in another form, a call that names the expectation. They adopted it because the bare comparisons of the older suites printed 0 or 1, and that does not tell a block that failed from a block that never ran. That gap is closed now; the runner reads the promise as a claim. The study that counted them recommends one form for new test blocks: the classic form, a title, the questions and the promises under them. The converted blocks are left as they are. The record holds that recommendation, and no ruling of mine on it.

**I refused to rewrite code when the language changed its name.** When the language beneath became Haro, the code in my articles was renamed and nothing else. The forms are the asset; the name is a label.

**The forms of the vocabulary were made data, and three old features were refused as grammar.** In Haro, the forms a verb can take are declared once and generated. In the language's spike, the library's shipped names are judged against that declaration. Its charter reports 584 of the 595 shipped spellings of the string class in agreement (entry of 3 October 2026). The design of that work refuses three things the record counted as features: names that tolerate a misspelling, parameters in any order, and method names in other human languages. The founding deck counted the last two; the narration of the forms counted the first. Misspelling tolerance and free order stay in the library, in part; the multilingual names survive only in an archived file. None of the three is part of the grammar a machine is held to. The refusal is written in the language's principles file, as its seventh principle, marked designed. The book prints it as the design's, and the record does not say it is mine.

**I chose goals before laws.** The 2022 design held seven goals, expressiveness, flexibility, reliability, consistency, human metaphors, practical abstractions and manageability, and every feature served one. The operating rules of today are laws paid for by incidents, which say how and not why. Chapter 7 puts the goals back beside the laws.

## 5. The court

`coderule_narrated` proves that a house rule is a rule over the code graph and that the findings come out on planted code. It proves that clean code passes, and that the library's house rules are fourteen. `testoor_promises_narrated` proves that the promise is judged by a machine in the house's own form. Each rule is planted both ways: the lie it must catch, and the truth it must not accuse. `05_containsemptystrings` is the mental model's steps on the same list, a classic block kept as a guard. `466_keeping_the_history_of_updates_of_a_softanza_objec` proves the history of a chain.

Section 6 sets the court beside the field.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in enforcement. Most mainstream languages have a formatter and a linter, and most projects run them before a commit or in continuous integration. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.) The court that would judge the style of a program written with Softanza is a specification; the one that exists judges the library's own classes.

The field leads in reach and in people. There is a labour market for its tools and none yet for this one, and the field's generators are the largest that exist.

The field also leads, today, over Softanza's own pages. The study that measured the record found that the site's code blocks carry no named parameter and no scene: a reduced dialect of the library, shown to every visitor. An agent that learned from the site would learn that dialect. I did not measure the site's blocks again. A style that is not shown is not learned.

Where I think Softanza stands apart is in what the style is tied to. A formatter judges the shape of a line; a fluent interface makes a line pleasant. Here the line is tied to a step of thought, and the verb's form is a contract about the object. The promise under the line is the example, the test and the documentation at once.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the forms of the vocabulary, in thousands of method names; the house rules as a court over the library's own code; the promise judged by a machine.

What is designed: a court of style for the code a programmer or an agent writes with the library. It would read a positional argument where a named one exists, a question with no promise, a chain on one line past two steps. And the writing grammar declared in Haro, so that a model's output is held to it before it is written.

What is absent: any machine that judges, today, the style of a program written with the library. The founding slide's chain does not run as written. In this book the discipline carries the name of its motto: what you think is what you write.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Your lines follow your reasoning: select, contain, count, find, act. Each question carries its promise, and a court reads it whether you wrote it or an agent did.

**The analyst.** The method starts from your problem in plain words. The keywords you use become the names of the steps.

**The designer.** The verb's form is an interface contract: by the library's convention, the past participle does not touch the object. A court for that rule is designed, not built.

**The decision-maker.** I hold this as a view, not a result: a style a machine can be held to can be reviewed goal by goal, not line by line. It is the only reading one person can give to hundreds of thousands of lines.

**The educator.** Teach the discipline whole, and in order: formulate, then express, then be judged.

**The agent.** You will be corrected by the library, not by folklore. Write the question, then the promise under it.

## 9. Exercise

Take the list `[ "tea", "", "rice", "", "fish", "" ]`. Write the questions of the mental model in order, one line each: does it contain empty strings, how many, where. Then one act, with a named parameter. Under every question, write the line it must print, before you run anything.

Run it. Then ask an agent to solve the same problem, and judge its answer with two questions only: does each line follow a step of thought, and does each question carry its promise.

I left this one in your hands, on purpose. The Basket class of section 3 fails the court because `AddQ` returns `42`. Change that one value to `This` and run the question again:

```haro exercise
cCode = "class Basket" + nl + "def Add(x)" + nl + "	return x" + nl + "def AddQ(x)" + nl + "	return This" + nl
? StzCodeIsClean(cCode)
```

> run: exercise

Do not stop at the court's answer. Ask whether the class, as it stands, follows the way a person thinks, and what the court read to answer you. I do not give you the verdict: section 6 says which court exists, and section 7 says what it does not read. When you have settled it, you will know why a court that passes a class has not yet judged the style of a program written with the library.

The next chapter is the mental model, and the metaphors. Where you would write a loop, it asks you to name the move you are making instead: walk, check, yield or perform.


=====

# The mental model, and the metaphors

Source: Chapter 6. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the one-dimensional walker, the checker, the yielder and the performer on lists; the condition language compiled by the engine, with its host-code twin; designed: the four metaphors as native engine modules (only the yielder is one); a walker over any indexed structure; absent: runnable code for the three models of the article 'Bridging Minds and Code'; the edge table of the walker narration as written

Run: 7 of 7 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:13 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take the seven steps of the mental model, which chapter 5 ran on a list, and run them on a string, in the same order. Then I come to the loop, the place where the steps repeat, and to the four metaphors the library gives you instead: the walker, the checker, the yielder and the performer. I show the condition that all four carry inside an argument, the W condition the engine reads as data, and its WF twin, which runs host code. I say why I made the metaphors a design goal, why I replaced `eval` with a language the engine owns, and where the code of the articles this chapter rests on is not yet runnable at this commit. The principle under all of it is the chapter's own: when you would write a loop, name the move you are making, walk, check, yield or perform.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field has one tool for repeating a thing, the loop, and a family of cousins that hide it: the iterator, the comprehension, the stream with its map, filter and reduce, the lambda that carries a condition. They are good tools, and they all share one habit. They are written in the language of the machine's motion: an index that starts at zero, a counter that is incremented, a variable that accumulates. A person who reads the loop has to recover the question it asks.

The field also has a vocabulary of metaphors for programming, but it keeps them in the documentation. The code stays a loop.

## 2. The first principle

**Say the problem in plain words, ask the same questions in the same order of any object, and when you would write a loop, name the move you are making: walk, check, yield or perform.** The site's Craft page says it, in the words of a session of 2 October 2026, as the page reads on 9 October 2026: "A loop hides which move it is making. Softanza names the four moves, and a task is decomposed into them instead." That sentence is the site's, not mine; the sentence above is this chapter's principle.

## 3. The Softanza form

The mental model is seven steps, and they are the order in which a person asks. Define the problem in natural terms and take its keywords. Select the object that holds the data. Ask whether it contains what you care about. Ask how many. Ask where. Act on those positions, or call the verb that does it. And celebrate, and repeat. In chapter 5 you ran those steps on a list with empty strings. They do not belong to the list. Do they hold on a string? Here they are, in the same order:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("You and you")
? o1.ContainsCS("YOU", FALSE)
#--> TRUE
? o1.NumberOfOccurrenceCS("YOU", FALSE)
#--> 2
? o1.FindNthCS(2, "YOU", FALSE)
#--> 9
o1.ReplaceSection(9, 11, :With = "Me")
? o1.Content()
#--> You and Me
```

> run: kept

Contain prints `TRUE`, count prints `2`, find prints `9`, and act leaves `You and Me`, with the same suffix that says whether case matters. The three questions you ask of a string are the three you asked of a list.

When the steps repeat, the loop appears, and this is where the library gives you four metaphors instead. Each one names a move.

**The Walker moves along a route.** A walker is a map of its whole journey, built when it is created. It holds the positions it will visit, the positions it will skip, where it stands and what remains. What does it know before it takes a step? Ask it:

```haro
oWalker = Wk(1, 10, 2)
? @@( oWalker.Walkables() )
#--> [ 1, 3, 5, 7, 9 ]
? @@( oWalker.Unwalkables() )
#--> [ 2, 4, 6, 8, 10 ]
? @@( oWalker.Walk() )
#--> [ 1, 3 ]
? oWalker.CurrentPosition()
#--> 3
? @@( oWalker.RemainingWalkables() )
#--> [ 5, 7, 9 ]
```

> run: kept

A loop does not hold its route as a value you can ask. A walker does, before it moves. What does it do at the edge of the road? Beyond the end, when the walk is underway, it stops in silence. Before the start, it refuses:

```haro
oWalker = Wk(1, 10, 3)
? @@( oWalker.WalkNSteps(10) )
#--> [ 1, 4, 7, 10 ]
? @@( oWalker.Walk() )
#--> [ 10 ]
oWalker = Wk(1, 10, 3)
try
	oWalker.WalkBackward()
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Can't walk backward! Already at the beginning of walkable positions.
```

> run: kept

Ask for ten steps from a walk that has four and you get the four: `[ 1, 4, 7, 10 ]`. Walk again and it prints `[ 10 ]`, with no error. Ask it to walk backward from the start and it refuses! Its own words: `Can't walk backward! Already at the beginning of walkable positions.` The article argues that this mirrors how we walk in the physical world. You stop when you hit a wall behind you, and that is an error. When the road ahead ends, you stop in silence.

**The Checker asks yes or no.** All of them, any of them, or where:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ "A", 5, "B", "C" ])
? o1.CheckW('{ isString(@item) and IsUppercase(@item) }')
#--> FALSE
? o1.ContainsW('{ isNumber(@item) }')
#--> TRUE
? @@( o1.FindW('{ isNumber(@item) }') )
#--> [ 2 ]
```

> run: kept

One item fails the test, so `CheckW` prints `FALSE`; a number is in the list, so `ContainsW` prints `TRUE`; and `FindW` says where, `[ 2 ]`.

**The Yielder produces new data from old, and leaves the old alone.** **The Performer is its opposite number: it changes the items where the condition holds.** Which of the two touches the list? Put them side by side on the same list and look:

```haro
o1 = new stzYielder([ 1, -2, 3, -4, 5 ])
? @@( o1.Filter(:IsPositive) )
#--> [ 1, 3, 5 ]
? @@( o1.Map(:Abs) )
#--> [ 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 ]
? o1.Reduce(:Sum)
#--> 3
o2 = new stzList([ 1, "haro", 3, "python", 5, "ruby" ])
? @@( o2.YieldW('isString(@item)', 'upper(@item)') )
#--> [ "HARO", "PYTHON", "RUBY" ]
? @@( o2.Content() )
#--> [ 1, "haro", 3, "python", 5, "ruby" ]
o2.PerformW('isString(@item)', 'upper(@item)')
? @@( o2.Content() )
#--> [ 1, "HARO", 3, "PYTHON", 5, "RUBY" ]
```

> run: kept

Look at the two `Content()` outputs, before and after the `PerformW` call. The yield returned `[ "HARO", "PYTHON", "RUBY" ]`. The list was untouched! The perform changed the list: `[ 1, "HARO", 3, "PYTHON", 5, "RUBY" ]`. The verb tells you which, so a design can say "this step only reads" and mean it.

Now the thing that all four share, which is the last piece of the title: a condition travels inside an argument. It is a small string, kept in a variable if you want. It goes in the same place for find, for items and for contains. And it has a twin that takes a real function:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ 3, 8, 5, 12, 1, 9 ])
cCond = '{ @item > 5 }'
? @@( o1.FindW(cCond) )
#--> [ 2, 4, 6 ]
? @@( o1.ItemsW(cCond) )
#--> [ 8, 12, 9 ]
? o1.ContainsW(cCond)
#--> TRUE
? @@( o1.FindWF( func x { return x > 5 } ) )
#--> [ 2, 4, 6 ]
```

> run: kept

The two agree: `FindW` with the string and `FindWF` with the function both print `[ 2, 4, 6 ]`. `ItemsW` gives the items themselves, `[ 8, 12, 9 ]`.

The string form is the W condition, W for where; the function form is WF. They look alike, and the difference matters. The engine reads a W condition as data, in a small language of its own: literals, `@item`, arithmetic, comparisons, and a short list of functions. It cannot call your code. So what happens to a condition that tries to? It is not run:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ 3, 8, 5, 12, 1, 9 ])
? @@( o1.FindW('{ @item > 5 and print("hacked") }') )
#--> [ ]
? @@( W('{ @item > 5 }') )
#--> [ "where", "{ @item > 5 }" ]
```

> run: kept

Nothing was printed! The positions came back empty, `[ ]`, and `W()` shows the condition for what it is, data: `[ "where", "{ @item > 5 }" ]`. A W condition can be stored, shown to a person, and approved before it runs, because it cannot escape. The WF twin runs host code, and the library's own table says it is not safe with input you do not trust.

The code of the article "Bridging Minds and Code" does not run at this commit, as section 7 says.

The three models of that article, the semantic, the mental and the expression, are one idea in three places. The semantic model is the world of the program as knowledge: an apple is a fruit. The mental model is what you just did: create an object, store data in it, find things inside it, act on the positions. The expression model is the code that says it: select the object, type the name of the action, continue the flow of actions, convert the result into another object. Keep doing so until you have the final answer. So I tell them in words.

The next section gives the decision, and what I refused.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I made the metaphors a design goal.** The 2022 presentation lists "human-centric metaphors" as one of seven design goals, and its four features are these four. A goal is what makes a feature justifiable or refusable. I hold this as a view, not a result. So I did not leave the metaphors as a nicety of the documentation. They are one of the reasons the library has the shape it has.

**I replaced `eval` with a language the engine owns.** The condition seat used to be compiled by evaluating the string as code, with a more expressive but slower extended form on the side. I removed both. The engine now reads the condition in its own small language, and the library keeps the host-code form as a separate verb, WF, with its warning. I refused arbitrary code from text, and I refused to split speed from expressiveness: the extended form is retired because the plain one does everything it did.

**I kept the seven steps as written.** When the course was designed, a reading of the technical core found five steps: select, contains, count, find, apply. The course teaches the seven as the source narration has them. Nobody renumbers the source.

**I let the edge rule be a design principle.** Before the start is an error, and beyond the end is ignored. The narration gives its reason in a sentence: it mirrors how we walk. The record does not date it as a ruling, so I tell it as the library's design.

The court comes next, and its guards do not cover every line above.

## 5. The court

The four metaphors have no single guard. The yielder has a narrated one, `00_yielder_narrated`: four map operations, three filter operations and a sum. The walker has `597_walker_basics`, named walkers and what they walked, and `495_walking_until_and_until_before`, a walk that stops on a condition, including the item that stops it, or not. The checker has `611_checkw`, which says a check is false when one item fails. The performer has `621_perform`, which says it changes the list in place. `483_w_and_wf_forms_in_conditional_code` proves that a W condition written with cursor words such as `@NextNumber` and the same one written with raw index arithmetic such as `This[@i+1]` give the same positions. `597_walker_basics` and `495_walking_until_and_until_before` test the list's named walkers and walks, through `StzListQ`, not the `Wk` walker of the blocks above.

None of the guards named here covers `Walkables`, `WalkNSteps` or `WalkBackward`; those lines rest on the runs kept for this chapter.

Next, what the field does better than the walker.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in laziness. A generator produces the next item on demand and can be endless. The walker builds a map of its whole journey the moment it is made. That is a win for foresight: you can ask which positions it will skip before it moves. It is a loss for a large or endless traversal.

The field leads in openness. A lambda carries any logic. The W language is bounded on purpose, and the price of the bound is the second path, WF, which has full host power and is meant for input you trust. There is also a quieter cost I measured once, on one case: a condition that calls something the language does not know gives an empty list and no error.

The field leads in reach and in tooling. Iterators and comprehensions are in every mainstream language, with editor support, type checking and a literature. These four metaphors exist in one library.

And I will not print the library's own comparison of walkers with loops and iterators. Its table has thirteen rows, all of them in the walker's favour, none of them run. A table is worth printing when its rows have been run and a loss stands beside the wins; for this one, that is the next step. This is my view, held on this table: thirteen rows, none with a loss.

Next, the limit, in the stage words built, designed and absent.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the one-dimensional walker, in the max layer, and the walks on lists; the checker as `CheckW`, `ContainsW` and `FindW`; the yielder as a class backed by the engine, and as `YieldW`; the performer as `PerformW`; and the W condition language compiled by the engine.

What is designed: the metaphors as native engine modules. The engine's design marks all four as planned; only the yielder has an engine file. And a walker that is not about lists at all but about any indexed structure.

Six things are absent, or still to be aligned with their articles, at this commit, from my runs.

- The walking-at-the-edge article's table and the library differ in two cases; aligning them is the next step. A single step larger than the range raises where the table promises silence, and a mixed-direction path raises where it promises acceptance. The two principles hold in the cases shown above. The book states the principle and prints only the cases that run; the table is not printed.
- The "Beyond Loops" article's code differs in places. The history of a walker is a list of walks; a backward walk takes no argument now; the direction prints as "backward" where the article says "reverse". The site's own run of the article kept ten of eighteen blocks.
- The three models of "Bridging Minds and Code": none of its code runs as written; in the site's run twelve blocks raised and one differed. So I told them in words.
- The mental-model article's getter `EmptyStrings()` is absent.
- On the dedicated checker class, `ContainsW` is in development: it raises today. The string walker's backward-with-condition walk is absent.
- And "no eval" is still on its way to everything. The near-natural resolver, the other direction of chapter 13, still evaluates host code. So do `StringsW` and `ItemsWhere` on the list, and `ContainsW` on the dedicated checker class: each still calls `eval` on a condition string. What I said above, that the engine reads a W condition and cannot run it as code, holds for the engine's path; for these three it is the next step.

Next come the six doors, from the programmer to the agent.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Ask the same four questions of any object, and when you reach for a loop, name the move: walk, check, yield or perform.

**The analyst.** Say the problem in plain words, and the verbs you used are the steps: "are all of them", "which ones", "give me the big ones", "make them negative".

**The designer.** The yielder never changes the list and the performer does, and the verb tells you which.

**The decision-maker.** A W condition is a string the engine reads and cannot run as code, so it can be stored, shown and approved. The price is a small language, and a call it does not know gives an empty list, not an error.

**The educator.** The self-study book already has the order: find, then apply, then walk, ask, produce and act.

**The agent.** Write the condition as the string, not as code. When the answer is empty, check the condition's words before you trust the emptiness.

## 9. Exercise

Take the list `[ 4, 9, 12, 7, 20, 3 ]`. Four sentences are given. One: are all of them above 2? Two: give me, as a new list, those above 8, doubled. Three: make the ones above 8 negative, in the list itself. Four: which positions are 2, 4 and 6?

For each, say first which move it is, walk, check, yield or perform, and write under the line you will write what you expect it to print. Then write the line, with the condition as a string wherever there is a condition, and run it. After the second and the third, print the list again. Say, before you look, in which of the two the list has changed.

I left this one in your hands, on purpose. Here is the block of section 3 with one change: the list is the exercise's list, and I print nothing under it.

```haro exercise
o1 = new stzList([ 4, 9, 12, 7, 20, 3 ])
? @@( o1.FindW('{ @item > 5 and print("hacked") }') )
```

> run: exercise

Run it. The condition begins by asking whether an item is above 5. Look at what comes back, and at what does not, and say why before you go back to the sections. Section 3 says what the engine does with a condition that calls code; section 6 says what that costs, and section 8 says what an agent should do about it. When you have solved it, you will know why an empty answer is not yet a proof that nothing matched.

The next chapter turns to the writing style, and to the seven goals. Its principle is one sentence: what you think is what you write.


=====

# The writing style, and the seven goals

Source: Chapter 7. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the library's fourteen house rules as a court over its own code; the promise judged as a claim; the small languages of ranges, lists and conditions in strings; the code seat; the viz prefix; alternatives and named parameters in any order, in part; designed: a court that judges the style of a program written with the library; a court that reads the goals; the writing grammar declared in Haro; the classic form of a test block as the one form; absent: any machine that judges the style of the code you write today; the writing in any human language of a function's name; the near-natural sentence as the deck wrote it; the eval-based code on a parameter's name

Run: 10 of 10 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:13 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take the rest of the writing style, as a grammar of fourteen rules. I print the state each rule is in at the commit this book runs, a block where it runs and a limit where it does not. Under the grammar I put the seven goals, and I show three of them in blocks and leave the four I have not run to the study. I print the house court, the fourteen rules the library holds over its own code. I say what it does not read: the code of a program written with the library. The principle is chapter 5's, and I do not argue it again: what you think is what you write.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The word *style* means two things in the field, and I have to say which one I mean.

The first is style as layout, enforced by a tool. Python's style guide dates from 2001, Go's formatter from 2009, and every project runs a linter before a commit. They judge the shape of a line: spaces, capitals, length, unused names. They are good at it, and they run by default. They say nothing about whether a line follows the order in which its writer reasoned.

The second is the small language inside the language. Programmers already write small languages in strings, and nobody calls that a style: a regular expression, a SQL text, a format string such as `"%5.2f"`. Each is a separate notation, with its own tool, learned apart.

For goals, the field says *quality attributes*. The ISO standard for software quality, of 2011, lists eight of them, and architecture methods rank them before a system is drawn. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.) The seven goals of this chapter are a different list, chosen for a different purpose. They do not say what a product promises its users. They say what a feature must serve to be admitted into one library.

## 2. The first principle

**What you think is what you write.** It is chapter 5's sentence, from the diagram of 2020, and I do not argue it again. Chapter 5 took five rules of the style. This chapter takes the rest, as a grammar, and then the goals under which the grammar was designed.

For the goals, the point is the author's, as the study reports it, and not a dated quotation. A software architecture that is goal-oriented is the only way one architect masters a project of this size. I hold this as a view, not a result. What stands behind it on this page is one case: the 2022 matrix served as a skeleton for one architect over about three hundred thousand hand-written lines (section 6).

## 3. The Softanza form

The study of the record wrote the style down as fourteen rules, in its ruling 12.16, from reading the code. They are readings of the code, stated as rules, and not a standard anyone signed. Here they are with the state each is in at the commit this book runs. I put the table first because it shows which rules carry a block and which carry a limit.

| # | Rule | State at this commit |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | A value is lifted to its object by `Q()` or a typed lifter | runs; chapter 5 |
| 2 | A scene is a brace block on one object | runs; chapter 5 |
| 3 | Every printed line carries its answer under `#-->` | judged by a machine; chapter 5 |
| 4 | A parameter is named, and the name reads as English | runs; chapter 5, and here in any order |
| 5 | A chain mirrors the order of thought, one step per line | runs; chapter 5 |
| 6 | The verb's form says what happens | runs; chapters 5 and 8 |
| 7 | Suffixes and prefixes are small and mean one thing each | in part: `CS`, `XT`, `viz` run; the deck's `FF`, `dft` and `inf` families are not defined |
| 8 | A function has alternatives for the way a person says it | in part |
| 9 | Precision over generality, when it costs nothing | in part |
| 10 | Code travels inside an argument as a small string | the seat runs; the `@` on a parameter's name is retired |
| 11 | Small literal languages live in strings | runs in one form; the braced form raises |
| 12 | A sentence can be near-natural | not this chapter's; none of the deck's three forms ran as written |
| 13 | A function may be named in any human language | absent; section 7 |
| 14 | A comment speaks before the code, in the teacher's voice | runs |

Rule 11 first, because it is the strangest. The host language counts in integers and cuts the decimals. What does a range written inside a string keep? The comments below speak before the code, in the teacher's voice, which is rule 14. Run it:

```haro
# The host's own range counts in integers: the decimals are cut. Softanza reads
# a small language inside a string, and keeps the decimals, the numbered words,
# and the letters of any script:
? @@( 1.02 : 3.08 )
#--> [ 1, 2, 3 ]
? @@( L("1.02:1.05") )
#--> [ 1.02, 1.03, 1.04, 1.05 ]
? @@( L('"♥1" : "♥3"') )
#--> [ "♥1", "♥2", "♥3" ]
? @@( L(' "ا" : "ج" ') )
#--> [ "ا", "ب", "ة", "ت", "ث", "ج" ]
```

> run: kept

The host answers `[ 1, 2, 3 ]` to `1.02 : 3.08` and cuts the decimals. The string answers `[ 1.02, 1.03, 1.04, 1.05 ]`, and the two Arabic bounds give six letters! Softanza reads a small range language inside the string and keeps the decimals, the numbered words and the letters of any script. The range language is an argument like any other. Does it give the replacements for each occurrence, in order, and does the extended form wrap the palette when the occurrences outnumber it?

```haro
o1 = new stzString("haro php ruby haro python haro")
o1.ReplaceByMany("haro", L('"♥1" : "♥3"'))
? o1.Content()
#--> ♥1 php ruby ♥2 python ♥3
o2 = new stzString("haro php ruby haro python haro ruby haro")
o2.ReplaceByManyXT("haro", L('"♥1" : "♥3"'))
? o2.Content()
#--> ♥1 php ruby ♥2 python ♥3 ruby ♥1
```

> run: kept

The extended form wrapped the palette, and `♥1` came back at the end!

A list can also be carried as a string in its short form, recognised, converted between forms and evaluated back:

```haro
? StzStringQ('1:3').IsListInString()
#--> TRUE
? @@( StzStringQ('[1,2,3]').ToListInShortForm() )
#--> "1 : 3"
? @@( StzStringQ(' "A":"C" ').ToList() )
#--> [ "A", "B", "C" ]
```

> run: kept

Rule 10: code travels inside an argument as a small string with a placeholder, `@char` for the characters of a string and `@item` for the items of a list. Chapter 6 showed the seat on a list. Here it is on a string, where it finds and removes by a rule and not by a loop:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("SOooooFTAaaannnNZA")
? @@( o1.FindW('{ Q(@char).IsUppercase() }') )
#--> [ 1, 2, 7, 8, 9, 16, 17, 18 ]
o1.RemoveW('{ Q(@char).IsLowercase() }')
? o1.Content()
#--> SOFTANZA
```

> run: kept

The deck of 2022 writes the same call with a named seat, `:Where = '...'`, and that form raises on a string in this commit. I do not show it. The same deck puts an `@` at the end of a parameter's name, `:With@`, to mark that its value is code to be evaluated. That form is retired. A guard's header says so: the eval-based forms are retired. The deck itself marks the form "TODO". Code now travels in the seat as a bounded string that the engine evaluates, or as a function.

Rule 8: the same act may be said in two orders of words, with the container's name, or with the side spelled four ways. Each pair is one act, with two definitions. The library's own narration calls the first with two arguments and it raises `less number of parameters`; the guards pass the starting position, and so does the block:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ 1, 2, "♥", 4, 5, "♥", "♥" ])
? o1.FindNthNext(2, "♥", :StartingAt = 1)
#--> 6
? o1.FindNextNth(2, "♥", :StartingAt = 1)
#--> 6
? Q("---haro").NumberOfOccurrenceOfCharLeftSide("-")
#--> 3
? Q("---haro").HowManyOccurrenceOfCharStartSide("-")
#--> 3
o2 = new stzList([ "C", "B", "A" ])
o2.SwapItems(:AtPositions = 1, :And = 3)
? @@( o2.Content() )
#--> [ "A", "B", "C" ]
```

> run: kept

The deck promised more. A family of five names ending in `CharsRemoved`, for removing characters at the ends, is named in the deck. Two are defined, `LeadingCharsRemoved` and `TrailingCharsRemoved`. Three are not: `LeadingAndTrailingCharsRemoved`, `TrailingAndLeadingCharsRemoved` and `RepeatedCharsRemoved`. `RemoveLeadingAndTrailingChars` is defined, and is not one of the five.

Rule 9: precision over generality. The precise name says what is counted. A string's characters are counted by `NumberOfChars`, and `Size()` is the host's byte length. Do the two answers differ on an Arabic word? Run it:

```haro
? Q("سلام").NumberOfChars()
#--> 4
? Q("سلام").NumberOfBytes()
#--> 8
? Q("سلام").Size()
#--> 8
o1 = new stzList([ "tea", "rice", "fish", "salt" ])
? o1.ContainsBoth("tea", "fish")
#--> TRUE
? o1.ContainsAll([ "tea", "oil" ])
#--> FALSE
```

> run: kept

The run answers `4` to the one and `8` to the other. The word has four letters! The library's own documentation line for `NumberOfChars` lists *size* among its alternative words. The run shows that `Size()` does not mean it. I list it among the requests.

Rule 7: a prefix `viz` shows the answer under the word, and the suffix `XT` adds the positions as numbers:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("fjharoljharodjharog")
? o1.vizFind("haro")
#--> fjharoljharodjharog
#--> --^-----^-----^----
? o1.vizFindXT("haro", [ :Numbered = TRUE ])
#--> fjharoljharodjharog
#--> --^-----^-----^----
#-->   3     9     15
```

> run: kept

The narration and the deck write `vizFindAll` on a string. It does not exist there at this commit: the research run answered `Calling Method without definition: vizfindall`. `vizFind` does exist. A list has its own `VizFindAll`.

### The goals

Under the grammar there are seven goals, written in the 2022 deck as a matrix of thirty-three features. The study's ruling 12.14 reads them as the library's architectural skeleton: a feature that serves none is refused, and a goal with no feature is a gap. The study's summary is a session's reading and not a text of the library, and no document in the library states the rule in these words. The nearest is the engine's design document: the engine must enable all seven.

I will not list the seven as a table of slogans. Here is what runs of three of them.

**Flexibility: the order of named parameters is free.** Are two calls, with the parameters in two orders, the same call?

```haro
o1 = new stzString("----@@--@@-------@@----@@---")
o1.ReplaceNextNthOccurrence(2, :Of = "@@", :StartingAt = 12, :With = "##")
? o1.Content()
#--> ----@@--@@-------@@----##---
o2 = new stzString("----@@--@@-------@@----@@---")
o2.ReplaceNextNthOccurrence(2, :With = "##", :StartingAt = 12, :Of = "@@")
? o2.Content()
#--> ----@@--@@-------@@----##---
```

> run: kept

Both orders print `----@@--@@-------@@----##---`! A tension must be told with it. The design of Haro refuses free-order parameters as grammar, and the library keeps them. Two facts are true today. The block above runs them, so they are in the library. The grammar a machine is held to leaves them out, by the Haro principle. Whether the library keeps them past that grammar or retires them is a decision the record does not hold. I do not make it here. They are kept, and they are not grammar.

**Consistency: one vocabulary across a string and a list.** Does the same verb answer on both?

```haro
? @@( StzStringQ("hello").FindAllCS("L", :CaseSensitive = 0) )
#--> [ 3, 4 ]
? @@( StzListOfStringsQ([ "h", "e", "l", "l", "o" ]).FindAllCS("L", :CaseSensitive = 0) )
#--> [ 3, 4 ]
? Q("").IsEmpty()
#--> TRUE
? Q([]).IsEmpty()
#--> TRUE
```

> run: kept

The loss beside it, from the same slide group: the deck's advice to use the keyword Item with any enumerable type does not hold. `NumberOfItems()` on a string raises in the research run: `Calling Method without definition: numberofitems`. On a list it answers.

**Manageability: a failure that says where, what, why, and what to do.** The library raises an error in four fields. I do not print its message, because in the research run its first line carried a line number of the library, and a line number is not a promise.

The other four goals (expressiveness, reliability, the human metaphors and the practical abstractions) I leave to the tables of the study. I have not run them for this chapter, and I will not describe from the deck what I have not run.

Next comes the decision, and what I refused.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused a style that was changed when AI became the main writing tool.** The study reports the author's disappointment in these terms, and chapter 5 tells it. The implementation code that the AI wrote is accepted as good. What is demanded is that every line a programmer, human or agent, is asked to write with Softanza embraces the historic style.

**I refused a goal that lives only in a document.** The seven goals are written in one design document and in tags that no court reads. The study's finding is that there is no court over either.

**I refused to hide the cost of the style.** The study measured markers per thousand lines. The narrated guards, the test files written to be read as articles, carry the promise `#-->` in under one line per thousand. They carry a call to the assertion helper in eighty-five. The older numbered tests carry the promise in fifty-two. The library's code carries the promise in less than two. The style is densest where the author wrote for a reader, and thinner where the AI wrote for others; carrying it there is the next step. A marker is a reading of the text, not a judgement of the code; the study says so itself.

**I refused to recount the tags as mine.** The forms of the functions are marked in the code by info-tags, in comments that begin with `#<` and an `@`, applied by hand. The study reads them as the feature-level vocabulary of two goals, expressiveness and flexibility, and counted 6,090. I counted again on 8 October 2026, with the command in the research file, and found 6,126, with 89 spellings. But 1,360 of them sit outside the archive folders, and 4,766 sit in the archive folders. So the sentence "applied by hand at 6,090 points" is, at this commit, mostly a sentence about the archive. No guard reads them: the reflection layer reads a method's name, with a grammar, and not its tag.

**I refused the tally as a count of passes.** The study counts thirty-three features: twenty-four built, four transformed with the project, two that changed form, one partial, one archived, one a name only. The arithmetic holds. But it is a count of names found, and a definition found is not a passing test. Several of the features are named in the deck and not defined in the library's code, the archive aside. They are the families `FF` and `FX` for sections, the families `dft` and `inf` for defaults and infinities, `WalkFF` and `AddWalkerFF`, and the `Check` on a list. That last one is the deck's checker, which answers 1 or 0 for each item. Two are defined and do not yet match some of the deck's examples. `Yield` should give the result of an expression for each item. `Perform` should apply an action to each item. I print the tally, and I print its losses with it. The deck also says "7 root causes of programming complexity", beside the seven goals. No slide lists those seven causes, and no other file in the record names them. The chapter names none and does not match them to the goals one by one.

The court comes next, and I run it.

## 5. The court

`coderule_narrated` proves that a house rule is a rule over the code graph; that planted code is found by name; that clean code passes; and that the library lists fourteen. `testoor_promises_narrated` proves that the promise `#-->` is judged as a claim of a stop: kept, diverged, unreached or unjudged. `602_l_function` and `59_use_of_the_l_small_function` prove the range language. `527_identifying_lists_inside_a_string` proves the lists hosted in strings. `48_replacebymany` proves that the replacements go in order. `483_w_and_wf_forms_in_conditional_code` proves the condition in a string, in two spellings of the same scan. `28_numberofoccurrenceofcharleftside` proves the alternative names in Arabic as well as Latin, and carries a note that the archive's promise was wrong and the implementation is the authority. `646_content` proves the named parameters. `972_narration_viz-finding_a_recurring_substring` proves the viz tour.

Here is the house court itself, the fourteen rules of the library and not the fourteen of the style. The numerical coincidence is a coincidence. What does it answer to a planted misspelling, and to a planted write to a constant? Run it:

```haro
? @@( StzCodeRuleNames() )
#--> [ "no-len-method", "q-returns-object", "no-aggressive-verbs", "engine-first", "q-has-plain-twin", "no-case-collision", "dead-knob", "setter-resets-on-reject", "setter-only-moves-one-way", "misspelled-name", "library-prints", "empty-method-body", "writes-a-mutable-constant", "value-called-as-function" ]
cOne = "class Inbox" + nl + "def Recieve(x)" + nl + "	return x" + nl
? StzCheckCode(cOne)[1][:rule]
#--> misspelled_name
? StzCodeIsClean(cOne)
#--> TRUE
cTwo = "class Inbox" + nl + "def Count()" + nl + "	nl = 3" + nl + "	return nl" + nl
? StzCheckCode(cTwo)[1][:rule]
#--> writes_a_mutable_constant
? StzCodeIsClean(cTwo)
#--> FALSE
```

> run: kept

The misspelling is found by name, `misspelled_name`, and the code stays clean, `TRUE`, because it is a warning. The write to a constant is found by name, `writes_a_mutable_constant`, and the code is refused, `FALSE`, because it is an error. The court refused the write to a constant!

A detail of the spelling, for Appendix B: the names list uses hyphens, and the finding of eight of the fourteen comes back with underscores. The block shows it: the list prints `misspelled-name`, and the finding prints `misspelled_name`.

What the court does not read is the code of a program written with the library. The fourteen rules of the house are rules of the library's own code; a rule of writing for the code written with it is designed (section 7).

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in enforcement by default. A formatter runs on every commit. For the code a programmer writes with Softanza, the court that would enforce the style is designed and not yet built, and chapter 5 says so.

The field leads in having its goals somewhere a reader looks first. The seven goals are in one design document and in tags that no court reads, and the operating notes of the library's own repository name none of them. An AI reader's account of the library says that sixty paradigm documents could easily be sixty disconnected ideas. The study reads it as a sign that the discipline is in the code and written nowhere a reader would look first. It is a session's document, and not an independent witness.

The site once showed a reduced dialect: its blocks had no named parameter and no scene. It has since put the fourteen rules on its Craft page and the rules as data. I did not re-measure the site's blocks after that.

Where I think the style stands apart is that every rule serves a step of thought, and every step has a written form (study 12.17). The verb's form is a contract on the object. And the promise under the line is the example, the test and the documentation at once. The 2022 matrix served as a skeleton for one architect over about three hundred thousand hand-written lines.

The limit, in the next section, says what is built, what is designed and what is absent.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the fourteen house rules of the library as a court over its own code, run in the block above. Also built: the promise judged by a machine; the small languages, the seat, the prefix. And the features that run (`CS`, `XT`, `ZZ`, `viz`, named parameters, the same verb on a string and a list).

What is designed:

- **A court that judges the style of a program written with the library.** It is a specification in the study: the fourteen rules as house rules over a snippet's graph. Its examples are a positional argument where a named one exists, or a printed line with no promise. I searched the library for it and it is not built yet; it is the next step.
- **A court that reads the goals.** It would read the tags. To come.
- **The classic form of the test block as the one form for new blocks.** The study's ruling is that the revival "is not a rewrite" but a decision. The classic form is the one form for new blocks, and the converted blocks are left as they are. The test tree's own README still calls the classic form the authoritative test surface. I found no ruling that makes it the one form, so it stays a decision, and chapter 5 names it.
- **The writing grammar declared in Haro.** The vocabulary half exists: the forms of the library's names are data in Haro, and the session's record is 584 of 595 shipped spellings agreeing. The usage half, the scene, the chain, the code seat and the promise, is not declared.
- **The discipline declared once.** Partly done on the site, as data, which is the page's source and not a court.

What is absent:

- Any machine that judges, today, the style of a program written with the library; beside it, the house court judges the library's own code.
- Rules 12 and 13 as running forms. In the research session's runs, the deck's near-natural samples did not run as written, and neither did a function named in Chinese or in French.
- The `@` on a parameter's name as a working form.
- The braced form of the range language, `L('{ "♥1" : "♥6" }')`, which raises, and ranges that change width, such as `day8 : day11`, which also raise, with an uninitialised-variable error from the host. The list constructor from a string raises too. These three come from the research session's runs; no block of this chapter keeps them.
- The visualised call stack, and the cache and log decorators, which exist as names in comments.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Fourteen rules you can hold your own line against. Lift the value, ask in a scene, put the answer under `#-->`, name the parameter, chain one step per line, read the verb's form; and put a range, a condition or a palette in a string.

**The analyst.** The words of your problem become the names of the steps. A rule written in a string is a rule you can store and read, and not code you must trust.

**The designer.** The viz prefix draws the answer where the eye expects it. The goals are the headings under which a design is judged, one feature at a time.

**The decision-maker.** Seven goals and thirty-three features are a reading grid. In the study's reading, a feature that serves none is refused, and a goal with no feature is a gap. The tally is printed with its losses.

**The educator.** The discipline is taught whole and in order. The converted guard for lists in strings keeps the old promise's wrong spacing in a comment, which is a lesson on the cost of hand-typed answers.

**The agent.** The rules are given to you as data on the site. For the code you write, the court is designed and not yet built. Write the question, then the promise under it, and put your condition in the seat, not in an evaluation of a string.

## 9. Exercise

Write, before you run anything, the line each task needs and the promise under it:

1. From one string, the list `[ "♥7", "♥8", "♥9" ]`.
2. The string `"haro php haro"` with the two `haro` numbered `♥1` and `♥2`, in one call that uses the small language of task 1.
3. The list `[ "C", "B", "A" ]` ordered by swapping the first and the third items, with the positions named.
4. The count of characters of `"سلام"`, with the name that says what is counted.
5. A guess: what does `L("day8 : day11")` print?

```haro exercise
# 1.
# 2.
# 3.
# 4.
# 5.
```

> run: exercise

Run it. For the fifth, you were allowed to be wrong, and you were probably wrong: the range changes width, so the host reads `day8` as a variable, and it raises with `Using uninitialized variable: day8`. That is the limit of the small language, learned by being surprised once. Do not change your promise to match the output. Explain the output first.

I left this one in your hands. The first call of the rule 8 block, with its named start taken out, has two arguments. Nothing else changes:

```haro exercise
o1 = new stzList([ 1, 2, "♥", 4, 5, "♥", "♥" ])
? o1.FindNthNext(2, "♥")
```

> run: exercise

Run this one and read what comes back. Then repair the call. Section 3 says where this form of the call comes from and what the guards do instead. When it is repaired, you will know why a call that the library's own narration writes raises, and what the guards pass that it does not.

Chapter 8 takes a function's name whole, as a linguistic expression, and then scope: a function's name is a sentence, and its prefixes, suffixes and parameters are a grammar that the library can read.


=====

# Functions as linguistic expressions, and scope

Source: Chapter 8. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: fourteen of the twenty-two forms, some with examples other than the article's, and three more in another shape; the library reading a name as a sentence; scope in the regex verbs, in the system and in the placement of a deployment; designed: the forms declared once as data and generated, in Haro; the scope paradigm for time, units, memory and style; absent: five forms: partial, future, free, default and multilingual; concurrency as a scope: a plan with no code

Run: 13 of 13 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:14 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I read a function's name the way the library reads it: a sentence whose prefixes, suffixes and parameters are a grammar. I walk the forms of a name that run, and I count the five that do not. Then I turn to scope: the same verb, three verdicts, with the frame in the verb and not in a flag set earlier. I say what Haro's design keeps of the forms, what it refuses, and what the court proves. The principle is this: a function's name is a sentence, and its prefixes, suffixes and parameters are a grammar that the library can read.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

In the field, a function's name is a label. It follows a convention, camel case or snake case, and it tells you roughly what the function does; the details are in the signature and the documentation. A name does not carry grammar. The related tools are all about structure. Python and Swift offer the keyword argument, so that a call can say which parameter is which. The fluent interface chains calls so that a line reads like a sentence, and the overload gives one name several signatures.

And when the field wants a function to behave differently in a different context, it changes its meaning by a flag set somewhere else. The flag is a mode, a locale, an environment variable. The function's name does not tell you which frame it will run in.

## 2. The first principle

**A function's name is a sentence, and its prefixes, suffixes and parameters are a grammar that the library can read.** The paper that first made the claim says, in the words the study of its roots quotes, that it was achieved "without machine learning or NLP systems". It was done by naming conventions that encode meaning, a parameter order that mirrors speech, flexible forms and intelligent defaults.

## 3. The Softanza form

Look at a name the way the library looks at it. `FindNextSTCS`: find next, from a start position, with a case flag. `RemoveManyCS`: remove, many things, a case flag. Each suffix is small and means one thing, and, in these names, the order of the suffixes is the order of the parameters. Here the first name finds. What does the second leave when it removes every occurrence of three letters, whatever their case? Run it:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("123456789HARO")
? o1.FindNextSTCS("haro", 5, FALSE)
#--> 10
o2 = new stzString("SOOooooFFfffTANNnnnZA")
o2.RemoveManyCS([ "o", "f", "n" ], FALSE)
? o2.Content()
#--> STAZA
```

> run: kept

The article promised `SOFTANZA` on the second line. The run prints `STAZA`! The code is right and the article is wrong: a removal that does not care about case removes the capitals too. I print the run.

Does the library only follow this grammar, or does it read it? Its name parser, `_StzParseName`, is an internal helper that no guard covers. The public way in is `ExplainMethod`, which explains a method in those terms:

```haro
? @@( _StzParseName("FindNextSTCS") )
#--> [ "find next", [ "case-sensitive", "from-position", "active" ] ]
? @@( _StzParseName("RemoveManyCS") )
#--> [ "remove", [ "case-sensitive", "plural", "active" ] ]
? StzSelfDocQ("stzString").ExplainMethod("RemoveMany")
#--> RemoveMany -- Remove every occurrence of each given substring (mutating).
#-->   (plural form -- applies the operation to many items at once)
#-->   reads as: remove -- over many values at once
```

> run: kept

It reads it. That is the claim, made mechanical: the grammar is in the code and not only in the article. The printed line `reads as: remove -- over many values at once` is the parser's reading of `RemoveMany`. The parser decodes the prefixes and the suffixes, the words "except" and "is not", and the voice: a name that ends in `ed` is passive.

I will walk the forms now. Chapter 5 showed four of them: the active, the passive, the fluent and the named parameter. Here are the others that run.

**Z and ZZ** say whether a position comes as a number or as a section. A suffix, and the answer changes shape:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("haro php haro ruby haro")
? @@( o1.FindZ("haro") )
#--> [ 1, 10, 20 ]
? @@( o1.FindZZ("haro") )
#--> [ [ 1, 4 ], [ 10, 13 ], [ 20, 23 ] ]
o2 = new stzString("SOFTANZA")
? @@( o2.FirstHalfZ() )
#--> [ "SOFT", 1 ]
? @@( o2.FirstHalfZZ() )
#--> [ "SOFT", [ 1, 4 ] ]
```

> run: kept

**The prefix viz** changes the kind of answer: a drawing. The position is shown under the text, and an option can number it:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("fjharoljharodjharog")
? o1.vizFind("haro")
#--> fjharoljharodjharog
#--> --^-----^-----^----
? o1.vizFindXT("haro", [ :Numbered = TRUE ])
#--> fjharoljharodjharog
#--> --^-----^-----^----
#-->   3     9     15
```

> run: kept

**The plural** extends a verb to many things without a loop, and **the exceptional form** keeps only what you name. The article's example for the second, `RemoveNonLettersExcept`, does not exist. The form does, in other names:

```haro
o1 = new stzString("__H~~~AR***O__")
? @@( o1.FindMany([ "~", "*" ]) )
#--> [ 4, 5, 6, 9, 10, 11 ]
o1.RemoveMany([ "_", "~", "*" ])
? o1.Content()
#--> HARO
o2 = new stzString("--Haro--&__Softanza__")
o2.RemoveAllExcept([ "Haro", "&", "Softanza" ])
? o2.Content()
#--> Haro&Softanza
```

> run: kept

**The negative** is a word in the name, not a NOT around the call:

```haro
? Q("*").IsNotLetter()
#--> TRUE
o3 = new stzList([ "R", "I", "N", "G" ])
? o3.IsNotAString()
#--> TRUE
? o3.DoesNotContain("♥")
#--> TRUE
```

> run: kept

**The deep form** answers a path, a list of indices that goes as deep as the data does, and the same family replaces and removes at that depth:

```haro
o1 = new stzList([ "you", "other", [ "other", "you", [ "you" ], "other" ], "other", "you" ])
? @@( o1.DeepFind("you") )
#--> [ [ 1 ], [ 3, 2 ], [ 3, 3, 1 ], [ 5 ] ]
o1.DeepReplace("you", :By = "♥")
? @@( o1.Content() )
#--> [ "♥", "other", [ "other", "♥", [ "♥" ], "other" ], "other", "♥" ]
```

> run: kept

**The immutable form**, QC, runs the whole chain on a copy. After that chain, what does the original hold?

```haro
o1 = new stzString("haxxrxo")
? o1.RemoveQC("x").UppercaseQ().Spacified()
#--> H A R O
? o1.Content()
#--> haxxrxo
```

> run: kept

The chain printed `H A R O`, and the original still holds `haxxrxo`!

**The misspelled form** is accepted, with a note on what stands behind it. A misspelling of a name works:

```haro
? Q("   Haro ").WithoutSapces()
#--> Haro
```

> run: kept

The article says the library recognises and corrects minor typos, and that the intelligence resides in the library itself. The code shows how the form works: `WithoutSapces` works because that spelling was written by hand as an alias. Another misspelling, `WithoutSpces`, fails. So the form is real, and in what I traced it is a list of declared aliases; the correction engine the article describes is not among what I traced. I did not trace every file that mentions correction; one message in the object class only suggests a name, with "Did you mean".

Now the scope half of the chapter. The same fault shows up everywhere in programming: behaviour with an invisible governing frame. I hold this as a view, not a result; what stands behind it here is three instances: the regex verbs, the system and the placement of a deployment. A function does one thing here and another there, and nothing in its name says which frame it is in. The answer, in four moves, is the paradigm I call scope-oriented programming. Name the hidden frame. Turn it into a small, closed set of named scopes. Choose the scope at the call site, in the verb, and not in a flag set earlier. And give the library the scope to reason with, a semantic handle and not a comment.

It was born in regular expressions, where the frame is how much of the text a pattern must cover. The same pattern, the same text, three verbs: what does each answer?

```haro
cText = "First: Line1" + nl + "Second: Line2" + nl + "Third: Line3"
o1 = new stzRegex("^Second:.*$")
? o1.Match(cText)
#--> FALSE
? o1.MatchFirst(cText)
#--> FALSE
? o1.MatchLine(cText)
#--> TRUE
```

> run: kept

`Match` answers `FALSE`: it asks whether the pattern covers the whole value. `MatchFirst` answers `FALSE` too: it asks whether the pattern occurs anywhere, with the line anchors off. `MatchLine` answers `TRUE`: it makes `^` and `$` bind at each line. The frame is in the verb. There is a word scope too, which supplies the word boundaries so that you do not write them. It rewrites the pattern of its object for good, which is why the second instance is a new object with the same pattern. Does the same pattern answer alike under `MatchFirst` and under `MatchWord`?

```haro
o2 = new stzRegex("pre\w+")
? o2.MatchFirst("compress")
#--> TRUE
o3 = new stzRegex("pre\w+")
? o3.MatchWord("compress")
#--> FALSE
```

> run: kept

The same pattern answers `TRUE` under `MatchFirst` and `FALSE` under `MatchWord`!

The second instance is the system. Feature code is written in a named scope, and the same verb gets a different verdict in each. The scope can make a laptop behave like a device it does not have, or refuse what the target forbids. Here, one solution with a backend on a Linux server and a firmware on a small board. What does each system answer when the same verb, `Spawn`, is asked of it? Run it:

```haro
oSol = new stzPlatformProfile("my-iot-product")
oSol.DevelopedOn(:Windows)
oSol.WithServer(:backend, :LinuxServer)
oSol.WithApp(:firmware, :ESP32)
oBack = oSol.App(:backend).System()
oFirm = oSol.App(:firmware).System()
? oBack.Spawn("worker")
#--> native
? oFirm.ReadPin(4)
#--> rehearsed
try
	oFirm.Spawn("worker")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> scope 'esp32' (espidf) forbids 'process' -- spawn 'worker'. This target has no such capability.
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The same verb, `Spawn`, has two verdicts! The server answers `native`; the board answers `scope 'esp32' (espidf) forbids 'process'`. The scope decides, not the operation. And the laptop has no pin to read, so the scope answers `rehearsed`. This is the fifth move: when a scope's capabilities differ from the host's, the library bridges the difference, in both directions. Two declared systems can be compared as data, with a closed set of capabilities:

```haro
oEsp = DeclareSystem("esp32-firmware")
oEsp.SetOSName(:rtos)
oEsp.SetCapabilityList([ :gpio, :network, :clock, :threads ])
oBox = DeclareSystem("laptop")
oBox.SetOSName(:windows)
oBox.SetCapabilityList([ :filesystem, :process, :network, :clock, :threads ])
? @@( oBox.Forbids(oEsp) )
#--> [ "filesystem", "process" ]
? @@( oBox.Requires(oEsp) )
#--> [ "gpio" ]
```

> run: kept

The run printed `[ "filesystem", "process" ]` for what the laptop can do that the board cannot, and `[ "gpio" ]` for what the board needs that the laptop lacks. That is the second instance. The third is deployment: the target is the frame, and the scope decides not to ship what the target already does well; chapter 4 showed the plan. The fourth is concurrency, and it is a plan.

The design is next, with what Haro keeps of these forms and what it refuses.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**In the Haro design, principle 7 makes forms a grammar that the language owns.** It is designed and not built: the forms are declared once, and the compiler would generate and check each family. The passive form may not mutate; a verb with Q returns the object; suffix order is parameter order; a W takes a literal parsed at compile time. The spike judges names only: it does not read bodies and it does not judge suffix order. Its first measure is the library's string class: of its 595 shipped spellings of the verbs find, replace and remove, 584 agree with the declaration and 11 do not. I told in chapter 5 what Haro refused as grammar: the misspelled form, the free order of parameters and per-language method names. Here is what it means for this chapter: five forms do not run.

**The future form was trimmed on purpose.** The header of the natural-code file dates it 10 July 2026: the global queue of deferred actions left, and its FQ and FFQ wrappers were already gone. The reason is written in the design: natural language demands building the whole computational model before executing, and the engine's token plan now does that. It is a plan of the natural engine and no longer a suffix.

**The conditional form changed its mechanism.** The narration says it no longer goes through `eval()` but through a small text language that the engine compiles, and that the old extended form, WXT, is now a deprecated alias of W.

**For scope, the design document refuses the floating current.** Feature code is never written against an ambient "current system". It names its scope. And for concurrency the document records two refusals before there was any code. One is a method per operation that smears the frame across many verbs, such as `DoubleEachConcurrently`. The other is a mode set earlier. That mode is the antipattern this paradigm was founded against. It also refuses a scope that no host can perform. Its first guess for concurrency was thread vocabulary, and the language beneath has no threads.

**I refused to ship the whole engine to the edge.** The placement rule is: ship only what is critical and either unique to Softanza or weak on the target. A target that already excels at something gets nothing shipped.

Now the court, and which guards prove which of these claims.

## 5. The court

`00_regex_narrated` proves the verbs `Match` and `MatchFirst`: `Match` anchors, `MatchFirst` searches, Z hands back positions and does not print them. It asserts nothing for `MatchLine` and `MatchWord`: the blocks above show them, and only classic blocks, which are not guards, cover them. `972_narration_viz-finding_a_recurring_substring` proves the drawn answer. `full_scope_model_narrated` proves the system scope, with its three verdicts. `system_scope_model_narrated` proves the closed set of capabilities; one of its assertions is a timing limit, and in one probe run under load it did not hold, a run `data/` does not record. I print it beside the proof. `delivery_narrated` proves the placement plan.

Where the field does better comes next.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in convention. A name that is a label is easier to teach and easier to type. The grammar of this chapter has to be learned, and some of its forms are rarely used.

The field leads in the guarantee. Python's keyword arguments are checked at the call, and Swift's labels by the compiler, for every function, from the first day. The forms here are judged by the Haro spike, on names and not on bodies, and by the library's own parser, and only in the names that follow them.

On scope, the field leads in maturity. The closest field practice is the explicit context: the Go habit of passing a context to every call, a locale object that carries a frame. They are older and more widely used. What this paradigm adds is that the frame is one of a small, closed set, chosen in the verb, and the library can reason with it.

Last, the limit, with what is built, what is designed and what is absent.

## 7. The limit

What is built: fourteen of the twenty-two forms of a name run, some with examples other than the article's, and three more run in another shape. The library reads a name as a sentence. And scope runs in the regex verbs, in the system, and in the placement of a deployment. The article counts twenty-three sections: the twenty-two forms of a name, and "small functions", which are functions and not a form of a name. The book's number is twenty-two, the forms of a name.

What is designed: Haro's forms as data, with generated families; and the scope paradigm for time, units, memory and style, as rows of a table.

What is absent, and the page should say each with what exists beside it:

- **Partial**, `@Removed`, is in the archive only: the call is not defined.
- **Future**, `BeforeQ`, was trimmed on purpose, as I said.
- **Free**, the suffix that lets a call give its parameters in any order and any subset: there is no definition of it outside the archive. The library's own parser knows the name `FX` and not `FF`. The two narrations that tell it use two names, and the code has neither.
- **Default**, `dftRange` and `infRange`: not defined; the library's comment calls the informing form not yet built.
- **Multilingual**, a method named in French or Arabic: not in the live library. The names exist in the archive. The nearest thing today is the language packs of chapter 11.
- The three that run in another shape. The exceptional form exists, but the article's example does not. Free order works for the first order only: in my probe of 8 October, the swapped call ended the program with a status of one and printed nothing. And the statement form is half in place: the "All" half works, and the "No" half is the next step. The library's own classic test promises true on two of its four "No" lines, and the run prints false on all four.
