On the scope side: the regex verbs differ from the design table in two places. `MatchLine` lets a dot cross a line, where the table says it stays within one, and `MatchOneSegment`, which the table offers for the lazy case, is not defined. The system scope is built, and the design document still calls it designed: the record contradicts itself. The scope design document, version 1.2 of 16 August 2026, is quoted here as the document's text. The record does not say who wrote it, and I do not print it as mine. The system foundation document of 20 July 2026 says the foundation is implemented. In one probe run, which `data/` does not record, 61 of the 62 assertions of its narrated guard passed, and the one assertion that did not hold was a timing limit. I state what the code does: the system scope is built, with that result printed beside it, and the design document's word "designed" is the document's, out of date. And concurrency is a plan of record with no code: the word `Concurrently` has no definition.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Read a name as a sentence: its suffixes are its parameters, in order.

**The analyst.** A position can come as a number or as a section; a name says which.

**The designer.** The prefix changes the kind of answer: viz draws it.

**The decision-maker.** A scope is chosen in the verb, so the frame is visible where the call is made, and a review can see it.

**The educator.** The library parses a name into its sentence, so a learner can ask it what a method says.

**The agent.** Write the scope in the verb. A call that names a scope is not moved to another frame by a flag set elsewhere.

## 9. Exercise

Take one verb from a library you use that behaves differently in two frames: a mode, a locale, a target. Say in one sentence what the hidden frame is. Then write, as you would like it to be, two names for the same act in the two frames, with the frame in the verb. Before you run anything in Softanza, take `Match` and `MatchLine` on a text of three lines and write what each prints for a pattern that anchors at the start.

Run it. If your two names differ only by a flag, you have not yet moved the frame into the verb.

I left this one in your hands, on purpose. In section 3 the article promised `SOFTANZA` from a removal, and the run printed `STAZA`. Here is that block with one change, the last argument of the call:

```haro exercise
o2 = new stzString("SOOooooFFfffTANNnnnZA")
o2.RemoveManyCS([ "o", "f", "n" ], TRUE)
? o2.Content()
```

> run: exercise

Run it. Set what it prints beside what the article promised and beside what the first run printed. Section 3 says why the first run did not give the article's line; I do not print what this one gives. When you have solved it, you will know what the case flag decides in a removal, and whether it alone can keep the article's promise.

Chapter 9 turns to patterns of thought. It presents the Softanzuter, a computational representation of a thinking machine. It is based on one or many Regexuters, which identify and react to patterns of thought. With it come Reaxis and the engines.


=====

# Patterns of thought: the Softanzuter, Reaxis and the engines

Source: Chapter 9. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the Regexuter and the Listexuter, reacting with a state that remembers; Reaxis, reactive programming in plain words, on the engine's event loop; the timeline, the grid and deep operations; the agent whose skills cascade to a fixpoint; designed: the Softanzuter proper, many faculties sharing one state and cascading until it settles; the universal engine behind it; its four siblings, analyser, genetic, linguistic and quantic; absent: a dependency chain that links one trigger to another; a section-merging verb the article presents as its point

Run: 8 of 8 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:14 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take the three subjects of the title in turn: the Softanzuter, Reaxis, and the engines, of which the timeline and the grid run here. The Regexuter binds a pattern to code that fires and remembers why; Reaxis is reactive programming with its vocabulary redesigned. I climb the ladder the architecture draws, four rungs, and at the third I run the block that fails to cascade rather than claim a rung that is not built. I say what I refused: regex as a way to read text, the field's words for reactive programming, the four siblings, and the cascade. Everything serves one sentence: a Softanzuter is a computational representation of a thinking machine, based on one or many Regexuters, enabling it to identify and react to patterns of thought.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field has three tools for making a program react. Regular expressions recognise a shape in text, and nothing more: they say "this matches", and they stop. Reactive programming, the family of observables, subscribers and schedulers that Rx made popular, lets data flow through a program over time. Its vocabulary comes from plumbing, telephony and publishing: pipes, backpressure, hot and cold sources. And rule engines, the production systems of the field with their forward chaining, fire rules when facts appear, and keep firing while new facts appear. (General knowledge; the rule engines of the field are older and more mature than anything in this chapter.)

Each tool is separate. A regular expression does not trigger a computation, a reactive stream does not recognise a pattern, and a rule engine does not have a vocabulary that a person would use to say a thought. I hold this as a view, not a result.

The next section gives the one sentence I answer with.

## 2. The first principle

I gave this paradigm one sentence, and everything below tries to make it mechanical: **a Softanzuter is a computational representation of a thinking machine, based on one or many Regexuters, enabling it to identify and react to patterns of thought.**

The next section shows how far the library has made it mechanical.

## 3. The Softanza form

The architecture draws it as a ladder with four rungs, each adding one thing.

The first rung is the pattern. You will see it in chapter 15: a shape in a medium, a string, a list, a number, that can say "this matches, and here is how", and do nothing more. The second rung gives a pattern a reaction: the Regexuter, a reflex arc. You bind a pattern to code, process a text, and the code fires on every match. A Regexuter reacts, and it remembers what it did. Here it redacts the secrets in a sentence. What does the state it keeps say about each one: what it matched, what it computed, where, and which trigger fired?

```haro
rxu() {
	Trigger(:SensitiveData = "(password|secret|key):\s*(\w+)")
	@C(:When = :SensitiveData, :Do = '{ @value = "REDACTED-" + len(@value) }')
	Process("User credentials: password: abc123 secret: def456")
	? @@( Results() )
	#--> [ "REDACTED-16", "REDACTED-14" ]
	? @@( Positions() )
	#--> [ 19, 36 ]
	? State()[1][:matchedvalue]
	#--> password: abc123
	? State()[1][:computedvalue]
	#--> REDACTED-16
	? @@( State()[2][:dependson] )
	#--> [ "sensitivedata" ]
}
```

> run: kept

The state is provenance: every reaction that changes a value keeps its why. The last line, `[ "sensitivedata" ]`, is also an honest limit, and I will come back to it: what the Regexuter records as a dependency is today only its own trigger.

Now the third rung, the one the definition is about. It is many Regexuters, over different media, sharing one state, so that what one faculty writes is a medium another faculty can match over. A thought is a state entry with its provenance, and a settled thought is a state in which nothing more fires. That rung is not built, and the block shows how I know. The second trigger below is written for numbers of three digits or more. The first trigger turns the 7 into 700, which is one. If the machine thought in cascades, would the second fire on the first's result? Run it:

```haro
rxu() {
	Trigger(:Number = "\d+")
	Trigger(:Big = "\d{3,}")
	@C(:When = :Number, :Do = '{ @value = @number(@value) * 100 }')
	@C(:When = :Big, :Do = '{ @value = "BIG" }')
	Process("Found 7 items")
	? @@( Matches() )
	#--> [ "7" ]
	? @@( Results() )
	#--> [ 700 ]
	? @@( Triggers() )
	#--> [ "number" ]
}
```

> run: kept

It did not: the results read `[ 700 ]`, the triggers that fired read `[ "number" ]`. `BIG` is nowhere! Every trigger is matched once against the original text, and what one trigger writes is not offered to the others. That is the whole difference between the second rung and the third.

The cascade runs one rung above, at the fourth rung, the agent: the thinking machine given a body in a world, and a goal. Its mind is made of skills, each with a condition and an action, which chain. The second skill fires on the fact the first one wrote. `Pursue()` returns how many skill firings were verified, and goes on until the world stops changing, or for twenty rounds at most. What does it answer when asked again?

```haro
oAgent = new stzPIAgent("cook")
oAgent.MemoryQ().Learn("order", "state", "placed")
s1 = new stzAgentSkill("cook")
s1.SetWhen(func oM { return oM.Fact("order", "state", "placed") })
s1.SetDoes(func oM { oM.Forget("order","state","placed") oM.Learn("order","state","cooking") return 1 })
s2 = new stzAgentSkill("serve")
s2.SetWhen(func oM { return oM.Fact("order", "state", "cooking") })
s2.SetDoes(func oM { oM.Forget("order","state","cooking") oM.Learn("order","state","served") return 1 })
oAgent.AddSkill(s1)
oAgent.AddSkill(s2)
? oAgent.Pursue()
#--> 2
? oAgent.MemoryQ().Fact("order", "state", "served")
#--> TRUE
? oAgent.Pursue()
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

It answers `2`, then `0`, and between the two the fact `served` reads `TRUE`. That is a fixpoint. The guard that proves it labels the scene in its own words: `the cascade to fixpoint (Softanzuter at agent level)`. In chapter 17 you will see this same agent refused at a gate. Here is the other face of it: the agent that chains, because its mind is programmatic.

So the word has three meanings in the record, and the reader deserves to be told which is which. In this chapter, "Softanzuter" is the third rung, the thinking machine of the definition: designed, not built. The engine has a module with the same name. The macroplan presents it as the foundation for agents. Its slots hold a state and a bounded mailbox, and a message into a full queue is refused, counted and never dropped quietly. The agent of the block above, `stzPIAgent`, never calls it. And the design of a universal engine behind all of it, a medium where any pattern language becomes a computation, is planned.

**Reaxis** is the other half of the chapter's title, and it is a different idea. It is reactive programming with a vocabulary redesigned from the ground. A reactive thing is a Container, a Stream and an Rfunction: a function in waiting, which is not a callback. Data flows through a stream in plain words, a transformation, a filter, what to do with what passes. Four prices go in, 80, 90, 100 and 120: which ones come out, and at what value?

```haro
aSeen = []
Rs = new stzReactiveSystem()
Rs {
	oStream = CreateStream("prices")
	oStream {
		Transform(func price { return price * 1.20 })
		Filter(func price { return price >= 100 })
		OnPassed(func item { aSeen + item })
		FeedMany([ 80, 90, 100, 120 ])
	}
	RunLoop()
}
? @@( aSeen )
#--> [ 108, 120, 144 ]
```

> run: kept

`aSeen` reads `[ 108, 120, 144 ]`: the 80 became 96 and was dropped by the filter; the 90 became 108 and passed. Three functions are in waiting, and nothing here is called a subscriber; the old word `Subscribe` survives as an alias of `OnPassed`.

Reaxis replaced backpressure with overflow, a word you do not need a diagram to understand. And it was audited. A reading of the four strategies the article documented found that three of them lost data and reported none. The default discarded everything past the capacity while the counter read zero. That is fixed, and proved for all four strategies. Here, four items go into a buffer of two. What does the stream say happened?

```haro
oRs = new stzReactiveSystem()
oS = oRs.CreateStream("probe")
oS.SetOverflowStrategy(BUFFER_REJECT_NEWEST, 2)
for i = 1 to 4
	oS.Receive(i)
next
? oS.OverflowStats()[:currentBuffer]
#--> 2
? oS.OverflowStats()[:droppedCount]
#--> 2
```

> run: kept

It says `currentBuffer` is `2` and `droppedCount` is `2`. The counter that read zero now counts!

And for what the field calls debounce, Reaxis says what it means: wait for the attribute to settle. Three changes in quick succession: how many reactions, once the value has stopped changing for fifty milliseconds?

```haro
nSettled = 0
oRs4 = new stzReactive()
oX = oRs4.ReactiveObject()
oX.WaitForAttributeToSettle("query", 50, func(attr, oldV, newV) { nSettled++ })
oX.SetAttribute("query", "s")
oX.SetAttribute("query", "so")
oX.SetAttribute("query", "sof")
oX { ProcessPendingReactions() }
oRs4.RunLoop()
? nSettled
#--> 1
oRs4.Stop()
```

> run: kept

One reaction. `nSettled` reads `1`.

The chapter's third subject is the engines, the small paradigms the library rethought one by one. Chapter 3 gave you truth and emptiness. Two more, run here. Time is a workspace, not a number. A timeline holds spans and computes the gaps and the overlaps. What does it say of the gap between two phases, and what does it do with a point that would fall into a blocked region?

```haro
oTl = new stzTimeLine("2024-01-01 00:00:00", "2024-12-31 23:59:59")
oTl.AddSpan("PHASE1", "2024-01-01 00:00:00", "2024-02-15 23:59:59")
oTl.AddSpan("PHASE2", "2024-03-01 00:00:00", "2024-04-15 23:59:59")
? len(oTl.Gaps())
#--> 1
? oTl.Gaps()[1][:after]
#--> PHASE1
? oTl.Gaps()[1][:before]
#--> PHASE2
? oTl.HasOverlaps()
#--> FALSE
oTl.AddSpan("PHASE2B", "2024-04-01 00:00:00", "2024-05-15 23:59:59")
? oTl.HasOverlaps()
#--> TRUE
oB = new stzTimeLine("2024-01-01", "2024-12-31")
oB.AddBlockedSpan("MAINTENANCE", "2024-06-15 09:00:00", "2024-06-15 17:00:00")
oB.AddPoint("EVENT1", "2024-06-15 08:00:00")
try
	oB.AddPoint("EVENT2", "2024-06-15 12:00:00")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Point 'EVENT2' falls within a blocked span or blocked point
```

> run: kept

The gap is named by what it lies between, `PHASE1` and `PHASE2`. The blocked span refused `EVENT2`! The refusal carries its reason: `Point 'EVENT2' falls within a blocked span or blocked point`.

And a grid has a cursor that knows where it is: position is column and row, and moving past an edge stays at the edge. Two moves down and three to the right, then three more to the right: where is the cursor?

```haro
o = new stzGrid([ 5, 5 ])
o.MoveDown()
o.MoveDown()
o.MoveRight()
o.MoveRight()
o.MoveRight()
? @@( o.CurrentPosition() )
#--> [ 4, 3 ]
o.MoveRight()
o.MoveRight()
o.MoveRight()
? @@( o.CurrentPosition() )
#--> [ 5, 3 ]
```

> run: kept

At `[ 4, 3 ]`, then at `[ 5, 3 ]`. Three more moves to the right gained one column, and the cursor stayed at the edge!

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused to leave regex as a way to read text.** I made it a computational medium. A trigger fires a computation, the computation writes a state when it changes the value, and the state records its why. The Regexuter is a faculty and not a mind, and it holds no goal. I added the goal two rungs above it, at the fourth rung, in the agent, on purpose.

**I refused the field's vocabulary for reactive programming.** Observables, subscribers, backpressure, hot and cold sources, schedulers: borrowed from plumbing, telephony and publishing, and not from the problem. This is my view of those words, not a result. A function in waiting is an Rfunction. Data flows, and nothing is called a producer or a consumer. Overflow replaces backpressure. Waiting for an attribute to settle replaces debounce. The old spellings still work as aliases: feed, emit, send and receive all call one method, so my change was one of naming and not of removal.

**I refused to build the siblings.** The article that tells the Regexuter describes four more: an analyser, a genetic one, a linguistic one and a quantic one. In the genetic one patterns evolve against a fitness, in the linguistic one triggers are grammar rules, and in the quantic one a pattern can be in several states at once. The article gives each a sample call, and no class stands behind it. The design later found a purpose for two of them. Answering by example demands pattern induction, the genetic one's ground; several acceptable answers demand superposed candidates, collapsed by rules or by choice, the quantic one's. Chapter 18 tells where that stands. They remain a vision; two have their purpose written.

**I refused to claim the cascade.** The library's architecture says it itself: the scaffolding anticipates cascading but does not yet re-fire dependent triggers to a fixpoint. I would rather show you the block that fails to cascade than a block that does.

## 5. The court

The Regexuter and the Listexuter have classic tests, and no narrated guard. The Regexuter's eight classic tests ran without error: the folder's log reads `PASS 8 ERR 0`. Nothing there checks what they print. Five of the Listexuter's seven fail their own expected output, and two promise nothing. The agent's cascade is proved by `piagent_narrated`, scene five. Reaxis has many guards. `00_overflow_narrated` proves the four overflow strategies and the case that loses nothing; `60_reaxis_on_reactor_narrated` proves a one-shot timer, the repeating timer, non-blocking HTTP and the settling attribute on the engine's loop. The timeline has `test_timeline_regression`, which covers construction, duration, adding points and moving the ends, and not gaps, overlaps or blocked spans. The grid has `00_grid_narrated`, and deep operations `01_deeplist_narrated`.

The next section sets this record beside the field's.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field's rule engines lead. They are older and more mature than the Regexuter, they chain to a fixpoint, and the algorithms behind them are decades old. What I claim is not a rule engine. It is the condition side: rules that are sentences of the pattern languages, over the two media that have an Uter built, text and lists. The state keeps, for every firing that changes a value, its why. The library's own architecture calls the Regexuter a nascent forward-chaining rule engine, and I will not call it more.

The field's reactive libraries lead in reach. Rx exists in most mainstream languages, with a large set of operators. (General knowledge.) Reaxis is one library on one event loop. The engine's own module for it is a small table of substring rules: the Container, Stream and Rfunction model lives in the library, on the event loop of the engine.

I will not print the star-rating comparison with the field's tools that the library's article on reactive objects carries (`softanza-reactive-objects-narration.md`). It is undated and unsourced.

The next section says where all of this stops.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the Regexuter and the Listexuter; Reaxis on the engine's event loop; the timeline, the grid and deep operations; the agent whose skills chain.

What is designed: the third rung, many faculties on one shared state, cascading until it settles. The universal engine for it is planned. The four siblings are vision. The macroplan marks the engine module as done, and the architecture table marks it planned: the same name for two things. That table marks every module of its fifth layer planned, including ones that have source, so it is stale on this point. The universal medium is planned in the engine design document.

What is absent or broken at this commit:

- The dependency chain is not one. Each state entry depends only on its own trigger, so the chain records which trigger was active and never a link between two. Asking for the chain overflows the stack: the run ends with `Error (R4) : Stack Overflow`. No test calls it.
- Five of the Listexuter's seven classic tests fail their own expected output, and two promise nothing. The error log of that folder records, in the sixth file, `Error (C8) : Parentheses ')' is missing`.
- A nested list that matches as a whole is reported twice.
- The article's repeating timer takes three arguments and the library takes two; the form with units is `RunEveryXT`.
- Two of the deep-lists article's path verbs, `ItemAtPath` and `ItemsAtPaths`, live on a subclass, `stzDeepList`, and not on a plain list; `Paths()` is on a plain list.
- The section-merging article presents a comprehensive verb as its point, and that verb does not exist. The two that do exist, merging what overlaps and merging what is included, run. Section merging has no test file of its own.
- There is no native engine module for the Container, Stream and Rfunction model: it runs in the library, and the engine's own reactive module is a small table of substring rules. The timeline's guard checks no gap, no overlap and no blocked span; in the classic timeline files, seven fail their expected output and eighteen promise nothing. The grid's engine-backed navigator, `stzGridNav`, has no test.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Bind a pattern to code, and process; or declare a stream and say in words what happens to the data that flows through it.

**The analyst.** Time is a workspace: gaps, overlaps and blocked regions are computed, and a point that would fall in a blocked region is refused with the reason.

**The designer.** The state of a reaction that changes a value keeps its why: the match, the computed value, the position, the trigger.

**The decision-maker.** Overflow is a number you can read: kept, dropped. A strategy that lost data and said nothing was found by an audit, and fixed.

**The educator.** The ladder is a curriculum: a pattern, a reaction, a state, a goal.

**The agent.** Your mind can be skills that chain on what they perceive, until the world stops changing.

## 9. Exercise

Write a Regexuter with two triggers, one for numbers and one for numbers of three digits or more, with code that turns a number into its triple. Before you run it, write under each question what you expect: what the matches are, what the results are, and whether the second trigger fires on the first one's result. Run it. If the second trigger fires, you have found something I did not.

Then say in one sentence what would have to change in the machine for it to fire, and which rung of the ladder that change belongs to.

I left this one in your hands. Take the second block of section 3 and write its two `Trigger` lines in the other order, the three-digit one first:

```haro exercise
rxu() {
	Trigger(:Big = "\d{3,}")
	Trigger(:Number = "\d+")
	@C(:When = :Number, :Do = '{ @value = @number(@value) * 100 }')
	@C(:When = :Big, :Do = '{ @value = "BIG" }')
	Process("Found 7 items")
	? @@( Matches() )
	? @@( Results() )
	? @@( Triggers() )
}
```

> run: exercise

Before you run it, write down whether the order changes what `Results()` and `Triggers()` hold. I do not print the output of this version, and I do not tell you the answer: section 3 says what each trigger is matched against, and section 4 says what I refused to claim. When you can say why, you will know what separates the second rung from the third.

Chapter 10 turns from the machine that thinks to the code that changes. Its title is *Programming becomes refinement*, and it holds to one principle: stop trying to govern the intelligence, and govern the change.


=====

# Programming becomes refinement

Source: Chapter 10. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the refinement point declared in the source; the gate with five checks; the cascade of a point and of a class; reversal by step, by checkpoint and to zero; the trust floor and governed refining; designed: the experience the article describes: asking a point to explain itself, applying a plan, a one-line refinement; the cascade of the code graph joined to the gate; the refinement seat of Haro; absent: a saved history: the file keeps the source and nothing of the timeline

Run: 9 of 9 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:14 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I say what I mean by refining rather than programming. It is a change to code declared to be changeable, proposed in a typed form, judged by a gate, recorded as a step, and reversible. I run the library's form of it: the refinement point, the proposal, the gate, the cascade, the trust floor and the reversal. And I print the gate as it is, five checks and not the four of my design. I say where the field leads and where the gate stops. The principle is the sentence I wrote on the back cover of my first book: stop trying to govern the intelligence, and govern the change.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field has a name for changing infrastructure with a rehearsal: plan, then apply. A tool computes what would change, shows it, and applies it only when you say so. Terraform made the pattern familiar, and the field's admission controllers apply the same idea to a request before a cluster accepts it. More recently, the field has a name for a way of working with code-writing agents: specifications first, and the agent fills in. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.)

What these share is where they stand. The plan and the admission control are about infrastructure and requests. For the code itself, much of the field's answer to a change is still a diff, a patch of text, and a person's review of it. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.) That worked while every change had a person behind it who could be asked. It is a weak instrument when most changes arrive from agents, in volume; I hold this as a view, not a result.

Against that weak instrument I set one sentence.

## 2. The first principle

I wrote the sentence on the back cover of my first book, and I have not found a better one: **stop trying to govern the intelligence, and govern the change.** The library's header says the same thing in the form of a type: a change is a typed proposal, not a diff.

The rest of the chapter runs that sentence.

## 3. The Softanza form

The practice has changed names in my life. I used to say I programmed. I now say that, more and more, I refine, and I mean something exact by it. A refinement is a change to code that was declared to be changeable, proposed in a typed form, judged by a gate, recorded as a step, and reversible. The first book of this imprint, *Refinement-Oriented Programming*, tells the discipline through the story of a bank facing a regulator's letter. This chapter tells what the library carries of it.

It begins by declaring where the code may change. A refinement point is a named knob written in the source as a tag, with its kind, its value and its bounds:

```haro
cSrc = 'vat_rate = <R:PARAM name="vat" value="0.20" min="0" max="0.25">' + nl +
       'sort_by  = <R:ALGO name="sort" value="quick" options="quick|merge|heap">'
o = new stzRefinableCode(cSrc)
? o.NumberOfPoints()
#--> 2
? @@( o.RefinementPoints() )
#--> [ [ "vat", "param", "0.20" ], [ "sort", "algo", "quick" ] ]
? o.KindOf("vat")
#--> param
```

> run: kept

A change is then a proposal. It names a point and a value; if it clears the gate it takes effect and is recorded as one step:

```haro
r = o.Refine("vat").To("0.22")
? r[:admitted]
#--> 1
? o.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.22
? o.NumberOfSteps()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

And what does a proposal that does not clear the gate change, and what does the gate say? Run it:

```haro
r2 = o.Refine("vat").To("0.99")
? r2[:admitted]
#--> 0
? r2[:why]
#--> constraint: 0.99 above max 0.25
? o.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.22
? o.Refine("sort").To("bubble")[:why]
#--> constraint: 'bubble' not in options quick|merge|heap
? o.Refine("ghost").To("1")[:why]
#--> structural: no refinement point 'ghost'
? o.NumberOfSteps()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Three refusals, and the timeline did not grow by a step! The library's comment calls this its third law: a rejected proposal never ends changed. Each refusal names its stage first, `constraint` twice and `structural` once, as in `structural: no refinement point 'ghost'`, so that a person, or an agent, can correct the proposal without reading a diff.

**The cascade** is what you read instead of the diff. Before the change, ask what it reaches. There are two cascades, and they are separate. For a point, it is the line it sits on and the other points on that same line:

```haro
cPrice = 'price = <R:PARAM name="vat" value="0.20" min="0" max="0.30"> * <R:PARAM name="margin" value="1.1" min="1" max="2">' + nl +
         'floor = <R:PARAM name="floor" value="0.10" min="0" max="0.30">'
oc = new stzRefinableCode(cPrice)
aC = oc.Cascade("vat")
? aC[:lines][1]
#--> 1
? @@( aC[:alsoTouches] )
#--> [ "margin" ]
? oc.Cascade("floor")[:lines][1]
#--> 2
? len(oc.Cascade("floor")[:alsoTouches])
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

`vat` sits on line 1 and also touches `[ "margin" ]`; `floor` sits on line 2, alone.

For a class, the cascade is a property of the structure of the code: who descends from it and who inherits a method. The library computes it from the code graph, and it can scan a text without touching a file:

```haro
cCls = 'class Tariff' + nl +
       '	def Rate()' + nl +
       '		return 0.20' + nl +
       'class Invoice from Tariff' + nl +
       '	def Total()' + nl +
       '		return 1' + nl +
       'class Receipt from Invoice' + nl +
       '	def Print()' + nl +
       '		return 1' + nl +
       'class Quote from Tariff' + nl +
       '	def Rate()' + nl +
       '		return 0.18' + nl
g0 = new stzRingCodeGraph("")
g0.ScanSource(cCls, "world.ring")
g0.BuildGraph()
? @@( g0.Cascade("Tariff")[:descendants] )
#--> [ "Invoice", "Quote", "Receipt" ]
? g0.Cascade("Tariff")[:blastRadius]
#--> 3
? @@( g0.ImpactOf("Rate")[:inheritedBy] )
#--> [ "Invoice", "Receipt" ]
```

> run: kept

Change the rate of the tariff and three classes are in the blast radius. Two of them inherit the method, and `ImpactOf` names them: `[ "Invoice", "Receipt" ]`. The third, `Quote`, redefines it, so it owns the method and does not merely inherit it. That is the difference `ImpactOf` draws.

How does a change reach its dependents? By a rule that spans several points, judged on the state the change would produce. If the rule fails, the value is put back:

```haro
d = new stzRefinableCode(cPrice)
d.DeclareDerivation("vat-above-floor",
	func oCode { return ring_number(oCode.ValueOf("vat")) >= ring_number(oCode.ValueOf("floor")) },
	"vat must not drop below floor")
? d.Refine("vat").To("0.30")[:admitted]
#--> 1
r3 = d.Refine("vat").To("0.05")
? r3[:admitted]
#--> 0
? r3[:why]
#--> derivation: rule 'vat-above-floor' violated -- vat must not drop below floor
? d.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.30
```

> run: kept

The rule refused the change and named itself: `derivation: rule 'vat-above-floor' violated -- vat must not drop below floor`. The value is still `0.30`.

And who proposed it? Every proposal carries its origin, and a point can set a floor of trust that refuses a lower origin, without refusing a person. What happens to a proposal from a language model when the floor of the point asks for a trusted origin? Run it:

```haro
d.TrustFloor("vat", :trusted)
r4 = d.Refine("vat").As(:llm).To("0.25")
? r4[:admitted]
#--> 0
? r4[:why]
#--> trust: posture :llm below the required floor for 'vat'
? d.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.30
? d.Refine("vat").As(:trusted).To("0.25")[:admitted]
#--> 1
? d.PostureOf(d.NumberOfSteps())
#--> trusted
```

> run: kept

The model's proposal was refused: `trust: posture :llm below the required floor for 'vat'`. The point kept `0.30`! The same proposal from a trusted origin was admitted, and the step records `trusted`.

And who may turn which knob? That is declared, too: a permission, and an authority level against a risk tier. Take a delegated actor and a knob of the third tier. Run it:

```haro
gv = new stzRefinableCode(cPrice)
gv.SetGovernedBy(new stzGovernance("release-ops")).SetActor("release-bot")
gv.SetRiskFor("vat", 3).SetAllowRefine("vat").SetAuthorityLevel(:Delegated)
r5 = gv.Refine("vat").To("0.22")
? r5[:admitted]
#--> 0
? r5[:why]
#--> governance: refused: 'release-bot' holds 'delegated' authority (level 2) but 'refine-vat' is risk tier 3 (SHOULD does not cover it)
? gv.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.20
gv.SetAuthorityLevel(:Autonomous)
? gv.Refine("vat").To("0.22")[:admitted]
#--> 1
? gv.GovernanceQ().NumberOfDecisions()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

The delegated actor was refused, `'release-bot' holds 'delegated' authority (level 2)`, and the value stayed `0.20`. The same proposal was admitted once the actor was escalated! The decision is recorded: `NumberOfDecisions()` printed `1`.

Last, can a step be taken back? Reversibility is part of the data model: every admitted step keeps its prior value. Rewind to a named checkpoint, rewind to zero, go forward again:

```haro
t = new stzRefinableCode(cPrice)
t.Refine("vat").To("0.21")
t.Refine("vat").To("0.22")
t.Checkpoint("reviewed")
t.Refine("vat").To("0.28")
? t.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.28
t.RevertToCheckpoint("reviewed")
? t.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.22
? t.NumberOfSteps()
#--> 2
t.RevertTo(0)
? t.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.20
? t.CanRevert()
#--> 0
t.Redo()
? t.ValueOf("vat")
#--> 0.21
```

> run: kept

At zero, `CanRevert()` printed `0`. One `Redo()`, and the value is `0.21`, the first step again.

That is the gate in the form the library has it. Five checks, in the order the code runs them. The structure of the proposal, then the constraints of the point, then the governance, then the trust of the origin, and last the derivation across points. The header of the file calls this a four-stage gate in one line and five in another. The code numbers its checks 1, 2, 3, 4 and 4b. The trust check was added to the four on 16 July 2026, and the design's count was not changed. I report what runs: five checks.

Behind the gate stand decisions, and what I refused.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused to govern the intelligence.** Much of the field's approach to agents is to make the model safer: alignment, guardrails, a prompt that asks for confirmation. (General knowledge, as above.) I refused that frame. A model's output is whatever it is, from whichever model, and the intelligence of the next generation will not hold still for me to govern it. The change is the thing that stays, and it is the thing that reaches the world: this is my view. So the gate stands where the change arrives, and it asks one thing: what is proposed, from where, and does the code say this point may change that way.

**I refused the diff as the unit.** A diff is a text change. A refinement is a typed proposal about a declared point, with a kind, bounds and an origin. I kept the diff as an artefact, and I refused it as the thing to review.

**I made the library carry the verb, and left the environment elsewhere.** The refinement as a library verb, the point, the gate, the cascade and the reversal, belongs to the open foundation. The environment in which a team lives with it day to day, the screens, the live mode, the book, is a product. It does not leak into the library.

**I printed the gate as it is.** I wrote the design with four stages, and the code has five checks. A page that quotes my design and not my code would be wrong, and it is the code that runs.

## 5. The court

`refinablecode_narrated` proves the point declared, the proposal that passes, the two refusals that leave the source whole (bounds and options), the cascade, the reversal, and the saved file. `refine_deepening_narrated` proves the timeline with its checkpoints and its redo, and the trust floor. `gate_deepening_narrated` proves the refusal of an unknown point, the derivation across points with its rollback, and the governed refusal with its lineage.

Next comes the field, and where it leads.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in infrastructure, where plan and apply are the daily practice of many teams and the apply talks to real providers. (General knowledge, as in section 1.) Softanza's gate is about the change to code, which the field handles with a review.

The field's tools lead in breadth: they are not tied to one library, one language, or one declared knob. (General knowledge, as in section 1.) Softanza's gate judges what the code declared as a point. A change to a place that was never declared is not a refinement here; it is outside the gate.

And on trust, much of the field's approach is built to scale to any model. (General knowledge, as in section 1.) Mine rests on one declaration per point.

Now, where the gate stops.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the refinement point, the gate with its five checks, the cascade of a point and of a class, the reversal, the trust floor, and governed refining. The guards of section 5 cover them, with two exceptions. The cascade of a class has no guard here, because the code-graph guard scans a live path of the library's working tree. And no guard asserts the other points on a line.

What is designed, and absent from the library by name: the experience the original article describes. The class that article names, `stzPolyCode`, is not in the library at this commit. The one-line refinement of the design document, a point that explains itself, a plan that is applied, are not in the library. The cascade of the code graph and the cascade of the gate are not joined. And Haro has a gate in a spike written only in the former host language, with its own seat for refinement not built.

What is built, and where it stops: the limits of the gate as it stands.

- It checks the constraints of three kinds of point, a parameter, an algorithm and a library. A point of another kind, a block of code for instance, parses and passes without a check; checks for those kinds are the next step.
- An unknown trust posture ranks as trusted: the code gives it the top rank. So a proposal from an origin the code does not know clears a floor of trusted.
- A derivation is applied tentatively and restored on failure. So I say "never ends changed", not "never touched".
- The saved file holds the rendered source; the history, the postures, the timeline and the load-back are absent, and are the next step. Reversibility lives in the running object, and carrying it into the file is still to come. The library writes this file in the format `.zrfn`, as shipped. The Haro charter, ratified on 1 October 2026, takes `.rfn` for the sentence and leaves `.zrfn` to the library's comment-tag lift.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Declare the places that may change, and propose changes to them as typed proposals. A refusal names its stage.

**The analyst.** The rule that spans several points is yours to declare, and the gate judges it on the state the change would produce.

**The designer.** The cascade is what you read instead of the diff: for a point, which line and which points on it; for a class, which classes inherit. They are two cascades, not yet one.

**The decision-maker.** Who may turn which knob is a permission and an authority against a risk tier, and the decision is recorded with its authority at the time.

**The educator.** Step back one, rewind to a named checkpoint, rewind to zero, go forward again. A learner can try without fear because every step keeps its prior value.

**The agent.** You may propose. Your origin is on the proposal, and a point can set a floor your origin does not clear.

## 9. Exercise

Take two settings of a program of yours: a rate and a ceiling, say, with a rule that the rate must stay below the ceiling. Declare them as points, with bounds, and declare the rule across them. Before you run anything, write under each proposal whether it is admitted and, if not, at which stage it is refused. Propose a change of the rate above the ceiling, and one from an origin you declare as a model, against a floor you set. Then rewind to zero and go forward one step.

Run it. If you were wrong about the stage, you have found a place where your own gate disagrees with your own expectation.

I left this one in your hands. Section 7 says what the gate does with an origin the code does not know, and the trust block of section 3 is the block to run. Its first lines are below; change one thing, and replace `:llm` with a name of your own, one the code was never told about.

```haro exercise
d.TrustFloor("vat", :trusted)
r4 = d.Refine("vat").As(:yourorigin).To("0.25")
? r4[:admitted]
```

> run: exercise

Run it, and read what the gate does against what section 7 says it does. I will not tell you whether the gate is right. When you have decided, you will know what the trust floor does with an origin it has never met.

The next chapter states a law: a language is declared and closed, and whatever speaks it, a person or a model, is judged by it. Here the thing judged was a change; there it is whoever speaks.


=====

# Law: a language is declared and closed, and whatever speaks it, person or model, is judged by it

Source: Chapter 11. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the natural layer's vocabulary as data in five languages, with its refusals; the court of structured output, its grammar compiler and its sampler; the pattern languages, which accept a word they do not know; designed: the forge that produces a language's parser, court and grammar from its declaration alone; a grammar emitted for every declared language; the writing style as a declared grammar; absent: a natural layer closed all the way down: one form of list still reaches host code; pattern classes that refuse a word they do not know; the standard Hausa letter ɗ in the Hausa pack

Run: 11 of 11 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:14 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I state the law of Part III and run it. I begin with a sentence that runs because every word of it is in a dictionary the library owns, in English, Hausa, French and Arabic. I show what the library says about a word it does not know. Then I declare the shape of an answer as a schema, judge answers against it, and show the one thing a grammar cannot kill: a false answer in the right shape. I say which guards prove this, where the field is ahead, and where the natural layer is not yet closed, which the law states. The law itself is one sentence: a language is declared and closed, and whatever speaks it, a person or a model, is judged by it.

## What the AI age changes here

In Part II, the age changed who writes the code. Here it changes who speaks the sentence. A sentence of a language used to have a person behind it, someone who could be asked what they meant. Today the writer of a sentence can be a model, which cannot be asked, and which will write a fluent sentence whether or not it is true.

So the grammar must be closed, and the court, the part that accepts or refuses a sentence by its grammar, must read it. A language whose sentences may contain anything cannot refuse anything, and a court cannot judge a sentence it cannot read. The vision of the platform puts the whole movement in one line, which I ratified: humans and machines meet in declared languages, small, closed and judged, not in general-purpose code.

This is also where the order of the book turns around. The cover goes from the familiar to the new: programming, languages, intelligence. The record's logic goes the other way. The first act is to declare a language. Programming, the discipline of Part II, happens inside declared languages: the forms of the vocabulary are already a declaration in Haro, in a spike outside the library. Intelligence, Part IV, is governed by both: a model is held to a declared grammar before it speaks, and its sentence is judged after. The book keeps the cover's order, so this chapter sits in the middle; in the record's order it would come first.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

For a working programmer, a language is a general host, Python, JavaScript, Java. A "domain language" is usually a library whose calls read like sentences, while the file around them may contain any code of the host. The estate's own study of the field, written in August 2026, names what happened to those designs. Internal languages died of openness, because a build file is declarative only until someone writes arbitrary code in it. Language workbenches died of the weight of their tools. And it names the languages that won: SQL, regular expressions, Make, HTML and CSS, the configuration languages. They won because they are closed.

The model era added one practice: when a model writes, either check what it wrote, or constrain its output while it writes, with a grammar or a schema. The reference implementation of grammar-constrained sampling is an open project, and the record says so where it uses the same algorithm. And in the field's common language today, "programming in natural language" means one thing: prompting a model.

## 2. The first principle

**A language is declared and closed, and whatever speaks it, a person or a model, is judged by it.**

## 3. The Softanza form

Can a sentence run without a model guessing what it means? It can, when every word of it is in a dictionary the library owns:

```haro
Naturally("
    Make a string with 'hello niger' inside
    Spacify it and uppercase it
    @Box it while the box@ is rounded
    Display it on the screen
")
#--> ╭───────────────────────╮
#    │ H E L L O   N I G E R │
#    ╰───────────────────────╯
```

> run: kept

Four lines of English became a rounded box around spaced capitals.

The dictionary is data, keyed by meanings, not by English words. So can the same meaning be said in another language by adding a block of data, not code? Here is one instruction in English, in Hausa, in French and in Arabic. The Hausa sentence is the narration's own, and the French one comes from the library's guard with its data changed. I composed the Arabic one from the library's vocabulary. No native speaker has read the Hausa or the Arabic as far as the record shows:

```haro
? Naturally("Create a string with 'hello' and uppercase it").Result()
#--> HELLO
? NaturallyIn("hausa", "Yi rubutu dauke 'hello' Maida shi").Result()
#--> HELLO
? NaturallyIn("fr", "Crée une chaine avec 'hello' et mets la en majuscules").Result()
#--> HELLO
? NaturallyIn("ar", "أنشئ نصا بـ 'hello' ثم كبّره").Result()
#--> HELLO
```

> run: kept

Four languages, one meaning, and `HELLO` four times.

And a program says back what it understood, in its own language. A person reading Arabic can check the machine's reading of their sentence without reading a line of code.

```haro
o1 = NaturallyIn("ar", "أنشئ قائمة بـ [ 5, 3, 5 ] ثم أزل تكراراتها")
? @@( o1.Result() )
#--> [ 5, 3 ]
? o1.Understood()
#--> أنشئ قائمة بـ [ 5, 3, 5 ] -> أزل التكرارات
```

> run: kept

The program said back `أنشئ قائمة بـ [ 5, 3, 5 ] -> أزل التكرارات`: the list it was given, and the removal of duplicates.

Now the part the library does by rule: say what it did not understand. What does it do with a verb it does not know? Misspell one and run it:

```haro
o1 = Naturally("Create a string with 'softanza' Uppercse it")
? o1.Result()
#--> softanza
? @@( o1.Unresolved() )
#--> [ [ "Uppercse", "uppercase" ] ]
? o1.UnderstoodAll()
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The misspelled verb is not guessed. `Unresolved()` answers `[ [ "Uppercse", "uppercase" ] ]`, the word it met beside the word the library thinks you meant, and `UnderstoodAll()` answers `0`: the program admits that it did not understand everything. That is the permissive mode: an unknown word is skipped and reported. An agent gets the strict mode, which refuses, and a world, a declared list of allowed operations, can limit its sentences to that list:

```haro
try
	NaturallyStrict("Create a string with 'x' Fooify it")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Strict natural mode: not understood: 'Fooify' (did you mean 'boxify'?)
try
	NaturallyStrictIn("en", "Create a list with [ 3, 1 ] Reverse it", [ "METHOD_REMOVEDUPLICATES" ])
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Operation not permitted in this world: METHOD_REVERSE
```

> run: kept

Strict mode refuses `'Fooify'` and still names the nearest word: `did you mean 'boxify'?`. In that world, a sentence that reverses a list is refused! The refusal names the operation: `Operation not permitted in this world: METHOD_REVERSE`.

The other direction of natural code in Softanza is the near-natural chain, where the person is the parser and the chain reads as a sentence. When it answers no, does it say why?

```haro
? IsQ().TheQ().LengthQ().OfQ("softanza").LessThanQ().TheQ().LengthQ().Of("Haro")
#--> 0
? Why()
#--> no: 8 is not less than 4
```

> run: kept

The chain answered `0`, and `Why()` gave the reason: `no: 8 is not less than 4`. A verdict says why.

Now the law at its sharpest: a model. The library declares the shape of an answer as a schema. What happens to a schema that names a type the library does not have? It is judged when it is declared, before any model is called:

```haro
try
	StzOutputSchemaQ([ [ :field = "x", :type = :colour ] ])
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> stzOutputSchema: unknown field type 'colour' (use string|number|boolean|oneof|list|structure).
```

> run: kept

The refusal names the unknown type, `'colour'`, and lists the six it accepts: `(use string|number|boolean|oneof|list|structure)`. The schema was refused before any model was called! A correct schema is a small closed language. The court judges any answer against it, whoever wrote the answer. And the schema emits its grammar, so that a model can be held to it while it writes:

```haro
oS = StzOutputSchemaQ([
	[ :field = "name", :type = :string ],
	[ :field = "age",  :type = :number, :must = [ [ ">=", 0 ], [ "<=", 130 ] ] ],
	[ :field = "mood", :type = :oneof,  :choices = [ "positive", "negative" ] ]
])
? oS.Accepts("name: Hadiza" + nl + "age: 41" + nl + "mood: positive")
#--> 1
? oS.Accepts("name: Hadiza" + nl + "age: 41" + nl + "mood: angry")
#--> 0
? oS.Accepts("name: Hadiza" + nl + "age: 900000" + nl + "mood: positive")
#--> 0
? oS.Accepts("name: Hadiza" + nl + "age: 62" + nl + "mood: positive")
#--> 1
? oS.ToGBNF()
#--> root ::= name-line age-line mood-line
#    name-line ::= "name: " text "\n"
#    age-line ::= "age: " number "\n"
#    mood-line ::= "mood: " ("positive" | "negative") "\n"
#    text ::= [^\n]+
#    number ::= "-"? [0-9]+ ("." [0-9]+)?
#    boolean ::= ("yes" | "no" | "true" | "false")
```

> run: kept

Look at the four verdicts. The first answer printed `1`. The second, with `mood: angry`, and the third, with `age: 900000`, printed `0`: the court refused both. The fourth printed `1` again. Hadiza is forty-one in the first answer and sixty-two in the fourth, and the court accepts both! A grammar kills malformed sentences, not false ones. The record says it in five words: structure kills malformedness, not falsehood. This book says the same: the truth of an answer is not for the grammar. The record says that truth needs reversibility, an auditor and a Principal, the person who rules. A grammar is the gate, not the judge of everything.

A template is a contract too. A sentence with an unfilled hole is refused before it runs:

```haro
try
	NaturallyWith("Create a string with {#1} and {#2} it", [ "x" ])
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Unfilled template hole! The narration still contains a {#n} slot with no value for it.
```

> run: kept

And patterns are small languages of their own, though not yet closed ones: chapter 15 shows the pattern classes accepting a word they do not know, without complaint. The shape of a list is declared, not written as a loop:

```haro
? Lx("[@N, @S]").Match([42, "hello"])
#--> 1
? Lx("[@N, @S]").Match(["hello", 42])
#--> 0
? Lx("[@N, [@N2], @N]").Match([1, [2, 3], 4])
#--> 1
? Lx("[@N, [@N2], @N]").Match([1, [2], 4])
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

Two shapes matched, two did not, and no loop was written.

The chapters of this part prove the law from five sides. Chapter 12 tells a language declared: the forge (the machinery that produces a language's parser, court, printer and grammar from its declaration alone), the closed grammar with no escape into the host, and the court born with the grammar. It tells too the grammar emitted so that a model can only speak valid sentences, and its surfaces. Chapter 13 tells the two directions of natural code, the machine as the parser and the person as the parser, in one discipline. Chapter 14 tells knowledge programming, one file that is a system. Chapter 15 tells the pattern languages, regex lifted to lists, numbers, tables, matrices, time and graphs. Chapter 16 tells the seven languages Softanza reaches through one governed door, and why a door is not a surface.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I chose the act: declare a language.** Not "use our language". Declare yours: the language of your domain, in your words. That is the kernel of the platform, and it is the reason this part exists.

**I refused the open escape.** The meta-language that declares languages in the estate lives outside this library. Its founding rule refuses to define any form that would let a sentence reach host code, call a function, evaluate a string or touch a file. Not forbidden: unexpressible. An internal language that cannot refuse arbitrary host code is, in the words of the estate's field study, an API wearing a language's costume.

**I refused natural language as a way to declare.** Natural language asks; it never declares. A model may draft a declaration, and by the vision's rule, which is designed and not yet built, the draft enters the world only as a proposal: the model proposes, the court decides, a person ratifies.

**I kept the promise of 2020 and changed its mechanism.** My first slide promised executable natural language. The vision of 2026 judged that slogan: it promised more than a court can defend, because a natural sentence can be ambiguous and a lie can be grammatical. So the naturalness stays at the door, where a sentence asks or drafts, and the execution belongs to closed languages a court can judge. I built the natural layer that way long before the vision named it. Only words defined in the dictionary are interpreted. Every other word is skipped and reported, or refused in strict mode, never guessed.

**I took governance out of the library and kept the refusals in it.** In July 2026, a kit for governing agents landed inside the natural layer. I removed it the same day: the strict mode, the refusals, the why and the understood belong to the library; a product for governing agents does not. And I removed a set of markers: their letter-order cipher had been inverted by its own inventor, from memory, and they duplicated meanings. A language a person speaks never marks a reference that way. An ungrammatical chain that runs is a bug.

**I refused a second embedding.** Chapter 1 states that decision; here I add only that a door brings a foreign runtime, a surface brings a familiar syntax over the same grammar, and Chapter 16 tells the difference.

## 5. The court

`multilingual_pack_narrated` proves the languages as data: the packs, the programs in French and Arabic, a pack added at runtime, and the program paraphrasing back in its own language. `semantic_resolver_narrated` proves the unknown word with its suggestion, the strict mode, and the allow-list under which everything off the list is grammatically impossible. Its run does not yet reach its end at this commit. My research for chapter 13 ran it twice on 8 October 2026, and request L45 in the memo of 9 October records the result. It stops with an error raised from a string-splitting function, before its last assertion. So the page counts its assertions and does not claim its verdict. `literate_templates_narrated` proves that a template is a contract. `question_narrated` proves that a yes-or-no verdict records its why. `structuredoutput_narrated` proves the court of structured output, including the lie it lets through. `schema_grammar_narrated` proves the grammar compiler, and `constrained_decoding_narrated` the sampler that holds a model to it.

Seven guards prove the law; the next section says where the field is ahead of it.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in tools. The language workbenches remain ahead on the axes the estate's design deliberately gave up: projectional tooling (editors that understand a language as you type), IDE integration, macro-theoretic depth. The estate's study says it in its own words: that is a bet, and it is named as one. I hold that bet as a view, not a result.

The field built constrained decoding first. Softanza's sampler follows the reference implementation's algorithm and vendors nothing. Measured against one small model of a hundred and thirty-five million parameters, on ten prompts, the answers were valid at the first attempt two times in ten when checked afterwards. Held to the grammar, the model was valid ten times in ten. That is one small model and ten prompts, and nothing in the record measures a large one.

And the age cuts both ways. Generated code makes careless language-making cheap too. That is why the court is not an accessory of a declared language. It is the core. This is my view.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the natural layer, its vocabulary as data in five languages, English, Hausa, French, Arabic and Turkish, and its refusals. Built too: the court of structured output with its grammar compiler and its sampler, and the pattern languages. These accept a word they do not know today; closing them is to come.

What is designed: the forge that would produce, from a language's declaration alone, its parser, its court, its printer (the part that writes a sentence back out) and its grammar for a model. The forge is built outside the library and designed for the library: a first sketch of it was built outside the library in October 2026. The library cannot run it yet. The vision's text says the forge has not yet forged (made a language's parts from its declaration). The sketch has since forged courts and grammars for several languages, judged by those languages' own fixtures, and the vision has not caught up. Chapter 12 tells it with its limits. A grammar for every declared language is designed and to come.

What is absent, and the law states it. The natural layer is not closed all the way down. One form, a list written inside brackets, is still handed to the host to evaluate. Does a host function inside the brackets run? Try one:

```haro
? @@( Naturally("Create a list with [ upper('x'), 1 ] and remove its duplicates").Result() )
#--> [ "X", 1 ]
```

> run: kept

The function `upper` is not a word of the natural dictionary, and the list came back as `[ "X", 1 ]`. It ran anyway! The record already knows this escape and has routed its repair. Until the repair lands, "the natural layer is a closed language" is true of its words and not of its lists, and this book will say so.

And the Hausa pack does not yet read the letter ɗ, which standard Hausa spelling uses. The pack stores it in an encoding that does not carry it yet. When I probed it on 8 October 2026 on the snapshot before this commit, a sentence written with the letter was not read, and the pack did not report it. The Hausa in this chapter is written without the hook, the way the pack reads it today. The record holds no reading of the pack's sentences by a Hausa speaker. The foreign-language sentences printed in this chapter come from the library's narration and guards, or are composed from its vocabulary. No native speaker has read them as far as the record shows.

These are the limits; the next section opens six doors on them.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You keep writing code. What you declare is a closed grammar, and the court judges your sentence and an agent's by the same rules.

**The analyst.** Your domain's words become a data pack, and the system understands them or refuses them with the reason.

**The designer.** The refusals are part of the language: what is off the list is refused in strict mode, so it never needs a warning.

**The decision-maker.** A model held to a declared grammar answers in the right shape. It can still answer a falsehood in the right shape, and the record prints that limit with the result.

**The educator.** A sentence in Hausa, French or Arabic runs and says back what it understood, so a learner reads the machine's reading of their own words.

**The agent.** You may speak only the declared language. Strict mode is your posture, and your draft is, by the vision's rule (designed), a proposal the court judges.

## 9. Exercise

Declare, with `StzOutputSchemaQ`, a record of three fields from a domain you know: a loan request, for instance, with an amount inside a band, a currency from a closed list, and a purpose in free text. Before you run anything, write three answers a model might give: one the court must accept, one it must refuse because of its shape, and one it will accept although it is false. Under each, write your prediction as a promise.

Run it. The third answer is the lesson of this chapter. Write down, in one sentence, who in your domain must judge it, since the grammar cannot.

I left this one in your hands. Section 7 says that a list written inside brackets is still handed to the host, and section 4 says why a language must refuse any form that reaches host code. Run the section 7 block with one change, a different host function inside the brackets:

```haro exercise
? @@( Naturally("Create a list with [ lower('X'), 1 ] and remove its duplicates").Result() )
```

> run: exercise

Look at what the library does with the function, and say in one sentence what a closed natural layer would have done instead. When you have, you will know why "the natural layer is a closed language" is true of its words and not yet of its lists.

Chapter 12 is *A language, declared*. Its principle: declaring a language is as easy as declaring a variable.


=====

# A language, declared

Source: Chapter 12. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: in the library: a declaration judged when it is declared; a court and a grammar from the one declaration, with the list of what the grammar dropped; a grammar machine that walks bytes and refuses by name; a description for a person and a prompt for a model from the same text. Outside the library: a forge that turns a declaration into a court and a grammar, run on the estate's own languages; designed: the forge inside the library; its full list of products from one declaration (printer, knowledge graph, natural door, narration, runtime, one static binary); the projections of a language to an application; absent: the forge's grammar for a menu word; a guard that asserts the refusal of a host function in a condition; the figure two of ten against ten of ten, which needs a model file the snapshot does not hold

Run: 8 of 8 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:14 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take one declaration of a restaurant's order and give it to three readers: a person, a model and the court. I let the same declaration produce a grammar, and I show where the grammar and the court agree and where they part. Then I leave the library for the forge. It is the machinery that turns a language's declaration alone into its court and its grammar, and I say what it has forged and what it has not. I decided that Haro is the language of languages, and I refused an open escape form. The principle is one rule of Haro: declaring a language is as easy as declaring a variable.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Two families of tools let you declare a language in the field. The first is the language workbench and the parser generator: JetBrains MPS, Xtext, Spoofax, and Racket's `#lang` give you the means to define a language with its grammar, its editor and its checks. The estate's own field study of August 2026 says why they did not win. They depended on an ecosystem, and Racket's languages live and die inside Racket.

The second is the internal DSL: a library whose calls read like sentences, while the file can still hold any host code. The same study has the sentence I keep: closedness is the product, and an internal DSL that cannot refuse arbitrary host code is an API wearing a language's costume. Its control group is the closed languages that won: SQL, regex, Make, HTML and CSS, HCL, GraphQL, Markdown, CUE.

For the grammar a model must obey, the record names one tool: the grammar sampler of a well-known local inference library, which the library's machine follows for its element encoding and its algorithm. Nothing of it is vendored. (General knowledge, not a study of the record: model vendors offer structured-output modes that take a JSON schema, and open libraries turn a schema or a regular expression into a mask on the model's tokens.)

Chapter 11 told what a closed language is, and what was lost when the field left it open. I take that as read. This chapter says what is new: what *produces* a closed language.

## 2. The first principle

**Declaring a language is as easy as declaring a variable.**

It is the heading of one rule of Haro, ruled on 1 October 2026, and the kernel of the vision says the same: the era makes declaring a language as easy as declaring a variable. The other half of the chapter has its sentence, from the alphabet's totality doctrine, ratified on 27 September: malformedness dies at the sampler, falsehood dies at the court, and non-termination is unexpressible.

And the one I must carry beside every win, from the library's contract for structured output: structure kills malformedness, and not falsehood.

## 3. The Softanza form

Take a declaration of what a restaurant's order must look like. It has three fields: a dish from a closed menu, a quantity between 1 and 12, and an optional note. One text is read by three readers. A person reads a description. A model reads a clause for its prompt. And the court reads the same text and judges an answer. What does it answer to a dish called pizza and forty portions? Run it:

```haro
oO = StzOutputSchemaQ([
	[ :field = "dish",     :type = :oneof,  :choices = [ "margherita", "tiramisu" ] ],
	[ :field = "quantity", :type = :number, :must = [ [ ">=", 1 ], [ "<=", 12 ] ] ],
	[ :field = "note",     :type = :string, :optional = 1 ]
])
? oO.Describe()
#--> schema 'output' -- 3 field(s)
#      dish : oneof (margherita | tiramisu) [required]
#      quantity : number [required], must be at least '1', must be at most '12'
#      note : string [optional]
? oO.PromptClause()
#--> Answer with ONLY this structure, one field per line, nothing before it and nothing after it:
#
#    dish: <one of: margherita | tiramisu>
#    quantity: <number>
#    note: <text>
aV = oO.Verify([ :dish = "pizza", :quantity = 40 ])
? aV[:ok]
#--> 0
? aV[:findings][1][:message]
#--> field 'dish' is a closed enumeration (margherita | tiramisu) and the model answered 'pizza'.
? aV[:findings][2][:message]
#--> field 'quantity' must be at most '12', and the model answered '40'.
```

> run: kept

The court answers in the declaration's words: `field 'dish' is a closed enumeration (margherita | tiramisu) and the model answered 'pizza'.` Now the same declaration produces a grammar. The court and the grammar are born of one text, and they agree on the shape. They part ways on a value. Forty portions has the right shape: which of the two lets it through, and does the library say what the grammar dropped? Run it:

```haro
cG = oO.ToGBNF()
cGood  = "dish: margherita" + nl + "quantity: 2" + nl + "note: no basil" + nl
cBand  = "dish: margherita" + nl + "quantity: 40" + nl + "note: no basil" + nl
cShape = "dish: margherita" + nl + "quantity: many" + nl + "note: no basil" + nl
? StzGrammarAccepts(cG, cGood)
#--> 1
? oO.Accepts(cGood)
#--> 1
? StzGrammarAccepts(cG, cBand)
#--> 1
? oO.Accepts(cBand)
#--> 0
? StzGrammarAccepts(cG, cShape)
#--> 0
? oO.Accepts(cShape)
#--> 0
? len(StzFind("lessequal", oO.UnenforcedByGrammar())) > 0
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

The second pair is the point of the chapter: the grammar answers `1` and the court answers `0`! I do not print the list of what the grammar dropped, because its text carries the former name of the court in one place. I test that it names the limit of the range.

A model is not asked to be careful. The sampler asks the grammar at every candidate token whether it could continue. So a word that is not on the menu is not refused after it is said. Can it begin at all?

```haro
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cG, "dish: t")
#--> 1
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cG, "dish: p")
#--> 0
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cG, "dish: margherita" + nl + "quantity: 2")
#--> 1
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cG, "dish: margherita" + nl + "quantity: <")
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

It cannot: `dish: p` answers `0`. The last line is a failure the measurement found in a small model: the placeholder `<number>` echoed where a number belongs. And a token is not a byte. A piece may be valid as a prefix and invalid as a completion, and the machine is fed the whole piece. What does it answer to `yesterday`, which begins like `yes`?

```haro
cY = 'root ::= "yes" "\n"'
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cY, "y")
#--> 1
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cY, "ye")
#--> 1
? StzGrammarCouldContinue(cY, "yesterday")
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The pieces `y` and `ye` answer `1`, and `yesterday` answers `0`!

What the machine will not hold, it refuses by name. A closed language refuses what it cannot carry, and never approximates. A left-recursive rule is refused by a code, and so is a rule referred to and never defined. A nested structure is refused by the grammar. Does the court still read it?

```haro
? left(StzGrammarRefusal('root ::= tail' + nl + 'tail ::= tail "x" | "x"'), 7)
#--> GBNF-M7
? left(StzGrammarRefusal('root ::= greeting'), 7)
#--> GBNF-M4
? StzGrammarRefusal(cG) = ""
#--> 1
oNest = StzOutputSchemaQ([
	[ :field = "title",  :type = :string ],
	[ :field = "author", :type = :structure, :fields = [ [ :field = "name", :type = :string ] ] ]
])
? oNest.IsExpressibleAsGrammar()
#--> 0
try
	oNest.ToGBNF()
catch
	? len(StzFind("GBNF-F5", cCatchError)) > 0
done
#--> 1
? oNest.ParseOutput('{ "title":"T", "author":{"name":"Ada"} }')[:ok]
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

The text of the nested-structure refusal carries the former name of the court, so I print only the codes. The last line is the point: the court reads what the grammar refuses to write.

The declaration language is itself closed. A typo in the law is refused when the law is declared. The library's contract says why. A key such as `:requird = 0` would leave a field required without a word, and that is the class of typo a court must not tolerate in its own law. What does the court answer to one?

```haro
try
	StzOutputSchemaQ([ [ :field = "x", :type = :string, :requird = 0 ] ])
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> stzOutputSchema: unknown key ':requird' in field #1 (use :field, :name, :type, :required, :optional, :choices, :oneof, :of, :fields, :must, :note, :description).
```

> run: kept

The typo was refused at once, and the message lists the keys that are allowed!

And a host function is not a word of the closed language of conditions. There are two ways to say a condition, the closed language and the door of a function. A square root is a word of the engine's expression language, and a sine is not. What does the textual form answer to the sine, and what does the function form, which runs host code?

```haro
o = new stzList([ 4, 7, 10, 3, 8 ])
? @@( o.FindW('{ sqrt(@item) < 3 }') )
#--> [ 1, 2, 4, 5 ]
? @@( o.FindW('{ sin(@item) < 2 }') )
#--> [ ]
? @@( o.FindWF( func x { return sin(x) < 2 } ) )
#--> [ 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 ]
```

> run: kept

What I saw, and the narration does not say: the refusal is silent and whole. The textual form printed `[ ]` and the function form printed `[ 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 ]`: nothing from the one, everything from the other. A condition with the true left branch and a sine on the right printed `[ ]`. A condition that calls a function of mine never called it. A syntax error also printed `[ ]`. The language is closed, and it refuses without a word. Chapter 11's natural layer says why it refuses, and this one does not.

### The forge, outside the library

A language declared once should give its court, its mouth, its voice and its readers. That is the forge, and it is where the study of this book meets its own limit: the forge is not in the library.

I will say what the record holds, and what I observed on 8 October 2026 on the older runtime, the oracle. The record is a charter and a spike in another repository, in pure core code that runs on the oracle. The forge reads a declaration and writes two files, a court program and a grammar. The spike's rungs, between 1 and 4 October 2026, forged courts and grammars for the machine language of the estate and for a fleet language declared over it. They did the same for the expression language and for four more letters of the alphabet. Each was judged against the pre-existing language's own cases. Those cases were unadapted for the expression language (W) and for Z, re-expressed for F and A, and, for Q, the older language's own answers, captured. In order: 117 of 120 for the machine language, the three left being the fleet file's kinds. Then 32 of 32 for the fleet language, 29 of 29 for W, 16 of 16 for Z, 25 of 25 for Q, 26 of 26 for F and 35 of 35 for A. This is chapter 3's method, declare then govern, applied to the forge itself.

I ran the forge on a small language I wrote for this book, a table's order in the words of the dining room. It is an example, written for the book and run once, outside the library:

```text
DEFINE LANGUAGE order AS ( VERSION "0.2", GRAMMAR "0.2", COURT "none", EXTENSION ".order" )
  RATIONALE "A table's order, in the words of the dining room"
DEFINE MENU dishes AS ( WORDS [margherita, tiramisu, chianti],
  MESSAGE "the kitchen cooks margherita, tiramisu or chianti" ) RATIONALE "The kitchen's closed menu"
DEFINE RANGE portions AS ( MIN 1, MAX 12,
  MESSAGE "{value} portions is not an order: one to twelve" ) RATIONALE "A table orders between one and twelve"
DEFINE DECLARATION line AS ( VERB "ORDER", SEATS [dish: dishes, quantity: portions, note: text?] )
  RATIONALE "One line of an order: what, how many, and a word for the kitchen"
DEFINE REFUSAL unknown_verb AS ( MESSAGE "The dining room speaks only of orders." )
  RATIONALE "The closure speaks when an alien verb arrives"
```

The forge printed that it had forged one kind and one refusal, in a fraction of a second of wall time, and wrote a court of about 2.6 kilobytes. What did that court answer to a dish off the menu, to forty portions and to an alien verb? It refused all three, in the declaration's own words:

```text
DISH pizza          ->  order (line 2): the kitchen cooks margherita, tiramisu or chianti, not 'pizza'
QUANTITY 40         ->  order (line 3): 40 portions is not an order: one to twelve
DROP TABLE orders   ->  order (line 1): Unknown verb 'DROP': The dining room speaks only of orders.
```

The refusals are the declaration's own words, written beside the grammar. Even `DROP TABLE orders` was refused in the dining room's words! This is the whole of the evidence for the `order` language: it ran once, outside the library, and it is not a declaration of the record.

Three more observations, each a limit.

**The meta-court refuses the escape.** The declaration of the language of declarations lists the operators and operands it may use. A declaration that listed `eval` among its operators, in a fixture pinned on 29 August 2026, was refused: `eval` is not in the closed operator menu, an escape hatch is unexpressible, not forbidden. A host call among the operands was refused in the same way.

**The forge's grammar holds shape only.** A menu seat comes out as a general identifier, so the forged grammar lets `pizza` through. The library's own schema compiler, in the blocks above, closes the same menu into an alternation. On the estate's machine language, the forged grammar accepts a dangling reference that the court refuses. The record names a difference between mouth and court, the part that a context-free grammar cannot say: duplicate names, dangling references. I found no record that states the menu gap as a gap.

**The vision has not caught up.** The vision's text, dated between 27 September and 5 October 2026, says the forge has not forged. The principles file, dated 1 October 2026, says that the forge does not exist. The same repository built the spike the same day and forged for several languages. So the true sentence has two sides. The forge has forged courts and grammars for several languages, outside the library, and it has not forged the full list the principle promises. And the vision's text has not been updated. In the book's stage words, the forge is built outside the library and designed for the library, and chapter 11's limit says the same.

### The facets of a declared language

By facet I mean here a surface of a declared language. The charter lists what the forge produces from a declaration, and I give each a stage word:

| facet | what it is | stage |
|---|---|---|
| the text | the declaration, plain and closed, with a rationale required on every declaration | built outside; the library's analogue is the schema declaration |
| the court | the judge generated for the language, speaking in the declaration's own words | built outside; the library's analogue is `Verify` and `Accepts` |
| the mouth | the grammar for a constrained model | built in the library for schemas; built outside for declared languages, shape only |
| the prompt | the instruction a model reads, tuned by measurement | built in the library; not known for declared languages |
| the person's reading | a description for a human | built in the library (`Describe`) |
| the voice | the language narrating a run, a sentence, a refusal; never stored and never parsed back | built outside; the library's analogue is `Understood()` |
| the tongues | a human language as a data file | built outside for two tongues; built in the library for five packs (chapter 11) |
| the projections | the world rendered to an application, a server, a page, a device | designed |
| the faces | a second syntax of Haro | designed; chapter 16 tells it |

The word *facet* has other meanings in this book, and I define mine here: a surface of a declared language. The second sense is a syntax of Haro for a programmer of another language: the table calls it a *face*, and chapter 16 tells it. The third is a tab of the environment's ribbon, one phase of a life, which is chapter 26's.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I decided that Haro is the language of languages.** The charter's header quotes me, from 1 October 2026: Haro is a language of languages, in which the crafters define a language for their domain and program in it, or let their agents. The charter records a reversal: its first decision, as the general-purpose floor under the family of declared languages, was reversed. The rest of that ratification was my "go with your recommendations" on seven decisions, and those are rulings by delegation.

**I decided to declare, and not to write.** The act of the kernel: declare a language. It was extraction and not invention: the first languages were declared after they already ran and had consumers.

**I refused an open escape form.** No clause, operator or operand denotes host code, calls, evaluation or input and output. Unexpressible, not forbidden. Two fixtures pin the refusal, and I re-ran them.
