When you have written that line, you will know why I hold the ungrammatical chain as a view and not as a result, and who the page says must judge it (sections 4 and 7).

The next chapter is about knowledge programming, where the analyst's domain words were sent. Its principle is that a system is a file, and that adding a line to it makes the system know more, with nothing coded or trained. Here a sentence asks, and never declares.


=====

# Knowledge programming

Source: Chapter 14. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: a system as a plain text file of facts, with laws (unique, transitive, symmetric) and the source of each fact; Prove with its steps, a no as certain as a yes; strict mode; the file written, read back and shipped, from a course catalogue to an agent's memory; designed: rules in the file; derivation on any graph; a query that joins; several relations between two things; class inheritance; validation on load; absent: the 2022 founding dialog, which does not run; Explain on a knowledge graph; the rules section of the file, which the reader skips

Run: 13 of 13 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:14 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take the three practices of the field that meet in this idea, the semantic stack, the property-graph database and rules over facts, and set beside them a system that is a plain text file. I run that file: two facts, one law and a proof with its steps, then a clinic in strict mode, where a refused rumour is kept and named. I write the file, read it back and show it at scale, from a course catalogue to an agent's memory. I say what I decided, that a business describes itself in one file and that the file is the program. I say what I refused: a parallel world, a contradiction resolved in silence, retrieval. I print the limit, a file that at this commit has no rules. It stands beside the principle: a system is a file, and adding a line to it makes the system know more, and nothing is coded or trained.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Three practices of the field meet in this idea, and none of them is the same as it.

The first is the semantic stack: RDF, OWL and SPARQL. A fact is a triple of subject, predicate and object, written in a shared vocabulary. An ontology says what classes and properties exist and what follows from them, and a reasoner derives the consequences. These are W3C recommendations, of 2014, 2009 and 2013. What the field asks of the author of a knowledge base is an ontology language of their own, a store and a query language. They usually stand beside the application and not in it.

The second is the property-graph database, with its query language. The record has one comparison that sets such databases against this library. It is a model's text kept in the library, and it credits the field with scale, persistence and transactions.

The third is rules over facts, in Prolog and in Datalog. Facts and rules in one program, a query by resolution. The record names Prolog only, as a language to reach, and not to rebuild.

A fourth practice stands behind the contrast, and the design document names it only to refuse it. It is retrieval, a pile of document fragments searched by similarity. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.)

So in the field the knowledge lives in a store and its logic in a reasoner, both beside the program. Here the knowledge is a plain text file that the program loads.

## 2. The first principle

**A system is a file. Adding a line to it makes the system know more, and nothing is coded or trained.**

Two sentences of the record carry it. The definition, in the design document written from my directive of 13 July 2026: intelligence is the computational ability to derive new knowledge from existing knowledge. And what the 2026 vision keeps from it: adding one fact augments the system's intelligence, with no code change and no training. Chapter 17 opened on the first, and I open here on the second. The design document of 13 July 2026 already says it, in nearly the same words, and the chapter keeps its opening.

Now the form itself, a text and what it does when you run it.

## 3. The Softanza form

Here is a system as a text. Two facts: flour is made from millet, and couscous from flour. Is couscous made from millet? Ask the graph, then add one line to the file, a law saying that *made from* is transitive, and ask again:

```haro
cFacts = 'knowledge "coop"' + nl + nl + "facts" + nl +
         "    flour | made-from | millet" + nl + "    couscous | made-from | flour" + nl
oA = new stzKnowledgeGraph("coop")
oA.ImportKnow(cFacts)
? oA.Prove([ "couscous", "made-from", "millet" ])[:narration]
#--> not derivable: no recorded fact or lawful chain gives 'couscous' made-from 'millet'
oB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("coop")
oB.ImportKnow(cFacts + nl + "ontology" + nl + "    made-from | transitive" + nl)
? oB.Prove([ "couscous", "made-from", "millet" ])[:narration]
#--> proved: couscous made-from flour made-from millet
```

> run: kept

Without the law the graph answers `not derivable`; with it, `proved: couscous made-from flour made-from millet`. The text is the program. The same two facts, one extra line, a different capability. The block does not touch the disk: `ImportKnow` takes a text, and takes a path when the string ends in the file's extension.

Now add a fact to the graph with the law, and nothing else. What do the two graphs answer to the longer question? Run it:

```haro
oB.KnowRelation("porridge", "made-from", "couscous")
? oB.Prove([ "porridge", "made-from", "millet" ])[:narration]
#--> proved: porridge made-from couscous made-from flour made-from millet
? oA.Prove([ "porridge", "made-from", "millet" ])[:narration]
#--> not derivable: no recorded fact or lawful chain gives 'porridge' made-from 'millet'
```

> run: kept

The graph with the law proves the longer chain and writes its steps out: `proved: porridge made-from couscous made-from flour made-from millet`. The graph without the law, which also never received the fact, still answers `not derivable: no recorded fact or lawful chain gives 'porridge' made-from 'millet'`, and I want you to notice that this no has two causes, named in its own line: no recorded fact, and no lawful chain. A refusal is as certain as a proof, about what is recorded and declared: the graph says "not derivable", not "false". The library's comment on `Prove` says it: `a certain no is still certain`.

The same growth reaches the domain model of chapter 19. Nothing is trained. But a model forged from a graph is a snapshot. Does the model learn the new fact when the graph does? Run it:

```haro
oKB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("coop")
oKB.Know("couscous", "dish").KnowRelation("couscous", "contains", "semolina")
oDLM = StzDlmQ(oKB)
? oDLM.Ask("what is millet?")
#--> That is outside the 'coop' domain (I know 1 entities and 1 relations).
oKB.Know("millet", "grain").KnowRelation("couscous", "contains", "millet")
? oDLM.Ask("what is millet?")
#--> That is outside the 'coop' domain (I know 1 entities and 1 relations).
oDLM = StzDlmQ(oKB)
? oDLM.Ask("what is millet?")
#--> Millet is a grain.
? oDLM.Ask("couscous contains what?")
#--> Couscous contains semolina and millet.
```

> run: kept

It does not. The second answer is the old model still refusing, `That is outside the 'coop' domain`, and only the model forged again answers `Millet is a grain.`

**The file the library writes.** A clinic. A relation declared unique, and a graph in strict mode, where every fact needs its provenance. What does it do with a fact that has no source, with one that has, and with a second, contradicting one from a rumour? And what does it then write to the disk? Run it:

```haro
oCl = new stzKnowledgeGraph("clinic")
oCl.ConstrainRelation("treated-by", :Unique)
oCl.EnableStrictMode()
try
	oCl.AddFact("fatou", "treated-by", "dr-diallo")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Strict mode: every fact needs provenance -- use AddFactXT(s, p, o, [ :source = ..., :confidence = ... ]).
oCl.AddFactXT("fatou", "treated-by", "dr-diallo", [ :source = "registry", :confidence = 0.95 ])
? oCl.AddFactXT("fatou", "treated-by", "dr-sani", [ :source = "rumour", :confidence = 0.2 ])
#--> 0
oCl.WriteToKnowFile("clinic")
? read("clinic.zknw")
#--> knowledge "clinic"
#
#    facts
#        fatou | treated-by | dr-diallo
#
#    provenance
#        fatou | treated-by | dr-diallo | source | text | registry
#        fatou | treated-by | dr-diallo | confidence | number | 0.95
#
#    contradictions
#        fatou | treated-by | dr-diallo | dr-sani | rumour
#
#    ontology
#        treated-by | unique
```

> run: kept (touches files)

The naked fact is refused with the library's own sentence, `Strict mode: every fact needs provenance`. The fact with its source and confidence is admitted. The rumour is refused, `0`, and the refusal is kept! That is what the library writes, a header and up to four sections. They come in this order: the facts, the provenance when a fact carries any, the contradictions when one was refused, and the ontology when a law was declared. One line per record, fields separated by a bar. A bar or a line break inside a value is escaped. The refused rumour sits in the file, named, and never resolved. The code comment on the contradiction list says it: `named, NEVER silently resolved`.

Does the file read back into a strict graph, with the law, the source of every fact and the refusal inside it? Load it:

```haro
oBack = new stzKnowledgeGraph("clinic")
oBack.EnableStrictMode()
aR = oBack.ImportKnow("clinic.zknw")
? aR[:merged]
#--> 1
? @@( oBack.MetaOfFact("fatou", "treated-by", "dr-diallo") )
#--> [ [ "source", "registry" ], [ "confidence", 0.95 ] ]
? len(oBack.Contradictions())
#--> 1
? oBack.RelationHasLaw("treated-by", "unique")
#--> 1
remove("clinic.zknw")
? fexists("clinic.zknw")
#--> 0
```

> run: kept (touches files)

It does: one fact merged, the source and the confidence back, `[ [ "source", "registry" ], [ "confidence", 0.95 ] ]`, one contradiction, and the law. A file of this kind was once written without its provenance. The export wrote only the bare triples, so knowledge read back from the disk no longer said where it came from. A strict graph could not load its own export. The fix is the two sections above. The guard that proves it is named in section 5.

**A law that fires when a fact is added.** On the library's default world, the one behind the natural-language sugar, a symmetric law derives the reverse fact as the first one is added, with no call to a derivation step. Nobody wrote that Ibrahim is married to Amina. Does the default world know it?

```haro
StzConstrainRelation("married-to", :Symmetric)
StzKnowRelation("amina", "married-to", "ibrahim")
? DefaultKnowledgeGraph().Query([ "ibrahim", "married-to", "amina" ])
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

It answers `1`. The reverse fact is there, and nobody wrote it! This is the only place where a declared law fires on add that a run in this book shows, and it is the default world. The graph class also has a switch, `EnableAutoDerive`, that runs the derivation rules after every edge added; it is off by default, and I did not run it. I show the same law on an instance graph in section 7, where it does not.

**A system at scale.** The education program of the library is a knowledge file. Its catalogue, `program.zknw`, says what the program is called and which courses and languages it has. What is its name, and which languages does it carry? Ask it:

```haro
oP = StzProgramQ("../../education/program")
? oP.Id()
#--> softanza-education
? @@( oP.Languages() )
#--> [ "en", "fr", "ar", "ha" ]
```

> run: kept

It is called `softanza-education`, and it has four languages. Adding a course is adding a line there, and the guard checks that every declared course is a folder and every folder is declared. In the education module the snapshot holds ninety-nine knowledge files, counted with `find` on 8 October 2026. They are courses, exercises, worlds and skills, a project world for a learner, and the overlays an institution lays over the core. An agent's memory is also a knowledge graph, saved through the same format, with provenance.

That is the form. Now what I decided, and what I refused.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I decided that a business describes itself in one file, and that the file is the program.** The design document records my directive: entities, relations, an ontology of domain-specific semantics, and rules, in a file that is readable, diffable and hand-editable, validated on load. The last two words I have not yet met, and section 7 says where.

**I decided on four-letter extensions.** `.zknw` for knowledge, and the others of the family; the old spellings still read. The snapshot writes `.zknw` and still reads `.stzknow`. The file of rules was not renamed: the graph still writes and reads `.stzrulz`, though the family names four letters for it. I report it as the record has it.

**I refused a parallel world.** The natural layer once held its entities in globals, and the design document called it the wrong home, the wrong shape and a parallel world. The resolution is one default graph standing behind the natural-language sugar. The old globals still exist, and the graph grows beside them: the library's header calls it a dual write. The law behind it is that naked domain globals are forbidden; a global form may exist only as thin sugar over one default instance. So the dialog of the symmetric law above is sugar over a graph of the same class as the first blocks used.

**I refused to resolve a contradiction in silence.** Strict mode, where every fact needs its provenance, is opt-in knowledge hygiene.

**I refused retrieval.** The design document says: no code change, no training, no RAG machinery. It is the same refusal as chapter 17's.

## 5. The court

`knowledge_integration_narrated` proves that the world answers `WhatIs`, that the graph grew alongside it, and that the law lives in the ontology. It proves that `Prove` narrates the law first and then the links of the chain, with certainty 1, and refuses what it cannot prove. It proves that the file round-trips. It proves that a symmetric law derives the reverse edge when the derivation step is applied after a fact is added through the graph. Last, it proves that strict mode admits a provenanced fact, refuses a naked one, and refuses and records a unique contradiction.

`provenance_survives_save_narrated` proves that a fact's source and confidence survive a save, that a strict graph loads its own export, and that a refused contradiction survives. It proves that a bar or a line break in a value is data, and that an old file with no provenance still loads. It proves that an agent's memory records who learned a fact, and when.

`program_narrated` proves that the program counts its courses, and that every course declared is a folder and every folder is declared. It proves that the texts read the same from a checkout with other line endings.

That is what the guards prove. What the field does better comes next.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The record's own graph comparison says where the traditional solutions win: large-scale systems that need distributed storage and processing, and performance-critical applications. Its table puts Softanza at business scale, in the hundreds to thousands of nodes, in a single process. That is the fair summary of the loss: the field leads in scale, persistence, transactions and tooling. (The comparison's row for persistence predates the file; the knowledge graph now writes one.)

The field leads in standards and reach, in general knowledge. A knowledge base in RDF can be shared with any tool that reads the W3C formats, linked to public vocabularies and queried across stores. As far as the record shows, a knowledge file is read by this library and by nothing else. A reasoner for a rich ontology language does what three laws and a class declaration do not. The field leads in the expressiveness of rules: Prolog and Datalog derive new facts from arbitrary rules. Here, in the runs I kept, one law derives a fact, and only on the default world. The field leads in multi-step queries, since SPARQL and Cypher join patterns. And in many relations between two things.

Here is where I think this stands apart, with the measured limits beside it. The knowledge is a text file a person can read, edit, diff and ship with the program. Every answer is provable with its steps, and a no is as certain as a yes. What a fact claims travels with its source and its refusals. And the same format carries a course, a learner's project and an agent's memory. None of that needs a store, a server, a model or a training run.

The losses are printed. The limit of the chapter comes next, with its runs.

## 7. The limit

The limit of this chapter is the one the structure line already carries: *entities, relations, an ontology, rules*. The file at this commit has no rules. Here is the file with a rules section. What does the reader make of it?

```haro
cRules = 'knowledge "pairs"' + nl + nl + "facts" + nl + "    margherita | pairs-with | chianti" + nl + nl +
         "rules" + nl + "    pairs-with | symmetric" + nl
oRul = new stzKnowledgeGraph("pairs")
aM = oRul.ImportKnow(cRules)
? aM[:merged]
#--> 1
? len(oRul.Ontology())
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The file merges, `1`, and the ontology is empty, `0`. The parser recognises the header `rules`, and has a handler for none of the lines under it. They are dropped: the ontology holds `0`, and no refusal comes back. The same law under the header `ontology` gives an ontology of length 1. Rules live elsewhere, as data in a rule attached to a graph, which are chapters 29 and 30, and in a separate file that names rule functions. In this file there are facts and laws. I print "facts and laws", and I send the rules to those two chapters. The single file that holds rules, which the design promises, is not there, and section 7 prints the gap as the chapter's limit.

The law is also not derived on an instance graph. Take the same symmetric law and the same fact as on the default world. Is the reverse there?

```haro
oSy = new stzKnowledgeGraph("pairs")
oSy.ConstrainRelation("pairs-with", :Symmetric)
oSy.KnowRelation("margherita", "pairs-with", "chianti")
? oSy.RelationHasLaw("pairs-with", "symmetric")
#--> 1
? oSy.Query([ "chianti", "pairs-with", "margherita" ])
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The law is recorded, `1`, and the reverse edge is not there, `0`! The class header says it: `only :unique and :transitive are acted on here`; symmetric is only recorded. Four more limits, each with its run.

**One relation between two things.** The graph stores one edge per node pair. Does it admit a second relation between the same two entities, `amina` and `bread`? Run it:

```haro
oShop = new stzKnowledgeGraph("shop")
oShop.KnowRelation("amina", "sells", "bread")
aV = oShop.Admit("amina", "buys", "bread", [ :source = "till" ])
? aV[:admitted]
#--> 0
? aV[:why]
#--> Can't add fact: 'amina' and 'bread' already carry the edge 'sells' (stzGraph stores one edge per node pair).
```

> run: kept

The second relation is refused, `0`, with the library's own sentence, `Can't add fact: 'amina' and 'bread' already carry the edge 'sells' (stzGraph stores one edge per node pair)`. A knowledge model that cannot hold *sells* and *buys* between the same two entities is a real limit. The code says it: `known limitation, revisit with multi-edge support`.

**A two-step question answers only its first step.** Does `QueryPath` follow its second pattern, and can the predicate of a query be a variable? Run it:

```haro
oQ = new stzKnowledgeGraph("coop2")
oQ.KnowRelation("amina", "sells", "couscous").KnowRelation("couscous", "made-from", "flour")
? @@( oQ.QueryPath([ [ "amina", "sells", "?p" ], [ "?p", "made-from", "?m" ] ]) )
#--> [ "couscous" ]
? @@( oQ.Query([ "amina", "?p", "?o" ]) )
#--> [ ]
```

> run: kept

The path answers `[ "couscous" ]`, the first step and nothing more: `QueryPath` ignores the rest of its patterns, so it is no multi-step query yet. The variable predicate answers `[ ]`.

**A line that is not a fact.** Four lines, three of them not facts: two fields, four fields, and no bars. What does the reader refuse?

```haro
cBad = 'knowledge "t"' + nl + nl + "facts" + nl + "    a | likes | b" + nl + "    c | likes" + nl +
       "    d | likes | e | f" + nl + "    g likes h" + nl
oT = new stzKnowledgeGraph("t")
aBad = oT.ImportKnow(cBad)
? aBad[:merged]
#--> 1
? @@(aBad[:refused])
#--> [ ]
```

> run: kept

Nothing. The file merges, `1`, and the refused list is `[ ]`; the reader is silent and the three lines vanish! The design document promises that the file is "validated on load". At this commit the parser keeps only a line with exactly three fields.

**The 2022 founding dialog.** The narration tells knowledge programming through a dialog: an apple as a fruit and as a company, an owner, relations asked as questions. At this commit its first line does not run. The text, which I show and do not run, is `@Q("Apple").IsA(:Fruit)`. On 8 October 2026 it stopped with `Calling Function without definition: @isfruit`; I did not keep that run as a block. The string class reads the predicate as natural language and looks for a function by that name. Two other forms stop elsewhere. The narration's promise lines are stale. The nearest form that runs keeps the point. Does the world know one name with two meanings?

```haro
StzKnow(:Apple, :Company)
StzKnow(:Apple, :Fruit)
? @@( WhatIs(:Apple) )
#--> [ "company", "fruit" ]
```

> run: kept

It knows both: `[ "company", "fruit" ]`.

The study of the record already says the dialog was kept at the margin, and that the centre moved to the file. The chapter keeps the file as its opening, and the 2022 dialog stays at the margin.

What is designed: rules in the file; derivation on any graph; a multi-step query; several relations between two things; class inheritance. `DefineClass` writes a fact, and no inheritance follows. The nearest that ran, in an earlier probe whose run I did not keep, is to declare `is-a` transitive and state the class as a fact of that relation. And the design's knowledge hygiene beyond provenance, bounded queries, scoped graphs and revision with rollback, has no method of that name.

What is absent or broken: `Explain()` on a knowledge graph, which calls `ApplyInference`, a function defined nowhere. And `ValidateOntology()`, which returns 1 whatever the ontology holds, as its own comment says, `known defect`.

One note on the design document's history. Its amendment of 22 August 2026 says that no knowledge file exists anywhere in the tree, and that this is hygiene and not a gap. At this commit that is no longer true: there are ninety-nine, all of them in the education module.

That is the limit, printed. Now the six doors.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** One class and a text file: `Know`, `KnowRelation`, `ConstrainRelation`, `Prove`, `Query`, `WriteToKnowFile`, `ImportKnow`. The file is diffable and ships with the program.

**The analyst.** The offering page names it: what you declare is your organisation's entities, rules and flows, in a plain-text knowledge file. Read "rules" as the limit above. Where to start, in the course: the chapter called "Teach a world".

**The designer.** The record is silent. Neither the offering page nor the study ties the knowledge file to the designer's door, and I do not invent one.

**The decision-maker.** The file is plain text that a manager can read. Every fact can carry a source and a confidence. A refused claim is kept, named and never resolved. An answer comes with its proof, and a no is certain. The design's second law says no capability may require anything paid or remote. I did not run the file offline.

**The educator.** The whole education program is knowledge files. A learner teaches a world, then questions it, and the project's guard checks the world file, the agent file and one printed answer. An institution adapts by overlay, never by fork.

**The agent.** Your memory is a knowledge graph with provenance, and your world is a file you load and query.

## 9. Exercise

Here is a world with two facts, and a question it cannot answer yet. Your promise is the line proving that class 3b is part of primary, with the chain written out. Change the world, and not the question.

```haro exercise
cWorld = 'knowledge "school"' + nl + nl + "facts" + nl +
         "    class-3b | part-of | grade-3" + nl +
         "    grade-3 | part-of | primary" + nl
oW = new stzKnowledgeGraph("school")
oW.ImportKnow(cWorld)
? oW.Prove([ "class-3b", "part-of", "primary" ])[:narration]
# this prints a refusal. Your promise: the line proving that class-3b is part of primary,
# with the chain written out. Change the world, not the question.
```

> run: exercise

Run it and see what prints. If you change the question to ask about grade 3, it will prove something, and it will not be the line you promised. Then ask the reverse question, whether primary is part of class 3b, once your world has its law, and see whether the law adds a fact in the wrong direction. I do not tell you the line to add.

I left this one in your hands. Section 7 runs a file of four lines, three of them not facts, and prints what the reader says about them. Here is that file again with one change: it also asks the graph about three of the lines, and I print none of the answers.

```haro exercise
cBad = 'knowledge "t"' + nl + nl + "facts" + nl + "    a | likes | b" + nl + "    c | likes" + nl +
       "    d | likes | e | f" + nl + "    g likes h" + nl
oT = new stzKnowledgeGraph("t")
aBad = oT.ImportKnow(cBad)
? aBad[:merged]
? @@(aBad[:refused])
? oT.Query([ "a", "likes", "b" ])
? oT.Query([ "d", "likes", "e" ])
? oT.Query([ "g", "likes", "h" ])
```

> run: exercise

Run it. Read `merged`, read `refused`, read the three queries, and find which lines the graph holds and what the reader told you about the others. Set the design document's promise of a file "validated on load", in section 4, beside what you find; section 7 says what the parser keeps. I do not give you the answer, and I did not fix the reader. When you have it, you will know why a file that merges is not a file that was validated.

The next chapter is about pattern languages: a pattern is a rule about the shape of data, written as data, and the same habit serves data of every kind. Here a law about a relation was a line in a file; there the rule is about the shape of the data itself.


=====

# Pattern languages

Source: Chapter 15. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: patterns for text, numbers and tables, each with a guard; the same small habit across the media (a constructor, a pattern in a string, Match, Tokens); designed: one pattern engine shared by every medium; the pattern as the condition side of the cascade; the pattern written from examples; absent: the refusal of a word the pattern language does not know, in all six media; sequences over a timeline in time; a narration and a small function for graphs; the genetic, linguistic and quantic siblings

Run: 13 of 13 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:14 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I start from the regular expression, which is what a pattern is for a working programmer, and carry its habit to data that is not text: lists, numbers, tables, matrices, time and graphs. I run the same small move in each medium, a constructor, a pattern in a string, `Match`, and I show you at once that the grammar is not quite one. I say what the guards prove and where the family is still in development, and I keep its one tension for the limit: a word the language does not know is not yet refused. The principle is this: a pattern is a rule about the shape of data, written as data, and the same habit serves every kind of data.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

For a working programmer, a pattern is a regular expression: a compact notation for the shape of a string, handed to an engine that says whether the text fits, and where. The engines are old and well known. The one beneath this library is PCRE2, and the site measures it against two others.

For data that is not text, the field uses other tools, one per medium and none shared. Structural pattern matching in the languages of the ML family, in Rust, and since Python 3.10 in Python. Schema checks for lists and records. Data-quality libraries for tables. Row pattern recognition in SQL, and stream engines, for sequences over time. And graph query languages, whose core is a path pattern. (General knowledge, well established; the record holds no dated study of any of them, and I invent none.)

The estate's own field study of August 2026 gives the small languages that survived a profile: a closed grammar, one domain question, plain text, a tiny surface, a specification with a conformance suite, decades of stability. A pattern language is exactly that. One question, does this data fit, in a few characters. What the field does not offer is the same notation across the media.

Next, the first principle.

## 2. The first principle

**A pattern is a rule about the shape of data, written as data. The same habit serves every kind of data.**

The sentence the library's articles return to, in each medium, is the one in the article on numbers: instead of instructing the machine how to test a condition, you state what you mean. The study of the record, which is not the field study above, also carries a phrase it calls mine, "for the first time in computer science history", and it is mine. I found it in no text of the library. The study does not let the site claim it. I keep the phrase here as what I said, and I claim no more than the study allows. The claim the study allows is the measured one: one regex-shaped grammar across seven kinds of data in one library, with the same small-function habit. And I will show you at once that the grammar is not quite one.

## 3. The Softanza form

A pattern called by name is a closed catalogue. The library has a data file of named patterns. What does it say when you ask for a name it does not hold? Run it:

```haro
? rx(pat(:isoDate)).Match("2023-12-25")
#--> 1
? rx(pat(:isoDate)).Match("25/12/2023")
#--> 0
? rx(pat(:isoDate)).Explain()
#--> Matches ISO 8601 date format (YYYY-MM-DD)
try
	pat(:banana)
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> The pattern name you provided does not exist in stzRegexData file.
```

> run: kept

It refuses, and says why: `The pattern name you provided does not exist in stzRegexData file.`

A pattern can also answer the half-written value, which is what a form wants as the person types:

```haro
o1 = new stzRegex("^\d{3}-\d{2}-\d{4}$")
? o1.MatchAsYouType("123-45")
#--> 1
? o1.MatchAsYouType("abc")
#--> 0
? o1.IsPartialMatch("123-45")
#--> 1
? o1.IsPartialMatch("123-45-6789")
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

Now the move that gives the chapter its title. Take a list. Its pattern is written in square brackets, and counts, nesting and negation are written, not looped. `@N` stands for a number, `@S` for a string, `+` for one or more, `@N1-3` for one to three, and `@!` for not. Does `[@N1-3, @S]` accept four numbers before the string? The second call answers:

```haro
? Lx("[@N+, @S]").Match([1, 2, 3, "end"])
#--> 1
? Lx("[@N1-3, @S]").Match([1, 2, 3, 4, "end"])
#--> 0
? Lx("[@S, [@N+]]").Match(["tea", [1, 2, 3]])
#--> 1
? Lx("[@S, [@N+]]").Match(["tea", ["x"]])
#--> 0
? Lx("[@!N, @N]").Match(["a", 1])
#--> 1
? Lx("[@!N, @N]").Match([1, 1])
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

No: the second call prints 0, because four numbers are one too many. Every call here uses a fresh object, for a reason that section 7 gives. Chapter 11 showed four calls of the same language, and I do not repeat them.

The numbers have properties, and a property is meaning, not procedure. Which numbers between 1 and 500 are perfect? The last line turns a pattern from a test into a search:

```haro
? Nx("{@Property(Even) & @Property(Prime)}").Match(2)
#--> 1
? Nx("{@Property(Even) & @Property(Prime)}").Match(4)
#--> 0
? Nx("{@Digit(:unique)+}").Match(1234)
#--> 1
? Nx("{@Digit(:unique)+}").Match(1223)
#--> 0
? Nx("{@Relation(Mod:5=0)}").Match(10)
#--> 1
? @@( Nx("{@Property(Perfect)}").MatchingNumbersBetween(1, 500) )
#--> [ 6, 28, 496 ]
```

> run: kept

Three numbers answer, and no loop was written.

Tables: a data-quality rule is a sentence of the language. The person who owns the data can read it. Niamey appears twice, so the cities are not unique, and the negation of that is true:

```haro
oT = new stzTable([
	[ :ID, :NAME, :AGE, :CITY ],
	[ 1, "Ali", 28, "Niamey" ],
	[ 2, "Sara", 32, "Paris" ],
	[ 3, "Omar", 41, "Niamey" ],
	[ 4, "Awa", 25, "Dakar" ]
])
? Tbx("{cols(4) & unique(id) & avgcol(age:>30)}").Match(oT)
#--> 1
? Tbx("{cols(4) & unique(city)}").Match(oT)
#--> 0
? Tbx("{@!unique(city)}").Match(oT)
#--> 1
? Tbx("{sorted(id) & numeric(age) & alphabetic(name)}").Match(oT)
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

A pattern is data, so patterns compose: one is built from patterns.

```haro
oA = new stzTablex("{cols(4)}")
oB = new stzTablex("{rows(4)}")
oAnd = oA.And_(oB)
? oAnd.Pattern()
#--> {cols(4) & rows(4)}
? oAnd.Match(oT)
#--> 1
? oA.Not_().Pattern()
#--> {@!cols(4)}
? oA.Not_().Match(oT)
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

A matrix: a concept, square and symmetric, is one line.

```haro
? Mx("{shape(square) & property(symmetric)}").Match([ [1,2,3], [2,4,5], [3,5,6] ])
#--> 1
? Mx("{shape(square) & property(symmetric)}").Match([ [1,2,3], [4,5,6], [7,8,9] ])
#--> 0
? Mx("{size(2x3)}").Match([ [1,2,3], [4,5,6] ])
#--> 1
? Mx("{property(identity)}").Match([ [1,0], [0,1] ])
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Time, in the part of it that runs. A duration must fit a rule: one to two hours, on a step of a quarter of an hour. Which of three durations fits: ninety minutes, eighty minutes or three hours?

```haro
oTm = new stzTimex("{@Duration(1h..2h:15min)}")
? oTm.Match(new stzDuration("1 hour 30 minutes"))
#--> 1
? oTm.Match(new stzDuration("1 hour 20 minutes"))
#--> 0
? oTm.Match(new stzDuration("3 hours"))
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

Only ninety minutes fits: eighty is inside the range and off the step, and three hours is outside.

A graph: a pattern reads like the path it looks for. The labels match without regard to case. Does direction matter? The second line answers:

```haro
g = new stzGraph("Order")
g.AddNodeXT(:a, "Placed")
g.AddNodeXT(:b, "Cooking")
g.AddNodeXT(:c, "Served")
g.AddEdgeXT(:a, :b, "starts")
g.AddEdgeXT(:b, :c, "finishes")
? (new stzGraphex("{@Node(placed) -> @Edge(starts) -> @Node(cooking)}", g)).Match(g)
#--> 1
? (new stzGraphex("{@Node(served) -> @Node(placed)}", g)).Match(g)
#--> 0
? (new stzGraphex("{@Node(placed) -> @!Node(refunded)}", g)).Match(g)
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Yes: the path from `served` to `placed` finds nothing, and the line prints 0.

Last, the habit. The pattern is data in every language, and a language that exposes its tokens can be read back by a person and by an agent. Five of the six languages do:

```haro
? len( Lx("[@N, @S]").Tokens() )
#--> 2
? Lx("[@N, @S]").Tokens()[2][:type]
#--> string
? Nx("{@Property(Even) & @Property(Prime)}").Tokens()[1][:type]
#--> conjunction
? len( Mx("{shape(square) & property(symmetric)}").Tokens()[1][:conditions] )
#--> 2
```

> run: kept

Graphs have no `Tokens`, no `Pattern` and no working `Explain` at this commit, and no small function either. There is no `Gx`, and there is no `Tx` for time, although the study's table names both. The small functions are `rx`, `Lx`, `Nx`, `Tbx`, `Mx` and `Tmx`.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I decided that a pattern states a meaning and not a procedure.** That decision is in the articles, and the articles are mine. The same ones decided to carry the regex idea to non-text data with one habit: braces, an `@` for a token, `&`, `|`, `@!` for not, `@cs:` for case. Lists keep square brackets. The other five use braces. That is the first honest correction of the phrase "one grammar".

**I refused to reinvent the notation for text.** Simple matches are kept: a classic pattern string, named groups and captures work as written. Clearer names are put around it. The older work on naming is kept as history, for educational value. I hold that, as a view and not a result, to be the right way to retire a mechanism.

**I refused, for now, to build the siblings.** The articles planned classes in which patterns are genetic, linguistic or quantic. The article says that they are in an early prototype and not in the library, and a search of the regex folder for "induce", "from example" and "infer" finds nothing. The study dates the finding of their purpose two years later, in the answer protocol (the way a person may answer by examples): induction from examples, and superposed candidates. That is chapter 18's ground, and chapter 9's refusal.

**I refused the declarative builder as a headline.** The builders are the classes that write a regex from named steps in code, not from a string. Eighteen of the fifty-one files in the builder's test folder are marked retired: the parser of a declarative pattern is pending. The article's lead example, `CanContainAChar(:Between = ...)`, raises. What runs is the conditional builder, and the look-around object. I do not build the chapter on the builder's headline form.

**I have not yet refused a word the language does not know.** That is the tension of this chapter, and I keep it for section 7.

## 5. The court

`00_regex_narrated` proves the anchors and the search, the classes, the exact quantifiers, the groups, the four match types, partial matching as you type, and positions as data. `00_numbrex_narrated` proves prime, perfect, Fibonacci, even and odd, square and composite, each with a number that matches and one that does not. Its header records a regression in which `Match()` once returned TRUE for every input. `tablex_narrated` proves the parser, counting, uniqueness, sorting, types, sums and averages, the three connectives, sequences, and building patterns from patterns. Its header names the fault it killed. The parser sliced with an end position through a function that counts, and about ninety patterns parsed into a token of the right type with no value. `00_matrex_narrated` proves exact size, any size and the square shape. Its header records that `Match()` once returned FALSE for every input. `00_graphex_narrated` proves that a pattern compiles to a graph, present and absent labels, a sequence as a subsequence, the case rules, negation, alternation, numeric properties, and the cache by graph signature.

Note the placement: the guard for tables lives in the folder for regex, and its stem has no `00_` prefix.

There is no narrated guard for lists, time, or the regex builders. The courts of those are classic files without usable promises. The tests of lists sit after a function definition, so the host language reads them as the body of the function and runs none of them. I ran a copy with the function moved to the end, and I note it in my research notes, not in a block. Sixty checks ran, and two of them disagree with the file's own expectation. The classic file of time says that a promise is TRUE and the run prints 0, and the central record filed this on 1 October 2026 as still open.

Next, the field, and where it leads.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

**Strings.** The field leads on safety and speed, and Softanza leads on naming. The site's page for the area says it itself: it trails the modern engines on safety and throughput, with no JIT and no linear-time matcher. The control against catastrophic backtracking is not reachable from Haro, although the code for both is already compiled into the binary. The engine's header confirms that the hardening exists underneath: a match limit, a recursion limit, length caps.

**Lists, numbers, time, graphs.** The field has older, more established single tools. The study says it without ranking them: one tool has list patterns, uniqueness and stepped ranges; three languages have structural matching; graph query languages exist; event processing exists for time. What the field lacks, and what the record claims, is the one habit across the media.

**Tables.** Data-quality libraries lead in reach and ecosystem. Here a rule is a sentence the person who owns the data can read. The loss is that it runs only on `stzTable`.

**Where the family is still in development.** The class headers print their known gaps as warnings: for matrices and graphs. That is rare, and I say it. Graphs: the quantifiers are parsed but not yet used by the match. Matrices: known gaps today, each carried as a warning on its method.

**A date for the family's one recurring bug.** The same root cause hit the family language by language: the parsers sliced with an end position through a function that counts. The numbers and matrices were repaired on 14 June 2026, the tables on 5 October, and the graphs in a fix whose date the file does not give. Time still carries it. This is evidence that the family shares one design and one parser's habits; it is also a likely reason time does not yet match.

Next, the limit.

## 7. The limit

What is built: strings, numbers and tables, each with a guard and a small function. The shared habit.

What is built in part: lists, matrices, time and graphs, in that order.

- **Lists**: types, counts, nesting, negation, alternation and sets run. Stepped ranges raise. In my run (research notes, no block) the uniqueness flag was ignored, so `[@N2-3{1; 2; 3}U]` accepted `[1, 1]`, though the class has code for a uniqueness check. The wildcard of the article, `@A`, is not a token; the real wildcard is `@$`. And a reused object answers from a cache whose key is the list's length and the sum of its characters' codes, which does not depend on order. A fresh object answers 0 for `["hello", 1]`, and the same object after `[1, "hello"]` answers 1.
- **Matrices**: size, shape, element and three properties run. The class header lists the terms that accept every matrix: `row`, `col`, `pattern`, `determinant` and `sum`. A matrix of two by two is accepted by `{row(99)}`.
- **Time**: instants and durations with a step run. In my run (no block) a range without a step raised. And sequences over a timeline do not run at all: here is a timeline with a meeting, a break and a lunch, and the pattern the article promises. Does it match?

```haro
oTl = new stzTimeLine("2025-10-22", "2025-10-22")
oTl.AddInstant("Meeting", "2025-10-22 09:00:00")
oTl.AddSpan("Break", "2025-10-22 10:00:00", "2025-10-22 10:15:00")
oTl.AddInstant("Lunch", "2025-10-22 12:00:00")
o1 = new stzTimex("{@Event(Meeting) -> @Duration* -> @Event(Break)}")
? o1.MatchPartial(oTl)
#--> 0
o2 = new stzTimex("{@Event(Lunch)}")
? o2.MatchPartial(oTl)
#--> 0
? @@( o2.Tokens()[1][:label] )
#--> "Lunch)"
```

> run: kept

The article promises that the first is TRUE. The second shows that even a single event the timeline holds does not match! The third shows why: the token's label is `"Lunch)"`, read with its closing parenthesis. That is the shared bug; repairing it in time is the next step.

- **Graphs**: labels in order along a path, negation, alternation and numeric properties run. The arrow means *later on one path*, and not *next*. The quantifiers (`+`, `*`, `?` or a number after a token) are read and ignored. The graph of section 3 has three nodes; here `@Node10`, a node with the quantifier ten, is asked for between them. Does the pattern match?

```haro
? (new stzGraphex("{@Node(placed) -> @Node(served)}", g)).Match(g)
#--> 1
? (new stzGraphex("{@Node(placed) -> @Node10 -> @Node(served)}", g)).Match(g)
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Cooking lies between the first and the last, so the first answer is the arrow's meaning. The second answers 1 too! The quantifier is ignored. The cache of this class is keyed by the node and edge counts and the node ids. A second graph with the same ids and other labels is answered from the cache, until `ClearCache()`: the answer can be stale.

**The refusal the family has not yet made.** Chapter 11's law is that a language is declared and closed, with refusals. What does the family answer when it is asked a word it does not know? Run it:

```haro
? Nx("{@Banana(3)}").Match(7)
#--> 1
? Nx("{@Banana(3)}").Match(1000003)
#--> 1
? @@( Nx("{@Banana(3)}").Tokens() )
#--> [ ]
? Nx("{@Property(Banana)}").Match(7)
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

A pattern with a word the language does not know matches every number! The tokens are `[ ]`, and nothing is raised. The matrix and table languages return an ERROR token that nothing raises, and the number and time languages return an empty list. The Haro charter session filed this as a finding, which the central record relayed on 28 September 2026: the six pattern classes accept unknown tokens silently. I reproduced it on this commit, and the request is not closed. A closed language that cannot refuse is not yet the law of chapter 11. The chapter prints it as a finding of the court, and not as a debt of mine. I hold that as my view. The run above, on this commit, is what stands behind it.

What is designed: one pattern engine shared by all the media. The engine design says the backtracking engine, the constraint solver and the pattern compiler are the same across all domains, and that only the vocabulary changes. Its status is planned. At this commit the vocabulary changes and so does the code. The engine's own file named *pattern* is another thing: palindromes, repeats and arithmetic and geometric sequences. The pattern as the condition side of the cascade, many faculties firing one after another on a shared state, is chapter 9's third rung, and designed.

What is absent: induction from examples, which is reserved. The genetic, linguistic and quantic siblings, which are vision only. The explanation of an unnamed pattern, since `rx("[a-z]+").Explain()` raises because the analyser class was never built. And any use of the pattern classes by the rest of the library: outside the regex folder, the tests and the docs, no class takes a pattern. `stzList`, `stzNumber`, `stzTable`, `stzMatrix` and `stzGraph` have no `Matches(pattern)`. In the docs, the education course uses them, in a chapter and an exercise whose answer is checked by running the learner's program, and the gallery has a few scripts that use them.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** One habit across six kinds of data, graphs apart: a small constructor, a pattern in a string, `Match`. The pattern is data you can read and compose. Use the ones with a guard: text, numbers, tables, matrices and graphs, and build nothing on the cache of a reused list pattern.

**The analyst.** A data-quality rule is a sentence of the table language. The person who owns the data can read it.

**The designer.** A pattern states a shape the interface can show before the data arrives, and a half-typed value is answered on the way.

**The decision-maker.** A rule is a line and not a procedure. It can be reviewed, and its verdict is 1 or 0. Say beside it that a pattern with an unknown word is not refused, and that a graph arrow means *later*.

**The educator.** The course teaches patterns in two chapters, in English, French, Arabic and Hausa. The ladder is a curriculum: text, list, number.

**The agent.** A pattern is a small closed notation that an agent can be held to and can read back. Be told that none of the six media can yet refuse a word it does not know, and that writing a pattern from examples is reserved.

## 9. Exercise

Three small tables are given. Write one pattern that accepts only the first. Before you run anything, write under each of the three printed lines what you expect and why.

```haro exercise
t1 = new stzTable([ [ :ID, :EMAIL ], [ 1, "ali@x.org" ], [ 2, "sara@x.org" ] ])
t2 = new stzTable([ [ :ID, :EMAIL ], [ 1, "ali@x.org" ], [ 1, "sara@x.org" ] ])
t3 = new stzTable([ [ :ID, :EMAIL ], [ 1, "ali@x.org" ], [ 2, "" ] ])
# write ONE pattern, then print whether it matches t1, t2, t3, on three lines
cPattern = "{ ... }"
? Tbx(cPattern).Match(t1)
? Tbx(cPattern).Match(t2)
? Tbx(cPattern).Match(t3)
#--> 1
#--> 0
#--> 0
```

> run: exercise

Run it. One clause rejects the second table and another rejects the third; which is which? And what would the pattern say if the email were a single space? I do not answer first. If a promise breaks, do not change it: explain the output.

I left this one in your hands, on purpose, and I name it a defect. Take the matrix block of section 3 and change one thing in its last line: put `{row(99)}` where `{property(identity)}` stands, and keep the matrix.

```haro exercise
? Mx("{row(99)}").Match([ [1,0], [0,1] ])
```

> run: exercise

Say what you expect before you run it, then run it. Do not change the pattern to make the output agree with you: section 7 says where the library prints this defect, and why. When you have found it, you will know which terms of the matrix language accept every matrix.

The next chapter is about seven languages and one door. A door is a runtime and never a syntax, and the door is one: it is the answer to "what about language X".


=====

# Seven languages, one door

Source: Chapter 16. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: a closed registry of six foreign runtimes and a process-based call that brings Python's and NodeJS's results home as lists; the trust postures; the twin's rehearsal of a spawn; a reader of Python and of JavaScript into one code graph; designed: the door governed, with a posture asked at the call; the engine's bridge and its three tiers; the domain library on top of the door; Haro wearing a Python syntax and a JavaScript one; absent: a door whose way back is closed; a door for R that runs; a passing run of a foreign runtime in the library's own court; the model as a door

Run: 10 of 10 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:14 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take the field's two lessons about reaching another language, an escape that is open and a border with no way out, and I put the door where they meet. I ask the door which languages it knows, run Python and NodeJS through it, read Python and JavaScript into one code graph, and show the governance refusing an executor with no posture. Then I say what I decided: one door, and no further host embeddings. And I say where the door stands at this commit: its governance is designed, a way back that is closed is absent, and the R door, in development, raises today. The principle is the one chapter 11 promised: a facet is a syntax of one grammar and never a runtime, a door is a runtime and never a syntax, and the door is one.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

For a working programmer, reaching another language from the one you write means one of two things. The first is a foreign function interface, a call into a shared library. The second is a child process, a run of an interpreter whose output you parse. Notebooks and polyglot virtual machines make the same move with more comfort. (General knowledge. The record holds no dated study of these tools, and says so itself.)

The estate's own field study of August 2026 has two sentences on this, and they pull in opposite directions. On the way out, the host language is the escape hatch, and the escape hatch is always taken. It gives two internal languages that died of openness. On the way in, the differentiator is escape; a language you make must be consumable from any stack. It gives one language that stalled at the border of its own world.

So the lesson of the field is double. An escape that is open destroys a closed language. And a language with no way out dies at its own border. A door is where those two lessons meet.

There is a second habit, which looks like the first and is not. Tools that let you write in the syntax you know over another engine, a transpiler, a second front end for one compiler, I will call by the word of this book, *facets*. The estate's design memo for them is blunt about their cost. The promise of a second face is readability for a human who knows the other language, and not behavioural transfer.

## 2. The first principle

**A facet is a syntax of one grammar, and never a runtime. A door is a runtime, and never a syntax. The door is one, and it is the answer to "what about language X".**

The vision's own sentence, ratified in session on 28 August 2026, says it of the door: foreign code in the selected languages, governed, one door, with no further host embeddings. The study of the record reads the pair in a line I keep: the facet brings the programmer, and the door brings the ecosystem. Chapter 11 promised that this chapter would tell the difference; chapter 1 promised it the seven layers. Here is the difference.

## 3. The Softanza form

The door's list is closed. The foreign languages are a registry, and the host is not on it. Which languages does the door know? Ask it about six, then about the host, then about one that was never on the list:

```haro
oDoor = new stzExterCode(:python)
aNames = [ "c", "nodejs", "julia", "r", "prolog", "python", "ring", "cobol" ]
for cLang in aNames
	? cLang + ": " + oDoor.IsLanguageSupported(cLang)
next
#--> c: 1
#--> nodejs: 1
#--> julia: 1
#--> r: 1
#--> prolog: 1
#--> python: 1
#--> ring: 0
#--> cobol: 0
```

> run: kept

"What about language X" has one answer, and for a language not on the list it is a refusal by name:

```haro
try
	oNo = new stzExterCode(:cobol)
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Language 'cobol' is not supported
```

> run: kept

The door refused it by name: `Language 'cobol' is not supported`!

Now the door runs a real runtime. A short note on the machine first. The door writes a script, starts the runtime by it, and reads a result file back. This book was built on a machine where Python lives at its own path, so the path is given as a short path without spaces. Node and R are at the library's default places. The blocks that start a runtime are marked, in the reader, as touching the system, and they show the output that was recorded on the date of the build.

What comes home from a Python call? The result arrives as the host's own structure, a list, and the call leaves a trace:

```haro
cPython = "C:/Users/ASUSV3~1/AppData/Local/Programs/Python/Python313/python.exe"
oPy = new stzExterCode(:python)
oPy.SetRuntimePath(cPython)
oPy.SetCode("
res = { 'numbers': [1, 2, 3, 4, 5], 'mean': sum([1, 2, 3, 4, 5]) / 5 }
")
oPy.Execute()
? @@( oPy.Result() )
#--> [ [ "numbers", [ 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 ] ], [ "mean", 3 ] ]
? oPy.CallTrace()[1][:language]
#--> python
? oPy.CallTrace()[1][:mode]
#--> interpreted
? isNumber(oPy.LastCallDuration())
#--> 1
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The Python float `3.0` came back as `3`, in `[ "mean", 3 ]`. The trace also holds a duration. The block prints only `1`, the answer to `isNumber`, because a duration is a clock, and a clock cannot be promised.

That is the rationale of the whole plane, in one picture. Each layer of a solution is in the language best at it, and the host passes data in as text and reads data back. The library's own article lists the layers: system performance in C, event-driven systems in NodeJS, scientific computing in Julia, statistics in R, logical reasoning in Prolog, and machine learning in Python. It adds AI reasoning in a language model, and solution integration in the host. Here are two of them in a pipeline, Python computing the averages of two students and NodeJS deciding. The thresholds are mine; the data is the article's:

```haro
aStudents = [ [ "Alice", [ 85, 90, 78 ] ], [ "Bob", [ 72, 65, 70 ] ] ]
oPy2 = new stzExterCode(:python)
oPy2.SetRuntimePath(cPython)
oPy2.SetCode("
students = " + @@(aStudents) + "
res = [ [ s[0], round(sum(s[1]) / len(s[1]), 2) ] for s in students ]
")
oPy2.Execute()
aAverages = oPy2.Result()
? @@(aAverages)
#--> [ [ "Alice", 84.33 ], [ "Bob", 69 ] ]
oNode = new stzExterCode(:nodejs)
oNode.SetCode("
const avgs = " + @@(aAverages) + ";
const res = avgs.map(a => [ a[0], a[1] >= 80 ? 'Excellent' : 'Needs work' ]);
")
oNode.Execute()
? @@( oNode.Result() )
#--> [ [ "Alice", "Excellent" ], [ "Bob", "Needs work" ] ]
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The data goes in by composing the code string with the host's own list printer, since the article's marshalling functions are absent (section 7).

**Which seven?** The title commits to a number, and I ruled on 12 October 2026 which one: the six foreign runtimes of the registry above and the host, as the article on the plane and the article on performance count them. The language model is not one of them, because it is not a door of this plane at all. Chapters 17 and 20 make the model a proposer that cannot commit, while the narrations run it as a first-class runtime through a function that is absent. Section 7 prints that as a limit. The record counts it differently elsewhere: seven domain modules with one for a language model, seven domains with the host as an eighth, the registry's six without the host, the vision's "7 selected languages" that it never lists, and the engine design's five with COBOL. The registry above is the one that runs.

**A facet is not a door.** Reading a foreign language is also not running it. The two languages that the author names as Haro's planned facets are also the two that the library can already read into one code graph. It does not run them and does not write them. One set of questions answers over both:

```haro
cPy = "class Animal:" + nl +
      "    def speak(self):" + nl +
      "        return '...'" + nl +
      "" + nl +
      "class Dog(Animal):" + nl +
      "    def speak(self):" + nl +
      "        return 'woof'"
cJs = "class Animal { speak() { return '...' } }" + nl +
      "class Dog extends Animal { speak() { return 'woof' } }"
oPG = StzPyCodeGraphFromSourceTS(cPy)
oJG = StzJsCodeGraphFromSourceTS(cJs)
? oPG.Language()
#--> python
? @@( oPG.ParentsOf("Dog") )
#--> [ "Animal" ]
? @@( oPG.OwnersOf("speak") )
#--> [ "Animal", "Dog" ]
? oJG.Language()
#--> javascript
? @@( oJG.ParentsOf("Dog") )
#--> [ "Animal" ]
? @@( oJG.OwnersOf("speak") )
#--> [ "Animal", "Dog" ]
```

> run: kept

This is a parser into an index of classes, methods and inheritance, and not a program graph that can print a program. It is a coincidence of the order of work, and not yet a facet. The record has one statement of mine on facets. I made it in session on 8 October 2026, and it says that a link should be made with the future vision of Haro and its facet languages. In that vision the former host language is already there, and Python and JavaScript are planned. The charter of Haro on this machine speaks of one grammar and one text. Its design memo for a second face, which proposes and rules nothing, calls them faces and recommends printers read by humans before any parser. Its own table lists the collisions: a Python reader expects zero-based indexing and Haro's is one-based. The chapter says "planned" only with that statement named as its source, and the memo's word for the same thing is face.

**A door is governed.** The governance has the word for foreign code. `external` is the posture the library defines as out-of-process. What does the governance answer before that posture is declared, and after? Ask it:

```haro
oGov = new stzGovernance("shop")
? oGov.MayExecute("python")
#--> 0
? oGov.Why()
#--> refused: 'python' has NO declared trust posture
oGov.DeclarePosture("python", :External)
? oGov.MayExecute("python")
#--> 1
? oGov.Why()
#--> allowed: posture 'external' declared
```

> run: kept

The refusal says why: `refused: 'python' has NO declared trust posture`. An executor with no declared trust posture never proceeds.

And the twin can rehearse a spawn before it runs, flagged as a risk. Chapter 24 owns the twin, and one block here is enough to say what the door does not use:

```haro
oTwin = new stzVirtualEnvironment()
oTwin.Spawn("python analyse.py")
? oTwin.NumberOfPendingSpawns()
#--> 1
oPlan = oTwin.GenerateUpdatePlan()
? oPlan.Narration()
#--> Update plan (1 of 1 operations to commit):
#-->   * 1. spawn process: python analyse.py
? @@( oPlan.Risks() )
#--> [ [ 1, "RUNS an external command (side effects): spawn process: python analyse.py" ] ]
```

> run: kept

Nothing is run there, and the plan flags the spawn: `RUNS an external command (side effects): spawn process: python analyse.py`. The script named does not exist.

Next, what I decided about the door, and what I refused.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I decided on one door.** Its vision name is stzx, the door of the foreign languages, the x standing for external. The retired names are two older ones. The letter X was refused for algorithmics, because it would have sat beside the door, the clash that the vision's own file on the platform's letters named as its sharpest. The door took a letter and kept it.

**I refused further host embeddings.** The door is the answer to "what about language X". Chapter 1 promised this chapter on those words: seven languages were chosen for seven layers of a solution.

**I refused to surrender the domains to generated code.** The article on the domain library puts it as a stance. Rather than forcing programmers to master each domain, or to surrender to AI-generated code, provide carefully crafted abstractions. The site says the article has been in the repository since 23 April 2025 (read 9 October 2026). Its code is absent.

**I refused a facet as a runtime.** The study's plan says it in a line: a facet is a syntax of one grammar and never a runtime. So the door stays the only way to foreign code. It is a plan, proposed. It is not ruled.

**I refused an approximating printer.** The design memo's rule for faces: a printer refuses before it approximates. A face spells the graph and never gives it the meaning of Python or JavaScript.

Now the court, and what it proves of the door.

## 5. The court

`51_extercode_narrated` proves that the registry knows Python, R, Julia, Prolog, C and NodeJS, and rejects an unknown name. It proves that the lookup ignores case (tested for Python); that the code set is the code read back; and that the factory constructs and forwards. The smoke test it narrates says in its header: no `Execute` (the L99 guardrail). `governance_narrated`, scene six, proves that an execution without a posture refuses and that a declared posture proceeds and names itself; scene seven, that postures travel through save and load. `virtual_environment_twin_narrated` proves that a queued spawn does not run until commit, by a marker file; that a scope refuses; and that the plan narrates and flags the spawn. `pycodegraph_narrated` and `jscodegraph_narrated` prove classes, methods, inheritance chains, owners, impact and cascade over Python and over JavaScript sources, offline and without the runtime. Both skip their parser scenarios when the engine's tree-sitter is absent, and the first also skips its Python-AST ones when Python is not on the path.

I want to be exact about the court of the door. A guard that runs a foreign runtime and passes is absent from the snapshot; what the door's court holds today is a registry and a construction test. The folder's own run record, under an older runtime version, lists thirty-seven failures. Several promises cannot match by construction, because the file names carry a random suffix and the calls a duration.

The field comes next, and where it leads.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in speed, and speed is what the door exists for. The article on performance measures seven runtimes on three tasks, and the host comes last in two of them. I carry its caveats from chapter 22: it has no versions and no dates inside its text, and it says that its times are relative to a specific computer environment. Sorting a million elements takes the host about thirty seconds and Python about four, in the article's table. I print no more of it.

The field leads in runtimes it can actually run. Of six doors, three ran on this machine: Node, Python, and R, which raised. The other three had no runtime to start. The study says its description of the field's polyglot tools was written from the genre and not re-read, and I claim no measured comparison.

The field leads in the ecosystem and in being used. The compare page speaks of Python only as a rival; to cover its thirty rows in Python you assemble numpy, pandas and others. The door is how a Softanza solution takes them without leaving. I hold this as a view, not a result: section 3 shows the door carrying two runtimes in one pipeline, and nothing on this page runs numpy or pandas.

The court's reach stops today at the door: the foreign process is outside it, and chapter 21's threat model puts code already running in the process out of scope.

And the cost of a facet, in the memo's own table: a Python reader expects zero-based indexing, and Haro's is one-based. The literal `\n` is a backslash and an `n`; two letters of different case are one variable. "Any model writes Haro in the shape it knows" is true only of the spellings that are the minority.

## 7. The limit

The word *governed* is a design. Does the door ask the governance before it runs? This is the block that shows it:

```haro
oGov2 = new stzGovernance("shop")
? oGov2.MayExecute("python")
#--> 0
oPy3 = new stzExterCode(:python)
oPy3.SetRuntimePath(cPython)
oPy3.SetCode("res = 6 * 7")
oPy3.Execute()
? oPy3.Result()
#--> 42
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The governance said no, and the door ran anyway, and returned `42`! Nothing in the door's folder mentions governance or posture. The door calls the system directly, and not the twin.

The way back is not yet closed. The result file is read by the host's own `eval`, and a string that comes out of the foreign runtime is wrapped in single quotes without escaping. Can a foreign string that carries a quote and a call reach host code? The function used here is `upper`, harmless on purpose:

```haro
oPy4 = new stzExterCode(:python)
oPy4.SetRuntimePath(cPython)
oPy4.SetCode(`
res = "x' + upper('y') + 'z"
`)
oPy4.Execute()
? oPy4.Result()
#--> xYz
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The result file held `'x' + upper('y') + 'z'`, and `Result()` evaluated it. The data became a call, and the block printed `xYz`! A plain apostrophe in data, `it's fine`, writes a string the host cannot read, and the method prints the error and returns the raw text. It is the same class of open limit as chapter 11's list in a natural sentence. I did not test the other five languages' marshalling.

The R door is in development: today it raises on every call, because its generated script holds an identifier that begins with an underscore, which R rejects. I confirmed it with R on a one-line file. And the trace records an exit code of 0 for a call that failed. The code passes a literal 0 to the recorder, and failure is detected only by the absence of the result file:

```haro
oStat = new stzExterCode(:R)
oStat.SetCode("res <- c(1, 2, 3)")
bRaised = 0
try
	oStat.Execute()
catch
	bRaised = 1
done
? bRaised
#--> 1
? oStat.CallTrace()[1][:exitcode]
#--> 0
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The call raised, and the trace still holds `0`! R's message was in French on this machine, so I do not promise it. This is the limit of the door at this commit, in the voice of chapter 11's open list. The runs above are its evidence. Governing the door is designed and not yet built, so the vision's word "governed" stays a design. The way back is not yet closed, since data returns through a file that the host evaluates. And the R door, in development, raises on every call today.

What else is designed: the engine's own polyglot bridge, which the engine design marks as planned. A module of the same name in the macroplan, a string registry, was once marked done with no source. Then the three tiers for Python, of which only the external subprocess exists. Then the domain library on top of the door, none of whose classes I found in code. And the facets, a memo that proposes and rules nothing.

What is absent: the article's functions that bring data in, `@@R`, `@@C`, `XC()` and `XAI()`, which are defined in no file. Factories exist for Python, R, Julia and Prolog. The C and NodeJS doors are reached only by the class. The model is not a door of this plane at all; the article's function that runs it inside the host is the opposite of chapter 17's law, and the book does not teach it.

And a door shape I did not run: Julia, C and Prolog, for which no runtime is installed here. And a third relation, named in the study beside the door: the shim that pastes a solution found on the internet in another language and changes it a little. `range0`, Python's range as a host function, has a guard. The folder's own record lists for it five failures on ranges that should be empty and one on the slice form `':5:-1'`. I ran none of it.

Six readings of the door follow, one for each person who meets it.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You write Haro and, for the layer another language does best, you hand it a string of code and a list of data and take a list back. In the plan, a Python or JavaScript programmer will also write Haro in a syntax they know. Two different things: a syntax brings you, and a runtime brings your libraries.

**The analyst.** The statistical layer is R's, the machine-learning layer Python's, the logic layer Prolog's. You stay in one file and one list of results. The R door is in development and raises today, and the record says so.

**The designer.** There is nothing to design at the door itself. The record's lesson is its refusal: a language not on the list is refused by name, and "what about my language" has one answer.

**The decision-maker.** The door is the one place where the closure ends, by decision. At this commit its governance is designed and not yet built, and its way back, not yet closed, reaches host code. The vision's word "governed" is a design. Every call's duration is recorded in the trace today.

**The educator.** A learner sees which layer belongs to which language and runs a two-door pipeline. The lesson is the difference between reading a language, running it, and writing in its syntax.

**The agent.** You never reach the door by yourself. A function in an agent's file must carry a posture, and `external` fits a foreign runtime; and a model has no capability to produce effects (chapter 20).

I left this one in your hands. Section 7 says that a plain apostrophe in data makes the way back write a string the host cannot read, and I printed no output for it. Here is the block of section 7 with one change, `it's fine` as the foreign string:

Chapter 17 opens the part on intelligence with its law: programmatic by default, the model last, admission over generation. A model there is one faculty among others, the last one called, and what it says is a proposal that the court admits or refuses.


=====

# Law: programmatic by default, the model last, admission over generation

Source: Chapter 17. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the domain language model's first rung and its second, a small bigram; the conversation that asks; derivation with its proof; the programmatic agent; the gate that refuses an ungoverned model; designed: the thinking machine's cascade across faculties; the decision stack, its pieces beside the agent and not yet inside it; the typed unit of prediction, a design of the estate's with five validation stages and no chain of them in the library; the negotiation between an agent and its reviewer; absent: four of the library's five own agents, induction from examples among them

Run: 8 of 8 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:14 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I state the law of Part IV and run it. I begin with my definition of intelligence and a domain language model with no neurons at all, which answers, says why, and refuses what it was never told. Then I show a conversation that asks you, a fact nobody recorded proved from one declared law, a model held to proposing, and an agent whose mind is code, refused at the gate. I say what I refused, which guards prove each block, and where the field leads. Then comes the limit, printed as the stage it is: a question asked in other words, answered where a refusal is the next step. The law itself is one sentence: the mind is programmatic by default, a model is one faculty among others, the last one called, and what it says is a proposal that the court admits or refuses.

## What the AI age changes here

In the two movements before this one, the AI age changed one thing each. In programming, it changed who writes the code. In languages, it changed who speaks the sentence. Here it changes everything, because this movement is about intelligence itself, and the field now mostly uses the word to mean one thing: a large language model inside your software.

So this chapter does what the other two law chapters do, and states the law of its part before the evidence. It also says how the part depends on the others, because the order of this book runs from the familiar to the new. Intelligence, in Softanza, is governed by what came before it. The court that judges a model's sentence is the court of Part II, which judges code whoever wrote it. The sentence it judges is a sentence of a closed language, which is Part III. A model can only be put last because there is something solid to put first.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Ask the field what makes an application intelligent and the answer is a model. An agent, in the field's sense, is a model in a loop with tools, a memory and a goal. The shopping list for building one in 2026 is long. It takes a project in Python, a model provider, a framework that wires the loop as a graph, a store of vectors for memory, tools with typed schemas, a tracing service, and a chapter of safety advice. Memory is often retrieval: a collection of documents, cut into pieces, searched by similarity and pasted into the prompt.

Safety, in that picture, is a property of the agent. It is alignment, guardrails, a prompt that asks for confirmation, a box marked "human in the loop". The field has also learned to hold a model's output to a shape, with grammars that constrain what it may emit. All of this is serious work, and some of it is good. It shares one assumption: the model is the mind, and everything else is around it.

## 2. The first principle

**The mind is programmatic by default; a model is one faculty among others, the last one called, and what it says is a proposal that the court admits or refuses.**

## 3. The Softanza form

Begin with my definition, because everything here follows from it. Intelligence is the computational ability to derive new knowledge from existing knowledge. Nothing in that sentence needs a model.

Here is a domain language model with no neurons at all, forged from a small knowledge graph of a restaurant. What does it answer from what was recorded, and what does it say about the 1998 world cup? Run it:

```haro
oKB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("restaurant")
oKB.Know("margherita", "dish").
    KnowRelation("margherita", "contains", "tomato-sauce").
    KnowRelation("margherita", "contains", "mozzarella")
oDLM = StzDlmQ(oKB)
? oDLM.Ask("margherita contains what?")
#--> Margherita contains tomato-sauce and mozzarella.
? oDLM.Why()
#--> recorded fact(s): 'margherita' contains tomato-sauce, mozzarella
? oDLM.AskXT("margherita contains what?")[:certainty]
#--> 1
? oDLM.Ask("who won the 1998 world cup?")
#--> That is outside the 'restaurant' domain (I know 1 entities and 1 relations).
```

> run: kept

It answers, says why (`recorded fact(s): 'margherita' contains tomato-sauce, mozzarella`), and states its certainty (`1`). And when it is asked something it does not know, it does not guess. It refuses, and it tells you how small its world is: `I know 1 entities and 1 relations`. A refusal that names its boundary is worth more than a fluent answer that has none. This is my view.

Now the second piece, the one that turns the usual dialogue around. In the field, you prompt the machine and keep what comes out. Here the system asks you. What does it ask, and what happens if you try to conclude before you have answered?

```haro
oMenu = new stzKnowledgeGraph("menu")
oMenu.Know("margherita", "dish").Know("tiramisu", "dish")
oMenu.AddConversationQ("setup").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"))
? oMenu.AskIn("setup")
#--> What does 'margherita' have for 'contains'?  (why: every dish needs 'contains')
try
	oMenu.ConcludeIn("setup", "menu")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Can't conclude: 2 gap(s) remain -- the wise-coding loop is not done. Ask the next question.
```

> run: kept

A goal was declared: every dish must say what it contains. The gap between the goal and what is known generated the question, and the question carries its reason. The world cannot be closed while it is incomplete: `Can't conclude: 2 gap(s) remain`. Chapter 18 shows the five ways you may answer: an option from a list, a data structure, a formula, a sentence, or examples. Three are wired; the formula and the examples are absent. A model, where there is one, comes after all of them, not first.

The third piece is derivation, my definition at work. One law is declared, and a fact nobody recorded is proved:

```haro
oKG = new stzKnowledgeGraph("kitchen")
oKG.ConstrainRelation("contains", :Transitive)
oKG.KnowRelation("margherita", "contains", "tomato-sauce").
    KnowRelation("tomato-sauce", "contains", "tomato")
? oKG.Prove([ "margherita", "contains", "tomato" ])[:narration]
#--> proved: margherita contains tomato-sauce contains tomato
? oKG.Prove([ "margherita", "contains", "luigi" ])[:narration]
#--> not derivable: no recorded fact or lawful chain gives 'margherita' contains 'luigi'
```

> run: kept

New knowledge from existing knowledge, with its chain written out! And a "no" that is as certain as the "yes", because it is the result of a search, not of a mood. The page printed it as `not derivable: no recorded fact or lawful chain gives 'margherita' contains 'luigi'`. It is certain about what is recorded and declared, not about the world: it says `not derivable`, not "false". No retrieval, no training. Adding a fact makes the system know more; adding a law makes it reason further.

Then the model. It is welcome, and it is held. Wire a model's output straight to an effect, here sending a message. Is the composition sound?

```haro
oG = new stzAgentGraph("mailer")
oG.AddLLMActor("writer")
oG.AddEffect("send")
oG.Proposes("writer", "send")
? oG.IsSound()
#--> 0
? len(oG.Violations())
#--> 3
```

> run: kept

It is not: `IsSound()` printed `0`, and it is refused before anything runs! Three laws fire, and `len(oG.Violations())` printed `3`: every effect must pass a gate, open text must have somewhere safe to land, and every effect must leave a witness. What does a model actor hold that can act? Ask one:

```haro
oF = new stzLLMFunction("sum")
oF.SetPrompt("summarize: {input}")
oF.Budget(2)
oF.SeedAnswer("hello world", "a greeting")
oLLM = new stzLLMAgent("summarizer")
oLLM.SetSkillFrom(oF)
? oLLM.Propose("hello world")
#--> a greeting
? oLLM.HoldsEffectful()
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

Nothing. A model actor holds no effectful capability at all; it proposes, and that is the whole of its power. `HoldsEffectful()` printed `0`. No model is loaded in this block: the answer is seeded, the way a test fixes a golden answer, so that the page runs the same on every night. What the block proves does not depend on the model: whatever it says, it holds nothing that can act.
