And the agent itself has a mind made of code. A skill is a condition and an action, and it fires on what the agent perceives. Governance gates the act before it happens. When the stock is low and the skill wants to order, does the act go through?

```haro
oRog = new stzPIAgent("rogue-bot")
oRog.MemoryQ().Learn("stock", "level", "low")
oRog.GovernanceQ().DeclareRisk("order-stock", 2)
oSk = new stzAgentSkill("restock")
oSk.SetWhen(func oM { return oM.Fact("stock", "level", "low") })
oSk.SetDoes(func oM { oM.Learn("stock", "level", "ordered")  return 1 })
oRog.AddGovernedSkill(oSk, "order-stock")
? oRog.Pursue()
#--> 0
? oRog.DecisionLog()[1][:why]
#--> refused: 'rogue-bot' lacks PERMISSION (can) for 'order-stock'
```

> run: kept

The stock is low, the skill wants to order, and the agent was never given the permission. `Pursue()` printed `0`: governance refused it! Nothing happens, and the log says why, by name: `refused: 'rogue-bot' lacks PERMISSION (can) for 'order-stock'`. The world is as it was.

Put the pieces in a line and you have the stack the vision of the platform names (its North Star, ratified on 27 September 2026): perceive, decide, react, learn. None of it needs a language model. It is a design, not an assembled agent. In it, perception classifies the world into the knowledge graph. A planner decides. Rules react. The agent scores its plans and keeps the best. The pieces exist in the library beside the agent, not yet inside it: the agent above calls none of them. A model may sit beside each step as one more faculty, and none of them depends on it.

Chapter 18 tells the five registers of an answer and the conversation that asks. Chapter 19 tells the domain language model, from its first rung, with no neurons, to its second, trained on the first one's own facts and nothing else. Chapter 20 tells the agent that cannot hurt you: it proposes, a workbench rehearses, the court judges, and only a governed actor commits. Chapter 21 tells the court itself. Chapter 22 compares all of it with the field, and prints where the field leads.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused the model as the mind.** When the models arrived, the move was to put one at the centre and fence it. I wrote the opposite into the architecture: the mind is programmatic by default, and a model is a pluggable faculty, one among several, never the definition of the mind. The industry's loop is a special case of this design, with one faculty and no governance. The record holds no earlier sentence of mine than the architecture of July 2026, and the chapter opens as it does.

**I refused anything paid or remote as a requirement.** Every capability has a floor that needs no setup, no subscription and no network. A model can be added on top. It is never the price of admission. "Last" means after the programmatic path, and under the court and a budget, whether the model is local or remote. The record supports both readings, and the chapter does not choose between them.

**I refused guessing.** Every ambiguity refuses rather than guesses. The field's habit of prompting and keeping what comes out has a name now; here, guessing is replaced by asking, and vibes are replaced by governance.

**I refused retrieval as memory.** Memory is a governed graph, not a pile of similar fragments. Derivation replaces retrieval: the system knows what it was told and what follows from it by law, and it can prove both.

**I refused a teacher I cannot see.** The domain language model's second rung is trained on the facts of the first rung's own knowledge base and on nothing else. No remote model teaches it. The design goes further and says the second rung's training text is validated by the first rung's grammar before it is admitted, so that it is correct by construction. In the library the facts are rendered directly into text, with no such check yet; chapter 19 says where that stands. I gave the concept of a domain language model, first designed for one of the estate's products, to the open foundation. Any project that supplies a knowledge base can ship its own model free.

**I refused a stub.** The library declares five agents of its own and has wired one. Asking for one that is not wired raises an error; it does not return something that looks like an agent. Pretending would be easier, and it would make every other refusal in this chapter worth less.

Behind the blocks stand six guards, and the next section names them.

## 5. The court

Each block above stands on a guard. `dlm_narrated` proves the domain language model: answers with their why, refusal outside the domain, completion held to the domain's entities and relations, a self-contained bundle, and a second rung trained on its own corpus. `wisecoding_narrated` proves the conversation that asks and the refusal to conclude. `knowledge_integration_narrated` proves derivation and the certain "no". `agentgraph_narrated` proves that a model straight to an effect is not sound, that the governed shape is, and that a model actor proposes and never commits. `piagent_narrated` proves the programmatic agent and its refusal at the gate. `ownagentstack_narrated` proves the honest roster of the library's own agents.

These six guards prove what ran; the next section says where the field leads.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in reach. Its frameworks connect to hosted services of every kind, its stores search open collections of documents, and its loops call the largest models available for each step. Softanza's inversion, curate a domain and derive from it, leads on a curated domain, and on the open web the field leads today. When the task is open-ended language work over material nobody can curate, summarising arbitrary documents or research over the web, the field's stack is the right tool. The record says so in those words.

The field leads in the market. There is a labour market for its stack today; the one for this stack is still to come.

The field leads in models. The second rung of the domain language model, as built, is a small neural model of word pairs, and the foundry beyond the bigram is designed (chapter 19). Softanza does not build the large generators and does not compete with them.

Where I think the law stands apart is in three places. A decision here costs nothing, runs offline and gives the same answer every day, so it can be tested with ordinary promises. A model here cannot act, by construction, not by vigilance. And a model's sentence, where one is used, can be held to the grammar of a declared language before it is emitted, instead of being checked and retried after.

The next section says where the law stops.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the domain language model's first rung and its second, a small bigram, the conversation that asks, derivation with its proof, the programmatic agent, and the gate that refuses an ungoverned composition.

What is designed: the thinking machine of Chapter 9, where many faculties share one state and cascade until it settles. The decision stack of perceive, decide, react and learn, whose pieces exist beside the agent and not yet inside it. The typed unit of prediction, which chapter 19 tells as a design of the estate's, validated in five stages in that design and chained nowhere in the library. The conversation between an agent and the one who reviews its plan: the plan now reaches the agent, and the conversation about the plan does not yet.

What is absent: four of the library's five own agents. How many does the library declare, and how many are wired? The roster says it plainly:

```haro
? len(StzOwnAgents())
#--> 5
? StzOwnAgentIsWired("wise-coder")
#--> 1
? StzOwnAgentIsWired("induction")
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The validator, the ranker, the planner and the agent that would induce a pattern from your examples are declared and reserved. So the fifth register of an answer, examples, waits on an agent that does not exist yet.

And one limit of the first rung that you will meet the moment you rephrase a question. It recognises a relation only by the word it was declared with. What does it do when you ask in other words?

```haro
? oDLM.Ask("what does margherita contain?")
#--> Margherita is a dish.
```

> run: kept

It did not refuse! It printed `Margherita is a dish.` That answer is true, and it is not what was asked. By the law of this chapter, the answer here is a refusal, and that refusal is the next step. I print it here because a law is shown by a page that prints its stage as it stands, limit included. I hold this as a view, not a result. The request to the library is to refuse a question whose verb is not a declared relation.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Your agent's mind is code you can test with ordinary promises. A model may serve as one faculty; it can never hold an effect.

**The analyst.** A fact or a rule you add makes the system know more, with no code change and no training, and every answer carries its why.

**The designer.** The system asks the user one accountable question at a time, and the question is handed to any door as a frame, so that a terminal, a page or an agent can render it. Only the terminal ran here.

**The decision-maker.** A decision costs nothing per call, runs offline and is the same tomorrow. The four agents still reserved are the next step.

**The educator.** The conversation asks and never answers first, and it uses a model's fluency only when a model is there, so a learner always sees the floor.

**The agent.** You may propose. You may not commit. A refusal names its law, and you can learn the platform from the platform and be corrected by it.

Now the exercise: your own domain, and the promises you expect.

## 9. Exercise

Build a domain of your own with three facts: a shop, a clinic, a class. Before you run anything, write under each line the promise you expect: one question inside the domain, one outside it. Add a goal that every item must have one relation, and write the first question the system will ask you. Then run it.

If a promise is broken, do not change the promise to match the output. Explain the output first.

I left this one in your hands, on purpose. Section 7 shows a question that the first rung answered when the law says it should have refused, and I did not fix it. Take your own domain and ask one of your promised questions again in other words, with a verb you did not declare as a relation. Look at what comes back and compare it with what you asked. Then say in one sentence why it answered, and which lines of section 7 tell you so.

When you have solved it, you will know why a first rung that recognises a relation only by the word it was declared with can answer a question you did not ask.

Chapter 18 is *The five registers, and conversations that ask*. Its principle: a reply may come in any of five forms, and whatever the form, it is only a candidate, and the world's rules decide whether it becomes knowledge.


=====

# The five registers, and conversations that ask

Source: Chapter 18. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the goal, the gap and the question with its reason; a reply as an option, a data structure or a sentence split on commas and the word 'and'; the governed door with its refusals; the checkpoint with its lifetime; the goal fulfilled or revoked; one wired agent that stops where it would have to invent; designed: several readings of one reply, enumerated for the person to choose; the nearest alternatives named after a refusal; the example written back as a promise; absent: the formula register; the example register; a conversation whose question is worded in Arabic, Hausa or French; a value with a space in it

Run: 15 of 15 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:14 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I show you the five forms a reply can take, an option, a data structure, a formula, a sentence and examples. Then I show what the governed door does with each of them. I tell you which three run and which two do not, and what becomes of a refusal once it is written: it waits for a person, with its context and a lifetime. I let the conversation with no one in it run, and I show what a conversation can and cannot do in Arabic and in Hausa. Whatever the form, a reply is only a candidate, and the world's rules decide whether it becomes knowledge.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Two practices meet in the idea of a conversation that asks, and a third stands behind its last register. (General knowledge for this whole section, not a study of the record; the dates are to be checked before print.)

The first is slot filling. A dialogue framework asks the user for the missing pieces of a form until the form is full. The frameworks of the field understand free text with trained models, speak on many channels and ship connectors, and they ask the designer to write intents and flows in their own language. They take the user's reply as the answer and act on it.

The second is vibe coding, a name put into circulation in 2025: the human prompts, the model produces, the human keeps what runs.

The third is programming by example. Spreadsheets fill a column from two examples you typed. Research calls it inductive synthesis, and the model era made the example the way one steers a model.

## 2. The first principle

**A reply may come in any of five forms. Whatever the form, it is only a candidate, and the world's rules decide whether it becomes knowledge.**

The design document says it in one sentence I keep: expression is free, admission is governed. The record holds this sentence, captured from my directive of 13 July 2026. It holds no sentence of mine on why these five registers and not four or six, nor on the moment I set wise coding against vibe coding. The chapter opens on this sentence.

## 3. The Softanza form

Chapter 17 showed a conversation asking, and refusing to conclude while a gap is open. This chapter is about the answer. The five registers are five forms a reply can take: an option picked by its number, a data structure, a formula, a sentence, and examples. I will show you each, and I will tell you the ones that do not run.

**The first register: an option.** When the world already knows values, how does the question offer them? Run it:

```haro
oKB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("pizzeria")
oKB.Know("pizza1", "dish").Know("pizza2", "dish").Know("pizza3", "dish").Know("pizza4", "dish")
oKB.AddConversationQ("frame").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "topping"))
oKB.AskIn("frame")
oKB.ReplyIn("frame", "cheese")
oKB.AskIn("frame")
oKB.ReplyIn("frame", "olives")
? oKB.AskIn("frame")
#--> Which 'topping' does 'pizza3' have? (1) cheese  (2) olives -- or answer freely.  (why: every dish needs 'topping')
? @@(oKB.ReplyIn("frame", 2)[:admitted])
#--> [ "olives" ]
```

> run: kept

The question offers the known values as numbers, and `2` picked `olives`.

What does the door answer to a number nobody offered? Run it:

```haro
? oKB.AskIn("frame")
#--> Which 'topping' does 'pizza4' have? (1) cheese  (2) olives -- or answer freely.  (why: every dish needs 'topping')
aV = oKB.ReplyIn("frame", 99)
? @@(aV[:admitted])
#--> [ ]
? @@(aV[:refused])
#--> [ [ "(99)", "no such option" ] ]
? oKB.ConversationQ("frame").Checkpoints()[1][:why]
#--> no option (99) was offered -- 2 option(s) on the table
? aV[:goalState]
#--> pursuing
```

> run: kept

The door refused it, and said `no such option`! The checkpoint says why: `no option (99) was offered -- 2 option(s) on the table`. A reason, and a count of the options on the table. It does not list them, and the next question offers the same options again: `(1) cheese  (2) olives`. The refusal never guesses a value, it reaches a person as a checkpoint, and the goal stays `pursuing`. That matters for section 7.

The question is data before it is text. It carries a force, which is *what* while the world knows nothing and *which* once it knows a value, and the options. Any medium can render it:

```haro
oKF = new stzKnowledgeGraph("pizzeria2")
oKF.Know("p1", "dish").Know("p2", "dish")
oKF.AddConversationQ("f").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "topping"))
? oKF.AskInXT("f")[:force]
#--> what
oKF.ReplyIn("f", "cheese")
aQ = oKF.AskInXT("f")
? aQ[:force]
#--> which
? @@(aQ[:options])
#--> [ "cheese" ]
```

> run: kept

**The second register: a data structure.** What becomes of a list of two values sent as one reply? Run it:

```haro
oKD = new stzKnowledgeGraph("desserts")
oKD.Know("tiramisu", "dish")
oKD.AddConversationQ("setup").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"))
oKD.AskIn("setup")
aV = oKD.ReplyIn("setup", [ "mascarpone", "espresso" ])
? @@(aV[:admitted])
#--> [ "mascarpone", "espresso" ]
? aV[:goalState]
#--> fulfilled
```

> run: kept

A list of values is taken as the values, and each one goes through the same door.

**The fourth register: a sentence.** I skip the third for a moment, because it is one of the two that do not run. How is a phrase of the form "X and Y" or "X, Y" read? The conversation keeps a transcript in which every admission is narrated and the fulfilment of the goal is recorded:

```haro
oKM = new stzKnowledgeGraph("menu")
oKM.Know("margherita", "dish")
oKM.AddConversationQ("setup").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"))
oKM.AskIn("setup")
oKM.ReplyIn("setup", "tomato-sauce and mozzarella")
? oKM.ConversationQ("setup").Transcript()
#--> SOFTANZA: Goal adopted -- this conversation is now accountable for it.
#--> SOFTANZA: What does 'margherita' have for 'contains'?  (why: every dish needs 'contains')
#--> YOU:      tomato-sauce and mozzarella
#-->   -> admitted: 'margherita' contains 'tomato-sauce'
#-->   -> admitted: 'margherita' contains 'mozzarella'
#--> SOFTANZA: Goal fulfilled: every required slot is filled in 'menu'
```

> run: kept

It is read as two values, each one judged: the transcript holds `admitted: 'margherita' contains 'tomato-sauce'` and `admitted: 'margherita' contains 'mozzarella'`. "Natural" here means a splitter on commas and on the English word "and". The natural layer of the library, which I will use in a moment, is not called by the conversation.

**When a reply carries several values, and the world allows one.** What does a relation declared unique do with a reply that carries two values? Run it:

```haro
oKS = new stzKnowledgeGraph("staffing")
oKS.AddConversationQ("s").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireOne("kitchen3", "led-by"))
oKS.ConstrainRelation("led-by", :Unique)
oKS.AskIn("s")
aV = oKS.ReplyIn("s", "mario and giovanni")
? @@(aV[:admitted])
#--> [ "mario" ]
? @@(aV[:refused])
#--> [ [ "giovanni", "'kitchen3' already bears 'led-by' (to 'mario'); the relation is :Unique" ] ]
? len(oKS.ConversationQ("s").Checkpoints())
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

The first value was admitted, and the second was refused with the law's reason: `'kitchen3' already bears 'led-by' (to 'mario'); the relation is :Unique`. The refusal reaches a person, as one checkpoint. Be careful what you read in this. The choice between Mario and Giovanni was made by order. It was not made by a person. The design promises something else, and section 7 says what.

**The third register, the formula.** Is a formula typed as a reply evaluated? Run it:

```haro
oKP = new stzKnowledgeGraph("shop")
oKP.Know("tea", "product")
oKP.AddConversationQ("price").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("product", "price"))
oKP.AskIn("price")
? @@(oKP.ReplyIn("price", "=12*3")[:admitted])
#--> [ "=12*3" ]
? oKP.Query([ "tea", "price", "?o" ])[1]
#--> =12*3
```

> run: kept

It is not. The price of the tea is now the text `=12*3`! I found this out by running it, and the code is silent about a formula register. The record lists the wise-coding work on registers 3 and 5 as waiting, not routed.

**The fifth register, examples.** What does the door do with examples offered as pairs? The agent that would read them is on the roster, and reserved:

```haro
oKX = new stzKnowledgeGraph("phones")
oKX.Know("contact1", "contact")
oKX.AddConversationQ("fmt").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("contact", "number-pattern"))
oKX.AskIn("fmt")
aV = oKX.ReplyIn("fmt", [ [ "+216-21-298-374", "valid" ], [ "+33-6-12-34-56-78", "valid" ] ])
? @@(aV[:admitted])
#--> [ ]
? @@(aV[:refused])
#--> [ ]
? aV[:goalState]
#--> pursuing
? len(oKX.ConversationQ("fmt").Checkpoints())
#--> 0
try
	StzOwnAgent("induction")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Softanza's own agent 'induction' is RESERVED, not wired yet -- refusing rather than returning a stub (LAW 3). See StzOwnAgents().
```

> run: kept

Neither admitted nor refused: `[ ]` and `[ ]`, the goal still `pursuing`, and not one checkpoint! This is not the refusal the protocol describes. It is silence. I hold silence to be the worse answer, as a view and not a result: the reader who offered the examples is not told they were not understood. I found a second form of the same fault. The same examples typed as one string, joined by a comma, are split and admitted as two literal values, and the goal reads fulfilled. The examples became the knowledge. Nothing was inferred.

So three of the five registers are wired, one of them in its simplest form, a splitter. Two are not. I would rather you meet that here than on a page of mine that said five.

That leaves the refusal itself, and what becomes of it.

### What happens to a refusal

A refusal waits for a person, with its context. A lifetime can be set so that a stale one leaves the live list without being deleted:

```haro
oKT = new stzKnowledgeGraph("ttl")
oKT.AddConversationQ("t").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireOne("kitchen", "led-by"))
oKT.AskIn("t")
oKT.ReplyIn("t", 7)
? len(oKT.ConversationQ("t").Checkpoints())
#--> 1
oKT.ConversationQ("t").SetCheckpointTTL(1)
oKT.AskIn("t")
oKT.AskIn("t")
? len(oKT.ConversationQ("t").Checkpoints())
#--> 0
? len(oKT.ConversationQ("t").AllCheckpoints())
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Nothing is deleted. Expiry is about what still needs a person, and the audit record keeps all of it. Every call here is chained from `ConversationQ`, because a held copy would silently do nothing: the guard's comment explains it.

A conversation is accountable for one goal, and it ends by fulfilling the goal or by revoking it on the record. A revoked conversation asks nothing and cannot conclude:

```haro
oKG = new stzKnowledgeGraph("lifecycle")
oKG.Know("d1", "dish").Know("d2", "dish")
oKG.AddConversationQ("s1").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "sauce"))
oKG.AddConversationQ("s2").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "garnish"))
? oKG.ConversationQ("s1").GoalState()
#--> pursuing
oKG.AskIn("s1")
oKG.ReplyIn("s1", "pesto")
oKG.AskIn("s1")
oKG.ReplyIn("s1", "pesto")
? oKG.ConversationQ("s1").GoalState()
#--> fulfilled
? oKG.ConversationQ("s1").GoalWhy()
#--> every required slot is filled in 'lifecycle'
oKG.AskIn("s2")
oKG.ConversationQ("s2").RevokeGoal("out of scope tonight")
? oKG.ConversationQ("s2").GoalState()
#--> revoked
? oKG.ConversationQ("s2").GoalWhy()
#--> out of scope tonight
? len(oKG.OpenConversations())
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

### Programmatic by default: the conversation with no one in it

Chapter 17's law says conversations happen in code first. Here is the conversation with no person and no model. An agent of the library asks, an answer source (a plain function) replies, and every reply passes the same door. Where the source has no answer, does the agent invent? Run it:

```haro
oKW = new stzKnowledgeGraph("menu")
oKW.Know("margherita", "dish").Know("tiramisu", "dish")
answers = [ [ "margherita", "tomato-sauce" ], [ "tiramisu", "mascarpone" ] ]
aR = StzOwnAgent("wise-coder").PursueGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"), oKW, func subj, rel {
	for a in answers
		if a[1] = subj
			return a[2]
		ok
	next
	return ""
})
? aR[:filled]
#--> 1
? aR[:asked]
#--> 2
? aR[:why]
#--> goal filled in 2 question(s), governed admission throughout
oKV = new stzKnowledgeGraph("desserts")
oKV.Know("panna-cotta", "dish")
aR2 = StzOwnAgent("wise-coder").PursueGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"), oKV, func subj, rel { return "" })
? aR2[:filled]
#--> 0
? aR2[:why]
#--> stopped: no answer for 'panna-cotta' / 'contains' -- gap left for a human
```

> run: kept

It does not. It stops, says `stopped: no answer for 'panna-cotta' / 'contains' -- gap left for a human`, and leaves the gap for a person. Of the library's five own agents, this is the one that is wired. The other four are reserved, and asking for one raises, as the fifth register's block printed: `RESERVED, not wired yet -- refusing rather than returning a stub`. It is never a stub.

### In Arabic, and in Hausa

Chapters 2 and 11 showed the natural layer in Hausa. Here is what the conversation can and cannot do with languages, said in three blocks, because the three are different.

First, the data. Names, values and lists in Arabic script pass the gate exactly as Latin ones do. The words around them, the frame of the question, stay English:

```haro
oKA = new stzKnowledgeGraph("مطعم")
oKA.Know("بيتزا", "dish")
oKA.AddConversationQ("c").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"))
? oKA.AskIn("c")
#--> What does 'بيتزا' have for 'contains'?  (why: every dish needs 'contains')
aV = oKA.ReplyIn("c", [ "جبن", "طماطم" ])
? @@(aV[:admitted])
#--> [ "جبن", "طماطم" ]
? aV[:goalState]
#--> fulfilled
```

> run: kept

The Arabic values passed the gate, and the goal reads `fulfilled`.

Second, the candidate. A Hausa sentence can produce a value in the natural layer, and the value, sent as a reply, goes through the same door. The two steps are the caller's: the conversation does not call the natural layer itself. This is the principle of the chapter in a small space, since the expression is free and the admission is governed. The graph folds the case of the value, so the printed `JIBINI` is admitted as 'jibini':

```haro
oKH = new stzKnowledgeGraph("menu")
oKH.Know("pizza", "dish")
oKH.AddConversationQ("c").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("dish", "contains"))
oKH.AskIn("c")
cCandidate = NaturallyIn("ha", "Yi rubutu dauke 'jibini' Maida shi").Result()
? cCandidate
#--> JIBINI
aV = oKH.ReplyIn("c", cCandidate)
? aV[:narration]
#--> admitted: 'pizza' contains 'jibini'
```

> run: kept

Third, the question in the learner's language. The tutor is a consumer of the conversation: it uses the gap the conversation names, and it chooses the words to say it in four languages. The sentences are templates. The code's own comment says that the French, Arabic and Hausa wording is a draft awaiting a native reviewer. Read the three below as drafts, and not as reviewed text:

```haro
oEx = StzProgramQ("../../education/program").CourseQ("elementary-introduction").ExerciseQ("ex-01-01")
? oEx.GapText("finds", "en")
#--> Where are the repeated items? Which line of your program asks for their positions? (why: the mental model finds before it applies)
? oEx.GapText("finds", "fr")
#--> Où sont les éléments répétés ? Quelle ligne de votre programme demande leurs positions ? (pourquoi : le modèle mental trouve avant d'agir)
? oEx.GapText("finds", "ar")
#--> أين العناصر المكررة؟ أيّ سطر في برنامجك يسأل عن مواضعها؟ (لماذا: النموذج الذهني يبحث قبل أن يطبّق)
? oEx.GapText("finds", "ha")
#--> Ina abubuwan da aka maimaita? Wane layi a shirinka yake tambayar wurarensu? (dalili: tsarin tunani yana nemo kafin ya aiwatar)
```

> run: kept

So the library cannot ask in Arabic or in Hausa through `AskIn`. What it can do is hold the data in any script, take a candidate computed in any pack, and let the tutor say the gap in four languages. A value with a space in it raises at the moment (section 7 gives the evidence). So the connectors "et" in French, "da" in Hausa and "و" in Arabic are not connectors. The three sentences above are the library's own, printed as they stand, and the code calls them a draft awaiting a native reviewer. I wrote no Arabic or Hausa sentence of my own.

### A model is a fluency, never the mind

Without a model, what does a conversation say of its own fluency? Run it:

```haro
oKF2 = new stzKnowledgeGraph("fluency-space")
oKF2.AddConversation("f")
? oKF2.ConversationQ("f").Fluency()
#--> plain
oKF2.ConversationQ("f").SetFluency(:neural)
? oKF2.ConversationQ("f").Fluency()
#--> neural
? oKF2.ConversationQ("f").IsFluencyNeural()
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

`plain`: it stays on its deterministic floor and says so. The word "neural" can be set, and `Fluency()` repeats it, but `IsFluencyNeural()` answers `0`! The word is claimed only when a model is there.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused to guess.** The record's phrase is: guessing is replaced by asking; vibes are replaced by governance. A gap is a question. A reply is a candidate.

**I refused a different gate for each way of answering.** A formula is not trusted because it is code, and a structure is not trusted because it is typed. The form does not buy trust, and I hold this as a view, not a result. All five registers go through one door.

**I refused a stub.** A reserved agent raises when it is asked for. The design document calls the raise the law working, and says it should not be read as a defect. I refused to pretend the fifth register works.

**I refused an agent that fills a gap to finish the job.** The wise coder stops, and leaves the gap for a person.

**I refused to let a conversation own a world.** It happens inside a knowledge space and never owns one. A copy of the knowledge held in a session goes stale.

**I refused a deleted checkpoint.** Expiry takes a refusal off the live list. The record keeps it.

**I refused to let the example look like the answer.** The record's closing law about the fifth register is that an admitted candidate writes its example back as a promise. That law is designed, and not built.

## 5. The court

`wisecoding_narrated` proves that the gap is computed from the space, and that the question is led by the system, names its subject and says why it asks. It proves that "X and Y" and a data structure admit. It proves that a unique law refuses through the conversation and the refusal reaches a checkpoint. It proves that concluding with a gap open is refused. `wisecoding_rich_narrated` proves the goal monitored to fulfilled or revoked, the frame, and the numbered options. It proves the refusal of a number nobody offered, the lifetime of a checkpoint, and the fluency that is claimed only with a model. `ownagentstack_narrated` proves the roster of five, the wired wise coder, the reserved agent that raises, and the gap left for a person. `tutor_gaps_narrated` proves the exercise's gap question in each language, and that a step with no question in a language is red, never another language. It writes folders, so the book counts it and does not run it; the tutor block above only reads the course.

Three of the guards print their own total and end by calling `pf()`, which prints the time and the runtime's version and then stops the run with a raise. That is their way to end. `tutor_gaps_narrated` ends with `Summary()`, which prints the total and a status, and does not raise.

What the field does better, and where the record stands apart, comes next.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in understanding. A dialogue framework, as the field holds it, reads free text with trained models, speaks on many channels and ships connectors. The record says what Softanza lacks, in its own count: the word "WhatsApp" appears in no code file and no design document of the library. The conversation asks one slot at a time, over a graph the user must first have declared, and it does not understand an open reply.

The field leads on examples. Programming by example is, as the field holds it, shipped in spreadsheets today. Softanza has no induction at all, and the record says so: nothing induces yet. A search for "induce", "from examples" and "infer" over the regular-expression folder finds nothing.

The field is honest about costs, and so is the record. Several candidates is the normal case when you ask for a rule from examples. A pattern induced from three examples overfits them. The remedy the record names is the question that tells two candidates apart. And the index that would carry the examples is as clean as the tags of the methods, with about eighty-five misspellings.

Here is where I think the record stands apart. Every reply, in whatever form, is judged by the world's laws before it counts, with the reason. The session keeps a transcript and a checkpoint that a person can read. The field's reply is a prompt, and the human keeps what comes out.

The limit comes next, and it names what is built, what is designed and what is absent.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the goal, the gap, the question with its reason, the frame; the first register, the second, and the fourth in the form of a splitter; the governed door with its refusals. And the checkpoint and its lifetime; the goal fulfilled or revoked; the transcript; one wired agent; the tutor's four-language gap sentence.

What is designed: the *several acceptable* branch as the design writes it. The protocol says that when several readings of a reply are acceptable, the system enumerates them and the person chooses. What is built is two different things. The values already known become numbered options in the question, before the reply. And a reply that carries several values has each one judged on its own, with the first kept under a unique law. No step presents competing readings of one reply. And the *none acceptable* branch as written: the reason is built, and the nearest acceptable alternatives are not. The refusal states a reason, and for a number, how many options were on the table. And the example written back as a promise: the site's page *by example* (read 9 October 2026) calls it a rule of the design that is not built. The site's two pages on this, *wise* and *by example*, say "with the nearest alternatives" and "the person chooses". The two pages promise more than the code does. I say it so that the page or the code is corrected. The code wires three of the five registers. The memo that names the formula and the examples lists them as waiting, not routed, and for this chapter they are absent.

What is absent: the formula register; the example register, and the induction agent behind it; a conversation worded in Arabic, Hausa or French; and a value with a space in it. In the research run of 8 October 2026, on the version of the library this book runs, `ReplyIn` raised on each value containing a space that I tried. The error was an uninitialised variable. The three were "tomato sauce", "fromage et tomate", "jibini da tumatir". Called directly, the door returned the refusal properly. No block of this book records either result, so the run of the book does not show them. The conversation should have refused with the reason and written a checkpoint, and it doesn't. It is in the memo as a request, and the exercise below does not use a value with a space.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** A reply is a verdict you can test with ordinary promises: what was admitted, what was refused, and the state of the goal.

**The analyst.** The goal is the schema of a complete model. The gaps are the list of what is missing. The transcript and the checkpoints are the evidence of how each fact got in.

**The designer.** The question comes back as data: force, subject, relation, options, reason. Any medium can render it. The library renders English, and the tutor shows the pattern for four languages.

**The decision-maker.** A reply is judged before it counts. A refusal reaches a person with its context and a lifetime. The roster is honest: one agent of five is wired, and two of the five registers are not available.

**The educator.** The tutor is wise coding. It names the missing step as a question and never as the answer, in the learner's language, and its wording in three of those languages awaits a native reviewer.

**The agent.** You can drive the loop yourself with an answer source, and you must stop where you would have to invent. You cannot offer examples and be understood, and you are told by name when you ask for a reserved agent.

## 9. Exercise

Choose a small world of your own, a shop, a class, a clinic, with four entities of one type and a goal that each needs one relation. Before you run anything, write under each of the five replies below the verdict you expect: admitted, refused with a reason, or neither.

1. a plain word;
2. a number that picks a value the first reply made known;
3. a number nobody offered;
4. two values at once, for a relation you have declared unique;
5. a list of two pairs of examples.

Then run it. An admitted reply uses up the question, so call `AskIn` again before the next reply. If a promise breaks, do not change the promise: explain the output first.

```haro exercise
oWorld = new stzKnowledgeGraph("my-world")
oWorld.Know("item1", "thing").Know("item2", "thing")
oWorld.AddConversationQ("mine").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("thing", "colour"))
oWorld.AskIn("mine")
# your five replies here, each with the verdict you expect
```

> run: exercise

The fifth reply produces silence today, and that is the lesson of the exercise. If the library later turns it into a refusal, the expected answer changes. Run it on the date and commit shown at the foot of the page.

I left this one in your hands. Take the fifth-register block of section 3 and change one thing: send the two phone numbers as one string, joined by a comma, and not as two pairs.

```haro exercise
oKX = new stzKnowledgeGraph("phones")
oKX.Know("contact1", "contact")
oKX.AddConversationQ("fmt").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("contact", "number-pattern"))
oKX.AskIn("fmt")
aV = oKX.ReplyIn("fmt", "+216-21-298-374,+33-6-12-34-56-78")
? @@(aV[:admitted])
? aV[:goalState]
```

> run: exercise

Run it, then read what was admitted and what the goal says, and decide whether that is what you wanted to hand to the door as examples. Section 4, in its last refusal, and section 7, in the absent example register, say why I call it a fault. When you can say why, you will know what the door did with your examples, and why that is not induction.

The next chapter is about the domain language model. There the next thing the model says is a checkable unit of your world, a fact your rules admit or refuse, and never a token.


=====

# The domain language model

Source: Chapter 19. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the domain model's first rung with no neurons; its facts rendered as a corpus; a closed vocabulary; completion by lookup; a plain-text bundle that travels with its tests; a small second rung, a bigram, exported to a model file; the admission of a fact by a law; designed: a grammar derived from a knowledge base by one call; the typed unit of prediction validated in five stages; a second rung whose corpus is checked before it trains; the foundry that would make it larger; absent: a pipeline that chains the five stages on any unit; an end-of-sentence word for the second rung; a completion that sees the sentence so far

Run: 7 of 7 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:15 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I forge a domain language model from a knowledge base, and no one writes its training text: every sentence is a recorded fact. It has two rungs. The first answers from those facts and has no neurons. The second, a small neural model, is fluent in the grammar of the domain and wrong about facts. I show the grammar that refuses a malformed sentence and the graph that refuses a false one. I say which pieces the library holds and which the reader still has to compose. The principle is this: the next thing the model says is a checkable unit of your world, a fact your rules admit or refuse, never a token.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

A language model predicts the next token. A token is a fragment of text with no type, no role and no constraint: it can be the end of a word, a space, a bracket. Everything a model says is built by choosing tokens one after another. Everything that makes the result useful, that it is a valid sentence, that it is true, that it fits a schema, is checked afterwards or enforced from outside.

The field has made this work with three tools. You adapt a general model to a domain by training it further on the domain's documents. You give it the relevant documents at the moment of the question, retrieved by similarity. And you constrain its decoding with a grammar, so that it cannot produce a malformed sentence, as chapter 11 showed. All three keep the unit the same: the token.

The next section names the one thing I change: the unit.

## 2. The first principle

**The next thing the model says is a checkable unit of your world, a fact your rules admit or refuse, never a token.**

In the design the gate is the same for every model of this kind: generations are proposals, and the graph and its rules decide admission.

Section 3 shows the form it takes, and runs it.

## 3. The Softanza form

I call it a domain language model, and it has two rungs. The first has no neurons at all and was shown in chapter 17: it answers from recorded facts, says why, and refuses outside its domain. The second is a small neural model, optional, which gives it fluency. In the design both rungs end at the same gate; here the gate is the `Admit` of the last block of this section, called by hand.

Where does the training text come from? Build a small knowledge base, render it, and read the corpus and the vocabulary it gives.

```haro
oKB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("restaurant")
oKB.Know("margherita", "dish").
    Know("tiramisu", "dish").
    Know("chianti", "wine").
    KnowRelation("margherita", "contains", "tomato-sauce").
    KnowRelation("margherita", "contains", "mozzarella").
    KnowRelation("margherita", "pairs-with", "chianti")
oDLM = StzDlmQ(oKB)
? @@( oDLM.GenerateCorpus() )
#--> [ "margherita is a dish.", "tiramisu is a dish.", "chianti is a wine.", "margherita contains tomato-sauce.", "margherita contains mozzarella.", "margherita pairs-with chianti." ]
? @@( oDLM.Tokenizer() )
#--> [ "<unk>", "margherita", "tiramisu", "chianti", "dish", "wine", "contains", "pairs-with", "is", "a", "tomato-sauce", "mozzarella" ]
? @@( oDLM.Tokenize("margherita contains something-new") )
#--> [ 2, 7, 1 ]
```

> run: kept

No one wrote that corpus: each of its six sentences is a fact of the knowledge base, rendered. The vocabulary is closed, every word the domain holds with a first entry for the unknown. Twelve entries, and `something-new`, a word outside the domain, maps to the first one: `[ 2, 7, 1 ]`. "Correct by construction" holds here in the one sense the code gives it: each sentence is a recorded fact. It does not yet hold in the sense the design intended; section 7 says where.

Completion is constrained to the domain. What does the model offer after an entity that bears facts, after a relation, after a fragment, and after a word the domain does not hold? Run it:

```haro
? @@( oDLM.Complete("margherita") )
#--> [ "contains", "pairs-with" ]
? @@( oDLM.Complete("contains") )
#--> [ "tomato-sauce", "mozzarella" ]
? @@( oDLM.Complete("tira") )
#--> [ "tiramisu" ]
? @@( oDLM.Complete("pizza") )
#--> [ ]
```

> run: kept

After an entity that bears facts, its relations: `[ "contains", "pairs-with" ]`. After a relation, only its objects: `[ "tomato-sauce", "mozzarella" ]`. A fragment completes to entities, and a word that the domain does not hold completes to nothing: `[ ]`. The guard that proves this calls it "the grammar spirit". It is a lookup in the facts, not decoding under a compiled grammar. And it sees one word, not the sentence so far: `Complete("contains")` returns the objects of every `contains` fact, whatever its subject.

The grammar itself can be derived from the knowledge base, and here the reader of this book has to write the derivation. There is no call in the library that turns a knowledge base into a grammar, and the design lists it as the first rung's task. But the pieces compose. The domain's vocabulary becomes the choices of a schema from chapter 11, the schema compiles to a grammar, and the grammar has no path for a word outside the domain:

```haro
aLex = oDLM.Lexicon()
acObjects = []
for aFact in oKB.Facts()
	if aFact[2] != "is-a" and find(acObjects, aFact[3]) = 0
		acObjects + aFact[3]
	ok
next
oS = StzOutputSchemaQ([
	[ :field = "subject",  :type = :oneof, :choices = aLex[:entities] ],
	[ :field = "relation", :type = :oneof, :choices = aLex[:relations] ],
	[ :field = "object",   :type = :oneof, :choices = acObjects ]
])
? oS.ToGBNF()
#--> root ::= subject-line relation-line object-line
#    subject-line ::= "subject: " ("margherita" | "tiramisu" | "chianti") "\n"
#    relation-line ::= "relation: " ("contains" | "pairs-with") "\n"
#    object-line ::= "object: " ("tomato-sauce" | "mozzarella" | "chianti") "\n"
#    text ::= [^\n]+
#    number ::= "-"? [0-9]+ ("." [0-9]+)?
#    boolean ::= ("yes" | "no" | "true" | "false")
cGood = "subject: margherita" + nl + "relation: contains" + nl + "object: mozzarella" + nl
cOut  = "subject: pizza" + nl + "relation: contains" + nl + "object: mozzarella" + nl
cLie  = "subject: tiramisu" + nl + "relation: contains" + nl + "object: mozzarella" + nl
? oS.Accepts(cGood)
#--> 1
? oS.Accepts(cOut)
#--> 0
? oS.Accepts(cLie)
#--> 1
? oDLM.Ask("tiramisu contains what?")
#--> I hold no 'contains' knowledge about 'tiramisu'.
```

> run: kept

Look at the last four lines together. The grammar accepts the lie! It has the right shape, and every word is in the domain. Asked what a tiramisu contains, the model answers: `I hold no 'contains' knowledge about 'tiramisu'.` A grammar is a gate for shape; the truth belongs to the graph.

A domain language model is a file that travels. It is saved as plain text, readable and diff-able, with no binary and no weights. Can a second model that has never seen the knowledge base answer from it, and run its own tests? Save it, read it back, and load it:

```haro
oDLM.AddGolden("what is margherita", "Margherita is a dish.")
oDLM.AddGolden("what is the weather today?",
	"That is outside the 'restaurant' domain (I know 3 entities and 2 relations).")
cFile = oDLM.Save("probe_dlm")
? cFile
#--> probe_dlm.zdlm
? read(cFile)
#--> dlm "restaurant"
#    entities
#        margherita | dish
#        tiramisu | dish
#        chianti | wine
#    relations
#        contains
#        pairs-with
#    facts
#        margherita | contains | tomato-sauce
#        margherita | contains | mozzarella
#        margherita | pairs-with | chianti
#    laws
#    goldens
#        what is margherita | Margherita is a dish.
#        what is the weather today? | That is outside the 'restaurant' domain (I know 3 entities and 2 relations).
oShipped = StzDlmQ("")
oShipped.LoadFrom(cFile)
? oShipped.Ask("what is tiramisu")
#--> Tiramisu is a dish.
? @@( oShipped.RunGoldens() )
#--> [ [ "total", 2 ], [ "passed", 2 ], [ "failed", [ ] ] ]
remove(cFile)
? fexists(cFile)
#--> 0
```

> run: kept (touches files)

The second model answers: `Tiramisu is a dish.` The refusal travels with the bundle as a golden test, and the model that has never seen the graph passes it too! The laws section is empty because no law was declared before the model was forged; declare that a relation is unique, forge again, and the line appears.

Now the second rung. It is a small neural model trained on the corpus you saw, with no teacher, and it can be exported as a model file. How small is it, and does it learn?

```haro
oDLM.TrainNeuralRung(500)
? oDLM.NeuralWhy()
#--> neural bigram rung: 15 pairs over a 12-word vocab; trained 500 epoch(s), lr 0.50: loss 2.57 -> 0.70
? oDLM.NextToken("is")
#--> a
? oDLM.NextTokenConfidence("is")
#--> 1.00
cGguf = oDLM.ExportNeuralGguf("probe_bigram")
? cGguf
#--> probe_bigram.gguf
? StzEngineNeuralGgufInspect(cGguf)
#--> 2
remove(cGguf)
```

> run: kept (touches files)

It is small: a single layer that predicts the next word from the previous one, `15 pairs over a 12-word vocab`. And it learns: `loss 2.57 -> 0.70`. The library inspects the exported file and prints `2`, the two tensors it holds, a weight and a bias. I do not claim another runtime loads it as a text generator. It trains in a fraction of a second, offline, and gives the same numbers on every run.

What does a model like this say? It is fluent in the grammar of the domain, and it is wrong about facts. That is the reason for the gate:

```haro
aCorpus = oDLM.GenerateCorpus()
for cSeed in [ "tiramisu", "chianti", "margherita" ]
	cSaid = oDLM.NeuralGenerate(cSeed, 3)
	? cSaid + "  ->  " + ( find(aCorpus, cSaid + ".") > 0 )
next
#--> tiramisu is a wine  ->  0
#    chianti is a wine  ->  1
#    margherita contains mozzarella is  ->  0
```

> run: kept

"Tiramisu is a wine" is false, because tiramisu is a dish. "Chianti is a wine" is true. And "margherita contains mozzarella is" is a run-on, because the bigram has no word that ends a sentence. The test against the corpus is a crude gate, and it would refuse a true fact nobody recorded. The real gate is the graph and its laws. What does the graph answer when the false sentence is proposed to it as a fact? Declare the law, then propose:

```haro
oKB.ConstrainRelation("is-a", :Unique)
aVerdict = oKB.Admit("tiramisu", "is-a", "wine", [ :source = "rung 2" ])
? aVerdict[:admitted]
#--> 0
? aVerdict[:why]
#--> 'tiramisu' already bears 'is-a' (to 'dish'); the relation is :Unique
? @@( oKB.Contradictions() )
#--> [ [ [ "subject", "tiramisu" ], [ "relation", "is-a" ], [ "existing", "dish" ], [ "attempted", "wine" ], [ "source", "rung 2" ] ] ]
? oKB.Admit("pizza", "is-a", "dish", [ :source = "rung 2" ])[:why]
#--> admitted: 'pizza' is-a 'dish'
```

> run: kept

The graph refused the false proposal! It says why: `'tiramisu' already bears 'is-a' (to 'dish'); the relation is :Unique`. The refusal is kept, with its source: the model's second rung. A new fact is admitted. That is the whole of the principle in six calls: a proposal, a law, a refusal with its why, and the refusal on the record.

The design describes the unit of prediction in three levels. A token is a fragment with no type. An embedding is a vector without a role. And a chunk is a typed node of a graph, with a tag, a payload and edges: a declaration, a relationship, a constraint. A chunk is validated in five stages before it enters the graph: type, pattern, graph integrity, compliance, and constructive contribution. I tell it as design, because there is no chunk in the library. What exists are pieces: the court of chapter 11 checks a text's type and shape; and the admission above enforces a law, which is compliance. Nothing chains them.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

On 13 July 2026, I ruled on this. The concept of a domain language model had been designed for one of the estate's products. I donated it to the open foundation, so that any Softanza project that supplies a knowledge base can ship its own model free to the users of its domain. The format is `.zdlm`. The first rung is in every model; the second is optional fluency. The record holds the ruling that a model is free to ship, and no licence text. I tell "free" as the ruling's word: a plain file, in the open foundation, with no licence fee or meter.

**I refused the token as the unit.** The unit is a chunk of the world, which a graph can admit or refuse.

**I refused a teacher I cannot see.** The earlier design of the idea used a large commercial model to write its training corpus. I put a law against it, and this model's corpus is rendered from the domain's own facts: the library's second law says a capability never requires anything paid or remote.

**I refused neural first.** The finding I start from is that the canonical model is not neural. A review of the earlier product archived a transformer of one to three billion parameters, in favour of rule-based prediction. Its ground was that hallucination is impossible when the model cannot generate almost-valid text. So the first rung has zero training and zero cost, and the neural rung comes second.

**I refused to let the shape stand for the truth.** The grammar says structure kills malformedness, not falsehood. The truth belongs to the graph, to its laws, and to a person.

Section 5 says what the court proves of this.

## 5. The court

`dlm_narrated` proves the model forged from a graph, its answers with their why, its refusal outside the domain and the completion. It proves also the plain-text bundle with its goldens, the closed tokenizer and the second rung with its model file. One of its assertions on the second rung checks only that the generated sentence begins with a fixed prefix, so it would also pass the false sentence above. `structuredoutput_narrated`, `schema_grammar_narrated` and `constrained_decoding_narrated` prove the court of chapter 11, the grammar compiler and the sampler. The last of them has two scenes that need a generative model file, which the snapshot does not hold; the record counts 50 assertions in that guard. `knowledge_integration_narrated` proves derivation and the refusal by a law. The admission I show in the last block is not called by its name in any guard. It is reached through the conversation, and by the strict form of adding a fact, in which a fact added without its admission is refused. That much the guard does assert.

Section 6 asks where the field leads, and where this model stands.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in scale. Training and serving models of every size is a mature practice. This second rung is a single layer over twelve words, and the steps that would make it more, a validated corpus, deduplication, a trained tokenizer, a larger network, quantisation, are not done. I do not compete with large models.

The field leads in fluency, which is the point of a model. A first rung that answers from facts will say "I hold no 'contains' knowledge about 'tiramisu'." where a general model would write something plausible, and for a customer the plausible thing is often more useful. The record's own answer is the second rung, and its present state is the one section 7 gives.

Where I think this stands apart is in what the output is. A general model's answer is text, and every check on it is after the fact. Here every answer of the first rung carries its why and a certainty of one. Every proposal of the second can be put to a gate, and the gate can refuse it with a reason. A model that can only say what the domain holds, and a gate that remembers what it refused: that is what a bank, a ministry or a school can sign. I hold that as a view, not a result.

Section 7 says what is built, what is designed and what is absent.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the first rung and its answers; the facts rendered as a corpus, with a closed vocabulary; completion by lookup; and the bundle that travels with its tests. Also built: a second rung that trains in a fraction of a second and exports a small model file, and the admission of a fact by a law.

What is designed comes in four points, and the page must say each.

- The grammar derived from a knowledge base by one call. Chapter 19's composition is the reader's, not the library's.
- The second rung's corpus validated by the first rung's grammar before it is admitted. The design says so; the code renders the facts directly, with no such check. So "correct by construction" holds for the training text, and not for what the model says.
- The five-stage validation of a chunk. None exists as a pipeline.
- The foundry's steps beyond the bigram.

What is absent: an end-of-sentence word for the second rung, which is why one of its sentences runs on. Absent too is a completion that sees the sentence so far; that is why a completion after a relation lists the objects of every fact of that relation. The first rung also answers a rephrased question with the type, as chapter 17 showed, where the law of this part says it should refuse.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** A model is a file you can read, diff and test: its facts, its laws and its goldens.

**The analyst.** A fact you add makes the model know more, and a law you add makes the graph refuse more, with no training; the model's own answers do not read it yet.

**The designer.** The model's refusal is part of its contract: the golden test says what it must decline to answer.

**The decision-maker.** The model can only say what the domain holds, and the gate keeps the record of what it refused and why.

**The educator.** Rung one is a model a learner can read in full. Rung two is small enough to train in front of a class.

**The agent.** What you propose is a fact the graph can admit or refuse, with a reason you can read.

## 9. Exercise

Forge a domain of your own with three entities and two relations. Before you run anything, write what you expect from three calls. They are the completion of an entity, the completion of a relation, and the answer to a question about a thing the domain does not hold. Then declare that one relation is unique and propose, in the form of a fact, something that contradicts it.

Run it. Then write a sentence of the right shape and the wrong truth, and say which of the grammar and the graph refuses it, and which does not.

I left this one in your hands. Section 3 says that completion sees one word and not the sentence so far, and section 7 lists it as absent. In your domain, give two entities the same relation with different objects, ask for the completion of that relation, and read the list against the sentence you had in mind. The library does not repair it, and I have not repaired it for you. Then compose, from what section 3 shows (the facts of a knowledge base, read one by one), a completion that sees the subject as well as the relation.

When you have solved it, you will know why a completion that sees one word lists the objects of every fact of that relation.

Chapter 20 turns from what a model says to what an agent does. Its title is *Agents that cannot hurt you*, and its principle is this: do not build safe agents; build a safe world, and let ordinary agents loose inside it.


=====

# Agents that cannot hurt you

Source: Chapter 20. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: reversibility as a declared contract and a registration gate; posture set against reversibility, at the door and at load; the workbench, the plan, the reviewer's rejection, the committing actor under a scope; the containment drill; the engine's bounded slots and queue (the bounded inbox is tested in Zig only, not run for the book); designed: the agent's side of the negotiation over a plan; the examination hall; branches as parallel minds; twins for databases, interfaces and networks; absent: a plan with no executor that is refused; a ranking of a plan's risks; code already running in the process, which is out of scope; the Right to a Human as a machine check

Run: 9 of 9 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:15 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take the action half of what chapter 17 said of the whole mind: what happens when the proposal meets the world. I show four steps in code, the agent proposes, the workbench rehearses, the court judges, and only a governed actor commits, and then the cases where a step does not hold. I say what I refused: safe agents, a permission check as the boundary, a second workbench for machines, an optional gate. And I say where it stops: a plan with no executor at all is allowed, and the loop checks that a coverage statement exists, not that it is true. The principle is the doctrine's: do not build safe agents; build a safe world, and let ordinary agents loose inside it.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

In the field, safety is a property of the agent. The doctrine document of the library lists what the field offers: alignment, guardrails, confirmation prompts, a checkbox that says a human is in the loop. Tool-using agents, and the autonomous agents that descend from them, let the model's tool calls touch reality immediately, with a prompt for confirmation. Computer-use agents run in a sandboxed virtual machine, which contains a process, and does not tell a file moved from random bytes churning.

The best advice of the field, which the narration says every serious guide of 2026 gives (it paraphrases one roadmap), is to separate planning from execution and put a human approval between them. It is good advice, and it is advice: a pattern you implement, and can forget. The threat lists of the field, the OWASP list of risks for agentic applications among them, are lenses, and the record uses them so.

So the field's posture, in a line, is that the agent is a model in a loop, and safety is vigilance around it. (General knowledge, not a study of the record, except where I name the document.) Chapter 17 said this of the whole mind. This chapter takes the action half: what happens when the proposal meets the world.

## 2. The first principle

**Do not build safe agents. Build a safe world, and let ordinary agents loose inside it.**

The sentence is in the library's doctrine document: "We will not build safe agents. We will build a safe world, and let ordinary agents loose inside it." The document gives its structural half in one more sentence. The only artifact that ever leaves its world is an update plan, and plans are executed by someone else. The document carries no date and no author line in its header, and git first records it in a commit of 9 July 2026. The chapter holds the sentence as the document's, dated by that first commit, and gives no date of writing.

## 3. The Softanza form

Four steps, in the form the study of the record gives them: the agent proposes, the workbench rehearses, the court judges, and only a governed actor commits. I will show each in code, and then the cases where a step does not hold.

**May this agent exist?** Before an agent runs a single tick, the governance asks the question, and it asks it when the answer is free. An agent that declares no coverage, one sentence saying what it covers, is refused. An agent that declares no reversibility class is refused too: reversible, compensable or irreversible. Both refusals are in the engine's own sentences, and the door of the former host language repeats them. What does it say to an agent that declares no coverage, and to one that declares no reversibility class? Run it:

```haro
oGov = new stzGovernance("shop")
oGov.DeclareReversibility("restocker", :Compensable)
? oGov.ReversibilityOf("restocker")
#--> compensable
? oGov.MayRegister("mystery", "", "reversible")
#--> 0
? oGov.Why()
#--> AGENTLOOP-R4: agent 'mystery' declares no coverage statement. Registration refuses it. Say what this agent covers, in one sentence, before the loop will run it. Law 18: nothing schedules what nobody has stated the reach of.
? oGov.MayRegister("mystery", "reorders stock", "")
#--> 0
? oGov.Why()
#--> AGENTLOOP-R5: agent 'mystery' declares no reversibility class. Registration refuses it. Declare one of: reversible | compensable | irreversible. Law 18: an agent whose reversal nobody stated cannot be scheduled by something that cannot undo it.
? oGov.MayRegister("mystery", "reorders stock", :Compensable)
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

The first refusal ends on its law, `Law 18: nothing schedules what nobody has stated the reach of.` With both stated, the third call answers `1`.

Reversibility is the sixth contract of the governance, beside risk, authority, commitment, decommission and lineage. It is orthogonal to risk: an act of small risk that cannot be undone deserves more ceremony than a large one that can.

**The harder an act is to undo, the more trusted the code that does it must be.** Which postures may perform which classes of act? Here is the whole table, printed by a loop of the book's own:

```haro
aPostures = [ "trusted", "external", "sandboxed" ]
aClasses = [ "reversible", "compensable", "irreversible" ]
for cP in aPostures
	cRow = cP + ":"
	for cC in aClasses
		if StzPostureReversibilityRefusal(cP, cC) = ""
			cRow += " yes"
		else
			cRow += " no"
		ok
	next
	? cRow
next
#--> trusted: yes yes yes
#--> external: yes yes no
#--> sandboxed: yes no no
? StzPostureReversibilityRefusal("sandboxed", "irreversible")
#--> a 'sandboxed' posture does not cover 'irreversible' work -- the harder an act is to undo, the more trusted the code performing it must be (reversibility x posture, ruling 3.2)
```

> run: kept

Read down the rows: trusted code may do all three classes of act, external code the first two, sandboxed code only the reversible. The last line asks for the refusal and prints its sentence.

**The agent runs, believes it acted, and the disk does not move.** An agent is a file and a few functions. Its function writes a price list. It is given a workbench, and the function reaches the bench as an ambient object during one cycle, with no change in its signature. The skill verifies its own work and the fact is learned. After one cycle, does the real file exist? Ask the disk, then the bench:

```haro
StzAllowAgentFunction("ScribblePrices", "trusted")
cFx = StzReplace(WorkingDirectory(), "\", "/") + "/_fixture"
StzDirCreatePath(cFx)
$cPrices = cFx + "/prices.csv"
cPia = "pia: 2
name: scribbler
kind: pi
coverage: writes a price list and records that it did
reversibility: compensable
schedule:
  timer: 20
skills:
  - name: scribble
    when: always
    does: ring:ScribblePrices
    verify: fact prices were written
    posture: trusted
"
oAg = StzAgentDeclarationQ(cPia).ToAgent()
oAg.GiveWorkbench()
? oAg.Cycle()
#--> 1
? oAg.MemoryQ().Fact("prices", "were", "written")
#--> 1
? StzFileExists($cPrices)
#--> 0
? oAg.WorkbenchQ().Exists($cPrices)
#--> 1
? oAg.WorkbenchQ().ContentOf($cPrices)
#--> espresso,2.40
#--> flat-white,3.10

func ScribblePrices(poMemory)
	StzActiveWorkbenchQ().WriteFile($cPrices, "espresso,2.40" + char(10) + "flat-white,3.10")
	poMemory.Learn("prices", "were", "written")
```

> run: kept

The file lives in the twin. The agent believes it acted, and the disk did not move!

Two lines decide who may act. `posture: trusted` is in the file, because a `ring:` clause must carry a posture. `StzAllowAgentFunction` is a line of the platform's code, and not of the agent's file, and it decides which functions a file may call.

**The only export is a plan, and it narrates itself.** The tick exports toward reality a plan and nothing else. The plan says what it would do before anything is attempted. A language model set as its executor is refused before any attempt: `actor 'eager-llm' cannot commit -- it lacks the 'effectful' capability (required by operation 1)`. A named actor under a scope that does not cover the path commits nothing: the disk still did not move. The same actor under a scope that covers the path commits once, and the audit trail reads `[ [ 1, "committed", "write_file", "shop-committer" ] ]`. Who may commit, and when does the disk finally move? In this block, reality changes here and only here:

```haro
oPlan = oAg.GenerateUpdatePlan()
? StzReplace(oPlan.Narration(), cFx, "<fixture>")
#--> Update plan (1 of 1 operations to commit):
#-->   * 1. write 29 bytes to '<fixture>/prices.csv'
oPlan.SetExecutor(LLMActor("eager-llm"))
? oPlan.MayCommit()[2]
#--> actor 'eager-llm' cannot commit -- it lacks the 'effectful' capability (required by operation 1)
oScope = new stzCommitScope()
oScope.AllowUnder("/somewhere/else")
oPlan2 = oAg.GenerateUpdatePlan()
oPlan2.SetExecutor(PIActor("shop-committer"))
oPlan2.SetScope(oScope)
oPlan2.Execute()
? StzFileExists($cPrices)
#--> 0
oScope3 = new stzCommitScope()
oScope3.AllowUnder(cFx)
oPlan3 = oAg.GenerateUpdatePlan()
oPlan3.SetExecutor(PIActor("shop-committer"))
oPlan3.SetScope(oScope3)
oPlan3.Execute()
? StzFileExists($cPrices)
#--> 1
? @@(oPlan3.AuditTrail())
#--> [ [ 1, "committed", "write_file", "shop-committer" ] ]
StzDirDeleteAll(cFx)
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

I replaced the absolute path in the narration so the output is stable. A plan embeds the path of the file it would write.

**The court at load.** Is the table above enforced when the file is loaded, before the first tick? Take the same file, make the agent irreversible, and make its code sandboxed:

```haro
cIrrev = StzReplace(StzReplace(cPia, "reversibility: compensable", "reversibility: irreversible"), "posture: trusted", "posture: sandboxed")
oD = StzAgentDeclarationQ(cIrrev)
? oD.IsValid()
#--> 0
? oD.CiteFindings()
#--> [pia-posture-reversibility @ skills.scribble.posture] a 'sandboxed' posture does not cover 'irreversible' work -- the harder an act is to undo, the more trusted the code performing it must be (reversibility x posture, ruling 3.2)
```

> run: kept

The court answers with the table's own sentence: `a 'sandboxed' posture does not cover 'irreversible' work`. The same rule, at load: the file is refused before its first tick!

**A proposal gone wrong.** A tidy-up that deletes every file of a folder, driven here by hand through the twin as an agent would drive it, proposes thirty deletions. The plan lists thirty operations and flags thirty as risks. A language model that asks to commit is refused. And when it calls `Execute()` anyway, how many files are left? Count them:

```haro
cDir = StzReplace(WorkingDirectory(), "\", "/") + "/_course"
StzDirCreatePath(cDir)
for i = 1 to 30
	StzFileWrite(cDir + "/lesson" + i + ".txt", "lesson " + i)
next
oBench = new stzVirtualFileSystem()
oBench.SetActor("tidy-bot")
for i = 1 to 30
	oBench.MirrorFile(cDir + "/lesson" + i + ".txt")
	oBench.DeleteFile(cDir + "/lesson" + i + ".txt")
next
oBig = oBench.GenerateUpdatePlan()
? oBig.NumberOfOperations()
#--> 30
? len(oBig.Risks())
#--> 30
oBig.SetExecutor(LLMActor("helper-llm"))
? oBig.MayCommit()[2]
#--> actor 'helper-llm' cannot commit -- it lacks the 'effectful' capability (required by operation 1)
oBig.Execute()
nLeft = 0
for i = 1 to 30
	nLeft += StzFileExists(cDir + "/lesson" + i + ".txt")
next
? nLeft
#--> 30
```

> run: kept (touches files, the system)

Thirty! The model asked, was refused, called `Execute()` anyway, and nothing on the disk changed.

The site shows this with a larger course, in its education demo of 30 September 2026, which I did not run for this book, and prints a count of six hundred and ten. That figure is the site's, quoted with its source and date. I print thirty, because thirty is what this block runs.

**A person can argue with a plan.** The plan is a medium. A reviewer rejects one step, with a reason. What do the other twenty-nine steps do, and what is left on the disk? The block prints the counts, not the name of the file:

```haro
oRev = oBench.GenerateUpdatePlan()
oRev.RejectOperation(2, "reviewer: keep lesson 2")
oRev.SetExecutor(PIActor("oncall"))
oScopeC = new stzCommitScope()
oScopeC.AllowUnder(cDir)
oRev.SetScope(oScopeC)
aRes = oRev.Execute()
? aRes[1][2]
#--> 29
? aRes[2][2]
#--> 1
nLeft = 0
for i = 1 to 30
	nLeft += StzFileExists(cDir + "/lesson" + i + ".txt")
next
? nLeft
#--> 1
StzDirDeleteAll(cDir)
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

A named actor under a scope commits the other twenty-nine (`29`); one is skipped (`1`); and one file is still on disk (`1`), the file of the step the reviewer protected.

**One rule, two layers, the same words.** The refusal that chapter 17 showed at the graph, and that the file showed above, is also enforced in the engine's own tick loop, at registration. It is enforced in the sentence of the graph rule, no-llm-effectful. A model with the capability to produce effects is refused. A model without it registers like any other slot, a slot being the place in the loop that one agent takes:

```haro
StzResetEngineAgentLoop()
nL = stzenginezutercreate("summarizer")
? stzengineagentloopregister(nL, 1, 1, 0, 1, 0, 10, "summarises tickets")
#--> -6
? stzengineagentlooplastrefusal()
#--> AGENTLOOP-R6: agent 'summarizer' (slot 0) breaks the rule no-llm-effectful -- llm actor holds 'effectful' -- an LLM proposes, only a pi-gate commits. Registration refuses it. Drop the effectful capability, or register it as a pi-gated composition rather than an llm actor.
nL2 = stzenginezutercreate("proposer")
? stzengineagentloopregister(nL2, 1, 1, 0, 0, 0, 10, "proposes summaries")
#--> 0
StzResetEngineAgentLoop()
```

> run: kept

The engine refused the summarizer, in the words of the graph rule! The arguments after the slot are, in the order the guard gives them: the kind (1 is a model), the reversibility class, the trigger and the capability. What remains is the priority, the interval in milliseconds and the coverage sentence. They are the engine's low-level names, and I show them so you can see the refusal come from the engine, in the same words.

**An agent of the estate's own roster, rehearsed.** Until 22 August 2026, an agent of the estate's own roster, the one that rolls the ledger, moved a real journal file directly, on a tick. It had no plan, no scope and no committing actor.

A ledger is a record of what happened, kept in the order it happened. This agent rolls the estate's own ledger. Chapter 21 has the security ledger, which the court writes and never the thing it judged, a hash chain in which an edited line is named. Chapter 23 has the constellation's ledger of crossings, with their timings. A register is something else: a form a reply can take, or Appendix A.

The doctrine had been written before it was true, and the record caught it the same day. Since then the function that writes for that agent has a rehearsing path. Inside the tick of an agent that holds a workbench it writes into the twin. Outside one, in the migration state the live estate still runs, it is still the direct write. The declaration below is my own, written for this book; it is not the live roster file, which I did not run. Given a workbench, the agent relocates the file in the twin. Two skills fire and verify; the real journal file is where it was; no archive exists; the tick has exported a plan. And only the committing actor, under a scope, moves the file:

```haro
cEstate = StzReplace(WorkingDirectory(), "\", "/") + "/_estate"
StzDirCreatePath(cEstate + "/dashboard")
StzDirCreatePath(cEstate + "/journal")
SetEnvVar("STZ_SOFTANZA_ROOT", cEstate)
StzFileWrite(cEstate + "/journal/2000-01-scar.md", "an old journal entry" + char(10))
StzFileWrite(cEstate + "/dashboard/SESSION-LOG.md", "# SESSION-LOG" + char(10) + char(10) + "2000-01-15 09:00 | someone | long ago" + char(10))
StzAllowAgentFunction("RollRelocateMonths", "trusted")
StzAllowAgentFunction("RollWritePointer", "trusted")
cRoll = "pia: 2
name: ledger-roll
kind: pi
coverage: moves journal months before the current one into archive and leaves a pointer
reversibility: reversible
schedule:
  event: roll
governance:
  authority: delegated
  risks:
    relocate-evidence: 2
  grants:
    - relocate-evidence
skills:
  - name: relocate
    when: always
    does: ring:RollRelocateMonths
    verify: fact archive holds month
    effect: relocate-evidence
    posture: trusted
  - name: pointer
    when: fact archive holds month
    does: ring:RollWritePointer
    verify: fact ledger holds pointer
    effect: relocate-evidence
    posture: trusted
"
oRoll = StzAgentDeclarationQ(cRoll).ToAgent()
oRoll.GiveWorkbench()
? oRoll.Cycle()
#--> 2
? StzFileExists(cEstate + "/journal/2000-01-scar.md")
#--> 1
? StzFileExists(cEstate + "/journal/archive/2000-01-scar.md")
#--> 0
oRP = oRoll.GenerateUpdatePlan()
? oRP.NumberOfOperations()
#--> 7
? len(oRP.Risks()) >= 1
#--> 1
oRP.SetExecutor(PIActor("roll-committer"))
oScopeR = new stzCommitScope()
oScopeR.AllowUnder(cEstate)
oRP.SetScope(oScopeR)
oRP.Execute()
? StzFileExists(cEstate + "/journal/2000-01-scar.md")
#--> 0
? StzFileExists(cEstate + "/journal/archive/2000-01-scar.md")
#--> 1
StzDirDeleteAll(cEstate)
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The count of seven operations is what this run measures. The library's own guard only promises at least five.

The blocks have shown what the world does; section 4 says what I refused to build instead.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused safe agents.** The doctrine's reason is that the field's safety tries to make a fundamentally unpredictable actor trustworthy. I hold this reason as a view, not a result. Asking whether the agent might escape is like asking whether a chess engine might move a piece on a physical board. The doctrine says the agent's tool surface contains no operation that touches the disk, a database or the network. That is the doctrine's claim, and section 6 gives its limits.

**I refused a permission check as the boundary.** It is not a check that the agent might route around. The powers are separated: creativity without authority, authority without creativity.

**I refused a second workbench for machines.** The sandbox is the same workbench that the human programmer already uses. The framework was conceived for human programmers, and everything designed to liberate one is exactly what is needed to contain an artificial one. I hold this as a view, not a result.

**I refused to leave the gate optional.** The ruling of 22 August 2026 says the registration gate is doctrine, and that opting in is a migration state, not the design. The record is honest about the finding behind the ruling: where the doctrine of agents that cannot hurt you bound that day was nowhere. The safe world existed, and the agentic folder did not mention any of it. The ruling closed it the same day.

**I refused a stub.** Asking for a reserved agent raises. The architecture says that this is the law working, and not a defect.

**I refused to drop quietly.** In the engine, a send to a full inbox is refused with a code and counted. That is tested in the engine's Zig code, in a test I did not run, and no block here and no narrated guard shows it. When the run queue is full, further visits are dropped and counted. The loop also states what it cannot see: the inside of a callback, the clock, and whether a coverage statement is true.

**I refused to let a machine be the last resort.** The interface constitution of the estate has a right, the sixth of six: the Right to a Human. Wherever a machine serves, a person is reachable, clearly, and without penalty. It is enforced by judgment, a person's, and not by a guard that runs; the library does not mention it. The constitution holds it as the human end of the loop; its row is in Appendix C, read from the rules file.

Section 5 names the guards that prove what the blocks showed.

## 5. The court

`safeworld_narrated` has seven scenes: reversibility as the sixth contract; the registration gate in the engine's sentences; the table of posture against reversibility; the file's court at load. Three more follow: what the file declares landing in the agent's governance; the workbench, from the disk that did not move to the audit trail; and the roster's agent rehearsed and then committed. `agentfile_narrated` proves that an agent is a file judged at load, with one refusal per gate, and that a folder is a deployment. `agentloop_engine_narrated` proves that the engine loop states its coverage at run time, that registration is a gate, that one rule gives the same words in two layers, and that an over-full queue drops visits and counts them. `containment_drill_narrated` proves a drill against a real target on loopback: a model investigator proposes the containment and commits nothing; a human commits, and containment holds when verified from outside. In the run I made for my research note on 8 October 2026, the drill read 443 ms to detect and 61 ms to contain, on this machine. The book's data does not hold that run.
