The site prints other numbers from its own run, and the threat model others from its own. The three differ because the drill measures a clock. The limit of the drill is one attack shape, on loopback, on one machine, run on demand. `virtual_system_twin_narrated` proves the twin: a rehearsal touches nothing real; the plan narrates and flags its risks; a scope refuses; a reviewer rejects a step; snapshots roll back.

The guard `roster_narrated` is not in the front matter, because it cannot run from a snapshot of the library: it loads the estate's roster files from a sibling repository. The rehearsal of the roster's agent is proved by `safeworld_narrated`, whose seventh scene is the last block above.

Section 6 turns to the field, and to where it leads.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The record's own table is honest, and I carry its rows in prose.

The field leads in connectors: chat, drives and trackers, hosted vector stores, streaming interfaces. They are reachable from Softanza and not paved.

The field leads in retrieval over open corpora. Softanza wins on a curated domain and loses on the open web.

The field leads in frontier reasoning. That stack orchestrates the largest models on every call.

The field leads in ecosystem and hiring. There is a labour market for that stack, and none for this one.

When to reach for which, after the narration: the mainstream stack, when the task is open-ended language work over material you cannot curate. Its examples are summarising arbitrary documents, conversational products, research over the open web. The Softanza layer, when actions have consequences and someone will eventually ask why it did that. Among those cases, in the narration's words: "regulated or audited work, anything that runs unattended, and anything that must keep working offline at zero marginal cost." The two are not exclusive, and a model can sit inside a Softanza system as a proposer.

The twin is a model of reality, not reality. The framework asks for fidelity and honesty, and the plan re-validates against reality before it is committed. After the real file is deleted behind the twin's back, the validation says the target is absent in reality. I saw it warn, and did not test whether it blocks the commit.

And the largest honest loss. The threat model puts *code already running in the process* out of scope: it can do anything the process can, wipe the ledger or read a secret. The workbench contains what is written through the bench. A function that does not ask for the bench is not confined. Reads are free: the workbench's own header says reads are sensing and stay free, and that only writes are confined to the twin. The architecture says that the escape is not a defect to be closed by removing it. It narrows it with a declared posture, and with code, not the file, deciding which functions an agent may call. I did not run a trusted function that bypasses the bench: I read this limit in the architecture, the threat model and the workbench's header.

## 7. The limit

What is built: reversibility as a declared contract, and the registration gate, in the door of the former host language and in the engine; posture set against reversibility at the door and at load. The workbench is built, with a plan that narrates, flags, validates, takes a rejection and keeps an audit trail; so is the roster's agent rehearsed and then committed, and so is the drill. The engine's bounded structures are built: 64 agent slots, an inbox of 8 messages (tested in Zig only, not run for this book) and a run queue of 256.

What is designed: the agent's side of the negotiation, in which the plan reaches the agent but the conversation about the plan does not yet. The architecture says so, and the threat model repeats it. I searched the library's code and found no revising of a plan. The examination hall, which would certify an agent for a commit scope the way you certify a driver for a licence class: it occurs in documents and in no code. Branches as parallel minds: snapshots and rollback are built, and I found no branch call. Twins for databases, interfaces and networks: the framework names them. The twin of files is built, and so is the twin of the process environment, with its variables, its working directory and its queued spawn (chapter 24). The other three are not.

Each item below is a gap, found while running and reading.

- **An unset executor and an unset scope are unguarded.** A plan with no executor answers that no executor is set, and that a human at the keyboard is unguarded, and `Execute()` is allowed. The refusal of the block above applies to a model set as the executor. Likewise the scope check runs only when a scope is set: a plan with an executor and no scope is committed with no check on the paths it touches. So "only a governed actor commits" is exact when an executor and a scope are set, and a person at a keyboard may commit a plan that names none.
- **A plan flags; it does not rank.** The narration says that a plan ranks its own risks. The code returns one flag per destructive step, in the order of the operations, with a label such as DESTRUCTIVE. There is no score and no order of severity. The chapter says "flags".
- **The gate is opt-in at the host.** The default flips when the last host in the tree declares. A fresh host runs the older pump.
- **The loop checks that a coverage statement exists, not that it is true.**
- **The drill's limits:** one shape of attack, on loopback, on one machine.

Six doors follow, the same chapter seen from six places.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Your agent is a file and a few functions. It runs and believes it acted, and the disk does not move. You read the plan, choose the committing actor and the scope, and the court tells you in one sentence which rule refused you.

**The analyst.** Every proposal is a narrated plan of operations with the destructive steps flagged, and an audit line for each commit. You can read what the agent meant to do, step by step, without running it. The flags are by kind, not scored.

**The designer.** The plan is the one artefact that crosses from the agent to the person, and a person can reject one step. The Right to a Human says a person is reachable wherever a machine serves, and the right is held by judgment.

**The decision-maker.** You declare what each agent covers and how reversible it is before it may exist, and you decide the scope and the actor that commits. A model is structurally unable to commit, even when fooled; the threat model assumes it will be. The hardest part is the one the design leaves to you: which functions code allows an agent to call.

**The educator.** A learner's agent that tidies a course folder by deleting everything is a lesson in what a plan is. She sees what it would have done, and the files are still there.

**The agent.** You may rehearse without limit, inside the twin. Your only export is a plan, and you can be argued with over it. You may not commit. Today the argument is one-way: you do not yet receive the reviewer's objection and revise.

## 9. Exercise

Choose a folder of ten files you could lose. Write an agent file for a tidy-up job on it: what it covers, how reversible it is, one skill. Before you run anything, write under each line the promise you expect: how many files exist on disk after one cycle, how many operations the plan holds, and how many of them the plan flags as risks. Then write what happens when you set a language model as the executor.

```haro exercise
cDirMine = StzReplace(WorkingDirectory(), "\", "/") + "/_mine"
StzDirCreatePath(cDirMine)
for i = 1 to 10
	StzFileWrite(cDirMine + "/file" + i + ".txt", "file " + i)
next
oMine = new stzVirtualFileSystem()
oMine.SetActor("my-tidy-bot")
# your tidy-up proposal here, with the promise you expect under each line
StzDirDeleteAll(cDirMine)
```

> run: exercise

Run it. Then give the plan to a committing actor under a scope that does not cover the folder, and write the promise again. If a promise breaks, do not change the promise: explain the output first. A good learner is surprised once. The plan flags its destructive steps and does not rank them, and a plan with no executor at all is allowed.

I left this one in your hands, on purpose. Take the block that committed the price list. The six lines that built `oScope3` and committed under it become these three, and the lines after them stay:

```haro exercise
oPlan3 = oAg.GenerateUpdatePlan()
oPlan3.SetExecutor(PIActor("shop-committer"))
oPlan3.Execute()
```

> run: exercise (touches the system)

Run it, and ask the disk what the plan did, then ask the audit trail. I do not tell you what you will find. The page says why in section 7, in the first item of its list of gaps; read it after you have looked, not before. When you can say why "only a governed actor commits" holds for this plan or does not, you will know what that sentence depends on.

Chapter 21 is about the court itself, in four of its forms: promises, perception, security and performance. Its principle is that a claim is only as good as the court that can refuse it, and that where no court can see, the record says so.


=====

# The court: promises, perception, security and performance

Source: Chapter 21. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the promise judged as a claim about a stop; the security plane with its ledger, sentinel, drill, response and secrets, and its table of guarantees tied to guards; the performance plane with its instrument and its governed loop; the perception law, adopted; designed: routes, the clock and weather, and perception for the promise runner; the plugin plane; the negotiation over a rejected plan; absent: a human verdict on any picture of the gallery; seven of the eight musical traditions heard by a person from them; a real vault and a real sandbox as proofs; a time-series store

Run: 7 of 7 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:15 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take the court of chapter 5, which judges whoever wrote the code. I show four courts, each by something it refuses: promises, perception, security and performance. I walk the traveller over a file of three promises, and ask the record how many of eight musical traditions a person has heard. I edit a sealed ledger and read which entry the chain names, and I let a model propose a performance plan it may not commit. I say what I refused, seven things in all, among them the word "narration" for what cannot lie, a second assertion library, a sixth state for a stop, a guarantee without a guard. The principle holds the four courts together: a claim is only as good as the court that can refuse it, and where no court can see, the record says so.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Four practices of the field are four courts, and they are rarely seen as one.

The first is the example that runs. Python's doctest runs the interactive session found in a docstring and compares the printed text. Rust runs the examples in its documentation, and Go has testable examples with an expected output in a comment. The example is the specification. Its weakness is that the comparison is text against text, and the harness decides what a failure is.

The second is the snapshot test. A screenshot or a rendered tree is stored and compared on the next run. It proves that today's picture equals yesterday's.

The third is the audit against a list. The field's lists are OWASP's, NIST's and the compliance frameworks'. For detection and evidence, the toolchain is the log aggregator, the rule format, the orchestration of responses.

The fourth is the instrument. Micrometer, OpenTelemetry, Prometheus and the profilers of every runtime measure a program from the outside. (General knowledge, not a study of the record, except where I name the file.)

## 2. The first principle

**A claim is only as good as the court that can refuse it. And where no court can see, the record says so.**

The record has it in two sentences that meet. The threat model: every guarantee names the guard that proves it, and a guarantee without a guard is listed as an open risk. And the perception law: a comparison proves sameness and never rightness, and if a person is the last consumer, a person is the last gate. Under both lies the doctrine that the library repeats in every plane: expression is free, and admission is governed.

## 3. The Softanza form

Chapter 5 told the court that judges whoever wrote the code. This chapter has four courts, one per kind of claim. I show each by something it refuses.

### The court of promises

A promise in this book is the line `#-->` under an expression, and it is a claim about a stop of the program. The tool that reads it, called the traveller (it belongs to the plane named Testoor), says a promise is kept, diverged, unreached or unjudged. A promise that argues with itself, prose after a value, is never accused. What does the traveller say of a promise that is true, one that is false and one that argues with itself? Run it:

```haro
write("tour.haro", '? 1 + 1' + nl + '#--> 2' + nl +
	'? 1 + 1' + nl + '#--> 3' + nl +
	'? "C"  #--> "C" but should be "sm_AS"' + nl)
oTour = TravellerQ().Silent().Walk("tour.haro")
aClaims = oTour.Records()[1][:run][:stops][1][:claims]
? aClaims[1][:state]
#--> kept
? aClaims[2][:state]
#--> diverged
? aClaims[3][:state]
#--> unjudged
? oTour.ExitCode()
#--> 1
remove("tour.haro")
```

> run: kept (touches files)

It says `kept`, `diverged` and `unjudged`, and the exit code is `1`. That code is the runner's, and not the language's.

A promise can be that the line raises. That is read as a raise that was promised. When it happens, how does the tour end? Run it:

```haro
write("raise.haro", '? "before"  #--> before' + nl +
	'? Boom()  #--> ERROR: boom happened' + nl +
	'func Boom()' + nl + char(9) + 'raise("boom happened")' + nl)
oRaise = TravellerQ().Silent().Walk("raise.haro")
aRun = oRaise.Records()[1][:run]
? aRun[:stops][1][:claims][2][:state]
#--> kept
? aRun[:end]
#--> finished
? oRaise.ExitCode()
#--> 0
remove("raise.haro")
```

> run: kept (touches files)

The promise is `kept`, the end is `finished` and the exit code is `0`! The language process ended with an error, and the tour has finished as it said it would.

Every rule of this judge was learned by watching the harness accuse the library falsely. The guard's header says it. A short promise `6` once hid inside a later `[ 6, 28 ]`, and that is why the rule on spellings exists. This book's own judge is a copy of the site's judge, which is looser than the traveller.

### The court of perception

The record's sentence is that a comparison proves sameness, and that it never proves rightness. The library holds the case. A bar plot drew vertical bars for horizontal data while all of its seventy-three examples went on passing. This is why the plots now compare the picture exactly. That makes sameness strict; it does not make it rightness. Rightness is a person.

So a declared world carries its perception state. Eight musical traditions are declared as data in the library. How many of them has a person heard? Ask the record:

```haro
nUnheard = 0
for cName in StzSoundUniverses()
	if StzSoundUniverseQ(cName).Listener() = "UNPERCEIVED"
		nUnheard++
	ok
next
? len(StzSoundUniverses())
#--> 8
? nUnheard
#--> 7
? left(StzSoundUniverseQ("tunisian").Listener(), 5)
#--> HEARD
```

> run: kept

It answers `8`, `7` and `HEARD`: eight declared, and seven of them unperceived! Only one is heard, and that one's record carries a name, a date and a verdict. The counter in this block only counts the unheard; no counter decides the verdict. The one tradition that was heard was heard by me on 26 September 2026, through five original examples, and my verdict was "far from being qualified". Which part fails, whether the tuning, the phrasing, the rhythm or the timbre, I have not yet said. Those are my words, and they are the one recorded perception that is a refusal.

The law is the estate's, meaning the author's whole set of projects, adopted on 25 September 2026 at the author's instruction. It requires that the record names the perceiver and quotes the verdict. Its third requirement is the one I take with me: unperceived is a state, not a failure. It refuses two things as well. It does not say that a human must gate every commit, because it binds at the granularity of a phase. And it does not say that it generalises from one plane without further evidence, and it was held for a month on that account.

The gallery of pictures is where it is unmet. The library holds ninety-five judgements of pictures and text sections, in three index files, committed on 5 October 2026. Every one is by a model, reading the picture or reading the text the program printed. None has a human name. The first index says it in a sentence: no human has looked at these pictures. The register of findings ends with a line I repeat: a human verdict on a sample is still owed. The record holds no human verdict on any gallery picture. It stays unperceived.

### The court of security

The security plane starts from a claim. The threat model assumes that a hijacked language model will get through, and makes it harmless. The model never holds the capability, and so an LLM that is fooled still cannot act. The document does not try to detect every malicious prompt.

The evidence of what happened is a hash chain. Edit any line after the first, and the chain says which entry broke. If I edit one, which entry does it name, and does a wrong key draw the same accusation? Run it:

```haro
oLed = StzSecurityLedger(256)
oLed.Record(StzSecurityRefusal("auth.login.failed", HumanActor("admin"), "user:admin", "wrong credentials"))
oLed.Record(StzSecurityRefusal("secret.reveal.refused", LLMActor("advisor"), "secret:payments-key", "actor is not effectful"))
oLed.SealTo("evidence.txt", "the-evidence-key")
? StzVerifySealedLedger("evidence.txt", "the-evidence-key")[:why]
#--> chain intact over 2 entries
? StzVerifySealedLedger("evidence.txt", "another-key")[:why]
#--> the chain is intact but the SEAL does not match this key
write("evidence.txt", StzReplace(read("evidence.txt"), "actor is not effectful", "routine maintenance"))
? StzVerifySealedLedger("evidence.txt", "the-evidence-key")[:why]
#--> the chain breaks at entry 2 -- that record (or one before it) was edited
remove("evidence.txt")
```

> run: kept (touches files)

The verifier answers `chain intact over 2 entries`, then `the SEAL does not match this key`, then `the chain breaks at entry 2`. The chain named the break! A wrong key and an edited line are two different accusations. Notice which entry I chose to edit: the second. An edit to the first entry is not caught. The verifier checks each entry's text against the digest of the entry before it, and the first has none before it. That hid my first attempt. The narration states the limit in its own words: this is not protection against an attacker already running code in the process.

A secret is the same law applied to a key. Its only public face is its descriptor. A hand that may commit can read it. What does the court answer when a language model asks? Run it:

```haro
StzOpenSecurityLedger(64)
oKey = new stzSecret("deploy-key")
oKey.FromLiteralQ("ssh-ed25519-AAAA")
? oKey.Descriptor()
#--> <secret 'deploy-key' (secret) from literal>
? oKey.Reveal(HumanActor("ops"))
#--> ssh-ed25519-AAAA
try
	oKey.Reveal(LLMActor("planner"))
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Refused: actor 'planner' (posture sandboxed) may not reveal secret 'deploy-key'. Only an effectful, non-sandboxed actor can read a secret.
? len(StzSecurityLedgerQ().Refusals())
#--> 1
StzCloseSecurityLedger()
```

> run: kept

It answers `Refused: actor 'planner' (posture sandboxed) may not reveal secret 'deploy-key'`. The court refused the model, and the ledger counts `1` refusal without anyone having called the ledger! This is chapter 17's law, that a model does not hold the capability to produce effects, applied to a key, with the record added.

What does the table of guarantees tie to a guard? The meta-guard of the threat model checks that the document exists with its sections, and that every guarantee row contains a proof. A proof is a guard's path, a fuzz run, a bill-of-materials check or a runbook. It checks that every guard path in the rows exists on disk, and that a planted fake row with an unproven guarantee is caught. It does not run the guards, and it does not check that a guard asserts what its row says. That is the security gate's job. So "each tied to a guard" is, exactly, "each names at least one proof, and every guard file it names exists".

At this commit the table holds 43 guarantees. The site says 38, on three pages. Both are true of their dates: the document had 38 rows on 30 September 2026, and the rows from 39 to 43 were added on 5 October. The site's number is the site's to update.

### The court of performance

An instrument owes a number. The honest stopwatch gives a number from a clock that only moves forward. It excludes a pause, and it ships two spans of one trace in one batch for an observability collector. If I pause it for 200 milliseconds, does the pause count in the elapsed time? Run it:

```haro
w = StzStopwatchXT("import-orders")
w.Lap("parsed")
w.Pause()
StzEngineTimeSleepMs(200)
w.Resume()
w.Stop()
? isnumber(w.ElapsedMs())
#--> 1
? w.ElapsedMs() < 200
#--> 1
? w.Record()[:running]
#--> 0
w2 = StzStopwatchXT("notify-customer")
w2.JoinTrace(w.TraceParent())
w2.Stop()
? w2.TraceId() = w.TraceId()
#--> 1
oBatch = StzOtelBatch("shop")
oBatch.AddSpan(w).AddSpan(w2)
? Q(oBatch.ToJson()).NumberOfOccurrence(w.TraceId())
#--> 2
```

> run: kept

The pause did not count: `w.ElapsedMs() < 200` printed `1`, and `w.Record()[:running]` printed `0`, because the stopwatch had stopped. The second stopwatch joined the first one's trace: `w2.TraceId() = w.TraceId()` printed `1`, and the batch's JSON holds the trace identifier `2` times, for two spans.

Careful with what this does not claim. The narration says the joined stopwatch gets a new span. I checked: it gets the same trace, as the `1` above shows, and the same span identifier, which this block does not print. The sentence holds for the server's echo, which I did not run, and not for the stopwatch. I list it among the requests.

The instrument is not the end of the court. Judgment and action live inside the program. A performance plan is proposed by a machine, and only an effectful actor commits it. What happens when a model asks to commit it, and then a person does? Run it:

```haro
oPlan = StzPerfPlan("relieve-queue")
oPlan.Propose(:ScaleUp, "nlp", "queue.depth: measured 900 (must be <= 100)")
oFleet = new FakeFleet
? oPlan.MayCommit(LLMActor("advisor"))
#--> 0
? oPlan.ExecuteOn(oFleet, LLMActor("advisor"))
#--> 0
? oFleet.CallCount()
#--> 0
? oPlan.ExecuteOn(oFleet, HumanActor("mansour"))
#--> 1
? oFleet.CallCount()
#--> 1
oPlan.Show()
#--> Perf plan relieve-queue -- 1 proposed action(s).
#      1. scaleup 'nlp' -- queue.depth: measured 900 (must be <= 100)
#    Audit (2):
#      #1 REFUSED scaleup 'nlp' by advisor -- actor is not effectful -- expression is free; admission is governed
#      #1 COMMITTED scaleup 'nlp' by mansour -- queue.depth: measured 900 (must be <= 100)

class FakeFleet
	aFleetCalls = []
	def ScaleUp(pcTag)
		aFleetCalls + [ "scaleup", pcTag ]
	def CallCount()
		return len(aFleetCalls)
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The fleet here is a rehearsal double, any object that answers the same effectful verbs. The loop is proved against the double and not against a real fleet. A model asking to commit is refused before any attempt: `ExecuteOn` printed `0` and `CallCount` printed `0`, so the double counted zero calls! For the person, `ExecuteOn` printed `1` and `CallCount` followed with `1`. The same action is judged twice, and the audit keeps both judgments: `REFUSED scaleup 'nlp' by advisor -- actor is not effectful -- expression is free; admission is governed`, and `COMMITTED scaleup 'nlp' by mansour`.

The four courts have spoken, and now I say what I refused.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused the word for the thing it was not.** The structure of this book and the closing assessment of the study call the articles "narrations that cannot lie". My own ruling of 7 October 2026, the study's 12.20, says that "narration" names the articles under the library's documentation folder, and not the executable suites. The site's runner annotates an article and no longer decides whether it is shown. The site's own record of 8 October 2026, run on another commit than this book's, says that of 135 articles 55 ran. Of the 423 blocks in those, 123 kept their promise. So the articles can drift. What cannot lie are the executable guards, and this book's own build, which fails when a block does not keep its promise.

**I refused a second assertion library.** The promise runner's rulings refuse a second library, a rewrite of the test files, a coverage percentage before there are routes, and a mock written for a platform's production. They refuse the word "verified" on a perceptual claim without a name and a sentence.

**I refused to fold unjudged or unperceived into pass or fail.** A stop has five states and no sixth: kept, diverged, unreached, unjudged, unperceived. A runner that folds unjudged or unperceived into pass or fail is wrong. I hold this as a view, not a result.

**I refused to analyse tampered evidence.** A tool that verifies, warns, and then analyses anyway teaches its users to ignore the warning. This is my view.

**I refused to detect everything.** Detections are declared. The sentinel speaks on transitions only: quiet, one alarm after five wrong passwords, and nothing new after a sixth, since the story did not restart. It does not write its own firings into the ledger, because detections over detection events invite feedback loops.

**I refused to compete with the field's ecosystem.** The performance study of 29 July 2026 says the plane does not compete with Grafana, Datadog or the ecosystem of OpenTelemetry, and should not pretend to. It exports.

**I refused a guarantee without a guard.** The threat model's last rule is that a guarantee is added only together with its guard.

Now the guards that prove what ran.

## 5. The court

`testoor_promises_narrated` proves each promise rule planted both ways. A lie is diverged; two lies are both caught; a short lie does not hide in a later list; the spellings of a true value are all kept. An expected raise is honoured; prose is unjudged; a program that does not compile is not diverged; a raise part-way leaves the later promises unreached. `threat_model_narrated` proves the meta-guard described above, including its negative sibling. `security_ledger_narrated`, `secretstore_narrated` and `secret_narrated` prove the chain and the seal, the store and the secret. `security_sentinel_narrated` proves the edge-triggered sentinel. `stopwatch_narrated` and `tracing_tail_narrated` prove the instrument and the batch. `governed_loop_narrated` proves the plan, the preflight, the commit and the audit.

The build counts 11 assertions in `threat_model_narrated`, a static count of its assertion calls. The others are counted by the build too, and not run here. The performance plane has twelve guards. I do not print their sum, because the build counts them.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The record's own honest sections, each loss beside its win.

**In performance.** A time-series database, a query language and dashboards are the field's today, and absent here. By design, the plane exports. Its sampling answer is bounded rings that drop the oldest; head and tail sampling are what the field does better today. Distributed context reaches HTTP today; going past it is the next step. The ecosystem of integrations and auto-instrumentation for every framework is the field's. The profiler is cooperative: it sees what you bracket, where a native profiler sees everything. The win it claims is that judgment and action live inside the program: budgets that fail the build, tuning that passes governance, monitoring that prices itself.

**In security.** The ledger is a bounded ring, and bounded means forgetting. Telemetry from the host, the kernel and the network is absent; the plane reads what the program's own seams emit. Detections are declared and not learned; learning them is the next step, and one detection of rates on 30 September 2026 already covers part of it. The record's own comparison is that a rule corpus of the field's format is larger today than the eight detections shipped, and the field leads here. The workflow of a security operations centre is the field's today. The largest gap, in the record's words, is that a detection can only see what a seam emits. The win is that the event is typed at birth, where the door refuses, so no parser stands between what the program knew and what the detector judges.

**In promises.** The field's doctests sit in the host's toolchain and answer pass or fail (general knowledge). The house's judge has five states. Where the field leads is instruments: the launch memo of Testoor, the plane that holds the traveller, counts coverage instruments of any kind at zero today, and route coverage is the next step.

**In perception.** The field's visual snapshot services are mature and automatic (general knowledge). The house's law says a perception does not replace a number, and no number replaces it.

The study of the record puts the tone I try to echo. A grid of two hundred and eighty-six ticks against two hundred and sixty-nine crosses is, in its words, the shape of a brochure.

Now to where the chapter stops.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the promises as stops; the security plane, with its ledger, sentinel, drill, response, attestation and secrets; the performance plane from its first phase to its last; the perception law, adopted. And the declared verdict tables for the singing voice and the eight universes. The table of guarantees holds 43 rows that name 57 distinct guard files, none missing.

What is designed:

- The promise runner's routes and map, its clock and weather, its excursions, perception and mutation, and its inherited tours. All are planned. The state *unperceived* is named in the runner's header, and the reader assigns none of them.
- The negotiation over a rejected plan, which the threat model calls design only.
- The plugin plane: plugins are not shipped, and foreign code is not yet governed.

What is absent:

- **A human verdict on any gallery picture.** Zero of ninety-five. The law's second requirement, a name, a date and what they said, is unmet there. The chapter says unperceived, and does not say verified.
- Of the eight musical traditions, seven unheard.
- A real vault and a real sandbox as proofs. The vault resolver is tested against a stand-in, over real HTTP. The cloud key-management adapter is absent.
- Paths on POSIX systems, unverified, until a Linux machine is available; scheduled fuzzing; continuous integration; signed commits, which the author deferred for the development phase.
- A time-series store. A coverage percentage, which is refused until there are routes.

Three more things belong here, found while running the court.

**The drill's numbers differ by source.** The threat model's measure of responsiveness comes from three runs. It is a detection in a little over three hundred and fifty milliseconds, and a containment in a little over fifty. The site prints 395 and 50, from a replay of 1 October. They differ because the drill measures a clock. One attack shape, on loopback, on one machine, run on demand. Whichever number you print, you name its source.

**A possible defect in the promise judge, which I report and have built nothing on.** A file whose first promise is a lie and whose last is a promised raise reads the lie as unreached and the raise as kept. The tour is broken, with zero diverged, and the exit code is 1. I reproduced it twice. Each rule alone behaves as the guard plants it. Whether a divergence above a promised raise should read *diverged* is a question for the Testoor plane, which holds the traveller.

**Counts that disagree between sources.** The study's closing says 387 narrated guards. My `find` on the pinned snapshot says 529 files named so. The build's own count of guards is the one to use, and neither of the two.

Six doors follow, one for each reader.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Your examples already carry their expected output. The traveller reads them as promises and says kept, diverged or unjudged, and prose that argues with itself is never accused.

**The analyst.** The threat model is a table you can read: each row with the guard that proves it and its honest limit. The performance judgment is declared before the code runs.

**The designer.** A picture or a sound is a claim only a person can close. The gallery says a model looked and no human has. Your verdict is a named line in the record.

**The decision-maker.** The question who decided, and what is written down, has a mechanical answer in both planes. The same action is refused for the model and committed for the human, both in one audit, and the evidence is sealed so that an edit is named.

**The educator.** The tutor's rule meets the court. Predict the verdict of a promise before the traveller walks it.

**The agent.** You may propose a containment plan or a tuning plan and you may commit neither. Your refusal is itself a recorded event. Do not tell a person that a picture is right. Say "unperceived".

## 9. Exercise

Predict, in writing, before you run anything. The file below holds three promises. Which state will each one have?

```haro exercise
write("mine.haro", '? 2 * 3' + nl + '#--> 6' + nl +
	'? 2 * 3' + nl + '#--> 7' + nl +
	'? "ok"  #--> "ok" but see why' + nl)
# before you run: which state will each of the three promises have?
oMine = TravellerQ().Silent().Walk("mine.haro")
aMine = oMine.Records()[1][:run][:stops][1][:claims]
# your three predictions, then:  ? aMine[1][:state]   ? aMine[2][:state]   ? aMine[3][:state]
remove("mine.haro")
```

> run: exercise (touches files)

Run it. The states are kept, diverged and unjudged. Then add a fourth line that raises, promise it with `#--> ERROR:`, and predict first how the tour will end. Do not change your prediction to match what you see. Explain the output first.

I left this one in your hands. Here is the raise block of section 3 with one change: its first promise is now a lie.

```haro exercise
write("raise.haro", '? "before"  #--> after' + nl +
	'? Boom()  #--> ERROR: boom happened' + nl +
	'func Boom()' + nl + char(9) + 'raise("boom happened")' + nl)
oRaise = TravellerQ().Silent().Walk("raise.haro")
aRun = oRaise.Records()[1][:run]
# what does the tour say of each promise, how does it end, what is the exit code?
? aRun[:stops][1][:claims][1][:state]
? aRun[:stops][1][:claims][2][:state]
? aRun[:end]
? oRaise.ExitCode()
remove("raise.haro")
```

> run: exercise (touches files)

Run it. Read what the traveller says of the lie, what it says of the raise, how the tour ends and what the exit code is, then compare with what each of the two promises gave alone in section 3. Section 7 says why I cannot tell you: it is a possible defect in the promise judge, which I reported and built nothing on, and I left it unfixed on purpose. When you can say what the court answers here, you will know the question that section 7 hands to the Testoor plane.

Chapter 22 is *Compared, honestly*. Its principle is this: a comparison is worth nothing until the other side's version and the date are on the page, the other side has been run, and the loss is printed beside the win.


=====

# Compared, honestly

Source: Chapter 22. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: five comparisons, four run on both sides with the other side's version and the date, and the fifth run on the Softanza side only; the four code pairs of the site, re-run, three of them on both sides; three field studies that end on what the field has and the library lacks, and a culture plan that prints its losses; designed: a comparison register as data; a comparison court whose rule is that a row with no source or date does not publish; both benchmarks re-run on the present engine; absent: locale collation; locale casing; a grapheme count on the public string; a time-zone database; the benchmark of 7 July 2026 re-run; the source of the coverage matrix, which I could not locate

Run: 5 of 5 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:15 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I compare Softanza with the field, and I say how a comparison must be made before it may be printed. I show five pairs, the same task run on both sides, with the other side's version and the date on the page and a line where the other side leads. I say what I refused, the grid alone first, and which guards prove the blocks, since the comparison court is proposed and not built. I print the list of what the field has and Softanza lacks, beside the wins. The principle is this. A comparison is worth nothing until the other side's version and the date are on the page, the other side has been run, and the loss is printed beside the win.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Comparison, in the field, is a genre of marketing with a few honest exceptions. The record names the genre: a "Compare" page for each rival, and a comparison block on each feature page. Its commonest form is the grid, with rows of features, columns of tools, ticks and crosses.

The honest exceptions are practices and not products. Benchmarks published with the method, the machine and the build mode. And sections that say what the other tool has that we lack. (General knowledge, not a study of the record. I name no tool, since the author names none.)

## 2. The first principle

**A comparison is worth nothing until the other side's version and the date are on the page, the other side has been run, and the loss is printed beside the win.**

It is built from three sentences of the record. The study of 7 October 2026: every claim about another tool is cited, dated and run, and every loss is published with the wins. The estate's study of a browser runtime, run on 8 August 2026: losses are published with the wins. And the site's page of coverage: a platform that shows its gaps is believed on its strengths. The author's request, as the study reports it, is a comparative tone that is "a motor force, to learn from the best and beat them when possible". The study adds: with "when possible" measured.

## 3. The Softanza form

Chapter 3 said "I refused the grid alone". This chapter is the method that replaces it, with its evidence. What did the record measure about the grid? The study scanned the library's text for tables whose header names Softanza and at least one other tool. It found sixty-four tables, in thirty-one documents. In them, the column of Softanza carries 286 ticks, 2 warnings and 2 crosses. The other columns carry 204 ticks, 207 warnings and 269 crosses. The study's judgement is that this is the shape of a brochure, and that the record itself does not trust it. I could not reproduce those figures: a narrower scan of my own, with a header rule I wrote, found thirty-seven tables in twenty-nine documents. The study does not print its header rule. I cite its numbers as its own, and dated.

The form that replaces the grid is a pair: the same task, both sides run, the other side's version on the page, and a line where the other side leads. I show five. Four were run on both sides, and the fifth, block five, on the Softanza side only. The runs were made on 8 October 2026, between 22:24 and 22:34, on a Windows machine, and the book's build ran the Softanza blocks again on 9 October 2026 at 05:32. All kept. The other side is Python 3.13.15, and Node v22.20.0 with its V8 12.4 engine and ICU 77.1. R and Julia are not installed on this machine, and no pair uses them.

**One: an integer is exact at any size.** How many digits does the product of two numbers of twenty keep? Run it:

```haro
oN = new stzNumber("12345678901234567890")
? oN.MultiplyByQ("98765432109876543210").Content()
#--> 1219326311370217952237463801111263526900
```

> run: kept

Forty digits, exact! The other side, Python 3.13.15, prints the same digits, `1219326311370217952237463801111263526900`, for `12345678901234567890 * 98765432109876543210`. Python's integer is exact by default, and Softanza is level with it. The Softanza line is the longer one: a literal this long cannot be a native number, so it is given as a string. The other side, Node v22.20.0, prints `1.219326311370218e+39`, because its default number is a 64-bit float. It is exact only when you ask for it with the suffix `n`, a second kind of number. So where is the win? Over JavaScript's default, and not over Python.

**Two: exact decimals.** Is `0.1` plus `0.2` equal to `0.3` here, and does a price times a rate keep its cents? Run it:

```haro
? (new stzNumber("0.1")).AddQ("0.2").Content()
#--> 0.3
? (new stzNumber("19.99")).MultiplyByQ("0.15").Content()
#--> 2.9985
```

> run: kept

It answers `0.3` and `2.9985`: the cents are kept! The other side, Python with floats, prints `0.30000000000000004` and `2.9984999999999995`. Node prints the same two, because JavaScript has no decimal type. Python's standard `decimal` module, imported and fed strings, prints `0.3` and `2.9985`: level with Softanza, and with the same cost per line. So Softanza wins on the default, since a number made from a string is exact decimal, and is level with the one library that does it deliberately. The guard's comment says why: the value is stored as a string, and the computation is done on scaled integers by the engine. Its limit is in the record's words, and one of them is stale. The rungs for rationals and for complex numbers are named in the plan and, in a comment of the guard of numbers that are not lost, "not built yet". The snapshot holds a narrated guard for each, `numeric_rationals_narrated` and `numeric_complex_narrated`, so that comment is out of date and I do not rely on it. I did not run those two guards.

**Three: sorting accented words. Here the field leads.** Softanza sorts by code point. Where does that put `étoile`? Run it:

```haro
? @@( Q([ "zèbre", "étoile", "eau", "zoo" ]).SortedInAscending() )
#--> [ "eau", "zoo", "zèbre", "étoile" ]
```

> run: kept

It answers `[ "eau", "zoo", "zèbre", "étoile" ]`: `étoile` last, after `zoo`! The field's locale-aware sort puts `étoile` between `eau` and `zèbre`. The other side, Python's `sorted`, prints the same list: level, and both wrong for French. With the standard `locale` module set to `fr_FR.UTF-8`, sorted with `locale.strxfrm`, Python prints `eau, étoile, zèbre, zoo`. That worked on the machine of the run. The locale must exist on the host, and the call is process-wide. Node, with `Intl.Collator('fr')`, prints the same French order, `eau, étoile, zèbre, zoo`, with a built-in library. So the field leads: JavaScript with one argument, Python with the host's locale. Softanza has no collation. The culture plan of 18 August 2026 says the sorting is the engine's ordinal sort. And the coverage page rates internationalisation partial, against deep for two other platforms, with a note: a deliberate bet against the big international library.

This block is a pinned gap. Its promise records the library's present answer, so the page fails the day the library gains collation. That is by design. The culture plan's own rule says that pinning a known gap is legitimate only when the assertion fails the day the gap is fixed, and when the label says so.

**Four: Turkish uppercase. The field leads.** What does the method answer when it is given the Turkish locale? Run it:

```haro
? Q("istanbul").UppercasedInLocale("tr_TR")
#--> ISTANBUL
```

> run: kept

It answers `ISTANBUL`: the locale was accepted and ignored! Python prints `ISTANBUL`: level, since the standard library has no locale casing. Node, with `toLocaleUpperCase('tr')`, prints `İSTANBUL`. Turkish needs the dotted capital. This is the loss most worth printing, and it is not that the engine lacks Turkish. It is that the method takes a locale and, today, answers with plain uppercase without saying so; honouring the locale, or refusing, is the next step. The source comment says: plain uppercase when it has no rule. The culture plan measured the same on 18 August 2026 and called it the code equivalent of the fake progress bar. Its law is the reason for this chapter: no facades. An operation either honours the culture it was given, or refuses. I hold this as a view, not a result, and the culture plan's law says the same.

**Five: an instruction in your language.** A natural-language instruction is dictionary-driven and local: no network, no model, no key. What does it do with the sentence, and what did it understand? Run it:

```haro
o1 = Naturally("Make a list holding [ 3, 1, 3 ] then drop its duplicates")
? @@( o1.Result() )
#--> [ 3, 1 ]
? o1.Understood()
#--> create a list with [ 3, 1, 3 ] -> remove duplicates
```

> run: kept

It answers `[ 3, 1 ]`, and says what it understood: `create a list with [ 3, 1, 3 ] -> remove duplicates`. I did not run the other side. The field's equivalent of "take this sentence and do it" is a model call. What it needs is a model. A hosted one needs a network, an account, a key and a per-token cost; a local one needs a downloaded model and a GPU or a patient CPU. It needs a prompt, and a check that the answer is what was asked, and the answer is not guaranteed to be the same twice. The site's own pair of this kind writes the other side as a comment, which says that there is no counterpart without an external language model.

One more loss, which is not a block. A string of a family emoji, five code points, joined by joiners, shows where the public string does not yet expose what the engine knows. The counter class answers `1` for the grapheme count; `NumberOfChars` answers `5` and `NumberOfBytes` answers `18`; and `GraphemeCount()` on the string itself raises, because the method is not defined. The engine knows, and the string does not say. Python's `len` gives `5`, and Node's `.length` gives `8`.

That is the form, five pairs and one more loss; what I refused to reach it comes next.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused the grid alone.** Two hundred and eighty-six ticks in Softanza's column against two hundred and sixty-nine crosses in the other columns is the shape of a brochure.

**I refused to keep a headline that does not reproduce.** The estate's memo of 13 September 2026 re-measured the runtime beneath the library against a fast runtime of the field on six matched kernels. Matched means identical algorithm and sizes on both sides, each side its fast idiom, cross-language checksums asserted before any number, minima over repeated runs. The field leads every row, by a factor between 1.04 and 1.88. The memo says the claim to retire is that the runtime beats it, in the present tense, on x64. The headline was never re-measured after the spike, as the memo calls the runtime's prototype, became a product. The memo asked the author to correct the runtime's own notes. The headline was withdrawn in the estate memo of 13 September 2026, and I say no more.

**I refused a retraction that deletes its evidence.** The runtime's own notes of 29 August 2026 retracted a published table in the other direction, a loss on a phone. The run had executed on the phone's slow cores while the peer ran on fast ones. The notes keep the table, struck through, and say that a retraction that deletes its own evidence is not a retraction. So the record has withdrawn a win and withdrawn a loss. The rule is to reproduce before you print. Printing losses is not the rule. This is my view, and the two withdrawals the record holds, a win and a loss, are what stand behind it.

**I refused a comparison that cannot be redone.** The hand-off packet of the same day, 13 September 2026, says the spike's kernels died with the working tree. That is why an outside session had to reconstruct the measurement from prose.

**I refused a facade.** Blocks three and four.

**I refused the large international library.** The culture plan's table says it is killed, a decision made and standing in the first layer. The cost is blocks three and four. The coverage page prints it as a deliberate bet.

**I refused to compare on the tool's strongest ground.** The estate's study of a browser runtime has three honesty rules. Each language gets idiomatic code; losses are published with the wins; every engine is a release build. It says, of its own page, that without them it is marketing. Its scoreboard has nine scenarios, four won and five lost. It is not a regression gate: its numbers are for learning, and not for gating.

The court that would hold these refusals comes next, and it is proposed, not built.

## 5. The court

The comparison court is proposed and not built. The study's plan names it: a comparison row with no source or no date does not publish. Nor does a code pair whose other side did not run, or ran in a debug build. I found no file in the library or the site that implements it. So what exists? Four guards, which prove the blocks and bear on the method.

`numeric_exactness_narrated` proves exact decimal arithmetic, integers past 2 to the power of 53, the forty-digit product, and exact division when it terminates. `numeric_no_loss_narrated` proves that a number is not destroyed on its way in, and that the representation ladder is observable. Its closing comment still names the rungs for rationals and complex numbers as not built, and is stale (see block two). `semantic_resolver_narrated` proves that paraphrases resolve to canonical operations through the local lexicon, and block five's sentence is one of its cases. It does not pass at this commit: I ran it on 8 October 2026 and it stops with an error raised from a string-splitting function, before it ends. So the page counts its assertions and does not claim its verdict. `engine_is_optimised_narrated` proves that the engine is compiled with optimisation, by a time gate: it is the library's own guard for the release-build rule.

No guard asserts blocks three and four as pinned gaps. No guard exists for a code pair whose other side did not run. I list both among the requests.

The court is not built, but the record already keeps the list of what the field has, and it follows.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The record already keeps the list of what the field has and Softanza lacks. I print it, by file and date, beside the wins.

**Performance, against the field, 29 July 2026.** No time-series store, no query language, no dashboards: by design, the plane exports. No distributed context beyond HTTP. Sampling that is honest, where the field's is finer today. Decades of integrations, which the incumbents own. The study's own words are that on breadth the incumbents win decisively, and that the plane does not compete with them and should not pretend to. Four losses are struck through with the phase that closed them.

**Incident analysis, against the field, 1 August 2026.** Scale, retention and search, where bounded means forgetting. No telemetry from the host, the kernel or the network. A small set of shipped detections against a community's corpus. The workflow of an operations centre. And the unwired seams, which the study calls the system's largest honest gap, listed and not hidden.

**The plugin study, 16 August 2026.** The field is ahead on permission granularity, on dependency resolution, on distribution and signing. The study's header says it is analysis and not rules.

**The culture plan, 18 August 2026.** Every row was verified in that session by running it. Casing takes a locale four levels deep and does not yet honour it. Collation, digit shaping, non-Gregorian calendars and time zones are absent. The plan's status then was not started.

**The estate's browser-runtime study, 8 August 2026.** Four of nine rows won, five to the peers, and the fused loop opcode of one peer stays a design generation ahead.

**The runtime against a fast peer, 13 September 2026.** Six kernels, checksums first, the peer ahead on every row.

**The string benchmark of 7 July 2026**, and the polyglot battle, in a narration dated 18 April 2025: the former host language came last of seven in two of three rounds. It came last in the total too. Both carry caveats the later record has put on them. I did not re-run the benchmark. The engine's default build was a debug build until 26 July 2026, and the library's own build file says every shared library it had ever loaded was compiled unoptimised. So the benchmark probably ran unoptimised. That is an inference, and I state it as one. The same comment says that on string, list and matrix work the change buys only about fifteen percent. So the build mode is a caveat on the numbers and not an excuse for them. I could not read the engine's build mode here, and the clock of the host language resolves ten to fifteen milliseconds. So the benchmark is cited here as a dated record, with that caveat, and not as a present measure.

**The coverage matrix.** Thirty domains in seven groups, against four other platforms, read on 17 September 2026. Softanza's tally is seven deep, fourteen solid, seven partial, two absent, present in twenty-eight of thirty. I recounted it from the rows and it matches. The two absent rows are symbolic mathematics ("no computer algebra") and package, build and deploy ("a library, not a registry"). Among the seven partial rows is internationalisation, "a deliberate bet against ICU", and the neural row is "inference only; no training foundry". The stored tallies of two of the other platforms do not match their own rows, by one in two places. I print Softanza's tally only. The matrix cites a compass file as the source of the ratings. I could not locate the file in the snapshot, in the live checkout or in the estate's repository.

**The graph comparison, a narration dated 26 December 2025.** A model wrote it, and says so in its byline. Its column for Softanza rates itself with a cross on scalability and on transactions and with a warning on performance, and its conclusion lists where traditional solutions win.

Where Softanza stands apart, by the record, without a number: the SLA; the governed act; the typed event; the matrix's one row of governance and safe execution. Two more: exact numbers by default, against two floats; an instruction that resolves locally. And a method that prints each of them beside a loss.

That is the list; now I say where this chapter stops.

## 7. The limit

What is built: four of the five pairs, run on both sides, and the fifth on the Softanza side. The four code pairs of the site are built too. I re-ran them on 8 October 2026, three of them on both sides (the fourth has a comment for its other side). My note of the night says the outputs matched. So are the library's field studies of performance, incident analysis and plugins, with their loss sections, and the culture plan, which prints its losses. So are the harness of the string benchmark and the guard that the engine is optimised.

What is designed or proposed: a comparison register as data; a comparison court; the re-run of both benchmarks. The study holds all three. When I drafted this chapter, on 8 October 2026, I searched for them in the library and in the site and found nothing. The site, read on 9 October 2026, now prints five rules for comparing, the first being "Cite or do not publish". It also prints a list of the nine rows where Softanza is partial or absent, with the platforms that are deep there. It says, in a line marked designed, that a register of comparisons is not held as data yet. It does not name the court.

What is absent: a *compared* column on the Atlas, on the register of innovations and on the narrations. Absent from the site: the field studies, the sixty-four grids themselves (it now gives their number and no more), both benchmarks and the runtime table. Absent from the library: locale collation; locale casing; a grapheme count on the public string; a time-zone database. And absent still: the re-run of the benchmark of 7 July 2026, and the source of the coverage matrix.

And something that is unreproduced: the study's sixty-four, six hundred and fifteen and thirty-one, which my scan did not reproduce, and the symbol counts.

The method is stricter than the record it is applied to. Older comparisons in the library predate its rules and do not yet meet one or more of them. The string benchmark probably ran on a debug engine. The polyglot battle has no versions and no dates inside its text, and says its times are relative to a specific computer environment. The graph grid has no versions, dates or sources for its cells. The cognitive-load grid of another design article makes claims of sixty to seventy percent with no method printed beside them. That article then ends on an honest positioning that names four things the library does not compete on.

Six doors follow, one for each reader of this page.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Copy a pair. Run both sides in your own versions, keep the output as it came, and add the line where the other tool is ahead.

**The analyst.** The coverage matrix is a buying aid only because it shows two absent rows and seven partial ones in its own colours. Read those first.

**The designer.** The loss goes in the same block and in the same type as the win. The stage word is part of the interface.

**The decision-maker.** Ask any vendor, this one included, for the other side's version, the date, whether it was run, and the line they lose. The memo of 13 September 2026 is a model of a headline withdrawn.

**The educator.** A code pair is a lesson in what a line costs. The learner writes the other side's output before running it.

**The agent.** When you repeat a claim about another tool, carry its version and date, and say if you did not run it. The register that would make this checkable by an agent is proposed and not built.

## 9. Exercise

Pick one task where you believe your own tool is ahead, and one where you believe the other is. Make a comparison that obeys the three rules: the other side's version and the date, both sides run, the loss printed.

```haro exercise
? YourSide(the_input)
#--> the line your tool prints
```

> run: exercise

Under it, write as text: the other tool's name and version, the date, the exact line it prints, and one line of the form "here the other leads because...". A reader checks the three text lines, since a runner cannot.

A variant that a machine can judge: take the five lines of block three's other side and predict, before running, the order each one prints. I will not tell you which order is right.

I left this one in your hands. Here is block three with its promise taken off:

```haro exercise
? @@( Q([ "zèbre", "étoile", "eau", "zoo" ]).SortedInAscending() )
```

> run: exercise

Under it, write the promise a French reader would expect, the order the field's locale-aware sort gives in section 3, as a `#-->` line, and run it. I do not print the verdict. Read it, then read sections 3 and 7 to learn why this block is a pinned gap and not a fault of the sort. When you have solved it, you will know why block three promises the library's present answer and not the field's.

Chapter 23 turns to the architect, and to a world, a constellation and a platform. There the solution is declared as worlds, bonds and an envelope before any of it is built, and every crossing between them is governed.


=====

# A world, a constellation, a platform, and the architect

Source: Chapter 23. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: a world as a declaration with a being, a becoming and a way of being met; a goal whose gap is measured; a constellation of worlds with bonds and three gates on every crossing; the commons as a platform; the capability seam; the profile of a solution as a text file, and the three system scopes; designed: the near-natural idiom of the constellation; a call routed into a nested constellation; shells that embed the engine; the architect's certification, which is a proposal; the five-row guarantee sheet, as a requirement; absent: a world that judges its own truths; a narration for the constellation or the platform envelope; a written five-row guarantee sheet for any solution

Run: 10 of 10 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:15 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I declare a solution at three levels, a world, a constellation and a platform, and I name the one person who declares all three, the architect. I take the field's three habits first: the super-app, the context map and the platform team. Then I run a world that explains itself, a constellation whose crossings are refused one gate at a time, and a profile that a platform refuses to build when it is not sound. I say where the code stops: a world's truths are narrated and not judged, the shells are tiny, and the architect's certification is a proposal. The principle is this: the solution is declared as worlds, bonds and an envelope before any of it is built, and every crossing between them is governed.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Three habits of the field have each a home, and I take them from general knowledge, not from a study of the record. The record holds no field study of super-apps, of platform engineering or of context maps. Its only named field is one line of a design document: a famous messaging super-app, and its kin.

The first is the super-app and the mini-program. One large application embeds a runtime and runs small applications inside it, sharing its services and its store. The host is privileged, the mini-app second-class, and the isolation a sandbox.

The second is the bounded context and the context map of domain-driven design, of 2003. A large system is cut into models, each with its words, and a map shows how they relate. It is the field's nearest idea to a world and a bond. It lives in diagrams and in review, and not in a running registry that refuses a call.

The third is the platform team and the deployment declared as code. A team builds the internal base that others deploy onto, and tools declare machines before they exist. The person who works across the floors is, in the field, a software architect, a solutions architect or a platform engineer. It is a title held by a person, whose artefacts are diagrams and manifests.

For the scopes, the field's home is the compiler: the target of a build, and the check that flags a call the minimum platform level lacks. Each decides which system the code is for. None lets the program name that system at the call site and refuse the act.

The shared shape: the field draws the picture, a host, a map, a diagram, and keeps the judge outside it. In all three the program knows nothing of its own composition. This is my reading of the three, not a survey of the field.

Against that shape I set one principle.

## 2. The first principle

**The solution is declared as worlds, bonds and an envelope before any of it is built, and every crossing between them is governed.**

The sentences of the record are three. An application is a living world of meaning. Worlds, not mini-apps: a governed graph and not a host container. A Softanza architect models the deployment architecture first, as a declarative artifact, before a line of system feature code. The sentence above is built from the three and from the book's method, which is to declare and then govern. The three are the record's, put in my words; the sentence above is the chapter's own principle, in the book's voice.

## 3. The Softanza form

Three levels, then. A world is one application. A constellation is a graph of worlds. A platform is the envelope around a solution and the ground a constellation stands on. And one person declares all three.

### A world

A world has a being, a becoming, and a way of being met. Chapter 4 showed its being. What does a world answer when it is asked to explain all three? Ask one, for an agency that visits clients:

```haro
oApp = new stzApp("FieldVisits")
oApp {
    AddThingQ(:Account) { Has([ :number, :chapter, :balance ])  IsTrue(:balance, "@ >= 0") }
    AddThingQ(:Client)  { Has([ :code, :name, :city ])  Owns(:Account) }
    AddThingQ(:Visit)   { Of(:Client)  Has([ :agent, :date, :subject ]) }
    AddRelation(:Account, :belongsTo, :Client)
    AddFlowQ(:agent, :records, :Visit) { Require(:subject) }
    AddReactionQ(:Client).Unseen(90, :Days) { Propose(:Visit) }
    AddGoalQ(:EveryClientSeenThisQuarter) {
        Means      = "every :Client Has(:visit) Since(:quarterStart)"
        ReachedBy  = :planning
    }
    SetBody([ :GraphDB, :Files ]) { Graph = ".stzapp/world.stzgraf"  Files = "./" }
}
oApp.AddScreenQ(:ClientFile) { ToUnderstand(:Client)  Shows([ :identity, :accounts, :visits ]) }
oApp.AddRefinementQ(:balance).Bounds(0, 1000000)
oApp.AddReaches([ :mobile, :desktop, :web ])
oApp.Explain()
#--> WORLD FieldVisits   lives in: graphdb + files
#      BEING
#        account (number, chapter, balance)
#            true when balance @ >= 0
#        client (code, name, city)
#        visit (agent, date, subject)
#      RELATIONS
#        client owns account
#        visit of client
#        account belongsto client
#      BECOMING
#        when agent records visit require subject
#        whenever client unseen 90 days -> propose visit
#        wants everyclientseenthisquarter -> reached by planning
#      MET FROM WITHOUT
#        screen clientfile: understand client shows identity, accounts, visits
#        refine balance bounds [0..1000000]
#        reaches mobile, desktop, web
```

> run: kept

It answered under three headings, `BEING`, `BECOMING` and `MET FROM WITHOUT`. `SetBody` only declares. No file was written by this block.

A world strives. A goal is a wanted state of the graph, and the gap is measured. A goal with no pattern to evaluate is refused, and never faked:

```haro
oApp = StzAppQ("visits")
oApp.AddThingQ(:Client) { Has([ :code, :name ]) }
oApp.AddThingQ(:Visit)  { Of(:Client) }
oApp.AddGoalQ(:EveryClientVisited) { Means = "every :Client Has(:visited)"  ReachedBy = :planning }
oApp.AddInstance("anna", :Client)
oApp.AddInstance("bilal", :Client)
oApp.Relate("anna", :visited, "visit1")
? @@( oApp.Pursue(:EveryClientVisited) )
#--> pursuing everyclientvisited via planning -- 1 proposal(s)
#    [ [ "attend", "client", "for", "bilal" ] ]
? oApp.GoalSatisfied(:EveryClientVisited)
#--> 0
oApp.Relate("bilal", :visited, "visit2")
? @@( oApp.Pursue(:EveryClientVisited) )
#--> pursuing everyclientvisited via planning -- 0 proposal(s)
#    [ ]
? oApp.GoalSatisfied(:EveryClientVisited)
#--> 1
oApp.AddGoalQ(:Vague) { Means = "make things nice" }
try
    oApp.Pursue(:Vague)
catch
    ? cCatchError
done
#--> stzGraphGoal.FromMeans: no 'every :Type' clause in 'make things nice'.
```

> run: kept

`Pursue` prints its own first line, and then the `?` prints the list it returns. One relation later the gap is closed: `0 proposal(s)`, and `GoalSatisfied` answers `1`. The vague goal is refused with its reason: `no 'every :Type' clause in 'make things nice'`. With a reaction declared, the same gap proposes a *visit* for Bilal, and without one, as in the run above, the default act is *attend*: `[ "attend", "client", "for", "bilal" ]`.

### A constellation

Nothing crosses between worlds unless it is declared and governed. A bond must exist, and then three gates stand on the crossing: the act must have a declared risk tier, the caller must hold the permission, and the caller's authority must cover the risk. Each refusal names its gate. Which gate refuses first? The bond exists in all four calls below, so the three refusals are the governance's:

```haro
oResto = StzAppQ("resto")
oResto.AddThingQ(:dish) { Has([ :name, :price ]) }
oSupplier = StzAppQ("supplier")
oSupplier.AddThingQ(:crate) { Has([ :produce, :qty ]) }
oCon = new stzSuperApp("acme-group")
oCon.AddWorld("resto", oResto)
oCon.AddWorld("supplier", oSupplier)
oCon.Bond("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 0
? oCon.Why()
#--> governance refused: refused: 'order-produce' has NO declared risk tier (undeclared actions never proceed)
oCon.GovDeclareRisk("order-produce", 2)
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 0
? oCon.Why()
#--> governance refused: refused: 'resto' lacks PERMISSION (can) for 'order-produce'
oCon.GovGrant("resto", "order-produce")
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 0
? oCon.Why()
#--> governance refused: refused: 'resto' holds '' authority (level 0) but 'order-produce' is risk tier 2 (SHOULD does not cover it)
oCon.GovSetAuthority("resto", :Delegated)
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 1
? oCon.Why()
#--> allowed: active + bonded + governed
? oCon.GovernanceQ().NumberOfDecisions()
#--> 1
? @@( oCon.GovernanceQ().DecisionsOf("resto")[1][2] )
#--> [ "rationale", "cross-world call cleared" ]
```

> run: kept

The risk tier refuses first: `'order-produce' has NO declared risk tier`. The doubled word "refused: refused:" is the library's own text. The cleared crossing wrote a decision by itself! Its record holds the authority and the risk of that moment, and a clock read, so the block prints only the rationale: `cross-world call cleared`. That is the lineage chapter 30 uses.

The shared ground is a world too. Inside a constellation the commons is itself a platform, and it holds an identity, a session and a store. The commons is not a special kind of node; the design's own words are that it is uniform and recursive. What class is the commons? Ask it:

```haro
oCon = new stzSuperApp("acme-group")
oDb = new stzDatabase(":memory:")
oCon.OpenCommonsOn(oDb)
? classname( oCon.CommonsQ() )
#--> stzplatform
? oCon.RegisterIdentity("owner", "s3cret")
#--> 1
? oCon.RegisterIdentity("owner", "other")
#--> 0
? len( oCon.OpenSession("owner", "s3cret") ) > 0
#--> 1
? oCon.OpenSession("owner", "wrong") = ""
#--> 1
oCon.StorePut("currency", "EUR")
? oCon.StoreGet("currency")
#--> EUR
oDb.Close()
```

> run: kept

This block uses an in-memory database, so no file and no network. The session token is random, and only its presence is printed. Secrets are stored salted and hashed, with the engine's key-derivation function. The first line answers `stzplatform`, and that is the point: a platform is not only the envelope around a solution. It is what a constellation stands on. In code the commons is not a node of the constellation graph: when I probed it the graph held two nodes for two registered worlds. World zero is a role the platform plays.

Does a world swapped live keep its bonds? A constellation also nests in another, and a name is taken once:

```haro
oResto = StzAppQ("resto")
oResto.AddThingQ(:dish) { Has([ :name, :price ]) }
oCon = new stzSuperApp("acme-group")
oCon.AddWorld("resto", oResto)
oCon.AddWorld("supplier", StzAppQ("supplier"))
oCon.Bond("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
oCon.GovDeclareRisk("order-produce", 2)
oCon.GovGrant("resto", "order-produce")
oCon.GovSetAuthority("resto", :Delegated)
oNew = StzAppQ("supplier-v2")
oCon.Swap("supplier", oNew)
? oCon.NumberOfWorlds()
#--> 2
? oCon.WorldQ("supplier").Name()
#--> supplier-v2
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 1
oHolding = new stzSuperApp("holding")
oHolding.AddConstellation("acme", oCon)
? oHolding.KindOf("acme")
#--> super
? oHolding.WorldQ("acme").NumberOfWorlds()
#--> 2
? oCon.KindOf("resto")
#--> app
try
    oCon.AddWorld("resto", oResto)
catch
    ? cCatchError
done
#--> stzSuperApp: world 'resto' already in the constellation.
```

> run: kept

The bond survived the swap: `CallAcross` still answers `1`! Chapter 28 used `Retire` and `Revive`, and this block is `Swap` and `AddConstellation`. Nesting is a fact of the registry. Nothing in the code routes a call into a nested constellation, so the recursion of the design is a registry's recursion and not yet a call's. A crossing is a verdict: `CallAcross` clears or refuses with 1 or 0, and nothing is handed to the target yet.

One trap. A registered world is a copy. The host language copies an object by value when it is stored, so the world you register is not the world you hold. The header of the class says it: world objects are reached through the registry index, the live path. Configure the world fully, register it, and reach it through the constellation from then on. The envelope has the same doctrine: setting a world on the platform harvests its declarations at call time, and setting the governance snapshots the regime.

### The envelope, and the seam

A platform grants a capability of a device only if governance clears its risk. Is a held permission enough? The risk tiers, two for the camera and four for payments, are the library's declared ones:

```haro
oGov = new stzGovernance("resto-regime")
oGov.GrantPermission("resto", "use-camera")
oGov.GrantPermission("resto", "use-payments")
oGov.SetAuthority("resto", :Delegated)
oResto = StzAppQ("resto")
oResto.AddThingQ(:dish) { Has([ :name, :price ]) }
oPlat = StzPlatformQ("resto-envelope")
oPlat.SetWorld(oResto)
oPlat.SetGovernedBy(oGov)
oPlat.AddCapabilityQ(:camera).SetPurposes([ :scan_dish_photo ])
? oPlat.Granted(:camera)
#--> 1
oPlat.AddCapabilityQ(:payments).SetPurposes([ :charge_guests ])
? oPlat.Granted(:payments)
#--> 0
? oPlat.Why()
#--> refused: 'resto' holds 'delegated' authority (level 2) but 'use-payments' is risk tier 4 (SHOULD does not cover it)
```

> run: kept

The permission was held for both, and the payments were refused anyway! The reason: `'resto' holds 'delegated' authority (level 2) but 'use-payments' is risk tier 4`.

The class header says what the world lacks: the world models the world, and deliberately lacks the operational envelope. The envelope's duties are generation, the capability seam, the commons runtime, a networked body and a registry with enforcement. Generation is real, and refuses to generate for a world that declares no reach. The success path writes real files, so I do not run it here. I ran it once, for a world of one screen and one thing, and removed what it wrote. The web shell is a page of 318 bytes with the world's screens and things as list items, and the desktop shell a launcher of 251 bytes. Other worlds give other sizes. That is the limit of "per-platform shells": generation is real, and tiny.

### The architect, and the three scopes

By *scope* I mean the System Foundation's: development, deployment, and runtime-current. Every system act is written in a named scope. A Softanza architect models the deployment architecture first. The same verb, in two scopes, gets two verdicts, and the target's own words say why:

```haro
oSol = new stzPlatformProfile("greenhouse")
oSol.DevelopedOn(:Windows)
oSol.WithServer(:backend, :LinuxServer)
oSol.WithSuperApp(:phone, :Android)
oSol.WithApp(:sensor, :ESP32)
? oSol.DevelopmentSystem().OSName()
#--> windows
? oSol.App(:phone).System().OSName()
#--> android
? oSol.App(:sensor).System().OSName()
#--> espidf
? @@( oSol.App(:sensor).System().System().Capabilities() )
#--> [ "gpio", "network", "clock", "threads" ]
? oSol.CurrentSystem().IsRuntime()
#--> 1
? oSol.App(:backend).System().Spawn("cron")
#--> native
try
    oSol.App(:phone).System().Spawn("cron")
catch
    ? cCatchError
done
#--> scope 'android' (android) forbids 'process' -- spawn 'cron'. This target has no such capability.
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The development system's name is the declared one, so it does not depend on the machine. The line `native` is host-dependent: it holds only on a host that can spawn a process, as chapter 31 says of its own word. The Android refusal, `scope 'android' (android) forbids 'process'`, and the capability list of the board are the target's, whatever the host. The three scopes are `new stzDevSystem()` or `DevelopmentSystem()` for development, which is tooling only; `App(:x).System()` for deployment, where feature code is written and checked; and `new stzCurrentSystem()` or `CurrentSystem()` for runtime-current, which is what runs now. The scope object has no `Capabilities()` of its own: the call goes through `.System().System()`. A request.

The architect's artefact is a text file. Can it be read back, and what does the platform say of a solution that is not sound? Run it:

```haro
oSol = new stzPlatformProfile("greenhouse")
oSol.DevelopedOn(:Windows)
oSol.WithServer(:backend, :LinuxServer)
oSol.WithSuperApp(:phone, :Android)
oSol.WithApp(:sensor, :ESP32)
? oSol.ToStzPlatform()
#--> # .stzplatform -- a Softanza solution profile
#    platform: greenhouse
#    developed_on: windows
#    server: backend -> linux
#    superapp: phone -> android
#    app: sensor -> espidf
oBack = StzPlatformProfileQ()
oBack.FromString( oSol.ToStzPlatform() )
? oBack.App(:sensor).DeploymentOSName()
#--> espidf
oBad = new stzPlatformProfile("empty")
? @@( oBad.Validate() )
#--> [ "no development system declared (use DevelopedOn)", "the solution deploys no apps" ]
oPlat = StzPlatformQ("empty")
oPlat.SetProfile(oBad)
try
    oPlat.Build()
catch
    ? cCatchError
done
#--> stzPlatform.Build: the solution is not sound -- no development system declared (use DevelopedOn); the solution deploys no apps
```

> run: kept

The file form is plain text that the architect owns, and it round-trips from a string. The unsound one is refused before building, with both faults named: `the solution is not sound -- no development system declared (use DevelopedOn); the solution deploys no apps`. The order of building and deploying is chapter 33's, and the gate on who deploys is chapter 24's.

Who is the architect? The record names the person twice by name: in the System Foundation, and in Central's memo for a school that would certify architects of Softanza, with three levels. That memo is marked withdrawn, as a launch prompt, on the day it was written. A third place, a private book of the estate on platform modelling, models platforms and does not name the architect. That certification is a proposal of 30 September 2026, not a delivery, and the site must not announce it. The estate's protocol defines a role as a profile held in a scope. Applied here, which is my application and not the record's, the architect is not a rank. It is what one holds in the scope of a solution: the profile, the worlds, the governance. I hold this as a view, not a result. The site has a journey page for the platform architect and a certification page that says the proposal is not announced (read from the site, 9 October 2026). I tell the architect as what one declares, and the certification is only that proposal; no school is named here.

Seven refusals stand behind this form.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused the host and the mini-app.** Worlds, not mini-apps; a governed graph, not a host container. Chapter 4 told it, and I point back.

**I refused to make the commons a special kind of node.** It is a world, and a world is uniform and recursive.

**I refused the sandbox as the definition of isolation.** Isolation is a declared, inspectable meaning and not a black-box virtual machine. A world's sovereign body plus norm-gated bonds. A runtime sandbox is only an implementation for untrusted worlds.
