**I refused to make governance one more facet, beside the design's constellation, bonds, commons, reach and presence.** It is the ambient. In code, one governance per constellation, configured through it, so that there is one live governance and never a stale copy held by a caller.

**I refused to give the world the envelope.** `stzApp` models the world, and deliberately lacks the operational envelope.

**I refused to freeze *current* under a better name.** The cure is not a better name: every system act is written in a named scope. The attempts it refuses are a pair of words, this system and the target system, and a triple, host, runtime and target.

**I refused to deploy a profile.** We do not deploy a profile; we deploy a platform and its parts. The profile only describes. The platform owns it and drives the building and then the deployment.

The court now says which of it runs.

## 5. The court

`06_stzsuperapp_narrated` proves that worlds register as nodes; that the commons holds an identity and a store; that a crossing is refused for an undeclared risk, accepted when governed, and refused when unbonded with the bond named; that a retired world breaks its bonds and revives; that a swap keeps the node and the bond; and that a constellation nests in another. `01_platform_narrated` proves that shells are generated per declared reach and a world without a reach is refused; that a capability is granted only when governance covers its risk tier, and a capability never requested is refused with a reason. It proves that the commons registers identities, opens sessions, posts and reads messages, and stores; that the world is served over a real port; and that the registry refuses a call to an unbonded or retired world. It writes shells beside itself and opens a loopback port. `full_scope_model_narrated`, which the guard calls the architect's common ground, proves that the solution is modelled first, that an unsound one is flagged, that the three scopes are distinct, that the scope refuses what the target forbids and rehearses what the host lacks, that the text file round-trips, that building precedes deploying, and that a deploy by a language-model actor is refused. `04_stzapp_pursue_narrated` and `05_stzapp_live_narrated` carry the goal and the reactions.

The guard the chapter does not have: none judges a world's truths, a flow, or the time of a reaction.

Next I set the record beside the field.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The super-app leads in users. The design names its model and replaces it; chapter 4 says the record measures nothing against them. The Platforms page prints the other half: the customer platforms run today on ordinary web stacks. No platform runs on the constellation in production.

The field leads in shells. The design promises a progressive web app, a desktop shell, a mobile shell. The code generates a page and a launcher of a few hundred bytes. The shells are designed. Generation is built, and tiny.

The field leads in what a bounded context carries: a team and a language. A world carries a graph and a narration. The persona a world is for is not a field of the class: a world has the list of surfaces it reaches and nothing else about whom it serves. The site's five things, a shared ground, a world for each person and device, bonds, rules, roles, are true of the commons, the bonds and the governance. The roles are the actors, permissions and authority of the governance. There is no role class, and a world per person and device is a naming convention.

The Atlas does not rate this plane. `stzApp` is named once, in the lane of the area for interfaces called App shell and windowing. That lane, not the world, is rated solid, with the note that there is no multi-window orchestration, dialogs, menus or tray. The platform in a lane of the area for the system. The constellation in none.

The compiler leads on scopes. It checks against a target in daily use at scale; Softanza's scope is a runtime object that a program asks. What it adds is a verdict at the call site in the target's own words, and a twin that rehearses what the host lacks, which is chapter 24.

Where the field and the record meet: a super-app is a graph of graphs. The field's context map is a diagram. Softanza's is a registry that refuses.

## 7. The limit

A world's truths are narrated and not judged. The number in a reaction is the first case. Do ninety days and one day give different proposals? A reaction fires for each instance that lacks a relation named after the thing it proposes, and the comment above `Pulse()` says so. Run two clinics:

```haro
oA = StzAppQ("clinic-a")
oA.AddThingQ(:Patient) { Has([ :name ]) }
oA.AddThing(:Checkup)
oA.AddReactionQ(:Patient).Unseen(90, :Days) { Propose(:Checkup) }
oA.AddInstance("alice", :Patient)
oA.AddInstance("bob", :Patient)
oA.Relate("alice", :checkup, "checkup-1")
oB = StzAppQ("clinic-b")
oB.AddThingQ(:Patient) { Has([ :name ]) }
oB.AddThing(:Checkup)
oB.AddReactionQ(:Patient).Unseen(1, :Days) { Propose(:Checkup) }
oB.AddInstance("alice", :Patient)
oB.AddInstance("bob", :Patient)
oB.Relate("alice", :checkup, "checkup-1")
oA.Live()
#--> [clinic-a] is live -- 2 thing(s), 0 flow(s), 1 reaction(s), 0 goal(s); 1 proposal(s)
oB.Live()
#--> [clinic-b] is live -- 2 thing(s), 0 flow(s), 1 reaction(s), 0 goal(s); 1 proposal(s)
? @@( oA.Proposals() )
#--> [ [ "propose", "checkup", "for", "bob" ] ]
? @@( oB.Proposals() )
#--> [ [ "propose", "checkup", "for", "bob" ] ]
```

> run: kept

Both clinics answer `[ "propose", "checkup", "for", "bob" ]`. Ninety days and one day give the same proposals!

The same reading holds for `IsTrue`, which is stored and narrated while instances carry no fields to judge it against. It holds for a flow's `Require` and `Then`, stored and narrated, where I found no executor; and for the bounds of a refinement. A world today declares that a balance must not be negative. It does not judge a balance. What it does judge is a goal's gap, a crossing and a capability. So I say "a world that wants" for the goal, and "narrated" for the truth. Chapter 4's `true when balance @ >= 0` is a declaration only. So the chapter says it in one line: declared and narrated; judged only for goals, crossings and capabilities.

The design writes more than the class speaks. Does the class speak the design's near-natural idiom for the constellation? Ask it for `Hosts`, `Admits` and `Explain`:

```haro
oSuper = new stzSuperApp("acme-group")
try
    oSuper.Hosts(:VisitApp)
catch
    ? cCatchError
done
#--> Error (R14) : Calling Method without definition: hosts
try
    oSuper.Admits(:VisitApp)
catch
    ? cCatchError
done
#--> Error (R14) : Calling Method without definition: admits
try
    oSuper.Explain()
catch
    ? cCatchError
done
#--> Error (R14) : Calling Method without definition: explain
```

> run: kept

Three times the answer is `Calling Method without definition`. `Shares`, `Provides`, `Norm`, `Reaches` and `Generate` are not defined on a constellation either. The class has `AddWorld`, `AddConstellation`, `Bond`, `Retire`, `Revive`, `Swap`, the `Gov*` family, `CallAcross`, `Crossings`, the commons calls, and calls that look: `Why`, `GraphQ`, `WorldQ`, `KindOf`, `IsActive`, `HasWorld`, `NumberOfWorlds`, `AreBonded`, `CrossingCount` and `LastCallMs`. The verbs of composition are these. The design's three aspects are reached today by `AddWorld`, `Bond` and `OpenCommonsOn`, and governance by `Gov*`; reach and presence are the part that is not there. The design's own header calls the document a forward design.

What is built: the world; the constellation with its gates, its ledger of crossings with timings, a decision written on a cleared crossing, and a refused crossing noted as evidence; the platform's capability seam, its commons, its registry, its shells, its profile with building then deploying; the three scopes.

What is designed: the near-natural idiom; a call routed into a nested constellation (today a crossing is a verdict, and nothing is handed to the target); loading one world without rebuilding a host; shells that embed the engine; the five-row guarantee sheet, which the vision asks for and the site's journey page marks designed: one row for each of five systems the solution answers to, technical, social, ecological, economic and cultural.

What is absent: a narration for the constellation or the envelope, since none exists. The narration for building a first app uses verbs the class does not have. Seven of its calls fail at this commit, and I cite it for the idea and not the syntax. A customer platform on the engine as a constellation. The written five-row guarantee sheet: no solution has one. A lane of the Atlas for the world, the constellation or the envelope.

The six doors come next.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You declare a world with braces, ask it to explain itself, and cross between worlds with a call that returns 0 and a `Why()` that names the gate. Watch the copy: configure, then register.

**The analyst.** You declare things, relations and a goal as a wanted state of the graph, and the gap is measured. Your truths and durations are narrated and not yet judged.

**The designer.** A world declares screens as intents, a refinement as a bounded knob, and where it appears. The shells generated today are stubs, and the persona a world serves is not a field.

**The decision-maker.** You declare who may cross where and at what risk, in three gates, and every cleared crossing leaves a decision with the actor, the authority and the risk of its moment. Your architect's file is text you own.

**The educator.** The record is thin. The education plan lists a ladder from practitioner to architect to contributor among five competing level schemes, and I say so. The exercise below is judged by running.

**The agent.** A world can be wrapped as an agent that the host ticks. A deploy by a language-model actor is refused, and nothing is deployed. The crossing between worlds is governed by the calling world's name and authority.

## 9. Exercise

Declare two worlds for a place you know: a shop and its supplier, a class and its library, a clinic and its pharmacy. Give each a thing. Join them with one bond for one act. Before you run anything, write what you expect `CallAcross` to return, and which reason you expect to read first.

```haro exercise
? YourConstellation.CallAcross("a", "b", "act")
#--> 1
```

> run: exercise

Then ask for the call, read the reason, and repair the declaration, not the court, one gate at a time, until it returns 1. Count the refusals. Which gate would you have forgotten without the court? The court tells you which gate refuses: a missing risk tier, a missing permission, an authority that does not cover the risk. It does not write the declaration. A harder one: retire the second world and predict the new reason before asking; then register a copy of the first, change your original, and predict what the constellation sees.

I left this one in your hands. Take the gates block of section 3 and delete one line: `oCon.GovSetAuthority("resto", :Delegated)`. Stop the block at its last `Why()`, as here, and I print nothing under it:

```haro exercise
oResto = StzAppQ("resto")
oResto.AddThingQ(:dish) { Has([ :name, :price ]) }
oSupplier = StzAppQ("supplier")
oSupplier.AddThingQ(:crate) { Has([ :produce, :qty ]) }
oCon = new stzSuperApp("acme-group")
oCon.AddWorld("resto", oResto)
oCon.AddWorld("supplier", oSupplier)
oCon.Bond("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
? oCon.Why()
oCon.GovDeclareRisk("order-produce", 2)
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
? oCon.Why()
oCon.GovGrant("resto", "order-produce")
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
? oCon.Why()
? oCon.CallAcross("resto", "supplier", "order-produce")
? oCon.Why()
```

> run: exercise

Run it and read the last `Why()`. Name the gate that refuses, and say what the block would have to say for the crossing to clear. Section 3 says what each of the three gates asks, and that each refusal names its gate. When you have solved it, you will know that the court names the gate that refuses and does not write the declaration.

The next chapter is about how a declared solution reaches reality, through exactly one door: rehearse on what touches nothing, commit through a governed actor, and let production refuse anything that is fake. Rehearse, then commit.


=====

# Rehearse, then commit

Source: Chapter 24. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: a twin of files and a twin of the process environment, each with its history, snapshots, a narrated plan, its risks and, for the file twin, a validation against reality; the placement plan; the emulator's bundle, wired into the production attach; a deployment as a graph of steps with a rollback; the registry of services with its postures and the gate that refuses a fake at production; designed: the engine seen running in a browser; a drift check for recorded answers; branches of a twin; twins for databases, interfaces and networks; the emulated phase set by the emulated deploy; absent: any live card-gateway adapter for payments (a hub adapter is built and proved only against a twin, chapter 31), a model, mail, SMS or an object store; live verification of a remote host; a person's look at the emulator's surface; a customer solution emulated and deployed through this path

Run: 11 of 11 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:15 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take the three practices the field keeps beside the program, plan and then apply, the test double and the emulator, and I show the one path the library puts in their place. I rehearse on a twin of files and a twin of the process environment, and I read the plan of placement. I build the emulator's bundle, run a deployment that fails and rolls back, and code against services by name before I subscribe to any. Then I ask production to refuse, and I print the one fake it does not refuse: a double that says nothing about itself. The principle is joined from three documents of the record. Reality is reached through exactly one door: rehearse on what touches nothing, commit through a governed actor, and let production refuse anything that is fake.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Three practices of the field stand beside the program, each with its own configuration.

The first is plan and then apply: infrastructure as code and deployment pipelines. The record's own references are the dependency graph of a pipeline's jobs, a tool's resource graph with apply-or-revert, and a cluster scheduler that places work by the resources it asks for. The field's habit is that the plan is a file for a tool, and whether the wrong actor may apply it is a policy. The record states the loss it answers: it's a convention, enforced by hope.

The second is service virtualization and the test double: a vendor's test mode with published test cards, a stub server for HTTP, and the libraries that record a call once and replay it. The field's habit is that the double lives in the test suite, and production is a different file. The record's complaint: no gateway will decline a card because you asked nicely.

The third is the emulator and the simulator: a phone emulator, a board emulator, a local stand-in for a cloud. The record's own sentence on the genre is that simulators lie. They mock the database, stub the device, approximate the compute.

What the three share is that each is a separate tool, and the program itself knows nothing of it. (General knowledge for the tools, not a study of the record, except where I quote the documents.)

## 2. The first principle

**Reality is reached through exactly one door: rehearse on what touches nothing, commit through a governed actor, and let production refuse anything that is fake.**

The record's first principle for this ground is the first clause, and the library's own label calls it a first principle: reality is reached through exactly one door. The default path for consequential change is to rehearse in a twin, generate a plan, and commit the plan. The plan is the door. The framework's summary is three words: rehearse, plan, commit. The structure line of this book's outline says the last clause: production refuses a fake. The first clause is a library document without an author line (`SP3`, in `SOFTANZA_SYSTEM_FOUNDATION.md`). The middle clause, commit through a governed actor, is the deployment design's (`SOFTANZA_DEPLOYMENT.md`: "only an effectful actor commits"). The chapter joins the three and attributes each part to its source.

## 3. The Softanza form

### The twin

The one artifact that crosses toward reality is a plan. It names each step. It flags the one that removes something. And asked to validate, it re-reads reality. Chapter 20 said "I saw it warn" and did not show the words. What does the plan say about the source it was going to move? Here are the words, and nothing is executed:

```haro
oBench = new stzVirtualFileSystem()
oBench.CreateFolder("config")
oBench.CreateFile("config/pump.ini", "threshold=40")
oBench.MoveFile("old_settings.txt", "config/legacy.ini")
oPlan = oBench.GenerateUpdatePlan()
? oPlan.Narration()
#--> Update plan (3 of 3 operations to commit):
#-->   * 1. create folder 'config'
#-->   * 2. create file 'config/pump.ini' (12 bytes)
#-->   * 3. move 'old_settings.txt' -> 'config/legacy.ini'
? @@(oPlan.Risks())
#--> [ [ 3, "MOVES (removes source): move 'old_settings.txt' -> 'config/legacy.ini'" ] ]
? @@(oPlan.Validate())
#--> [ [ 3, "source absent in reality: old_settings.txt" ] ]
```

> run: kept

The plan answers `source absent in reality: old_settings.txt`, and it flags the move: `MOVES (removes source)`. The library's narration prints a closing line, that reality is untouched until I choose to commit this plan. The run does not print it, so I do not show it.

The twin is not only a twin of files. A variable set, a directory changed and a child process queued are recorded, narrated and flagged, and no process started. Does the real environment have the variable? Ask it, after the twin has set it:

```haro
oEnv = new stzVirtualEnvironment()
oEnv.SetActor("agent-42")
oEnv.SetVar("SHOP_MODE", "rehearsal")
oEnv.Cd("/srv/shop")
oEnv.Spawn("backup-database --all")
? oEnv.Var("SHOP_MODE")
#--> rehearsal
? oEnv.NumberOfPendingSpawns()
#--> 1
? (new stzEnvironment()).Has("SHOP_MODE")
#--> 0
oEnvPlan = oEnv.GenerateUpdatePlan()
? oEnvPlan.Narration()
#--> Update plan (3 of 3 operations to commit):
#-->   * 1. set env var 'SHOP_MODE' = 'rehearsal'
#-->   * 2. change directory to '/srv/shop'
#-->   * 3. spawn process: backup-database --all
? @@(oEnvPlan.Risks())
#--> [ [ 3, "RUNS an external command (side effects): spawn process: backup-database --all" ] ]
```

> run: kept

The twin holds `rehearsal` and one pending spawn, and the real environment answers `0`! Chapter 20's limit paragraph said that only files are built. That was wrong, and I have corrected it there.

The twin has a second job. A programmer on a laptop with no pins writes pin code for a board. The scope says *rehearsed*, as chapters 8 and 31 showed. At deploy, the lowering bridge turns the rehearsal into the source of firmware. What happens when an assistant asks to deploy? `Build()` records the build command and does not run the toolchain, and `Deploy` lowers in memory, so no file is written:

```haro
oProduct = new stzPlatformProfile("greenhouse")
oProduct.DevelopedOn(:Windows)
oProduct.WithApp(:sensor, :ESP32)
? oProduct.ReadPinIn(:sensor, 34)
#--> rehearsed
? oProduct.WritePinIn(:sensor, 26, 1)
#--> rehearsed
oPlatform = StzPlatformQ("greenhouse")
oPlatform.SetProfile(oProduct)
oPlatform.Build()
try
	oPlatform.DeployAs(LLMActor("copilot"))
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> stzPlatform.Deploy: actor 'copilot' lacks the 'effectful' capability -- it may propose a deploy, not perform it.
? oPlatform.IsDeployed()
#--> 0
oPlatform.DeployAs(HumanActor("me"))
? oPlatform.ArtifactFor(:sensor)
#--> // firmware generated by the Softanza lowering bridge
#--> // target: espidf
#--> void setup() {}
#--> void loop() {
#-->   digitalRead(34);
#-->   digitalWrite(26, 1);
#--> }
```

> run: kept

The assistant is refused, and told that it `may propose a deploy, not perform it`! The person's deploy yields the firmware. The limit of this block is in section 7: only two verbs are lowered.

The twin rehearses; next, the plan says where each part would go.

### The plan of placement

Before a byte is built, the delivery planner states, for each capability a part needs, whether the target does it, the engine does it, a construct does it, or the server does it. It gives the reason in a sentence. The figure in parentheses is the catalogue's estimate and not a measure, so do not quote it as a claim. "1 parts" is the library's own grammar:

```haro
oShip = new stzDelivery("shop")
oShip.AddSuperApp(:phone, :Android)
oShip.NeedsIn(:phone, [ :Unicode, :PivotTable, :Collection, :Neural ])
oShip.Plan().Show()
#--> Solution 'shop' -- placement & scope plan (rehearsal; nothing built yet)
#--> ==============================================================================
#-->
#-->   Part 'phone' [superapp] -> android (mobile / edge)
#-->      Unicode            [target]    Unicode: the target-platform is strong at it -> use its own
#-->      PivotTable         [stz.wasm]  PivotTable: Softanza-differential, mobile weak -> the on-device engine
#-->      Collection         [stz.js]    Collection: ergonomic -> a Softanza construct in the target language
#-->      Neural             [server]    Neural: heavy -> runs on the backend, not the edge
#-->      -> stz.wasm carries: [ "PivotTable" ]  (~12 KB, 1 of 4)
#-->
#-->   Summary: 4 capabilities across 1 parts -> target 1, engine 1, construct 1, server 1.
#-->   Build() will compile exactly this scope; Deploy() will commit it.
```

> run: kept

The rule behind the plan, in the emulation design: the edge carries a capability only if it is critical, and either unique to Softanza or weak or absent on the target platform. A regular expression is a construct over the platform's own, and never the engine's matcher compiled for the browser.

The plan says where; the emulator builds what the plan says.

### The emulation

The emulator turns the plan into a bundle, and the bundle is what ships. In the next block the bundle generated for the rehearsal is wired straight into the production attach, and the actor decides. What does the site become when an assistant calls `Deploy(:Production)`, and what when a person does? The manifest that lands names the engine group the part carries. The target here is a local folder, the kind of site that needs no tool but file operations:

```haro
cHere = StzReplace(WorkingDirectory(), "\", "/")
oFront = new stzDelivery("shopfront")
oFront.AddSuperApp(:web, :Browser)
oFront.NeedsIn(:web, [ :Unicode, :PivotTable, :Collection ])
oBundle = new stzEmulator(oFront)
oBundle.CompileEngineQ(FALSE)
oBundle.SetOutDirQ(cHere + "/_bundle")
oBundle.Build()
oSite = new stzDeploymentSite("cdn")
oSite.SetKindQ(:LocalRepo).SetStoreAtQ(cHere + "/_live")
oFront.DeployTo(oSite, :web)
oFront.ShipSlice(:web, oBundle)
oFront.SetActor(LLMActor("assistant"))
oFront.Deploy(:Production)
? oSite.Status()
#--> absent
oFront.SetActor(HumanActor("ops"))
oFront.Deploy(:Production)
? oSite.Status()
#--> launched
? @@(StzEngineDirListFiles(cHere + "/_live"))
#--> [ ".stzsite", "deploy.json", "index.html", "stz.js" ]
? read(cHere + "/_live/deploy.json")
#--> {
#-->   "solution": "shopfront",
#-->   "part": "web",
#-->   "engine": "aggregation",
#-->   "artifacts": "index.html,stz.js"
#--> }
StzDirDeleteAll(cHere + "/_bundle")
StzDirDeleteAll(cHere + "/_live")
```

> run: kept (touches files)

The assistant left the site `absent`, and the person made it `launched`. The honest half: the manifest's artifacts line reads `index.html,stz.js`, and no engine file for the web part is in it, because the engine compile was off. The block says what the run shipped, and not what a full run would. A bare `Deploy(:Production)` with no actor at all returns a rehearsal and writes a warning, "no effectful actor -- returning a rehearsal (nothing committed)". I ran it too, in my research run of 8 October 2026 and not in a block shown here: the site stayed absent and nothing was written.

I did not run `Deploy(:Emulated)`, because it builds each part's subset through the engine toolchain, and the toolchain's build is something this machine must not run. What I ran is the bundle without the compile. In my research run of 8 October 2026, outside this chapter's blocks, its map named an engine file that is not there. Section 7 says what that means for the claim that the engine runs in a browser.

### The deployment

For a local site, a rollout that fails undoes what it had done; the other kinds of site only try, as a best effort. The web part deploys and passes its gate. The api part's store fails. The remaining steps are skipped and the deployment does not commit. What becomes of the web site, already live? The failing site is a server with no storage location, which fails without spawning anything:

```haro
cHere = StzReplace(WorkingDirectory(), "\", "/")
oShop10 = new stzDelivery("shop")
oShop10.AddBackend(:api, :LinuxServer)
oShop10.AddSuperApp(:web, :Browser)
oGood = new stzDeploymentSite("cdn")
oGood.SetKindQ(:LocalRepo).SetStoreAtQ(cHere + "/_site_cdn")
oBad = new stzDeploymentSite("prod-api")
oBad.SetKindQ(:Server)
oRun = new stzDeployment(oShop10)
oRun.SetActor(HumanActor("ops"))
oRun.SetTarget(:web, oGood)
oRun.SetTarget(:api, oBad)
oRun.RunAfter(:api, :web)
aRun = oRun.Run()
? aRun[1]
#--> 0
for aLine in aRun[2]
	? aLine[1] + " -> " + aLine[2]
next
#--> store:web -> done
#--> launch:web -> done
#--> verify:web -> done
#--> store:api -> FAILED
#--> launch:api -> skipped
#--> verify:api -> skipped
? oGood.Status()
#--> rolledback
StzDirDeleteAll(cHere + "/_site_cdn")
```

> run: kept

The api store reads `FAILED`. The web site, whose three steps read `done`, reads `rolledback`! With an assistant as the actor, the same run returns every step as *rehearsed*, the site stays absent, and no folder is created. I ran it in my research run of 8 October 2026, outside this chapter's blocks, and do not print it.

The deployment fails safely; the services come next.

### Code first, subscribe later

The code asks for a service by name and never learns which implementation it got. The sandbox sees the first call and none after I rebind the service as live; the second call goes to the live adapter. The surface is sound once every live binding names a secret that the store holds. The live gateway in this block is a stand-in class written here, since the library ships no live card-gateway adapter. A stand-in named `LiveGateway` that returns "authorized" is a fake in everything but its name. The registry takes it as live because it declares nothing, and section 7 says why that matters:

```haro
oPay = new stzPaymentsSandbox()
oReg = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oReg.DeclareMany([ :payments, :mail ])
oReg.Bind(:payments, oPay)
oReg.Bind(:mail, new stzMailSandbox())
? Checkout(oReg, 4200)
#--> authorized
? oPay.NumberOfAuthorizations()
#--> 1
oStoreCf = new stzSecretStore("shop")
oStoreCf.Register( (new stzApiKey("stripe-key")).FromLiteralQ("sk-test-123") )
oStoreCf.Register( (new stzApiKey("mail-key")).FromLiteralQ("mk-test-123") )
oReg.BindLive(:payments, new LiveGateway, "stripe-key")
oReg.BindLive(:mail, new LiveMailer, "mail-key")
? Checkout(oReg, 4200)
#--> authorized
? oPay.NumberOfAuthorizations()
#--> 1
? oReg.PostureOf(:payments)
#--> live
oReg.SetPhase(:production)
? oReg.IsSoundVia(oStoreCf)
#--> 1

func Checkout poReg, nCents
	aRes = poReg.Service(:payments).Authorize(nCents, "tok_visa")
	poReg.Service(:mail).Send("dana@example.com", "Receipt", "Thank you.")
	return aRes[:status]

class LiveGateway
	def init
		return
	def Authorize nAmount, cToken
		return [ :status = "authorized" ]

class LiveMailer
	def init
		return
	def Send cTo, cSubject, cBody
		return 1
```

> run: kept

The day you do subscribe, you change a binding and not a line. The key is an invented test string and not a real one.

### Production refuses

One report names five of the eight ways a surface is not fit for production. Four are errors: a declared service never bound (`unbound-service`), a fake (`sandbox-in-production`), a real database that vanishes on restart (`ephemeral-in-production`), and a live adapter whose key is not in the store (`live-without-secret`). The fifth is a warning: a live adapter that names no secret at all (`inline-credential`). Chapter 31 showed the unbound one and the raise. This is the rest of the list. In the development phase the surface is already not sound, since an unbound service and a missing key are errors in any phase. The other two errors appear only in the production phase. The code lists eight invariants, and the service plan (section 4) and the narration list five:

```haro
oStoreSf = new stzSecretStore("shop")
oSurf = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oSurf.Bind(:payments, new stzPaymentsSandbox())
oSurf.Bind(:scratch, StzMemoryDataSourceQ())
oSurf.BindLive(:cache, new stzString("cache-adapter"), "redis-url")
oSurf.BindLive(:sms, new stzString("sms-adapter"), "")
oSurf.Declare(:reporting)
? oSurf.IsSoundVia(oStoreSf)
#--> 0
oSurf.SetPhase(:production)
oSurf.ReportVia(oStoreSf)
#--> Service registry 'shop' [production] -- 4 bound: 1 sandboxed, 1 local, 2 live
#-->   [ERROR] unbound-service @ shop/reporting -- declared but never bound -- asking for it will raise
#-->   [ERROR] sandbox-in-production @ shop/payments -- still bound to a SANDBOX in a production phase -- a fake must never ship
#-->   [ERROR] ephemeral-in-production @ shop/scratch -- a LOCAL source that vanishes on restart (in-memory) is bound in a production phase
#-->   [ERROR] live-without-secret @ shop/cache -- credential 'redis-url' is not in the secret store
#-->   [WARN] inline-credential @ shop/sms -- live adapter bound without naming a store secret
```

> run: kept

Two different questions are asked at the door: who is refused, and what is refused. Why is each refused? Ask the registry twice, once with an assistant and once with a person, in the development phase:

```haro
oStoreWw = new stzSecretStore("shop")
oOne = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oOne.Bind(:payments, new stzPaymentsSandbox())
? oOne.Phase()
#--> development
? oOne.MayGoLive(LLMActor("assistant"), oStoreWw)
#--> 0
? oOne.WhyNotLive(LLMActor("assistant"), oStoreWw)
#--> actor 'assistant' is not effectful -- it may propose, not commit
? oOne.MayGoLive(HumanActor("dana"), oStoreWw)
#--> 0
? oOne.WhyNotLive(HumanActor("dana"), oStoreWw)
#--> sandbox-in-production: still bound to a SANDBOX in a production phase -- a fake must never ship
? oOne.Phase()
#--> development
```

> run: kept

The assistant is refused for who it is: `actor 'assistant' is not effectful -- it may propose, not commit`. The person is refused for what the surface is: `sandbox-in-production: still bound to a SANDBOX in a production phase -- a fake must never ship`. And asking leaves the phase where it was: `development` before, `development` after, though the question is asked in the production frame.

And the registry knows services while the delivery knows parts. Joined, they answer a question neither can alone. Which part is about to ship on a double? The registry itself reports nothing, since the phase is development and there are no findings. The part-level check answers anyway, by destination, and names the part:

```haro
oS3 = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oS3.DeclareMany([ :payments, :cache ])
oS3.BindSandbox(:payments, new stzPaymentsSandbox())
oS3.BindLocal(:cache, StzMemoryBlobStoreQ())
oD3 = new stzDelivery("shop")
oD3.AddBackend("api", "linux")
oD3.AddApp("web", "browser")
oD3.UseServicesQ(oS3)
oD3.NeedsServiceInQ("api", [ :payments, :cache ])
oD3.NeedsServiceInQ("web", [ :reporting ])
oD3.DeployTo(StzDeploymentSiteQ("host1"), "api")
? oS3.Phase()
#--> development
? len(oS3.Findings())
#--> 0
for aF in StzCheckServiceDelivery(oD3)
	? aF[:rule] + " @ " + aF[:where] + " [" + aF[:severity] + "]"
next
#--> production-part-uses-sandbox @ api [error]
#--> production-part-uses-ephemeral @ api [error]
#--> part-uses-undeclared-service @ web [warning]
```

> run: kept

It names `api` twice, with `[error]`, and `web` once, with `[warning]`, where the registry reported nothing!

That is what the run showed; the decisions behind it come next.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

Everything below is from the library's design documents and narrations, written in sessions with no author line. The record holds no ruling of my own for this chapter. Its decisions are the documents'.

**I refused the simulator that lies.** The emulator does not mock. Each part runs the same compiled engine artifact that will ship to its target. That claim has a caution, in section 7: the proof that the engine runs in a browser is not in the pinned tree.

**I refused to re-ship what the target does well.** The edge carries a capability only if it is critical and either unique or weak on the target.

**I refused tool sprawl, and the convention enforced by hope.** The target should be one config away, and deploying to it should be one governed act.

**I refused an inline secret.** A site carries an auth reference, not a key.

**I refused to touch reality by default.** Production commits only if the actor may commit. A bare `Deploy(:Production)` with no actor touches nothing.

**I refused the agreeable fake.** An agreeable fake is more dangerous than no fake at all. I hold this as a view, not a result. Silence is the worst answer: the lenient mode answers 501, because a miss must never be mistakable for success.

**I refused "fake versus real" as the whole truth.** It was too coarse. There are four postures now: sandbox, local, live, and conformance. The local posture was added when the database exemplar showed that a SQLite file is a real database in a file you own.

**I refused a gate that fails open.** The registry once kept its state in attributes. Attaching it to a delivery took a snapshot, so the gate would have passed an unsound surface. A gate that fails open is worse than no gate: this is my view. The state now sits in a table keyed by identity, so every copy is the registry. Anything that governs others must survive being copied.

**I refused to virtualize the quality of a model.** Frontier-model quality cannot be virtualized. What the sandbox sells instead is determinism, a cost you can assert, and working offline.

**I refused to ship the live adapters.** The service plan says the live Stripe, PayPal and Adyen adapter is deliberately not shipped, since an account, a key and a network are infrastructure-gated. A hub adapter is built, and chapter 31 shows it. The contract is defined, and the registry refuses to let the fake ship in its place.

**I refused to ask a question in the wrong frame.** `MayGoLive` once asked about soundness in the current phase, so with a fake bound it answered yes. Now it is asked in the production frame whatever the phase.

**I refused to say the restaurant's need was the design's origin.** The site says that the emulation and deployment designs take a solution as an example. It is an example, not the origin of the design. The sample solution in the documents carries a customer's product name, and I have renamed it in every block. Chapter 28 told the story, and I do not repeat it.

The service plan is headed "written 2026-07-23 in answer to the user's question". The question asks whether emulation covers databases, business APIs, frontier models and cloud providers. The aim is that a programmer can code a whole program without subscriptions or fees and still get a working solution ready to deploy. The chapter tells the plan's own heading and date. The record does not say who asked, and the chapter does not claim it was the author's question.

## 5. The court

`service_registry_narrated` proves the declared surface, that an unbound service raises, and that the application asks by name. It proves that a sandbox in production is a violation, with one finding per fake, and that the credential must be in the store. It proves that going live is a governed crossing, with an assistant refused and a person refused over a fake, and that asking leaves the phase. It also proves the CI helpers, the fourth posture, and a live adapter that needs a certificate. `service_delivery_narrated` proves the external surface rehearsed in the plan, and the pre-flight. It proves the gate, which in a production deploy refuses a fake and logs every reason, and opens once the surface is real. It proves that the registry is one registry however many copies are made, and the question of which part depends on a fake. My research run of 8 October 2026 ran both: 93 of 93 and 60 of 60. The second left a file of zero bytes beside itself, and I removed it.

`virtual_system_twin_narrated` proves, in twelve scenes, that the twin touches nothing real, that the plan narrates and flags, that commit is the one door, that a scope refuses, and that a reviewer rejects a step. It proves, too, that a hundred operations stay in memory until one commit. `virtual_environment_twin_narrated` proves the same shape for variables, a directory and queued spawns. `emulator_narrated` proves that the bundle generates, the map renders every part by role, and each part carries only its subset. `deployment_narrated` proves the site as config, the governed commit, the step plan, the rollback, feasibility, and the git backend; it spawns `git`, so the book does not run it. `full_scope_model_narrated`, scenes 13 to 16, proves that building precedes deploying, that an unsound solution cannot be built, the lowering bridge, and the governed deploy.

The refusal that production makes is proved by the first two guards, which ran. That the rehearsed engine runs in a browser is the next proof: no guard that runs checks it today.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

Real services lead. The plan says it: a double is not the real service, and you still need at least one real run before production. A scripted gateway is not a vendor's edge cases. Recorded answers drift, and a replay suite needs re-recording and contract tests against the real API. The library has the recording call; a drift check for recorded answers is designed.

Frontier models lead, and cannot be emulated. The model is judged against the real model, deliberately, as its own activity.

Live infrastructure leads. The deployment is infrastructure-gated: live verification against a real host or account. The commands are generated, and one reachable host away from literal.

The mature deploy tools lead. The narration says it plainly: this does not replace every mature deploy tool, it unifies the seams those tools leave exposed.

Fidelity has a stated boundary. Emulation is faithful but not omniscient, so it says which is which. What a double proves is narrower still: a capture sink proves what you would have sent, and a directory is not a CDN.

Where I think the record stands apart is the coupling. The double, the plan, the credential names, the gate and the actor are declared in the same program that ships, and one gate judges them.

That is the field; the limit is where I stop.

## 7. The limit

**A hand-written fake that says nothing about itself passes the gate.** The registry reads what the object says it is. A double that declares neither that it is a sandbox nor that it is local and real is taken for live: no opinion means live. So "production refuses a fake" is exact for the library's own doubles, and for any double that declares itself. What posture does the registry give a double that declares nothing, and is the surface sound? Ask it in the production phase:

```haro
oUndeclared = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oUndeclared.Bind(:payments, new AlwaysYes)
oUndeclared.SetPhase(:production)
? oUndeclared.PostureOf(:payments)
#--> live
? oUndeclared.IsSound()
#--> 1

class AlwaysYes
	def init
		return
	def Authorize nAmount, cToken
		return [ :status = "authorized" ]
```

> run: kept

It answers `live`, and `IsSound()` answers `1`: the always-yes double passed! The stand-in of the earlier block, `LiveGateway`, is such an object.

**The lowering knows two verbs.** Only `read_pin` and `write_pin` become code. Any other rehearsed operation becomes a comment line. In my research run of 8 October 2026, outside this chapter's blocks, I rehearsed `UseIn(:sensor, :gpio, "blink the status led")` and the firmware got a comment. For a server or a phone, the artifact is a header with no lines when nothing was rehearsed. The narration's "the board is the last inch" holds for reads and writes of pins only.

**The fidelity contract is by target class.** The design says each part declares what is faithful and what is approximated. The code returns one fixed text for a microcontroller, listing two approximated things, and one for everything else, "faithful".

**The sizes are estimates.** The plan prints a figure from a catalogue, and the narration prints two different figures for the same part. Do not print a size as a claim.

**`Deploy(:Emulated)` does not yet move the registry.** The plan says it resolves services to sandboxes. The code builds the emulator and returns; only the production deploy touches the registry's phase. The `:emulated` phase value that the registry has is set today only by a guard; setting it from the emulated deploy is designed.

**A refused `Deploy(:Production)` leaves the registry in the production phase.** After my refused deploy (my research run of 8 October 2026, outside this chapter's blocks), `Phase()` printed `production`. The pre-flight restores the phase, and the deploy does not.

**A plan flags today; ranking is the direction.** Chapter 20 said it, and the framework document says "ranked risk assessment".

What is designed: the engine running in a browser. The documents say that the phone's `solve 2 -8` returns `x = 4` in a page. The guard that checks it, `wasm_narrated`, says in its own header that it checks the wiring and the build, since the host language cannot execute wasm. It cites an in-browser page with "8/8 checks". That page is not in the pinned tree: I searched for it and found none. And I have not seen the emulator's surface in a browser, and the record names no one who has. By the law of chapter 33, that surface is unperceived. So the chapter says: wired, its build checked by a guard that I did not run, not seen in a browser by anyone the record names.

Also designed: branches of a twin and the plan's alternatives and feedback, for which no method is built yet; twins for databases, interfaces and networks; and a drift check for recorded answers.

What is absent: a live card-gateway adapter for payments (a hub adapter is built and proved only against a twin, chapter 31), a frontier model, an object store, SMS or mail. The contracts and doubles ship, and the live clients do not, deliberately. Live verification of a remote host or account: commands are generated, and nothing ran against a host. A customer solution emulated and deployed through this path: nothing in the record yet, which chapter 33 carries as the next line.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You code the whole integration against names, with doubles that raise when you forgot something and a counter that lets you assert a fee. The day you subscribe, you change a binding and not a line. The loss: a double you wrote yourself that declares nothing is taken for live by the gate.

**The analyst.** Before anything moves you can read the placement plan, the external dependencies with the credentials production will ask for by name, the step graph, and which part depends on a fake. The sizes are estimates, and the findings are by part.

**The designer.** The emulator generates a surface from the plan, with a parts grid by role and one window per part. I checked the generated text only, and never the look. Say "generated", and not "seen".

**The decision-maker.** "Who may deploy?" is a property. An assistant may plan the release and the site stays absent. A person may commit. Neither may ship a declared fake. The loss to print: live hosts and real providers are gated by infrastructure, and the record names no live run.

**The educator.** A learner can build a whole shop's integration with no account, no key and no fee, and watch the double refuse what she forgot. Her deployment fails safely and rolls back.

**The agent.** You may rehearse the whole integration and plan the whole release, in a world where nothing real can move: a sandbox payment moves no money and a mail sink sends no mail. You may ask whether you may go live and be told why not. You cannot commit. The loss: that guarantee covers the library's own doubles, and a surface bound to something live is outside it.

## 9. Exercise

Write, under each line, what you expect before you run it:

```haro exercise
oMine = new stzServiceRegistry("my-project")
oMine.Bind(:payments, new stzPaymentsSandbox())
oMine.Bind(:notes, StzMemoryDataSourceQ())
oMine.BindLive(:maps, new stzString("maps-adapter"), "maps_key")
oStoreMine = new stzSecretStore("my-project")
# under each line, write the line you expect before you run it
? oMine.IsSound()
? oMine.IsSoundVia(oStoreMine)
? len(oMine.FindingsForProductionVia(oStoreMine))
? oMine.MayGoLive(HumanActor("me"), oStoreMine)
? oMine.Phase()
```

> run: exercise

Then register a key named `maps_key` in the store, and write the number of production findings again. Then bind a hand-written class with an `Authorize` method that always says yes, and ask what posture the registry gives it. If a promise breaks, do not change it: explain the output first. I do not tell you where you will be surprised.

I left this one in your hands. The block below is the one from section 3, "Code first, subscribe later", with one name changed. I changed it on purpose, and I do not say what it does:

```haro exercise
oPay = new stzPaymentsSandbox()
oReg = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oReg.DeclareMany([ :payments, :mail ])
oReg.Bind(:payments, oPay)
oReg.Bind(:mail, new stzMailSandbox())
? Checkout(oReg, 4200)
? oPay.NumberOfAuthorizations()
oStoreCf = new stzSecretStore("shop")
oStoreCf.Register( (new stzApiKey("stripe-key")).FromLiteralQ("sk-test-123") )

func Checkout poReg, nCents
	aRes = poReg.Service(:payments).Authorize(nCents, "tok_visa")
	poReg.Service(:mail).Send("dana@example.com", "Receipt", "Thank you.")
	return aRes[:status]

class LiveGateway
	def init
		return
	def Authorize nAmount, cToken
		return [ :status = "authorized" ]

class LiveMailer
	def init
		return
	def Send cTo, cSubject, cBody
		return 1
```

> run: exercise

Run it. Compare the answer of its last line with the one section 3 gave, and explain any difference before you touch the name; section 3, "Production refuses", shows how to ask the registry for its report. When you have solved it, you will know what makes a live binding sound: a secret that the store holds, and not only a name.

The next chapter turns from the services behind a program to its users: your users keep their medium, and a new medium adds renderings, never verbs.


=====

# Your users keep their medium

Source: Chapter 25. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the governed conversation with its question as data; the SMS port and its sandbox, with the bill per segment in two alphabets; a verifier for the body of an inbound message; five semantic values rendered as colour, earcon and phrase; a voice that speaks, and hears a closed grammar; a closed set of sentences a business agrees to say; a message of three fields between identities of one platform; designed: the commons service of messaging; conversational surfaces for a messaging app, SMS, USSD and voice, with a satisfiability matrix as a court; templates as a grammar; absent: a messaging app, USSD or a telephone line, in any form; a conversational projection of a world; a channel, a session, a template, consent; the question in a language other than English; a voice for Hausa, Zarma or Fulfulde; the twenty-two verbs as a list the library holds; a reply with a space in it

Run: 11 of 11 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:15 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take one sentence of the interface constitution, a new medium adds renderings, never verbs, and I run it over the medium I can measure. A question becomes a line of text, goes out over an SMS sandbox, and is billed per segment in two alphabets. I show what the library ships, the question as data and the court, and what you write, the rendering and the reader. I report the record's refusals as the record's: a conversation that needs no model, and a voice that hears commands and not dictation. And I print the limit whole: no messaging app, no USSD, no telephone line, and a reply with a space in it, which the conversation cannot yet take.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Conversational commerce, or omnichannel: a business reaches its customers in the channels they already use, and a dialogue engine sits behind them. These are the practices, from general knowledge and not from a study of the record. Every date and every provider rule is to be re-read before print, as the study of the record says in its own "Not measured". That study is an outside reader's long study of the project's roots, and its section 12.25 is the one about messaging.

The dialogue frameworks own the dialogue model. The designer writes intents and flows in the framework's notation, and the framework understands free text with trained models and ships connectors to channels.

The business platform of a messaging app: messages arrive by a webhook, and the business may answer freely inside a service window that the customer opened. Outside it, only approved templates, through a verified business account.

The operators' gateways: SMS and USSD. SMS is billed per segment, and I measure this myself below. USSD is a session of short screens with a timeout, through an operator's gateway, country by country, on any phone without data. No file of the snapshot says anything about USSD.

And platform speech, in the library's own plan: Windows has one speech interface, macOS has another, and every modern browser has a third.

What they share is that the dialogue is written once per channel or once per framework, and the channel is a connector, a deployment detail. The chapter turns that picture around. The world is declared once, and the medium is a rendering of it.

## 2. The first principle

**A new medium adds renderings, never verbs.**

It is a sentence of the interface constitution, printed on the site: "The grammar is closed: a new medium adds renderings, never verbs." The record is silent on whether the wording is mine. I quote it as the constitution's. I do not tell it as my own sentence. The twenty-two verbs it speaks of are the constitution's, in six families. Appendix C prints them as I read them from the constitution's own file on 9 October 2026. The repository is unnamed, since it is not public yet.

The same sentence, built, for one family of values and not for the twenty-two verbs: the value is shared, and how it is rendered is each medium's own. It is a ruling by Central, the estate's coordination entity, on my delegation, of 30 September 2026. It proves that the principle is not only a slogan. It is the shape of three working faces: colour, earcon, phrase.

For the floor of the dialogue, the code's own sentence: deterministic floor, no model needed. Chapter 17's law, applied to a medium.

## 3. The Softanza form

The library ships the question as data: a force, a subject, a relation, options and a reason. It ships the verbs, ask, reply, refuse and conclude, and the court. A medium is a rendering of the data and a reader of the reply, both written by the crafter in a few lines. Nothing in the library renders a question to a phone. What it ships is an English line, `AskIn`, which is already a text menu: "(1) cheese (2) olives". I say that in those words.

Two small functions of my own are the whole of the medium: one that renders the question to a line of text, and one that reads the reply. The reading matters, and I show why in the third block. What does one question cost, in Latin letters and in Arabic? Run it:

```haro
aEn = QuestionAfter("couscous", "brik")
aAr = QuestionAfter("كسكس", "بريك")
? ToText(aEn)
#--> Which dish for order3? (every order needs 'dish') 1) couscous 2) brik. Reply with the number.
? StzSmsSegments(ToText(aEn))[:segments]
#--> 1
? StzSmsSegments(ToText(aAr))[:encoding]
#--> ucs2
? StzSmsSegments(ToText(aAr))[:segments]
#--> 2
oBill = new stzSmsSandbox()
oBill.Send("+22790112233", ToText(aEn))
oBill.Send("+22790112233", ToText(aAr))
? oBill.TotalSegments()
#--> 3

func ToText(aQ)
	cOut = "Which " + aQ[:relation] + " for " + aQ[:subject] + "? (" + aQ[:why] + ")"
	for i = 1 to len(aQ[:options])
		cOut += " " + i + ") " + aQ[:options][i]
	next
	return cOut + ". Reply with the number."

func ReadReply(cText)
	for c in cText
		if NOT isdigit(c)
			return cText
		ok
	next
	return 0 + cText

func QuestionAfter(cA, cB)
	oW = new stzKnowledgeGraph("stall")
	oW.Know("order1", "order").Know("order2", "order").Know("order3", "order")
	oW.AddConversationQ("c").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("order", "dish"))
	oW.AskIn("c")
	oW.ReplyIn("c", cA)
	oW.AskIn("c")
	oW.ReplyIn("c", cB)
	return oW.AskInXT("c")
```

> run: kept

The question is data, so any medium renders it. And the medium's own law, here that SMS is billed per segment and the alphabet decides, is a test and not a surprise. The English text is 93 characters by my count, and the block prints one segment. The same frame with two Arabic dish names is 89 units by my count (the two names are four letters shorter). The block prints `ucs2` and two segments, because the encoding changes. Four letters shorter, and one segment more! The telephone number is invented, and only the country code, which is Niger's, is real.

Now the whole conversation, rehearsed over the SMS sandbox before any account exists. A conversation is a loop of the library's verbs: the medium carries each question out and each reply in. The assertions are the bill and the refusal. What does the sandbox count, and what does the court say to "7"? Nothing in the loop is a model:

```haro
oKB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("stall")
oKB.Know("order1", "order").Know("order2", "order").Know("order3", "order")
oKB.AddConversationQ("c").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("order", "dish"))
oSms = new stzSmsSandbox()
for cIn in [ "couscous", "brik", "7", "2" ]
	oSms.Send("+22790112233", ToText(oKB.AskInXT("c")))
	oKB.ReplyIn("c", ReadReply(cIn))
next
? oKB.ConversationQ("c").GoalState()
#--> fulfilled
? oSms.NumberOfMessages()
#--> 4
? oSms.TotalSegments()
#--> 4
? oKB.ConversationQ("c").Checkpoints()[1][:why]
#--> no option (7) was offered -- 2 option(s) on the table
? @@(oKB.Query([ "order3", "dish", "?o" ]))
#--> [ "brik" ]
```

> run: kept

Four messages, four segments, and the court's reason verbatim: `no option (7) was offered -- 2 option(s) on the table`. The refusal of "7" is the library's own, as in chapter 18; the difference is that it arrived from a phone.

**The reader of the medium is the medium's job.** An inbound SMS is a string. The library separates numbers, which are option indices, from strings, which are values, and it does not guess. Its own comment: an index can never be mistaken for a literal value. So the medium's reader must decide which it is. With no options on the table, what becomes of the text "2"? Look:

```haro
oKT = new stzKnowledgeGraph("trap")
oKT.Know("order1", "order")
oKT.AddConversationQ("c").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("order", "dish"))
oKT.AskIn("c")
? @@(oKT.ReplyIn("c", "2")[:admitted])
#--> [ "2" ]
```

> run: kept

A dish named 2! In the block before, my `ReadReply` turned the text "2" into the number 2, and the option was picked. This is the sharpest sentence about the bridge: a medium adds renderings, and it adds a reading too. The reading is where a user's culture lives, the digits, the words, a local "OUI". I hold this as a view, not a result.

**An outage is not a rejection.** The medium refuses in its own terms. A malformed number, with no `+`, is not an E.164 number: fix the data. An outage is another matter: retry. What does the sandbox answer to each?

```haro
oPh = new stzSmsSandbox()
? oPh.Send("0612345678", "ready")[:status]
#--> rejected
oPh.FailNextQ()
? oPh.Send("+22790112233", "ready")[:status]
#--> refused
? oPh.NumberOfMessages()
#--> 0
? oPh.Send("+22790112233", "ready")[:status]
#--> sent
? oPh.NumberOfMessages()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

`rejected` for the number, `refused` for the outage, and nothing counted. The retry is `sent`, and the count is one.

**An inbound message is believed only after its signature is verified.** The way in is the pattern the payments plane built, and the verifier is generic, over the body. No provider's header or body format is modelled, and the JSON is invented. Times are passed in, so no clock is read. What does the verifier name when one character of the body has changed since it was signed?

```haro
oSig = new stzRequestSigner("inbound")
oSig.AddKey("provider", "a-shared-secret")
cBody = '{"from":"+22790112233","text":"2"}'
cSig = oSig.SignBody("provider", cBody)
? oSig.VerifyWebhook(cBody, cSig, 1000, 5000)
#--> provider
? "[" + oSig.VerifyWebhook('{"from":"+22790112233","text":"1"}', cSig, 2000, 5000) + "]"
#--> []
? oSig.Why()
#--> signature mismatch (tampered body, or a secret this receiver does not hold)
```

> run: kept

The first body gave `provider`. The altered one gave `[]`, and the reason is `signature mismatch (tampered body, or a secret this receiver does not hold)`. One character changed, and no provider was named! A replayed body inside the window gave "replay detected" in my probe.

**One value, many renderings.** The bridge's shape, built for the five semantic values of the interface constitution. Colour, earcon and phrase render one value. `muted` is silence in sound and a treatment in colour. A value the law does not know is refused:

```haro
? @@(StzSemanticValues())
#--> [ "danger", "warning", "info", "success", "muted" ]
oE = new stzEarcons()
? oE.WouldFireAt("Catastrophe", 1)
#--> 0
? StzColorToNumber("danger.muted") != StzColorToNumber("success.muted")
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

**The voice: spoken, heard, meant.** Text to sound, sound to text, text to meaning. The medium called voice renders and reads with a closed grammar, and the meaning is my declaration. This block needs a French voice and a French recogniser. This machine speaks two languages and hears one, and a machine without them would give another answer, so the output shown is the one recorded on this machine. No microphone, no file, no network: the recogniser is the in-process one. What does it make of a phrase it was taught, and of one it was not?

```haro
oV = StzVoiceQ()
oV.UseLanguage("fr-FR")
oL = new stzListener()
oL.AcceptMeanings([ [ "disque plein", :Danger ], [ "sauvegarde terminee", :Success ] ])
oL.HearSound( oV.ToSoundOf("disque plein") )
? oL.MeaningHeard()
#--> danger
oL.HearSound( oV.ToSoundOf("je voudrais un cafe au lait") )
? oL.WasNoMatch()
#--> 1
? oL.AcceptMeanings([ [ "tout brule", :Catastrophe ] ])
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The taught phrase gave `danger`, the phrase it was not taught gave no match (`1`), and `Catastrophe` was refused (`0`). The refusal's reason says that `catastrophe` is not one of the five semantic values. I do not print the recogniser's confidence, which varies by machine.

**The world declares its verbs and its reach, and its code names no medium.** A world holds screens declared by intent, five of the twenty-two verbs, and a list of what it reaches. Nothing refuses `:whatsapp` when it is declared:

```haro
oApp = new stzApp("stall")
oApp.AddThingQ(:Dish) { Has([ :name, :price ]) }
oApp.AddScreenQ(:Menu) { ToDiscover(:Dish)  Shows([ :name, :price ]) }
oApp.AddScreenQ(:Basket) { ToSelect(:Dish)  Acts(:Confirm, :Order) }
oApp.AddReaches([ :web, :whatsapp ])
oApp.Explain()
#--> WORLD stall   lives in: memory (not persisted)
#      BEING
#        dish (name, price)
#      MET FROM WITHOUT
#        screen menu: discover dish shows name, price
#        screen basket: select dish
#        reaches web, whatsapp
```

> run: kept

`Acts(...)` is stored and not narrated. And the stage as a run: what does the platform say when it is asked to generate the surface the world declared? This block creates an empty folder in the working directory:

```haro
oPlat = StzPlatformQ("stall-platform")
oPlat.SetWorld(oApp)
try
	oPlat.Generate(:whatsapp)
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> stzPlatform.Generate: unknown reach surface 'whatsapp' (web/desktop/mobile).
```

> run: kept

`unknown reach surface 'whatsapp' (web/desktop/mobile)`: the platform refused it! It names the three surfaces it has, and the same refusal answers `:ussd`.

**What may be said to a customer is a closed set.** The court of what is said has a mechanism today: a closed enumeration compiled to a grammar. Chapters 11 and 19 use it for a language and for a model's output. Here it holds the replies that a business is willing to send. It is not a messaging-template unit, and there is none. What does the court say to a sentence the business never approved? Two things to print with it: the message says "the model answered" whoever the speaker is, and the enumeration is case-folded:

```haro
oSay = StzOutputSchemaQ([ [ :field = "reply", :type = :oneof, :choices = [ "Your order is received.", "Your order is ready.", "We are closed tonight." ] ] ])
? oSay.ParseOutput('{"reply": "Your order is ready."}')[:ok]
#--> 1
aNo = oSay.ParseOutput('{"reply": "Your order is free, tonight only."}')
? aNo[:ok]
#--> 0
? oSay.CiteFindings(aNo[:findings])
#--> [oneof @ reply] field 'reply' is a closed enumeration (your order is received. | your order is ready. | we are closed tonight.) and the model answered 'Your order is free, tonight only.'.
```

> run: kept

The approved sentence passed (`1`), the invented one was refused (`0`), and the court's reason begins `[oneof @ reply] field 'reply' is a closed enumeration`.

### The restaurant's medium

Chapter 1 says that the owner of the restaurant made his first demonstration over a messaging app. The record does not support that sentence as it stands, and I flag it for the author. Chapter 2 already gives his tool as a spreadsheet. His own report of 4 July 2026 gives his current tool as a spreadsheet. It asks for a web page opened by a QR code or by a button for a social network. Nothing heavy to download. The line about various apps, a messaging app among them, is the label of an option in the scoping form, and it is not what he ticked. "Simple like a messaging app" is simulated in the project, as chapter 28 already found.

So the restaurant is the first form of the sentence of this chapter, and not an example of the messaging medium. The customer keeps their medium: a link that opens in the browser they already have. The messaging medium, as a thesis, is unbuilt. I tell this story and make no statement about the owner's use of a messaging app, since the record holds none. Chapter 1 keeps its own wording of that demonstration, and what the record shows more exactly is what this section says.

That is the form, built and run; next come what I decided, and what I refused.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

The refusals below, after the first, are the record's: a session's decisions, written in the headers of the library's sound files and in its voice plan. I report them as such, and they are not rulings of mine.

**Messaging, the question.** The study's report of my ask: conversations happen programmatically by default, or through minimal configuration, without obliging the users to change their usability cultures. The study's section is titled "ruled" and not "ruled by the author", and its proposals for the conversational surfaces are a session's. The record holds no ruling of mine that adopts them. I say *proposed*, not *decided*.

**A conversation needs no model.** The floor is deterministic. A model can upgrade the fluency behind the same surface.

**Voice is not a paradigm.** The plan of record says out until asked in writing: all of dialogue management, wake words, intent parsing and barge-in.

**Commands, not transcription: a measured refusal.** The gate was set before the numbers: free dictation ships at eighty per cent exact and 0.60 mean confidence. The run gave 66.7 per cent and 0.600, which failed. The closed grammar gave a hundred per cent and 0.898, which passed. So this face has no `Transcribe()`, and never will until a better recogniser exists.

**Refuse, never substitute.** Speaking French to an operator who asked for English is worse than saying nothing.

**No inferred meaning.** The author still declares the meaning. A sixth value would be a constitutional amendment wearing a library's clothes.

**A sink proves what you would have sent.** The SMS port's honest limit: carrier filtering, sender-ID rules, per-country alphabet quirks, and asynchronous delivery receipts. None of it is modelled here.

**The screen acknowledges, and the sound is not the acknowledgement.**

The next section names the guards that stand behind these runs.

## 5. The court

`sms_port_narrated` proves the segment law, with its boundaries at 160 and 161, 306 and 307, the emoji cliff at 100 to 102 units, 71 Arabic letters making two segments, and the nine extension characters. It proves E.164, the sink and its bill, the outage against the rejection, and the refusal in production. I ran it once, and 53 of 53 assertions passed. `wisecoding_rich_narrated` is chapter 18's: the frame, the numbered options, the refusal of a number nobody offered, the checkpoints with a lifetime. `payments_webhooks_narrated` proves that an inbound signature is verified before it is believed: unsigned, forged, replayed. `sound_semantics_narrated` proves that the five values are the law's, that four sound and `muted` is silent, and that an invented value is refused. `sound_listener_narrated` proves the closed grammar, that no match is a result, and that the confidence is a separate call; I ran it once, and 21 passed. `sound_convergence_narrated` proves that the loop closes for the four states, that the language is negotiated per direction, that a sixth value is refused at declaration, and that a stranger's phrase gives no meaning.

A caveat for the court line. The sound guards need the engine's sound library, and for the loop, a voice and a recogniser. On a machine without them the voice guards print "skipped" and pass. A pass there proves nothing about speech. The guards for SMS and for the conversation have no such dependency.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The record's own count, in the study of 12.25: of the constituent units of messaging, eighteen in all, what is missing. The study's sentence is that the word for a messaging app appears in no code file and no design document of the library, and USSD in none. I re-measured it at this commit and confirmed it. There is no hit in the code or the design of base, core or max. The matches in the rest of the snapshot are word-list and test data files, and USSD has none in text.

The frameworks lead in understanding and in connectors. Chapter 18 states Softanza's side from the code: the conversation asks one slot at a time, over a graph the user must first have declared. It does not understand an open reply.

The field leads in channel coverage and in delivery truth, receipts and provider-approved templates. The SMS port says it itself: a sink proves what you would have sent, not that a carrier would accept it, and the live adapter is infrastructure-gated. No live client for SMS ships.

The voice plan: a declaration is portable, and a voice is not. A browser's voice has no buffer, so the loop closes on the native tier only. This machine can speak English and cannot hear it.

What the field has in cloud dictation and in trained recognisers for many languages, the record chose not to ship. Free dictation reached 66.7 per cent on the plane's own clean synthetic audio.

What the record claims instead: the dialogue is a rendering of a declared world judged by a court, deterministic by default. The medium is a port with a sandbox, so the conversation and its bill are rehearsed before an account exists.

The field leads in places, and the limit comes next, printed whole.

## 7. The limit

The study (12.25) has two errors that this chapter's runs correct. The first: it says speech to text is missing. At this commit it exists, by decision, for commands and not for dictation. The second: it says no message exists. There is a message of three fields, between identities of one platform, and I show it:

```haro
oDb = new stzDatabase(":memory:")
oCom = StzPlatformQ("neighbourhood")
oCom.OpenCommonsOn(oDb)
oCom.PostMessage("customer", "kitchen", "one brik, no egg")
? @@(oCom.Inbox("kitchen"))
#--> [ [ "customer", "one brik, no egg" ] ]
```

> run: kept

It is a sender, a recipient, a body and a time in a SQL table. It has no medium, no status and no parts. It is the message the study found absent, in its smallest form.

What is built: the governed conversation; the SMS port and its sandbox; a body-signature verifier with replay protection, wired to no messaging provider; the semantic layer of five values rendered as colour, earcon and phrase. Also built: the voice; a minimal message in the commons; a closed set of sentences with a refusal; and the world's declared intents and reaches.

What is designed: `Provides(:Messaging)` as a commons service of the constellation. One design file says so, in two lines: a diagram that lists messaging among the commons, and a sketch that writes `Provides(:Messaging)`. The word `Provides` is in no code file of the application folder. The commons runtime as the operational counterpart of the declared provides and shares. Conversational surfaces for a messaging app, SMS, USSD, voice and web chat, the satisfiability matrix as a court, templates as a grammar, a channel in the platform profile: these are proposals B46 to B50 of the study. They are a session's, and not the library's design. A spoken analysis and a spoken transport: demos and compositions, and not a unit.

What is absent:

- Ports for a messaging app, Telegram, USSD, and the telephone call: zero hits. A USSD session has no shape in the library.
- A conversational projection of a world. The only projection is `Generate`, which knows three surfaces and refuses a fourth. No shell renders a question or a flow.
- The messaging units: channel, session with a window or a timeout, template, consent. No unit of consent exists for messaging. The word *consent* appears in the snapshot for the microphone's boundary, in comments, and in two design documents in other senses.
- The twenty-two verbs as a library list. `stzApp` holds five of them as five screen-intent methods, and the helper behind them does not check the word it is given. The other seventeen are in the constitution and not in the library. The bridge sentence is applied by hand today.
- The question in a language other than English. `AskIn` is English, and the tutor shows the pattern in four languages. In a research run at this commit, `NaturallyIn` asked for Zarma raised the opaque "Unsupported object type!" and not "no pack for this language"; no block of this book records that call.
- A voice for the languages that the product designs name, Zarma, Hausa and Fulfulde. None here; the library would refuse.
- **A reply with a space in it, from a phone.** In a research run at this commit I ran `ReplyIn("c", "grand couscous")` and it raised, with an uninitialised variable. With a hyphen it was admitted. No block of this book records either result, as chapter 18 says of the same fault. This is chapter 18's known limit, and it is decisive here, because people type spaces in a text message. The conversation above works only because the replies are digits and one-word dishes.
- Live adapters for SMS, for a messaging app and for a telephone line; delivery receipts; sender-ID rules.
- Nothing in the record runs on a real phone as a conversation.

`On(:whatsapp)`, `On(:ussd)`, `On(:voice)` and the satisfiability matrix are what a study proposed, and I do not present them as what I mean to build. I found no decision of mine to build them in the record, and the limit is the list above, in which none of them exists.

That is the limit, printed whole; the six doors follow.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** The question comes back as data. You write the rendering and the reader for your medium in a dozen lines, and every reply goes through the same door whatever carried it.

**The analyst.** The goal is the schema of the conversation. The transcript says which message produced which fact. The sandbox counts the bill before a provider exists.

**The designer.** Your user keeps the phone they have: a text line with numbers, or a spoken phrase. The constitution's verbs are the grammar you render, and a verb the medium cannot render should be a red cell, and not a silent omission. I hold this as a view, not a result. That is designed and not built, since the matrix does not exist.

**The decision-maker.** A conversation by SMS can be priced in a test: two Arabic dish names moved a 93-character question from one segment to two. The question to ask a vendor, what refuses a sentence your business did not approve, has an answer here: a closed set.

**The educator.** The tutor already says the missing step as a question in four languages, in templates that carry no code. The record does not name a learner on a plain phone, and nothing serves one yet: there is no USSD and no messaging app.

**The agent.** You may propose a reply, and only a sentence of the closed set passes. An invented one is refused with its reason. And you cannot give a meaning to a phrase: the author declares it.

## 9. Exercise

Take a question your own world asks. The one-relation world of chapter 18's exercise will do. Before you run anything, write under each line the output you expect:

1. the number of segments of your rendering of the question, with names in the Latin alphabet;
2. its segments if one option is a word in Arabic script;
3. what happens when the customer's reply is a digit that was not offered;
4. what happens when the reply is "yes please".

```haro exercise
? StzSmsSegments(YourRendering)[:segments]
#--> your prediction
? StzSmsSegments(YourRenderingWithOneArabicWord)[:segments]
#--> your prediction
```

> run: exercise

Run it, with the sandbox and never a real send, so that the exercise touches nothing. If a promise breaks, do not change it: explain the output first.

I left this one in your hands. The fourth prediction is a defect I did not fix, and section 7 lists it in bold: a reply with a space in it, chapter 18's known limit. Take the block of section 3 that sends a reply to a question with no options on the table, change the reply "2" to a text with a space in it, and run it:

```haro exercise
oKT = new stzKnowledgeGraph("trap")
oKT.Know("order1", "order")
oKT.AddConversationQ("c").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireEach("order", "dish"))
oKT.AskIn("c")
? @@(oKT.ReplyIn("c", "grand couscous")[:admitted])
```

> run: exercise

Look at what the call does, and do not repair it. Section 7 says why it matters on a phone. When you have explained the output, you will know why the conversation above works only because its replies are digits and one-word dishes.

The next chapter is the environment. It declares the interface once, and every shell is a projection of it; a projection, like a medium, may add renderings, never verbs.


=====

# The environment

Source: Chapter 26. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the interface as a text with a court that finds the element serving no verb; two projections of it, native and web, with a recorded table of divergences and a page that can say DISAGREE; the accessibility tree emitted from the declaration; a page the language serves on the loopback; a world that proposes and whose proposals clear when it is acted on; screens declared as intents; one generated browser surface; designed: the shell kit with two shells; the chrome as closed kinds (spine, ribbon, rail, status bar), none yet a class of the library; the facet as data; a rail bound to focus; status cells read from the courts, with a guard that a cell never shows a state the court did not emit; absent: an IDE of this family; a list of the twenty-two verbs in the library and a refusal of an unknown verb; a guard that judges a shell against the verbs; an editor; a measured latency

Run: 10 of 10 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:15 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take the shape the field's environments have settled on, a toolbar, a sidebar, an editor and a status bar, and I set against it a pattern in five marks. I run the pieces the library has under those marks: a shell declared as text and judged, two projections, a ribbon in the order of a life, a rail that proposes, a status bar that tells the truth. I say what I decided, to carry both shells, and what I refused, among it a flag that turns the accessibility tree off. And I say where it stands: at this commit, an IDE of this family is absent, and the pieces beneath it run. The principle is one sentence: declare the interface once, and every shell is a projection of it that may add renderings, never verbs.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field's environments have settled on a shape. A toolbar and a menu at the top, a sidebar of files, an editor, a terminal below, and a status bar at the foot. Lately an assistant sits in a pane or a terminal beside the code, and the human approves its edits. (General knowledge, marked.)

The record's own field studies, dated, are three. The notepad that ships with the former host language, measured on 12 August 2026 at thirty-three source files and about four thousand lines, seven dock widgets, a fifteen-key configuration and no language service. It exists, it ships in the box, and it works on the day you install it. The fast editors, in the ergonomics specification of March 2026, which sets for its own product: input to screen under sixteen milliseconds on any modern hardware, no exceptions. It wants minimal chrome, no status bar clutter, no toolbar forests. It asks for a dry run before a structural change, and for inspections that say why and offer a fix. And the status bar, as that specification wrote it: twenty-four pixels high, carrying only essential state, with five build states, one of which is *unchecked*, for a project where no build has run yet. The state of "not yet judged" has its own word. A bar that was green by default would be the field's usual lie. That is my view.

Where the field is silent, the chapter answers. The field orders its toolbars by the tool's features, and not by the life of the thing being made. And it lets an assistant's proposal land as an edit, with the human's approval as a habit. (General knowledge.)

## 2. The first principle

**Declare the interface once. Every shell is a projection of it, and a projection may add renderings, never verbs.**
