The library's header for its authored interface says it: the file is the contract, and the native markup and the web page are its projections. The study of the record (its sections 12.23 to 12.25) reads it as one declaration, two projections, proved to agree, resting on my ruling of 7 October 2026: carry both. The companion sentence is the interface constitution's, on the site: a new medium adds renderings, never verbs.

## 3. The Softanza form

First the stage, before the blocks. At this commit, **an IDE of this family is absent**. No class in the library is yet named for a ribbon, a rail, a spine or a status bar. The study's own stage line, proposed for the site, says it: no IDE of this family ships today; one shell does, for the sound plane. The foundation that would make the next one a projection is specified and not yet built. My reading agrees, with one addition: the library generates one browser surface, the emulator's, and I show it below with a loss.

What the library has are the pieces under each of the pattern's five marks. The five are one shell, two projections, a facet ribbon ordered by the life of the solution, a rail that proposes and a status bar that tells the truth. They run, and I take them in order.

### One shell, declared as text, judged by a court

The chrome is declared as text and judged before it is shown. The ribbon is generated from data, here a plain list; the facet as a library object is designed and not built. The four facet names are four of the five phases of the design corpus in the record: define, architect, build, govern, ship. I took four to keep the block short. Every declaration must carry its rationale, and a declaration without one is refused. Which element serves no verb? Run it:

```haro
aFacets = [ "Define", "Build", "Govern", "Ship" ]
cTabs = ""
cIds = ""
for cF in aFacets
	cId = "tab_" + lower(cF)
	cIds += cId + ", "
	cTabs += 'DEFINE TEXT ' + cId + ' ( CONTENT "' + cF + '", ROLE tab, SATISFIES select ) RATIONALE "A facet."' + nl
next
cShell = 'DEFINE PANEL shell ( SIZE [640, 360], DIRECTION column, FONT "app", SATISFIES locate, CHILDREN [ribbon, work, status] ) RATIONALE "One shell, three bands."' + nl +
	'DEFINE BOX ribbon ( HEIGHT 32, DIRECTION row, ROLE tablist, SATISFIES discover, CHILDREN [' + cIds + '] ) RATIONALE "Facets, in the order of a life."' + nl + cTabs +
	'DEFINE BOX work ( HEIGHT fill, DIRECTION row, CHILDREN [page, rail] ) RATIONALE "The workspace and its rail."' + nl +
	'DEFINE TEXT page ( CONTENT "the work", SATISFIES read ) RATIONALE "What is being made."' + nl +
	'DEFINE BOX rail ( WIDTH 160, ROLE group, SATISFIES preview, CHILDREN [advice] ) RATIONALE "What the court proposes."' + nl +
	'DEFINE TEXT advice ( CONTENT "no proposal", SATISFIES understand ) RATIONALE "The rail, empty."' + nl +
	'DEFINE BOX status ( HEIGHT 24, ROLE status, SATISFIES understand, CHILDREN [state] ) RATIONALE "The truth of the build."' + nl +
	'DEFINE TEXT state ( CONTENT "clean", SATISFIES understand ) RATIONALE "The court verdict."'
oShell = new stzUiDocument(cShell)
? oShell.IsClean()
#--> 1
? len(oShell.Declarations())
#--> 12
? @@( oShell.Orphans() )
#--> [ "work" ]
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

`Orphans()` answers `[ "work" ]`, and `IsClean()` answers `1` over the `12` declarations.

The generating loop is the book's own, and the roles are from the closed list in the library: `tablist`, `tab`, `group` and `status` are on it.

### The twenty-two verbs

Every element must trace to at least one verb, and a word that is not one of the twenty-two is found. There is a division of labour. The library records the claim, with `SATISFIES`, and finds the untraced elements, with `Orphans()`. It does not hold the list and does not refuse an unknown intent: the guard asserts that an intent nobody has defined is not an error. So the list below is typed from the constitution, which is in its own repository, and the book holds it. Does the court refuse an intent that is not on the list? Run it:

```haro
cShell = StzReplace(cShell, "DEFINE BOX work ( HEIGHT fill,", "DEFINE BOX work ( SATISFIES focus, HEIGHT fill,")
oShell = new stzUiDocument(cShell)
? @@( oShell.Orphans() )
#--> [ ]
aVerbs = [ "discover", "understand", "locate", "focus", "filter", "compare", "read", "scan", "scroll", "zoom", "select", "highlight", "preview", "mark", "act", "confirm", "cancel", "undo", "pause", "resume", "retry", "exit" ]
? len(aVerbs)
#--> 22
aStrangers = []
for cIntent in oShell.SatisfiedIntents()
	if not StzContains(aVerbs, cIntent)
		aStrangers + cIntent
	ok
next
? @@( aStrangers )
#--> [ ]
oOdd = new stzUiDocument(StzReplace(cShell, "SATISFIES preview", "SATISFIES transfer_funds"))
? oOdd.IsClean()
#--> 1
aStrangers = []
for cIntent in oOdd.SatisfiedIntents()
	if not StzContains(aVerbs, cIntent)
		aStrangers + cIntent
	ok
next
? @@( aStrangers )
#--> [ "transfer_funds" ]
```

> run: kept

With `SATISFIES focus` on `work`, `Orphans()` answers `[ ]`, and so does the loop over the book's list. Asked about `transfer_funds`, `IsClean()` answers `1`! The loop over the book's list answers `[ "transfer_funds" ]`.

`work` was a pure layout box, and the court counts containers too. Whether a container should be exempt from the verbs is the constitution's to say, and not the library's. The families, from the constitution: orienting, attention, information, selection, action, and continuity. And the interpretation I give: the library finds the stranger, and a list the library does not hold cannot refuse it. Chapter 25 said that the bridge sentence is applied by hand today. I tell the removal as the refusal at the centre of this chapter, in section 4: the library does not own the verbs, the constitution does. The library's own design text still says twenty-four; it is not the list. The twenty-two are printed in the block above, typed from the constitution as Appendix C prints them.

### Two projections

The same text yields the native markup and the web page. Every declared element is in both, and the two spell a property differently exactly where the profile records a divergence. How many names are in both? Count them:

```haro
cRml = oShell.ToRml()
cHtml = oShell.ToHtml()
nBoth = 0
for cName in oShell.Names()
	if StzContains(cRml, 'id="' + cName + '"') and StzContains(cHtml, 'id="' + cName + '"')
		nBoth++
	ok
next
? nBoth
#--> 11
? len(oShell.Names()) - 1
#--> 11
? StzContains(cRml, "--rmlui-direction")
#--> 1
? StzContains(cHtml, "--rmlui-direction")
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

Eleven elements and not twelve: the panel is the page itself, and not a `div`. The last two lines show where the two differ. The native markup holds `--rmlui-direction` (`1`), and the web page does not (`0`). The native tier lays out through the engine's layout library, vendored, and draws through the graphics plane. I did not open a window.

### The browser shell

The browser shell is a page that the language serves, on the loopback. One request, one process, no network beyond 127.0.0.1. A second reactor plays the client, so it is offline by construction. The handler reads a global, because lambdas of the host language do not capture locals. Where does the page live? Ask the server:

```haro
$cShellPage = cHtml
oSrv = new stzAppServer()
oSrv.Get_("/", func oReq, oResp { oResp.Html($cShellPage) })
oSrv.Start(0, "127.0.0.1")
oClient = new stzReactor()
cCRLF = char(13) + char(10)
nJob = oClient.SubmitTcp("127.0.0.1", oSrv.Port(), "GET / HTTP/1.1" + cCRLF + "Host: local" + cCRLF + "Connection: close" + cCRLF + cCRLF)
? oSrv.ServeOne(3000)
#--> 1
cAnswer = oClient.AwaitTcp(nJob, 5000)
? StzContains(cAnswer, "200 OK")
#--> 1
? StzContains(cAnswer, 'id="status"')
#--> 1
? oSrv.Host()
#--> 127.0.0.1
oSrv.Stop()
```

> run: kept

`Host()` answers `127.0.0.1`. The cost of this shape is named in the record. A locally bound port is a surface and must be treated as one. In the charter's words, the design intent has been measured for a sound studio and not for an IDE. The library's backend already refuses to leave the loopback unauthenticated, at bind time; the bare server above does not check. What does the backend do with `0.0.0.0`? Try it:

```haro
oT = new stzAppTopology("shell")
oT.AddDataset("notes", [ [ "first" ] ])
oT.SetDatasetColumns("notes", [ "text" ])
oBackend = new stzAppBackend("shell", oT)
bRaised = FALSE
try
	oBackend.StartOn("0.0.0.0", 0)
catch
	bRaised = TRUE
done
? bRaised
#--> 1
? oBackend.IsLive()
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

It raises! `bRaised` answers `1`, and `IsLive()` answers `0`.

### A status bar that tells the truth

A status cell is a function of the court's verdict, so it cannot say *clean* about a document the court refused. The function below is the book's own, over the library's `IsClean()` and its diagnostics. The status-cell class is not yet in the library, and the guard that a cell shows only what the court emitted is designed. The specification also names the state before any build, *unchecked*, and my function does not carry it. What does the cell say for a child declared nowhere, and for a role that is not on the list? Run it:

```haro
? ShellStatus(cShell)
#--> clean
? ShellStatus(StzReplace(cShell, "[page, rail]", "[page, ghost]"))
#--> refused: DANGLING_REFERENCE
? ShellStatus(StzReplace(cShell, "ROLE status", "ROLE sttus"))
#--> refused: UNKNOWN_ROLE

func ShellStatus(cText)
	oD = new stzUiDocument(cText)
	if oD.IsClean()
		return "clean"
	ok
	return "refused: " + oD.Diagnostics()[1][:code]
```

> run: kept

The cell answers `refused: DANGLING_REFERENCE` and `refused: UNKNOWN_ROLE`, and *clean* only for the document the court kept.

And what a screen reader reads is emitted from the declaration. A region declared `ROLE status` is emitted into the accessibility tree with its rationale as its description, in reading order, and no flag turns the tree off. The layout needs a font file, and this block uses the system's. What does the tree say of the status region? Ask it:

```haro
oShell.UseFont("C:/Windows/Fonts/segoeui.ttf")
oP = oShell.ToPanel()
oT = new stzAccessibilityTree(oShell, oP)
? oT.NodeCount()
#--> 12
? oT.NodeOf("status")[:role]
#--> status
? oT.NodeOf("status")[:description]
#--> The truth of the build.
? @@( oT.ReadingOrder() )
#--> [ "window", "tablist", 'tab "Define"', 'tab "Build"', 'tab "Govern"', 'tab "Ship"', "group", 'label "the work"', "group", 'label "no proposal"', "status", 'label "clean"' ]
```

> run: kept

The tree answers `status`, and for its description `The truth of the build.`, the rationale word for word! The library's header says why: every toolkit gates accessibility behind a performance flag, which is where users silently get nothing. So here there is nothing to gate and nothing to forget to switch on. The same file says what a screen reader can operate, an agent can operate.

### A rail that proposes

A rail is a view over what the world proposes. A pulse proposes and never relates. An act on the world clears a proposal, and the world's own status line counts from the same list the rail shows. What does the world propose, and what remains after one act? Run it:

```haro
oApp = StzAppQ("fieldvisits")
oApp.AddThingQ(:Client) { Has([ :name ]) }
oApp.AddThing(:Visit)
oApp.AddReactionQ(:Client).Unseen(90, :Days) { Propose(:Visit) }
oApp.AddInstance("amadou", :Client)
oApp.AddInstance("binta", :Client)
oApp.Live()
#--> [fieldvisits] is live -- 2 thing(s), 0 flow(s), 1 reaction(s), 0 goal(s); 2 proposal(s)
for aP in oApp.Proposals()
	? aP[1] + " " + aP[2] + " " + aP[3] + " " + aP[4]
next
#--> propose visit for amadou
#--> propose visit for binta
? oApp.Pulse()
#--> 0
? len(oApp.Proposals())
#--> 2
oApp.Relate("amadou", :visit, "visit-1")
oApp.Pulse()
for aP in oApp.Proposals()
	? aP[1] + " " + aP[2] + " " + aP[3] + " " + aP[4]
next
#--> propose visit for binta
```

> run: kept

`Live()` prints its line, `2 proposal(s)` at its end, and the judge compares what is printed. Before the act, `Pulse()` answers `0` and the rail still holds `2` proposals. After the act, one line remains: `propose visit for binta`. "2 thing(s)" counts the two things, Client and Visit, and not the instances. Chapter 20 owns the law beneath the rail, the proposal, the plan and the court.

And a screen is an intent. One place where the world speaks in verbs is its screens. Five methods exist: `ToDiscover`, `ToUnderstand`, `ToFocus`, `ToSelect` and `ToAct`. A sixth verb, `ToCompare`, does not exist. What answers when a screen asks for it? Run it:

```haro
oW = StzAppQ("fieldvisits")
oW.AddThingQ(:Client) { Has([ :name ]) }
oW.AddScreenQ(:Home)  { ToDiscover(:Client) }
oW.AddScreenQ(:File)  { ToUnderstand(:Client) }
oW.AddScreenQ(:Pick)  { ToSelect(:Client) }
oW.AddScreenQ(:Edit)  { ToAct(:Client) }
oW.Explain()
#--> WORLD fieldvisits   lives in: memory (not persisted)
#      BEING
#        client (name)
#      MET FROM WITHOUT
#        screen home: discover client
#        screen file: understand client
#        screen pick: select client
#        screen edit: act client
try
	oW.AddScreenQ(:Side) { ToCompare(:Client) }
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> Error (R3) : Calling Function without definition: tocompare
```

> run: kept

The runtime answers, `Error (R3) : Calling Function without definition: tocompare`, and no court does: the intent word is stored as a string, and is not checked against any list.

That is what runs; now I say what I decided with it, and what I refused.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I decided to carry both shells.** On 7 October 2026 I was asked whether the foundation takes the native shell or the browser shell, and I answered: carry both. The consequence the study states is that one chrome is declared once, and two shells project it.

**I decided that the mechanics are the library's and the IDE is the product's.** The library's design document: the paradigm mechanics are platform; the book, the brand, the IDE are product, and never leak. The foundation ships refinement as a library verb available to every program.

**I removed the interaction engine.** On 26 May 2026 my commit removed twenty-two modules invented by an AI that were not in the original Softanza, among them the interaction module. I read the date, the count and the list in the library's git history, commit `b70f3ad91`, and not in the pinned tree. Its design text, which says twenty-four verbs and laws enforced by the engine, is still in the tree. The constitution's list is twenty-two.

**I refused to resolve intents inside the language of the panel.** The panel declares no meanings: there is no `:Danger` and there never may be. The intent slot was left deliberately unresolved. Above, `IsClean()` answered `1` for `transfer_funds`: the panel judges no meaning.

**I refused to author the markup, and I refused a script engine.** The native markup is emitted, never authored; there is no script engine; flexbox only. That is the reason the chrome is a text file and not a class.

**I refused a flag that turns the accessibility tree off.** The tree block above printed `status` and `The truth of the build.` without a flag to set.

**I admitted a lens that cannot capture.** The estate's charter for its products: one truth, in files, and two lenses (the charter's two are the pair of studios, and not two shells). What the charter refused was frameworks that capture the work. A lens that cannot capture is admissible. The environment is a projection of plain text, and not a store.

**A session removed a facet, and I restored it.** The reference design records that the Connections facet was first-class in the prototype of June 2026, and was removed by an agent on circular reasoning. The removal cascaded into five documents. I reinstated it. Who decides the ribbon is the author.

**I accepted the browser shell with its cost, unmeasured.** The charter of the front-door IDE chose a browser tab, built with the engine, with no install step, and named the loss.

**The pattern was learned across six efforts in two years, one of them built**, the study counts. That count is the study's.

Next comes the court, with the guards behind these pieces and the ones that are missing.

## 5. The court

`gui_stzui_narrated` proves the closure of the panel court, a dangling reference, a duplicate, an orphan box, a missing panel, the mandatory rationale and a round trip. In scene 13 it proves the intent slot: `SATISFIES`, `Orphans()`, and that an intent nobody has defined is not an error. `gui_profile_narrated` proves that the profile is data, with three recorded divergences. It proves that one document has two projections and every declared element is in both, and that the fixture emits a self-checking page. The browser half of that guard is a procedure to reproduce. `gui_tier_agreement_narrated` proves that one display list rendered as vector and as pixels agrees to the pixel. It is not HTML against native. `gui_accessibility_narrated` proves the tree emitted from the declaration. `05_stzapp_live_narrated` proves that a live world proposes and acting clears. `emulator_narrated` proves the generated surface of the emulator.

Three guards are to come: one that judges a shell against the twenty-two verbs, one that a status cell shows only what a court emitted, and one that says the two projections of a chrome agree in the browser. The study records their absence, and I found nothing to the contrary.

**The two-projections check can fail, and it did.** The library's fixture is a page and not an assertion: it carries the web projection and the boxes the native layout produced. A script compares them and prints a verdict strip. A runner cannot judge it, so I read it by hand on 8 October 2026. On the chrome of the first block, eleven boxes with text in each region, the strip read "AGREE on every declared box of 11". Its note said that four boxes of automatic width differ, which is two shapers and not the profile. On the same three-band chrome with empty regions, the strip read "DISAGREE on 1 declared boxes of 3". The cause is in the web projection: an empty box is emitted as `<div id="body"/>`. An HTML parser reads that as an open tag, so the siblings that follow nest inside it. The native tier is unaffected. The fixture catches it! The library's guards do not yet, since their own showcase has no empty box: adding one is the next step. An empty workspace is the pattern's first state. So the strip tells the truth both ways, which is the chapter's fifth mark in one picture, and the web projection has a case to correct on exactly that state.

From the court I turn back to the field, and to where it leads.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The fast editors set a bar that the record writes down as law for its own product: input to screen under sixteen milliseconds, a cold start under four hundred, no exceptions. The record has not yet measured it on either shell: a latency number is absent, and it is the next step.

A text change dirties the whole document. One bound value changed re-shapes every string, where a colour re-shapes one. The field's editors patch, and lead here today. (General knowledge.)

The browser costs what it costs: file access through the browser's model, a port to guard, and an unmeasured latency. The native shell costs a window system, and a GPU for the raster path.

The field has a language service (general knowledge), and the record has a contract; the service is the next step. The explorer's model reads the world file the service would emit, and it is loaded by no entry file: a block that calls it without a hand-written load fails.

The field's fast editor treats the status bar as clutter. The pattern keeps one and asks it to be true. That is a bet, and I hold it as a view, not a result; the record's own corpus argues the field's side in the same breath.

That leaves the limit.

## 7. The limit

**The web projection does not yet carry roles, labels and intents.** `ToHtml` emits an identifier, a style and the text only. The tree that carries roles is a separate artefact, as the block above shows, so a browser shell would infer from bare `div`s what the native shell emits. The plan itself says that the accessibility library it adopts has no web adapter.

**The library's own mission control prints constants where the pattern asks for verdicts.** The emulator generates a browser surface, a parts grid with status chips and fidelity badges, a device window and a console for each part. Its status words are constants of the device class and of the template, and they are not measured. Here a plan with one firmware part and one GPIO capability, and no pump anywhere. What does its page say? Build it and read it:

```haro
oPlan = new stzDelivery("greenhouse")
oPlan.AddFirmware(:node, :ESP32)
oPlan.NeedsIn(:node, [ :GPIO ])
oEmu = new stzEmulator(oPlan)
oEmu.CompileEngineQ(FALSE)
oEmu.SetOutDirQ("_emu")
oEmu.Build()
cH = read("_emu/index.html")
? StzContains(cH, "needs a look")
#--> 1
? StzContains(cH, "2 approximated")
#--> 1
? StzContains(cH, "parts healthy")
#--> 1
? StzContains(cH, "Ready to ship.")
#--> 1
StzDirDeleteAll("_emu")
```

> run: kept (touches files)

The part gets "2 approximated" and, in the grid's detail, a text about pump timing and sensor noise: the greenhouse demo's text, returned for any microcontroller. The header says "parts healthy" and the banner says "Ready to ship." for the same plan, from literals! A backend-only plan prints "parts healthy" and "Ready to ship." too, and neither "needs a look" nor "2 approximated" (read in the source, not run). "Deploy to production" is an alert. The truthful half is real: fidelity is printed, and the console refuses a verb that the part's engine subset lacks. That is narration in the library, which I did not run.

This is the loss against the library's own page. It stands beside "a status bar that tells the truth" of section 3: the pattern asks a status cell to be a function of what a court emitted. And the library's own page prints its words from literals. The request to derive those words from the plan is open, and it is the next step.

**`ToPanel` needs a font file and raises without one.** The accessibility tree and the fixture need a laid-out panel, so the blocks that use them depend on a system font.

**`Orphans()` counts containers.** Whether a layout-only box must serve a verb is the constitution's rule to state.

What is built: the interface as text with a court; four closed kinds, a closed list of nineteen roles, a closed field set, a mandatory rationale, a round trip to a fixpoint. Two projections, with a recorded table of three divergences and a page that can say DISAGREE. The accessibility tree, and a bridge that reaches a real screen reader's interface on Windows, read and not run by me. A declared value changes and the screen follows, with focus kept. A page hosted on the loopback, and a backend that refuses to leave the loopback unsigned. A world that proposes, screens declared as intents, one generated browser surface. And one binary shell, the sound plane's listening bench, in about seven hundred lines of Zig, which I read and did not build.

What is designed: the shell kit with two shells; the chrome as closed kinds named for the spine, ribbon, rail and status bar, none yet a class of the library. The facet as data, with its group, its place in the life, its artefact kinds, its court and its profile. The rail bound to focus, and the governance cells read from the courts, with a guard that a cell never shows a state the court did not emit.

What is absent: an IDE of this family. A list of the twenty-two verbs inside the library, and any refusal of an unknown verb: `transfer_funds` passes the court. A guard judging a shell against the verbs. An editor. A measured latency. I tell the pattern as designed, with the running pieces as evidence: the court of the chrome, the two projections, the accessibility tree, the page hosted on the loopback. No IDE of this family ships today.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You write the shell as text, and a court tells you the one box that serves no verb before anything is drawn. The same file is a native window and a page, and the library has a page that says when they disagree.

**The analyst.** The ribbon is the order of your solution's life, and not of a tool's features. The status cell reads the court, and you can ask the world what it proposes before it is asked to act.

**The designer.** Your interface has a constitution of twenty-two verbs and a closed grammar that adds renderings and never verbs. Every element must say which verb it serves, and the library already finds the ones that say none.

**The decision-maker.** Ask of any environment three things. Does its status bar read a court? Does its assistant live in one panel and propose? And who judges that the two shells agree? Here the answers are: designed; designed, with the rail's law built beneath it in chapter 20; and a self-checking page, which caught the empty-box case on its first run, by the guard's account.

**The educator.** The exercise is a prediction: say what the court will answer before you run it. The status word that is a constant is a lesson in the difference between a label and a truth.

**The agent.** The shell is a text file that you can read and write, and the tree a screen reader reads is the tree you may read. Your proposals appear in a rail and commit nowhere.

## 9. Exercise

One exercise, in three moves.

1. Write a shell of your own in five elements: a title bar, a ribbon of three facets taken from your own solution's life, a work area, a rail, a status bar. Before you run anything, write on paper which of the twenty-two verbs each element serves, and which one of your boxes you expect the court to call an orphan.
2. Run `IsClean()` and `Orphans()`, and compare with your paper. Then break the file three ways: a child that is declared nowhere, a role that is not on the list, and an intent that is not one of the twenty-two. For each, write what your status function will answer, and whether the library, the book's function, or nobody will catch it.
3. Emit the page, open it, and write the line the fixture strip will print for a version with an empty work area. Then look. The page comes from `ToFixture`, which takes the box that `BoxOf` reports for each name on the laid-out panel from `ToPanel`; `gui_profile_narrated` has the loop, and a font file is needed.

```haro exercise
oMine = new stzUiDocument('DEFINE PANEL shell ( SIZE [640, 360], DIRECTION column, CHILDREN [bar] ) RATIONALE "Mine."' + nl + 'DEFINE TEXT bar ( CONTENT "mine", SATISFIES understand ) RATIONALE "Mine too."')
# under each line, write what you expect before you run it
? oMine.IsClean()
? @@( oMine.Orphans() )
```

> run: exercise (touches the system)

If a promise breaks, do not change it: explain the output first.

I left this one in your hands. The `ShellStatus` of section 3 is the book's own function, and I did not guard it against the case below: the one section 3 ran through `IsClean()`, and section 7 lists as absent. Run the line and read the word the cell gives. Then make the cell refuse a stranger to the twenty-two, as the book's loop does, with the loop over the list that section 3 already holds, and without asking the library for a list it does not own (section 4 says why).

```haro exercise
# the book's own call, with the one change of the second block of section 3
? ShellStatus(StzReplace(cShell, "SATISFIES preview", "SATISFIES transfer_funds"))
```

> run: exercise

When the cell is right, you will know what the library finds, and what only a list it does not hold can refuse.

Every shell of this chapter is a projection, and chapter 27 goes beneath them all: three layers, one engine, Haro. There every feature is implemented once, the surface is the promise, and the runtime is the part you measure, and never assert.


=====

# The engine beneath: three layers, one engine, Haro

Source: Chapter 27. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: three layers chosen by the entry file you load, each including the one beneath and none above; the dependency rule held at the loads; an engine of eighty-nine libraries, in Zig, built in optimised safe mode, with the engine's own count of what it exposes; Haro as a ratified charter and a spike whose courts run on the new runtime and on the old one; designed: the base layer inheriting the core layer; the max layer as a built layer; a court that checks the layers against their contract; a layer chosen by a declaration the compiler honours; the other faces of Haro over one program graph; a loader for one domain of base; the standalone engine repository and its command line; the engine's polyglot bridge, which is chapter 16's; absent: a lean core layer that runs its strings; a Linux or macOS engine in service; a measured pass of the library on the new runtime under Linux; a `haro` executable; Haro running the library; a single executable with no dependency; an engine test count that I may print

Run: 9 of 9 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:15 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I load the three layers of the library, one entry file at a time, and count what each holds. I read the engine beneath them, its eighty-nine libraries and the functions it registers, and I let the court say whether it is optimised. I set down the decisions, that the future is Haro and that every feature is implemented once, in the engine, and I print the rows where a fast peer still leads. The principle stands over all of it: the surface is the promise, and the runtime is the part you measure, and never assert.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

A platform inside one codebase is drawn as a stack with one rule: an inner layer knows nothing of an outer one. The cleanest peer, in the study's words, is Rust's three crates for three depths of host, chosen by the target. (General knowledge for the field: Rust enforces the rule by its compiler.) Softanza holds it by entry files, and section 7 says what that costs.

The field's habit for an engine is a native library with a plain C interface, wrapped thinly by each language. SQLite is the common example: one C library, bindings in nearly every language. The record states the same design for its engine: language clients are thin bridges that add syntax and paradigms, and never computation. (General knowledge for the field, well established.)

And the field has done, repeatedly, a third thing: a language keeps its surface and its libraries and changes the machine beneath. The record's own example is a typed descendant of Lua. Another is the alternative implementation of Python. (General knowledge for the field, well established.) Both teach the same thing the record teaches: the surface is the promise, and the runtime is the part you must measure.

The principle that follows is one sentence, and section 2 sets it down.

## 2. The first principle

**Every feature is implemented once, in the engine. The surface is the promise, and the runtime is the part you measure, and never assert.**

The first sentence is the engine architecture's design rule, in a session's document: every feature is implemented once, in the engine. Language clients are thin bridges that add syntax and paradigms, never computation. Chapter 4 told the author's ruling that follows from it, that I refused computation in the faces. The second sentence is the brief of the runtime beneath: performance and portability, measured, never asserted.

And the definition that governs the engine's dependencies, which chapter 4 already states: sovereignty is nothing anyone else can withdraw. In the vision's words, it never meant every line ours.

## 3. The Softanza form

Three claims, and a block for each of what a block can prove. I tell you first what a block cannot prove, because it matters. The dependency rule of the layers, that no core file loads base and no base file loads max, needs a scan of about eight hundred and seventy load lines. Only a guard could carry that, and none exists. I did the scan with a script of my own, and section 7 gives its result.

### A layer is the file you load

How many classes does each entry file load? Three entry files, three fresh processes. Each prints how many classes it holds, and whether `stkList`, `stzList` and `stzListXT` exist. The block spawns child processes, because this chapter's own process has already loaded the largest layer, and only a child can show the core alone:

```haro
cAsk = 'aC = ringvm_classesList()' + nl +
       '? "" + len(aC) + " " + (find(aC, "stklist", 1) > 0) + " " + (find(aC, "stzlist", 1) > 0) + " " + (find(aC, "stzlistxt", 1) > 0)' + nl
aEntries = [ "core/stkLib.ring", "stzLib.ring", "max/stxLib.ring" ]
for i = 1 to 3
    write("layer" + i + ".ring", 'load "../../../' + aEntries[i] + '"' + nl + cAsk)
    ? trim(systemcmd("ring layer" + i + ".ring"))
    remove("layer" + i + ".ring")
next
#--> 17 1 0 0
#--> 674 1 1 0
#--> 714 1 1 1
```

> run: kept (touches files)

The first process prints `17 1 0 0`. Core holds 17 classes, ten with the prefix `stk` and seven with `stzCore`, and no base class. The second prints `674 1 1 0`: base holds 674 and no max class. The third prints `714 1 1 1`: max holds 714. Each layer includes the one beneath and none above: core inside base inside max, loaded from below. A load of the core entry alone takes about a tenth of a second on this machine. A load of base takes between three and eight seconds, in single runs on 9 October 2026. My notes hold those times, and no block shows them.

The contract says base builds on core, by inheritance or delegation. What do the classes say? `ringvm_classesList()` returns, for each class, its name, its identifier, and its parent:

```haro
aClasses = ringvm_classesList()
? ParentOf("stkString")
#--> stzcorestring
? ParentOf("stzString")
#--> stzobject
? ParentOf("stzList")
#--> stzobject
? ParentOf("stzListXT")
#--> stzlist

func ParentOf(cClass)
    n = find(aClasses, lower(cClass), 1)
    if n = 0  return "(not loaded)" ok
    return aClasses[n][3]
```

> run: kept

The second line prints `stzobject`: `stzString` inherits `stzObject`, and not `stkString`! Core carries a parallel hierarchy, in which `stkString` inherits `stzCoreString`, the first line's `stzcorestring`. Max does build on base: `stzListXT` inherits `stzList`, the last line's `stzlist`. Exactly two base classes inherit a core class, `stzSciNumber` and `stzPointer`, and one delegates, in a line of `stzPointer`. They are two of about six hundred and seventy-seven. The contract is not met, and it is not met in the way chapter 4 asked about. I print it as a debt.

And the third layer by its prefix. In the process of the largest layer, how many classes begin with `stk`, how many with `stz`, and how many with `stx`?

```haro
nStk = 0  nStz = 0  nStx = 0
aClasses = ringvm_classesList()
for aClass in aClasses
    cPrefix = left(aClass[1], 3)
    if cPrefix = "stk"  nStk++
    but cPrefix = "stz"  nStz++
    but cPrefix = "stx"  nStx++
    ok
next
? @@([ nStk, nStz, nStx ])
#--> [ 10, 693, 0 ]
```

> run: kept

The block prints `[ 10, 693, 0 ]`. Ten, six hundred and ninety-three, and none! The forty classes max adds to base all carry the base prefix. The thirty-two classes with the third prefix exist, all in one folder, and no entry file loads them. Section 7 says it again.

### One engine

The layers are real at the binary. The build file declares the core domains and the base domains and builds one library per domain, in a loop. How many libraries does it declare for each layer? This block reads that file, and the claim is about that file:

```haro
$cEngineDir = "../../../engine"
cBuild = read($cEngineDir + "/build.zig")
n1 = substr(cBuild, "const core_domains")
n2 = substr(cBuild, "const base_domains")
n3 = substr(cBuild, "fn addSpeech")
? Q(substr(cBuild, n1, n2 - n1)).NumberOfOccurrence('.name = "stk_')
#--> 4
? Q(substr(cBuild, n2, n3 - n2)).NumberOfOccurrence('.name = "stz_')
#--> 85
? Q(cBuild).NumberOfOccurrence('stx_')
#--> 0
```

> run: kept (touches files)

The block prints `4`, `85` and `0`. Four core libraries, eighty-five base libraries, and no library for the third layer: eighty-nine in all. The marker `fn addSpeech` is the first function after the two tables, a boundary in plain text, arbitrary but visible. A court of layers would parse properly, and I list it among the requests. The study says eighty-six base domains at this commit and I read eighty-five.

The engine tells the library what it is. Its version is a constant. How many distinct functions does it register in the virtual machine once it is loaded?

```haro
? StzEngineEmbedVersionString()
#--> 0.8.0
aAll = ringvm_cfunctionslist()
aNames = []
for cName in aAll
    if left(lower(cName), 9) = "stzengine"  aNames + lower(cName)  ok
next
? len( Q(aNames).DuplicatesRemoved() )
#--> 2786
```

> run: kept

The block prints `0.8.0` and `2786`. Two thousand seven hundred and eighty-six distinct engine functions, the library's own view, taken at run time. The same call returns more entries, since the virtual machine's own are included, and several names appear more than once; I did not trace why. I assigned the list to a variable first. The call is re-evaluated at every turn of a loop, and I stopped two runs that grew to gigabytes before I learned it. The count moves with the built engine.

There are three counts of three different things, and the chapter says which it prints. The first is the count to the library at run time: the 2,786 above. The second is the count to any host through the C interface. A script of mine read the export tables of the eighty-nine libraries on 9 October 2026 and found 1,710 named symbols, of which eighty-nine are the loader's hook, so 1,621 functions. The third is the count in the Zig sources, as registration names: 2,703 distinct literals. The site says 752 and 2,652, at commit 010743cce as the page reads on 9 October 2026, and the library's typed constants say 1,513, dated 16 June 2026. I cannot reproduce 752 by any method I tried. Print only a count the build reads, with its method and date.

**The engine does the work.** What does the host's `upper` make of the German sharp s, and what does the engine's case mapping make of it, its own table over a vendored library? A second vendored library stems words. These are two of the nineteen:

```haro
? upper("Straße")
#--> STRAßE
? Q("Straße").Uppercased()
#--> STRASSE
? @@( Q("running runs ran").StemmedWords() )
#--> [ "run", "run", "ran" ]
```

> run: kept

The first line prints `STRAßE`: the host leaves the sharp s alone. The second prints `STRASSE`: the engine writes SS! The third prints `[ "run", "run", "ran" ]`: the stemmer folds `running` and `runs` to `run` and leaves `ran`. If Haro's `upper` becomes full Unicode, the promise changes.

And the engine is optimised. The default mode of the build is optimised and safe, since commit 1fdc2eda5, which the library's git log dates 26 July 2026. The pinned files hold the commit and not the date. Until then every library the library had ever loaded was compiled with no optimisation, and that closes the question chapter 22 left open about the build mode. A guard reads time, since it cannot read a flag. Do twenty sums over two million doubles that live in the engine finish in under a tenth of a second, and are they right?

```haro
oBuf = StzNumBufferOfSizeQ(2000000)
oBuf.RangeFrom(1, 1)
nT0 = clock()
for k = 1 to 20
    nSum = oBuf.Sum()
next
? (clock() - nT0) / clockspersecond() < 0.10
#--> 1
? nSum
#--> 2000001000000
oBuf.Free()
```

> run: kept (touches the clock)

The block prints `1` and `2000001000000`, the boolean and the sum, and no time, because a time is not a promise. In three runs on 8 and 9 October 2026 the elapsed times were 0.02, 0.04 and 0.03 seconds, from my notes and not in the run record. The guard's own table says 0.14 for the unoptimised mode and 0.04 for the optimised one. The guard itself ran on 8 October: seven assertions in the file, six reached here, six passed. The seventh sits on an else-branch that is not reached when the parser is built.

### What runs it

What runs every block of this book? I ask the runtime for its version:

```haro
? version()
#--> 1.27
```

> run: kept

It prints `1.27`: the version of the former host language, the oracle against which the new runtime is judged. Haro does not run the library. Under a future runtime the promise changes, as the proof line says.

The decisions come next, and the first of them is about that future.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I decided that the future is Haro, and not the former language.** My words of 7 October 2026, in the study's quotation, the bracket mine: the future is Haro, not [the former language], and the site must be clear about it. Haro's face of the former language will be maintained only so that the code already written continues to work. What is not carried is that language's runtime and its governance. The first ruling, on 28 August, was that it is not carried forward at all; it was superseded two days after, as the vision's amendment says.

**I decided that the rewrite is a rename.** The code of the articles, in the name of Haro, is the same as the former language's face. The charter measures the same: Haro's own stages run the same text, byte-identical on the new compiler and on the oracle.

**I decided that Haro is the language of languages.** The charter's header quotes my note of 1 October 2026. The charter was ratified that day, with my "go with your recommendations" on seven decisions. Its amendments of 4 October answer nineteen of the compass's questions, and the version stays ratified until I have read its section 15.

**I decided that the surface is kept.** Its surface today is the one the compiler compiles. The library is its compatibility specification. Other surfaces are faces over one program graph: every syntax a face, so that an agent fluent in the former language, in Python or in JavaScript writes Haro. Of these only the first face exists. A Python-shaped face is a spelling, not Python.

**I removed the old framework, with no fallback.** Engine-only: no fallback, no feature flags, no Qt dependency. The status of 12 June 2026 says zero of them remain. I checked. No loaded file loads a Qt library. Two external extensions are loaded by name: a zip one, which base loads, and a cryptography one, in a folder that no entry file loads.

**I directed that the language's own extensions be removed.** My directive of 13 June 2026: ensure that no external extensions are required, and that engine alternatives replace them. The library still loads one of them, a zip extension, as the entry above says.

**I put the engine in Zig, with no dependency on a library,** as the study reports my ask. It is qualified by the record's own definition of sovereignty: nineteen vendored libraries, each with a record.

**I let everything go into base.** My sentence, as the study reports it: under the pressure of time, everything went into base, waiting for the right moment to refactor again. By my count below, base is ninety-three per cent of the lines.

**I chose safe optimisation.** Not the fastest mode, and not the unoptimised one. I hold it as a view, not a result, that a clean panic beats silent memory corruption, and that here it costs nothing.

**I refused a headline that does not reproduce.** The memo of 13 September 2026 says the claim to retire is that the runtime beats the fast peer, present tense, on x64. Chapter 22 told the refusal, and section 6 prints the rows.

**I refused a percentage.** The figure is mine, and it should not be printed without the guard that produces it. Chapters 31 and 33 say so.

**I have no sentence on how a layer is chosen in Haro.** The study only proposes that, in Haro, layer selection should be a declaration the compiler honours. The charter has no text on core, base and max. Base inheriting core is the contract as designed, with the stage word designed. The code does not meet it today, as section 3 prints, and the book's account is what the code does: two implementations over one engine. And in Haro, a layer is chosen by the file you load, today. A declaration the compiler honours is the study's proposal, and is designed.

Section 5 reads what the court proves of all this, and what no guard proves.

## 5. The court

`engine_is_optimised_narrated` proves that the engine is compiled with optimisation: twenty sums over two million doubles under a tenth of a second, and the right answer. It proves that an index of 99 and an index of 0 are refused and the buffer unharmed, and that the vendored parser which the optimised mode once broke now answers. I ran it: six of the seven assertions in the file were reached, and six passed. `engine_senses_narrated` proves that the engine gives the process five senses: memory, its peak, system memory, processor time, and a bounded series. I ran it: thirty-six of thirty-six, and the run then stops with "STOPPED!" by design. `wasm_narrated` proves that the engine's entry for the browser exports its curated interface and that the build has a target for it. It builds a subset and asserts that it is smaller. I did not run it, because it calls a build of the toolchain. `threat_model_narrated` reads the threat model and checks that each guarantee row names a proof and that each guard file it names exists. Rows G11, the fuzz harnesses, and G25, the bill of materials with its digests, concern the engine.

What does not exist: **no guard judges the layers.** No file under the test folders loads a layer's entry file or reads the contract. There are 529 narrated guards in base, and none in core or in max. The study says 517 in one place and 387 in another, and the site 501; I print only what the build reads. And no guard judges the engine's counts: the numbers the engine's command-line status prints are typed constants dated 16 June 2026.

Section 6 sets the runtime beside the field, and the peer leads every row.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

**Speed.** The runtime beneath against a fast scripting runtime of the field, on 13 September 2026. The method is the memo's own: six pairs of kernels written fresh, identical algorithm and sizes on both sides, each side in its fast idiom, checksums across languages asserted before any number. The minima are over three inner repeats and two process runs, the new compiler built for speed, the peer interpreter-only with no JIT, on Windows, x64. The peer leads every row:

| row | the runtime beneath (min ms) | the peer (min ms) | the peer leads by |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| sieve | 128 | 74.5 | 1.72 |
| matmul | 349 | 185.6 | 1.88 |
| fib | 57 | 50.2 | 1.14 |
| mergesort | 376 | 206.7 | 1.82 |
| binsearch | 536 | 513.1 | 1.04 |
| bytescan | 53 | 37.5 | 1.41 |

The memo's words: the peer leads every row, fib and binsearch are near-ties, and nothing is worse than 2x. ARM is unmeasured. The win that the memo keeps: within 2x on every row, which no version of the former runtime ever was.

**The headline is still alive in the repository's own notes.** As I read on 9 October 2026, the brief of the runtime's own repository and its notes still say that the spike beats the peers on every row. The correction is carried in another repository of the estate and in the memo. In the runtime's own record it is carried only for its release-candidate configuration, in one section of the notes. The memo asked for it. The memo of 13 September 2026 withdraws the claim, and the six rows above are its rows, although the repository's notes still carry the old claim. I name no peer, and describe it as chapter 22 does: a fast scripting runtime of the field.

**Memory.** On the same day the note says: resident, running the library, this morning 733 megabytes, now 149, and the former runtime 139. A loss of ten to the former runtime, after a fall of 584. A win and a loss in one note.

**Compatibility, on 5 October 2026.** The wide suite of the library was run on a snapshot. The note records parity of assertions on every one the former runtime passes, that most programs agree on every stable line, and that nineteen rows differ in kind. The note does not say Windows in that entry, and the Linux pass is listed as the next step. These are a session's notes from a private branch, and no number of mine. I print them, dated 5 October 2026, with the stage word in construction: 8,540 of 8,540 assertions at parity, 1,253 of 1,253 tests that bear assertions, and 3,425 of 5,112 programs identical on every stable line. Another 1,267 are identical but for the line that prints the elapsed time, and 19 rows differ in kind: 13 print more, 5 are refused or raise, one writes a stderr line. They are the note's counts, and I ran none of them.

**The platform.** Built and run on Windows today. A Linux build of eighty-five of the eighty-nine modules exists on a working branch; the site says it is in construction. A whole program with its engine in one file, with no dependency, is absent.

**The engine against the field.** The threat model's row for fuzzing says it itself: no scheduled or coverage-guided fuzzing, no continuous integration and unsigned commits, deferred by the author for the development phase. The field's maintained native libraries have continuous fuzzing and signed releases. And chapter 22's loss, unchanged: no collation by locale, and no time zones.

Section 7 says where the layers and the engine stop.

## 7. The limit

**The lean layer does not run.** What does a string of the core give back when I ask it for its content?

```haro
o = new stkString("abc")
? o.Content()
#--> Error (R3) : Calling Function without definition: stkenginestringfrom
```

> run: kept

The runtime answers: `Error (R3) : Calling Function without definition: stkenginestringfrom`. The core string cannot give back `abc`! The core string class calls engine names that no engine library registers. In a process of the core alone, the engine's core library registers five hundred and thirty-four functions under the base names and none under the core names. That count is from my notes, and no block shows it. So `stkString` and `stkChar` find nothing. The study files it as an open item of the core and the engine. `stkList` runs, which chapter 4 showed, and the load of seventeen classes in a tenth of a second is real.

**The lean layer is not lean at the binary, for strings.** By file size, on the build of 5 October 2026, the core string library is 22,355,968 bytes and the base one 22,362,624. The read-only data is 20.8 megabytes in both; I did not diagnose what it holds. The four core libraries total 24.1 megabytes of the eighty-nine libraries' 122.4. The core libraries for dates, files and locale differ from their base twins by under one per cent.

**The dependency rule holds at the loads, with one exception at a call.** I parsed every load of every non-test, non-archive file of core, base and max, 871 lines, and resolved each. Core loads, outside its own folder, only the engine face; base loads core and the engine; no file under base loads anything under max or future; max loads base. But `FindMin`, in the core's list of numbers, calls a function that is defined only in base. In a core-only process, `FindMin([4,2,9])` answers 2, and `FindMin("not a list")` raises "Calling Function without definition". In a base process it raises the intended error. I found one instance by a search for base names in core, and I did not look for others.

**On a case-sensitive disk the core entry file does not load.** Its second line loads a file by a name in the wrong case. My scan finds twenty such loads in non-test files at this commit; the runtime's session counted fifty-five with tests, on 8 October 2026. The book is built on Windows, which hides it.

**The third layer is written and not wired.** Its thirty-two classes are all in one folder, and no entry file loads them. The first of them works when loaded by hand. I ran it and do not show it, a string of eleven characters counted by it. I tried none of the other thirty-one. A library layer for them in the engine does not exist.

What is built: a layer is a folder, a prefix and an entry file. I counted on 9 October 2026 at this commit, by files with the extension, excluding test and archive folders. Core, 27 files and 6,366 lines; base, 984 files and 519,579; max, 115 and 31,292; and a folder of the future, 51 files and 6,356. Base is 93.2 per cent of the three layers' lines. The engine: eighty-nine libraries from the Zig sources, 401 source files, 179,985 lines, and 2,464 declared tests, which I did not run. The toolchain is Zig 0.15.2 by the build file's own comment. Nineteen vendored libraries, each with a record of component, version, source, licence and digest. The record's own gaps: an archive digest is recorded for nine of the nineteen, and three have no version.

What is designed: the base layer inheriting the core layer. The seven shared domains are implemented twice over the same engine. Max as a built layer. A loader for one domain of base. A court that checks the layers against their contract. The standalone engine repository and its command line, which the architecture lists as a phase. The engine's polyglot bridge, which is chapter 16's.

What is absent: a Linux and a macOS engine in service; a measured pass of the library on the new runtime under Linux; a single executable with no dependency; an engine test count that I may print.

### Haro

I tell what is built, what is designed, and what is absent, as the record has it on 9 October 2026.

**Built, as a charter, a family of grammars and a guard.** The charter, version 0.2, ratified on 1 October 2026. A spike of nineteen rungs, between 1 and 4 October, each a grammar document with fixtures. Its guard reported a pass of the whole run on 5 October. Its courts are programs that run byte-identical on the new compiler and on the official former runtime. The new compiler also formats and dumps the library's files: of about six thousand, nearly all print the reference's bytes, and a few dozen are refused by both. A first slice of a graph of the code was built on 5 October.

**What Haro is today, in one sentence the record supports:** a ratified charter and a spike whose courts run on the new runtime and on the old one, over the surface the library is written in. There is no `haro` executable. The folder of binaries holds the compiler and two others. The forged courts load no library. **Haro does not run the library.** The vision of 27 September said that Haro does not exist and that the new runtime's Linux row is unmeasured. The first half is out of date, since a charter and a spike exist. The second half is still true of the wide suite on Linux.

**Designed:** the three altitudes, declaring, declared, and computing; the alphabet of six letters, five forged whole in the spike; refinement as the write surface; other faces over one program graph. Those faces arrive as printers before they arrive as parsers, and never as semantics. The verbs `haro forge`, `check`, `refine`, `run`, `project` and `narrate` are a recommendation, and not a program.

**The runtime beneath, as the record dates it.** Its brief: a register machine and a compiler from the former language's source to its bytecode, running existing programs. About three hundred thousand lines of Softanza are its compatibility specification. Its public face: the main branch is public and untouched; the work lives on a branch pushed to a private remote only. Its last commit at my reading is of 8 October 2026, at 22:26. The stage word is mine, and it is "in construction".

The six doors follow, one for each reader.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You choose your depth by the file you load, and you can prove it in three fresh processes. The lean layer does not run its strings today: reach for base. Read the engine's count the way the library sees it, and not the way a page types it.

**The analyst.** Three numbers for "how big is the engine", and three methods. Ask which one a vendor means. The core library is the size of the base one for strings.

**The designer.** A layer is a decision about what you ship, and not about taste. At the binary, core and base have a library per domain and the third layer has none; the web subset, which chapters 4 and 24 show, is a subset and not a layer.

**The decision-maker.** The two questions of chapter 4, plus two. What runs it: version 1.27 today, with the replacement in construction. What the replacement lost: every row against a fast peer, by 1.04 to 1.88 times, and ten megabytes of memory to the old one. This is my view: a record that prints its loss is the only kind to trust.

**The educator.** Teach the dependency rule by making a learner load the core alone and predict, then count. Teach "measured, never asserted" with the memo of 13 September 2026.

**The agent.** You may count the engine's functions yourself. Do not repeat a count from a page, and do not write a loop over the list of classes directly. Tell the reader which layer a class lives in by its parent chain, and not by its prefix.

## 9. Exercise

Before you run anything, write three numbers: how many classes each entry file will load, and which of `stkList`, `stzList` and `stzListXT` each holds.

```haro exercise
# core/stkLib.ring, stzLib.ring, max/stxLib.ring: write your three predictions here,
# then load each entry in its own process and count with ringvm_classesList().
```

> run: exercise

Then choose any class of the library, predict its parent, and read it with the parent chain. Write the line your prediction got wrong. A learner who guesses that `stzString` inherits `stkString` is in good company: the contract says so, and the library does not. I do not say which of the 17, 674 or 714 is which.

I left this one in your hands. The count by prefix in section 3, `[ 10, 693, 0 ]`, is correct, and it says nothing about layers. Write that count again so that it asks each class's parent chain, read with `ParentOf`, and not its first three letters, and look at what you get. Section 3 says what the prefix hides, and section 8, the agent's door, says why I refuse it. When your count answers, you will know why a class's layer is read from what it inherits, and not from what it is called.

The next chapter leaves the engine for a restaurant in Lyon, and for the one sentence the project there taught: deliver small, fast and useful, and let the foundations mature in parallel without ever blocking a delivery.


=====

# A restaurant in Lyon

Source: Chapter 28. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the constellation of worlds with its bonds and norms; the payments sandbox and the production gate that refuses a fake; the speed budget declared and judged; designed: the Lock as a rule of the library; the constellation written in the near-natural idiom the design describes; absent: the restaurant's live payment adapter; a measurement of the budget on a real mobile network; a speed expectation in the owner's own percentile; browser-side timing; the platform on the Softanza engine

Run: 5 of 5 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:15 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I tell a paid lesson in order, with its dates: a restaurateur in Lyon, two years of specification that produced nothing he could use, and the reversal of 4 July 2026 that put the customer first. I show what the library carries of each lesson: the Lock as a rule over a cycle's worlds, a payments sandbox that is not agreeable, a constellation that puts a world to sleep, a speed budget declared and judged. I say where it carries nothing, and what I refused: the grand system in one piece, silent drift, the agreeable fake, and crediting the owner with what a simulation said. The principle is one sentence on the site: deliver small, fast and useful, and let the foundations mature in parallel without ever blocking a delivery.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

Each lesson of this project has an ordinary home in the field, and the field keeps it outside the program.

A fixed scope for a cycle lives in a process. In Scrum the goal of a sprint does not change while it runs; in Shape Up, the time is fixed and the scope varies, and new ideas wait outside the cycle. Trying a service before paying for it lives in a vendor's test mode and in tools that stand in for an HTTP service. A performance budget lives in a continuous-integration check of a page's weight and speed. The web's own measure of speed is a figure taken over real page loads, at the seventy-fifth percentile. The picture of a whole constellation of small applications lives in the super-app: one host that embeds a runtime and runs mini-applications inside it. (All of this is general knowledge, not a study of the record.)

All four are good practices, and in all four the program itself knows nothing of them.

## 2. The first principle

The project was a paid lesson, and the lesson is one sentence on the site: **deliver small, fast and useful, and let the foundations mature in parallel without ever blocking a delivery.**

Its companion is this chapter's rule of evidence, taken from the same page: a lesson is listed only when a file of the library carries it.

## 3. The Softanza form

A restaurateur in Lyon owns a couscous restaurant, and he wanted a platform for neighbourhood commerce. He is not a programmer, and the project's own notes say so. I am going to tell the project in order, with its dates, and then show what the library carries of each lesson.

It began with an offer, on 2 May 2024, for a mission of business analysis. What followed was two years, in the owner's own count, of research and reflection, and of what I called specification: founding analyses, a novel of narrations, a first product specified in full. It produced nothing he could use. In the middle of 2025 the project came close to ending, and a silence of six months followed. That is where I learned the doctrine of section 2: deliver small, fast and useful.

On 3 July 2026, two of us rehearsed the project: a scoping of his answers that I wrote with a session, and a campaign of twenty-two simulated tests, run by the sessions playing the owner. I say "simulated" every time, because the answers in that file are not his. The next day, 4 July, he answered a real scoping report in his own words. He reversed the order of the work. We had imagined a line that ran from the supplier to the restaurateur and ended with the customer. He turned it around: customer first, restaurateur next, supplier last. And he wrote that the speed of ordering was his first priority, non-negotiable. His great fear was friction at the first load, and the second fear, in the same report, was the calendar: none of the deadlines until then had been kept.

The project's answer to his fear is a document and a notebook. We called it the Lock. In July, a second scoping proposed it to him, clause by clause. The first three: a single amendment; every new idea goes into a notebook, read at the end of the cycle; one entry needs one written exit. The last two: a demonstration every two weeks and no date beyond the next cycle; and a definition of done. He accepted four clauses as written and adjusted the fifth by adding a criterion of his own: that other merchants and partners must be able to install the product alone. In the record I am the one who asked for the Lock first, as a rule on myself: that the change of course had to be the only change of course allowed. On 4 August 2026, the first amendment closed. Twelve decisions were sealed; three were asked again in plain words, because he himself had flagged that he had not understood them. The lesson written down is about how to ask: the moment a question speaks of milestones and phasing and cycles, the owner stops following. So the questions moved into his trade's language.

On 15 August the first demonstration was designed for him alone. The checklist is titled with his name and "alone". He opens the delivery on his own computer, scans one code with his phone and another with his tablet, places his own order from the phone, and watches it land on the tablet. If he gets stuck, a button prepares a message to paste into a messaging app. The criterion of success reads: he orders from his phone, and it lands on his tablet, without us. The notebook shows it happened: his remarks of that day were entered in it on the 17th. And on 19 August he spent a field day with two phones and photographs sent over a messaging app, measuring on a real phone. A hotspot works with no card in it; the address of the gateway was stable across restarts. A phone is a Wi-Fi client or a hotspot, never both; an iPhone's system does not return by itself to a network with no internet. The project's notes then call him its co-architect. The record ends on 25 August 2026.

That is the story. What does the library carry of it?

**The Lock.** I have to be exact here. The Forged page of the site holds a rule I made: a lesson is listed only when a file of the library carries it. No file carries the Lock. It was a pact and a notebook, kept in documents. What the library can do is give you the nearest declaration of it: a rule over the worlds of a cycle, that a world without a written admission is a finding. What does the court say when an idea enters the cycle with no written admission? This block is a construction, not the restaurant's code:

```haro
oRule = new stzGraphRule("the-lock")
oRule.SetDomainQ("scope")
oRule.WhenQ("kind", "equals", "world")
oRule.WhenQ("admitted-by", "missing", "")
oRule.ThenViolationQ("a world entered the cycle with no written admission")
g = new stzGraph("cycle-1")
g.AddNode("customer-phone")
g.SetNodeProperty("customer-phone", "kind", "world")
g.SetNodeProperty("customer-phone", "admitted-by", "the opening scope")
g.AddRule(oRule)
? g.RulesAreSound()
#--> 1
g.AddNode("loyalty-club")
g.SetNodeProperty("loyalty-club", "kind", "world")
g.RulesReport()
#--> Rule report 'cycle-1': 1 finding(s), 1 error(s), 0 warning(s) -> UNSOUND (errors present)
#      [scope] 1 finding(s)
#        ERROR the-lock @ loyalty-club -- a world entered the cycle with no written admission
g.SetNodeProperty("loyalty-club", "admitted-by", "a notebook entry, and one exit written")
? g.RulesAreSound()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

The idea of a loyalty club arrives, and the cycle is no longer sound! The court answers `UNSOUND (errors present)`, and names the rule and the node: `ERROR the-lock @ loyalty-club`. The cycle is sound again when the idea carries its written admission.

**Try payments without a subscription.** The project's rule was to test without committing. That meant a payment institution's free sandbox and the library's simulated services, and no subscription before a gate that the notes call the go-live gate. The library's side of it is a payments sandbox that is not agreeable. It behaves like a gateway: a decline is an answer, an outage is not, and a capture cannot be made twice. The amounts are integers in minor units, so 4200 is forty-two euros. What does it answer to a token it was told to decline, to an outage, and to a second capture? Run it:

```haro
oPay = new stzPaymentsSandbox()
oPay.DeclineTokenQ("tok_chargeback")
? oPay.Authorize(4200, "tok_chargeback")[:status]
#--> declined
oPay.FailNextQ()
? oPay.Authorize(4200, "tok_visa")[:status]
#--> refused
aAuth = oPay.Authorize(4200, "tok_visa")
? aAuth[:status]
#--> authorized
oPay.Capture(aAuth[:id])
? oPay.Capture(aAuth[:id])[:why]
#--> this authorization was already captured
```

> run: kept

Three answers, and none of them is an empty string: `declined`, `refused`, and `this authorization was already captured`.

And the production gate refuses to open while a fake is still bound, even for an entitled person. Here the live binding is an HTTP sandbox standing in for an adapter, as the guard does it: the registry holds only the name of the secret. What does the production gate answer when the owner, entitled, asks for production while the fake is still bound? Run it:

```haro
oReg = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oReg.BindSandbox(:payments, new stzPaymentsSandbox())
oDel = new stzDelivery("shop")
oDel.AddBackend("api", "linux")
oDel.UseServicesQ(oReg)
oDel.NeedsServiceInQ("api", [ :payments ])
oDel.DeployTo(StzDeploymentSiteQ("host1"), "api")
oDel.SetActor( HumanActor("owner") )
? oDel.Deploy(:Production).WasCommitted()
#--> 0
? oDel.Log().EntriesOfLevel(:error)[1][:message]
#--> REFUSED -- the external surface is not production-ready
? oDel.WhyServicesNotReady()
#--> sandbox-in-production: still bound to a SANDBOX in a production phase -- a fake must never ship
oDel.ServicesQ().BindLiveQ(:payments, new stzHttpSandbox(), "payments_key")
? oDel.ServicesAreProductionReady()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

The owner is entitled, and he is refused! Nothing is committed, and the log says why: `REFUSED -- the external surface is not production-ready`; the reason under it is the rule: `a fake must never ship`. It opens when the binding is real.

**The constellation.** When he reversed the order of the work, the project's architecture was redrawn as a constellation of five worlds on a shared ground. The worlds are the customer's phone, the kitchen screen, the merchant's workshop, management, which is the first application kept and put to sleep, and the publisher's console. The shared ground is identity, the catalogue, orders, payments and the fleet. In the library, the last world in the order of work can be put to sleep, and it is revived when its turn comes. What does a call across a bond answer while the target world sleeps, and after it is revived? Run it:

```haro
oCon = new stzSuperApp("neighbourhood")
oCon.AddWorld("customer", StzAppQ("customer"))
oCon.AddWorld("kitchen", StzAppQ("kitchen"))
oCon.AddWorld("supplier", StzAppQ("supplier"))
oCon.Bond("customer", "kitchen", "send-order")
oCon.Bond("kitchen", "supplier", "order-produce")
oCon.GovDeclareRisk("send-order", 1)
oCon.GovDeclareRisk("order-produce", 2)
oCon.GovGrant("customer", "send-order")
oCon.GovGrant("kitchen", "order-produce")
oCon.GovSetAuthority("customer", :Delegated)
oCon.GovSetAuthority("kitchen", :Delegated)
oCon.Retire("supplier")
? oCon.CallAcross("customer", "kitchen", "send-order")
#--> 1
? oCon.CallAcross("kitchen", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 0
? oCon.Why()
#--> target world 'supplier' is not active.
oCon.Revive("supplier")
? oCon.CallAcross("kitchen", "supplier", "order-produce")
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

The call across the bond passes. The call to the sleeping world is refused! The reason is one line: `target world 'supplier' is not active.` Revived, it passes.

**The speed budget.** The owner's first priority was speed, so speed had to be a promise judged by something, not an aspiration. I hold this as a view, not a result. The budget has three numbers: the time to first display, the time to react to a tap, and the time for an order to reach the kitchen. The library declares them next to the code as expectations and judges them against what was measured. A breach fails the same gate as a security finding. The bounds and the samples in this block are mine, to show the form; they are not the project's budget. What does a breach look like? Run it:

```haro
oMon = StzPerfMonitor("remote-control")
oMon.NewTimer("first.display.ms").Record(900)
oMon.NewTimer("tap.ms").Record(40)
oMon.NewTimer("order.to.kitchen.ms").Record(1800)
oSla = StzSla("speed-budget")
oSla.ExpectP95("first.display.ms").Under(1200)
oSla.ExpectP95("tap.ms").Under(80)
oSla.ExpectP95("order.to.kitchen.ms").Under(2500)
oSla.CheckAgainst(oMon)
oSla.Show()
#--> SLA speed-budget -- 3 expectation(s), last check: MET.
#      [MET] p95-first.display.ms-under-1200: measured 900 (must be <= 1200)
#      [MET] p95-tap.ms-under-80: measured 40 (must be <= 80)
#      [MET] p95-order.to.kitchen.ms-under-2500: measured 1800 (must be <= 2500)
oMon.MetricQ("order.to.kitchen.ms").Record(3100)
oSla.CheckAgainst(oMon)
oRep = new stzRuleReport("cycle-gate")
oRep.Ingest(oSla.Findings())
? oRep.IsSound()
#--> 0
? oSla.Findings()[1][:message]
#--> p95-order.to.kitchen.ms-under-2500: measured 3100 (must be <= 2500)
```

> run: kept

The first check prints `3 expectation(s), last check: MET.` Then one order takes 3100 milliseconds, and the gate is no longer sound! The finding says it in the budget's own words: `p95-order.to.kitchen.ms-under-2500: measured 3100 (must be <= 2500)`.

Does the library carry what the project learned about interfaces? It does not. The project's file of interface rules is experience in a few lines: one clear action per screen; undo instead of confirm; a button that is refused is disabled in view, never hidden. And one test: a simulated first day on an empty database found the gravest hole, that no supplier could be created in production. Those rules were forged, in the project's own count, by eleven iterations and a campaign of twenty-two simulated tests with seventeen fixes. The library carries none of them as a check. So by the rule of this chapter they are the project's lesson and not the library's, and I tell them here without a block.

Six refusals follow, each with its reason.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused the grand system in one piece.** After two years of rich specification, the project nearly ended. The estimate of 2025 stays the reference for the project's scope; it is never to be presented as today's calendar. What replaced it is the doctrine of this chapter: deliver small, fast and useful.

**I refused to throw the first application away.** When the owner reversed the order, the application already built was kept, dormant, until the first version of the new order was in production. The project's own words for it: nothing is thrown away.

**I refused silent drift.** The Lock exists because a project that has failed once drifts without anyone deciding to. That is my view. A second change of course before the first version runs in production is refused, and so is an idea that enters the cycle without a written exit.

**I refused the agreeable fake.** A double that silently answers with an empty string produces a test that passes for the wrong reason. The sandbox declines when a gateway declines, refuses when a gateway is down, and refuses a second capture. And I refused a fixed fee before the go-live gate: no subscription to a payment institution before the owner's written go.

**I refused to credit the owner with what a simulation said.** The project has carried a rule since July 2026, that no action on its screens takes more than two gestures. The site prints it as part of the first amendment, and the project's documents carry it as the owner's constraint. Its earliest source is a line in the file of simulated answers of 3 July, written by me and a session. The project set it for itself, from its own simulation of his answers, and kept it. I tell it that way. What he did say, and what I take as his, is the priority on speed.

**I refused to say the library was born of his need.** The constellation design, the virtualization plan, the registry, the payments port and the performance judgment were all first committed after the reversal of 4 July. The plan is dated 23 July, and the performance study 29 July. The site says carefully that the project needed these things and that the library carries them. I will say no more than that. The record does not say which came first, his need or the parts: it shows the project finding them in the library, and I leave the order open.

Five guards stand behind the five blocks above.

## 5. The court

`06_stzsuperapp_narrated` proves the constellation: worlds joining, a call across worlds needing both worlds active, a declared bond and governance, and a retired world breaking its bonds and reviving. `service_delivery_narrated` proves that a production deployment by an entitled actor is refused while a fake is bound, with every reason logged, and that it opens once the binding is real. `payments_port_narrated` proves the sandbox's state machine: capture once, capture only an authorised charge, refund no more than captured, a decline is an answer and an outage is not. `judgment_narrated` proves an expectation declared in the operational vocabulary and judged against measured numbers, a breach as a finding that fails the gate, and an expectation on a number nobody measures as an error and never a silent pass. `graphrule_object_narrated` proves the rule as an object attached to a model, with the operators the Lock's construction uses.

The court proves what the library does; it does not say where the field does better.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in storage, in queries and in ecosystem. The library's own study of the performance field, written against the tools of early 2026, says it without softening: no time-series database, no query language for it, no dashboards, by design; export instead. Among the five properties it claims against them, the one this chapter uses is the first: nothing in the field couples a budget to the codebase's own rule system.

The field leads in the browser. The restaurant's worlds run in the browsers of customers and the kitchen, and I found nothing in the library that measures a first display or a tap there. The library's monitor observes a server, and the SLA judges whatever numbers are recorded into a timer. The owner's measurement protocol is at the seventy-fifth percentile on a mid-range phone. The library's terms cannot declare it exactly: they offer the fiftieth, the ninety-fifth and the ninety-ninth.

The field leads on real services. A double is not the service, and the plan says so: you still need at least one real run before production, and recorded responses drift. The real providers lead in the edge cases the sandbox does not model.

And the super-app hosts lead in installed base. The design names what the industry's super-app has, a store, a review, a native bridge, and replaces it; it does not claim users.

Now I say where the chapter stops.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the constellation of worlds with its bonds and norm-gated crossings, retirement, revival, hot swap and nesting; the payments sandbox and the production gate; the speed budget declared and judged; the graph rules the Lock's construction uses.

What is designed: the Lock as a rule of the library, of which the record has no code; the near-natural idiom of the constellation, with the verbs the design names and the class does not have.

What is absent: the restaurant's live payment adapter, which the library's charter calls a later task and the owner's decision. A measurement of the budget on a real mobile network, which the project's dashboard of 23 August postponed to the next cycle. The interface rules, the two-gesture rule among them, as checks, a seventy-fifth percentile expectation and browser-side timing, of which no document of the library holds a design. And the state of the platform after 25 August 2026, where the record ends. Nothing in it says the first version reached production at the restaurant, passed the go-live gate, or took a real order from a real customer. The record is silent on what happened after 25 August 2026, and I say so. Of the owner's tightened speed thresholds I print none, and say only that such a budget exists.

And what runs today is ordinary. The worlds are single HTML files, one per world, with a vendored framework, no build step and no content network. The server is a Node program of a few hundred lines.
