It uses five of Node's own modules, serves the worlds on the local network, and keeps one thing: a journal, chained by hashes, replayed at restart. The first management application has a phone wrapper around a web view. None of it runs on the Softanza engine. The library is the project's design reference. The constellation is, in the site's words, a design the library can run. The restaurant does not.

Six doors follow, one for each reader.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** You declare worlds and bonds and run a constellation in which a call to a world that is not yet active is refused with its reason. The restaurant's own code, however, is a web application.

**The analyst.** Rigour in numbers was the contract of the first application: a due date computed from the invoice date, a date required, a debt with no due date made visible.

**The designer.** Fifteen interface rules from one directive, the three-second test; and a refused button is disabled and visible, never hidden. On who held the phone in the eleven iterations, the project's files say both, the real user and me, and the chapter does not choose.

**The decision-maker.** The owner accepted the Lock in writing, tightened the speed budget himself, and kept the gate on fixed fees. The library's mirror is a deployment that an entitled person may commit and that is still refused over a fake.

**The educator.** The record supports only a small thing: the first application taught its user by doing, with guides whose words leave no doubt. There is no educator in this story, and I say so.

**The agent.** An agent can drive a production deployment and change nothing: the gate passes the services, nothing is committed, and the reason given is the actor and not the services. In the project, the agent is the session working beside me under the Lock, with a person deciding.

## 9. Exercise

Declare your own Lock for one cycle. Write the worlds of the cycle you are in, three of them, as a restaurant would: the customer's phone, the kitchen screen, and one more, each with the written reason it is in. Add a fourth, the idea you had this morning. Run the court. Change the declaration, not the court, until it says the cycle is sound.

```haro exercise
? YourGraph.RulesAreSound()
#--> 1
```

> run: exercise

The court will tell you the circumstance and the rule. It will not write the declaration. If you want a harder one, add a rule about the actions of your cycle, and find the action that costs a third gesture.

I left this one in your hands, on purpose. The owner's speed protocol is at the seventy-fifth percentile, and the budget of section 3 is judged at the ninety-fifth. Take that block, change the percentile of one expectation to another that the library offers, keep every other line, and run it. Read what the court judges against what he asked for. Section 6 says what the library's terms offer, and section 7 lists his expectation among the things absent. I give you neither the verdict nor the answer.

When you have solved it, you will know why a promise the court can judge is not yet the promise the owner asked for.

Chapter 29 is a bank in Niamey, where an organisation is judged by its own chart, under a regulator's rules, by a program that reads the chart as data. The chart says what is, the rule says what must be, and the verdict names the rule and the place.


=====

# A bank in Niamey

Source: Chapter 29. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the organisation chart as a text a person can edit; the validators of the regional regulator and of segregation of duties; the seven regimes as one mechanism; a rule written as data; one report with one verdict; designed: the content that would make the other six regimes differ from the first; a rule file that judges, and not only loads; absent: the delivered product running on the library; a word from the bank's own people; an auditor's reading of a finding the library produced

Run: 6 of 6 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:15 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take a bank in Niamey, under the central bank of the West African monetary union, and I say first where the lesson is: in the library, and not inside the product the bank runs. I write the chart of a small bank as a text and judge it under the regional rule and under segregation of duties. I print what each of the seven regimes holds, add a rule of the bank's own as data, and read the one report with its verdict. I say the four things I refused, the first of them to hide the structure in a database. The principle is one sentence: the chart says what is, the rule says what must be, and the verdict names the rule and the place.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

A bank is judged on its structure as much as on its numbers. A regulator asks who reports to whom: whether the audit function is independent of the executives it audits, whether there is a board, whether a risk function exists. It asks whether the person who operates a payment is the one who approves it. The field answers with documents: an organisation chart drawn in a slide, a policy written in a word processor, an audit that compares the two once a year.

The field also has a family of tools called governance, risk and compliance platforms, which keep registers of risks and controls and track findings through to closure. They record that a control exists and who owns it. They usually do not read the structure of the organisation and tell you that it is wrong. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.)

## 2. The first principle

**An organisation can be judged by its own chart, under a regulator's rules, by a program that reads the chart as data: the chart says what is, the rule says what must be, and the verdict names the rule and the place.**

## 3. The Softanza form

A commercial bank in Niamey, under the rules of the central bank of the West African monetary union, runs an organisation-assessment product I built. It is installed on the bank's own internal network, under licence, and the installation guide I delivered to the bank is the document that attests the reference. The product itself is a Python application: a small web server with a database. I say that first, because the lesson I am going to show you is in the library, and not inside that product.

The lesson is that an organisation chart is data a program can judge. It begins with a text that a person can edit, with no programming: positions, their titles, their departments, and who each one reports to. This is the chart of a small bank. What does the regional regulator's rule say of it? Run it:

```haro
cChart = '
orgchart "bank"

positions
    board
        title: Board of Directors
        level: executive
        department: board
        reportsTo:

    ceo
        title: Chief Executive
        level: executive
        department: executive
        reportsTo: board

    risk
        title: Chief Risk Officer
        level: management
        department: risk
        reportsTo: board

    audit
        title: Internal Audit
        level: management
        department: audit
        reportsTo: ceo
'
oOrg = new stzOrgChart("bank")
oOrg.ImportStzOrg(cChart)
? oOrg.NodeCount()
#--> 4
? @@( oOrg.ValidateXT(:BCEAO)[:issues] )
#--> [ "BCEAO-002: Audit reports to non-board position" ]
? oOrg.IsValidXT(:BCEAO)
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The regulator's rule reads the chart and refuses it! Audit reports to the chief executive, and the verdict says so in its own words, `BCEAO-002: Audit reports to non-board position`, with a rule code an auditor can look up. A chart is judged by more than one court at a time, each finding carries its own code, and the compliance report says compliant or not. What does a second court say, the segregation of duties, which holds that operations must not report to treasury? I add treasury and operations, operations under treasury, and run both courts:

```haro
oOrg.AddManagementPositionXT("treasury", "Treasury")
oOrg.SetPositionDepartment("treasury", "treasury")
oOrg.AddManagementPositionXT("ops", "Operations")
oOrg.SetPositionDepartment("ops", "operations")
oOrg.ReportsTo("treasury", "ceo")
oOrg.ReportsTo("ops", "treasury")
aV = oOrg.ValidateXT([ :BCEAO, :SOD ])
? aV[:validatorsFailed]
#--> 2
? aV[:results][1][:issues][1]
#--> BCEAO-002: Audit reports to non-board position
? aV[:results][2][:issues][1]
#--> SOD-001: Operations reports through Treasury
? oOrg.ComplianceReport()[:overallStatus]
#--> non-compliant
```

> run: kept

Two validators fail, each with its own code, and the compliance report says `non-compliant`.

The library names seven regimes: the regional one, Basel III, SOX, PCI DSS, ISO 27001, GDPR and HIPAA. They share one mechanism. How many rules does each hold? I print it, rather than write a sentence that says "Basel III is validated":

```haro
aBases = [ new stzBCEAORuleBase(), new stzBaselIIIRuleBase(), new stzSOXRuleBase(), new stzPCIDSSRuleBase(),
           new stzISO27001RuleBase(), new stzGDPRRuleBase(), new stzHIPAARuleBase() ]
for oBase in aBases
	? oBase.Name() + " : " + oBase.NumberOfRules()
next
#--> BCEAO : 4
#--> Basel III : 4
#--> SOX : 5
#--> PCI-DSS : 4
#--> ISO 27001 : 4
#--> GDPR : 4
#--> HIPAA : 4
```

> run: kept

Six regimes carry four rules, and one carries five! Four rules every base carries: nobody reports to themselves, no reporting cycle, no orphan position, and a span of control. SOX adds one of its own: no position holds both the approver and the executor role. It is also called the separation of duties, but it is a different rule from the second court above. The library's comment says it plainly: the regime classes differ by name today, and only one adds a rule of its own. I will come back to what that means.

A bank's own rule is data too. Take the rule that internal audit reports to the board. It is an object declared by calls. It names the department it governs, the line it requires and the sentence it prints, and it is added to a regime's rule base. Which rule broke, and where? The report says:

```haro
oRule = new stzGraphRule("audit-reports-to-board")
oRule.SetDomainQ("orgchart")
oRule.WhenQ("department", "equals", "audit")
oRule.ThenQ("reportsTo", "equals", "board")
oRule.ThenViolationQ("internal audit must report to the board")
oBasel = new stzBaselIIIRuleBase()
oBasel.AddRule(oRule)
oRep = new stzRuleReport("bank")
oRep.Ingest(oOrg.CheckCompliance(oBasel))
oRep.Report()
#--> Rule report 'bank': 1 finding(s), 1 error(s), 0 warning(s) -> UNSOUND (errors present)
#      [orgchart] 1 finding(s)
#        ERROR audit-reports-to-board @ audit -- internal audit must report to the board
```

> run: kept

The report says `UNSOUND` and names the rule and the place: `ERROR audit-reports-to-board @ audit -- internal audit must report to the board`.

Then the verdict follows the chart. Two edits to two reporting lines, audit now to the board and operations to the chief executive. What do the courts that said no say now? Run it:

```haro
oOrg.ReportsTo("audit", "board")
oOrg.ReportsTo("ops", "ceo")
? oOrg.IsValidXT([ :BCEAO, :SOD ])
#--> 1
? oOrg.ComplianceReport()[:overallStatus]
#--> compliant
oRep = new stzRuleReport("bank")
oRep.Ingest(oOrg.CheckCompliance(oBasel))
oRep.Report()
#--> Rule report 'bank': 0 finding(s), 0 error(s), 0 warning(s) -> SOUND
? oRep.IsSound()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

They say `compliant`, and the report says `SOUND`. That is the move the narration of a virtual bank tells, from a non-compliant structure to a governed one. Its own code does not run at the pinned commit; the calls above do. The report is one gate over the rule bases it is given. And here is the limit of it, as a block. A court reads what the chart declares and no more. What does it say when the chief executive's department is left blank? Run it:

```haro
oOrg2 = new stzOrgChart("bank")
oOrg2.ImportStzOrg( substr(cChart, "department: executive", "department:") )
? oOrg2.IsValidXT(:BCEAO)
#--> 1
? oOrg2.ValidateXT(:Banking)[:status]
#--> pass
oOrg2.SetPositionDepartment("ceo", "executive")
? oOrg2.IsValidXT(:BCEAO)
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

It says `1`: an audit function that reports to the chief executive passes! Declare the department, and the finding appears. The same block shows the banking validator answering `pass`: the library's own comment says it passes every time, a placeholder for rules not written yet.

My first book, *Refinement-Oriented Programming*, tells the discipline of governing change through the story of a bank facing a regulator's letter. This chapter does not retell it. It tells what the library carries.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused to hide the structure in a database.** In the delivered product, an organisation chart is rows in two tables. In the library, it is a text that a person can read and edit, and it imports into the object the court judges. A rule applied to a database row is a query; a rule applied to a text is something a person can read before it runs.

**I refused to claim more than the library holds.** The word "validator" can be read for two different things. The regional regulator has a validator of three rules, and a rule base of four universal ones. Basel III has the rule base only, and so do the others. I printed the count, and the block above shows it.

**I refused to carry a regulator's text in a code I cannot source.** The codes BCEAO-001 to BCEAO-003 are the library's own. The record ties them to no article of the central bank's text. They are the library's own rules, named after the regulator's regime; the record does not name a text they are drawn from.

**I refused to tell what I do not know.** What the bank's own people declare, operate or say is not in the record. The installation guide is my document to the bank: installation steps, a licence file, a support contact. The Forged page keeps no figure and no person of a client. The record is silent on who at the bank declares the chart, the questionnaire or the rules. It is silent on how long the product has been installed, and on how many people use it. I will invent none of it, and the chapter does not fill the gap. On "built with Softanza", the record shows a Python application, a small web server with a database, that calls nothing of the library. The library's validators are separate from it, and they are what this chapter runs. Chapter 1 says the product is built with Softanza and keeps its wording; this chapter says what the record shows.

Six guards stand behind the blocks.

## 5. The court

`orgrule_narrated` proves SOX, GDPR and HIPAA as rule bases over the chart's graph, with the four universal rules and the separation of duties that SOX adds, and the chart joining the one report. The block above prints the seven counts. `14_bceao_governance` proves two of the three rules of the regional validator. `15_multiple_validators` proves the combined verdict, and `13_segregation_of_duties` the second court. `rulereport_narrated` proves the one report over findings from code, agents and security. `36_import_from_stzorg` proves the text form of the chart.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in coverage. A governance and compliance platform usually holds a register of regulations, controls and findings, with workflows, owners and deadlines. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.) The library holds one structural check of a regional regulator, one of segregation of duties, and a mechanism. For GDPR, HIPAA and the others, it holds the mechanism and the four universal rules, and nothing that is specific to them.

The field leads in actual use: such a platform is often one that a bank's auditors already know, and that a regulator has already seen. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.)

Where I think this stands apart is that the chart is the data and the court is a rule over it. A chart is a text, a rule is data, a finding names the rule and the position, and the report is one verdict. The library's own comparison table for this feature is all wins, undated, with cost figures that cite nothing, and I will not print it.

Now the limit, in the stage words.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the organisation chart as a model and as text; the validators of the regional regulator and of segregation of duties; the seven regimes as one mechanism with the SOX exemplar. Built too: a rule written as data, and the one report with its verdict.

Four things are designed, or built in part.

- The content of the six regimes other than the regional one. Five of them hold the same four universal rules and nothing else; SOX adds a fifth.
- A rule file that judges. The library can load a file of rules in a `when` and `then` text, and the one in its test data loads two rules. The loader reads a rule's name, type, severity, function, params and message, and no `when` or `then` line. Such a rule loads and judges nothing. The format is admitted, and the rule is not enforced.
- A validator reads declared properties. It finds the board by the word "board" in a position title. It needs the departments to be declared.
- The succession validator flags every filled position, by the library's own comment a known defect. The reorganisation simulation raises when it is asked to change a reporting line, also a known defect. The article that tells the simulation names a method that exists nowhere in the library.

Four things are absent.

- The delivered product running on the library. It is a Python application; a search of its code found no regional or Basel check and no call to the library.
- A word from the bank. The record is silent.
- A perceived verdict. No document says that an auditor or a supervisor read a finding that the library produced.
- A delivery date. The delivered files carry file-system times, and those are not a delivery date.

The web layer of the product was born in its own repository: its first commit is dated 4 February 2026, and the interface constitution's text was first committed on 8 February. The constitution's own evidence fields say that its rules 117 to 121 were harvested from a banking-supervision product. The record does not say that product is the bank's installation. I will not say that it is.

Six doors follow, one for each reader.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** The chart is a text, the rule is data, the court is a call. A finding names the rule and the position.

**The analyst.** You change a reporting line in the object, or in the text and import again, and you re-run: the verdict follows the chart.

**The designer.** The report is one verdict over the rule bases it is given: sound or unsound, with each finding where it was found.

**The decision-maker.** Ask which of the regulations the library holds, and ask for the count. Seven regimes, one with a validator beside its rule base and one that adds a rule; the rest carry the universal four.

**The educator.** The narration of a virtual bank tells the move from a non-compliant structure to a governed one, and the book shows the move with calls that run.

**The agent.** You may read a chart and a report. A rule you add is data a person can read, and the court judges the chart with it.

## 9. Exercise

Draw the chart of an organisation you know as a text of positions, titles, departments and reporting lines. Before you run anything, write what you expect the regional rule, then segregation of duties, to say of it. Then write a rule of your own as data, one that governs one department, and say what the report will print.

Run it. Then leave one department blank and say which finding disappears, and why that is dangerous.

I left this one in your hands, on purpose. Take the limit block of section 3 and change one line: the import takes the chart whole, with the chief executive's department declared.

```haro exercise
oOrg2 = new stzOrgChart("bank")
oOrg2.ImportStzOrg(cChart)
? oOrg2.IsValidXT(:BCEAO)
? oOrg2.ValidateXT(:Banking)[:status]
oOrg2.SetPositionDepartment("ceo", "executive")
? oOrg2.IsValidXT(:BCEAO)
```

> run: exercise

Run it and put the three answers side by side. The regional court has already refused this chart, in section 3; now read what the second validator says of it, and why it is not the same as what the court said. Section 3 and section 4 say why. I do not give you the answer here. When you have it, you will know why a validator that answers `pass` has not judged the chart.

The next chapter leaves the bank for an organisation in the field, and its principle is one sentence: the agent proposes, and a person decides.


=====

# An organisation in the field

Source: Chapter 30. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: a rule built as an object on a model, its number changed and the model judged again; the rule governor that names a rule that governs nothing; a refusal kept as a checkpoint a person can read, with its context; authority set against risk; a decision kept with its authority at the time; offline as a capability a world must ask for; designed: the hub and its study; a rule language a local developer can learn; the study's promises that a rule is checked before it applies and can be undone; absent: the hub, built; a mechanism for work done offline, kept and sent later; a version history of a rule; a customer platform on the engine

Run: 7 of 7 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:15 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I take one organisation's design study and show the four things in it that the library carries. They are a rule as an object whose numbers can be changed, a refusal a person can read, authority set against risk, and a decision kept with its authority at the time. Then I show the one thing the organisation asked for that the library does not carry, offline, and I read that block as a limit and not as a win. I say "design study" and not "delivery" every time, because the hub is not built. The principle is the organisation's own: the agent proposes, and a person decides.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field has two practices that this project needed, and keeps them in separate places.

The first is the business rule that a business person can change. Rule engines and decision tables have existed for decades; the Decision Model and Notation, a standard of 2015, gives business analysts a table in which an approval ceiling is a number in a cell. The second is the application that keeps working with no network. Offline-first design is a practice of its own, with synchronising databases. Since an essay of 2019 it has a name for its philosophy, local-first software: the data is on your device, and the network is a convenience.

A third practice has come with the agents: a model proposes, and a person approves. In the field it is a button.

The organisation's study turns that third practice into a principle.

## 2. The first principle

The organisation's own study of the project gives it as the title of a section, and I take it as the principle of this chapter (my translation of the French title): **the agent proposes, and a person decides.**

## 3. The Softanza form

An organisation works in Niger, with a head office in Niamey and bases in the field. Its own specification preferred a low-code platform built on a large vendor's office suite. My offer of 5 August 2026 was the one retained. The study lists four gains, and ownership is the first it lists. The organisation owns its vocabulary and its rules; it changes them without programming; it pays no licence per seat for the platform; and it can hand everything to a programmer of its own country. We built the design study together, with its teams, from 22 September to 8 October 2026. I say "design study" and not "delivery" every time, because the hub is not built. The study's version 1.2 is submitted to the executive director for validation, and the record does not say it was validated.

Four things in that study the library carries. I will show them in the order the organisation would meet them, and then the one it asked for that the library does not carry.

**A rule is an object whose numbers can be changed.** An approval ceiling is a sentence with a number in it, attached to the model of the advances it governs. The model judges itself, and says how many subjects the rule looks at. The data below are invented for this chapter, four advances with an amount and a count of approvals. They are not the organisation's figures, and the numbers five hundred and two hundred are mine, not its thresholds. Does a ceiling of five hundred catch an advance, and how many advances does the rule look at?

```haro
oG = new stzGraph("advances")
oG.AddNodeXTT("a0", "A0", [ :amount = 120, :approvals = 1 ])
oG.AddNodeXTT("a1", "A1", [ :amount = 250, :approvals = 1 ])
oG.AddNodeXTT("a2", "A2", [ :amount = 600, :approvals = 1 ])
oG.AddNodeXTT("a3", "A3", [ :amount = 900, :approvals = 2 ])
oRule = new stzGraphRule("second-approval")
oRule.SetDomainQ("advances")
oRule.SetMessageQ("above the ceiling an advance needs two approvals")
oRule.WhenQ("amount", "greaterthan", 500)
oRule.ThenQ("approvals", "greaterthan", 1)
oRule.ThenViolationQ("two approvals needed")
oG.AddRule(oRule)
oG.RulesReport()
#--> Rule report 'advances': 1 finding(s), 1 error(s), 0 warning(s) -> UNSOUND (errors present)
#      [advances] 1 finding(s)
#        ERROR second-approval @ a2 -- two approvals needed
? len(oRule.SubjectsIn(oG))
#--> 2
```

> run: kept

The report names one advance, `a2`, at 600 with a single approval, and the rule looks at `2` of the four, `a2` and `a3`.

What happens when the ceiling is changed and the same model is judged again? A change of the rule is a change of its values, and the verdict and its scope follow:

```haro
oG.ClearAttachedRules()
oRule = new stzGraphRule("second-approval")
oRule.SetDomainQ("advances")
oRule.SetMessageQ("above the ceiling an advance needs two approvals")
oRule.WhenQ("amount", "greaterthan", 200)
oRule.ThenQ("approvals", "greaterthan", 1)
oRule.ThenViolationQ("two approvals needed")
oG.AddRule(oRule)
oG.RulesReport()
#--> Rule report 'advances': 2 finding(s), 2 error(s), 0 warning(s) -> UNSOUND (errors present)
#      [advances] 2 finding(s)
#        ERROR second-approval @ a1 -- two approvals needed
#        ERROR second-approval @ a2 -- two approvals needed
? len(oRule.SubjectsIn(oG))
#--> 3
```

> run: kept

The report now says `2 finding(s), 2 error(s)` where it said one, and the scope is `3` where it was `2`! I have to be honest about what this block shows. The "change" is a rule rebuilt in code with another number. That the organisation's own person changes it like filling in a form is the study's promise. I did not find a file of the library that makes it so. Section 7 says what does and does not exist.

What the library does carry is the answer to the worst thing that can go wrong when a non-programmer edits a rule: a rule that quietly governs nothing. I hold that as a view, not a result. Misspell a property, and the rule finds nothing, and the model reports that it is sound. What does a rule governor say of the same rule? The class is `StzRuleGovernance`; it asks rules what they govern and not what they say, and it names the dead rule:

```haro
oTypo = new stzGraphRule("second-approval-typo")
oTypo.SetMessageQ("above the ceiling an advance needs two approvals")
oTypo.WhenQ("amont", "greaterthan", 200)
oTypo.ThenQ("approvals", "greaterthan", 1)
oTypo.ThenViolationQ("two approvals needed")
? len(oTypo.Check(oG))
#--> 0
oWatch = StzRuleGovernance("hub")
oWatch.AddRule(oTypo)
oWatch.AddCase("advances", oG)
aF = oWatch.CheckRules()
? aF[1][:rule]
#--> scope_empty
? aF[1][:message]
#--> governs nothing in 1 pictures -- a dead rule and a misspelt scope predicate both report as passing
oWatch2 = StzRuleGovernance("hub")
oWatch2.AddRule(oRule)
oWatch2.AddCase("advances", oG)
? len(oWatch2.CheckRules())
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The misspelt rule finds nothing, `0`, and to the model nothing found means sound! The governor answers `scope_empty`, and says why: `governs nothing in 1 pictures -- a dead rule and a misspelt scope predicate both report as passing`. A dead rule and an all-clear produce identical output: silence. The court has to tell "nothing was wrong" from "nothing was looked at". The word "pictures" in the message is the rule governor's origin: it was built for the graph plane's drawings first, and I would say "cases".

**A person can read the refusal.** When an answer breaks a law the organisation declared, it is refused with its reason. The refusal is kept as a checkpoint a person can read, with its context. Chapter 17 showed the asking half of this machine. Here is the refusing half. A request needs one approval, and the law says a request has one approver. What does the machine do with a second approver?

```haro
oKB = new stzKnowledgeGraph("requests")
oKB.AddConversationQ("review").SetGoal(StzGoalQ().RequireOne("request-7", "approved-by"))
? oKB.AskIn("review")
#--> What does 'request-7' have for 'approved-by'?  (why: 'request-7' needs 'approved-by')
oKB.ConstrainRelation("approved-by", :Unique)
oKB.KnowRelation("request-7", "approved-by", "approver-1")
aR = oKB.ReplyIn("review", "approver-2")
? aR[:refused][1][2]
#--> 'request-7' already bears 'approved-by' (to 'approver-1'); the relation is :Unique
aCp = oKB.ConversationQ("review").Checkpoints()
? len(aCp)
#--> 1
? aCp[1][:attempted]
#--> approver-2
? aCp[1][:why]
#--> 'request-7' already bears 'approved-by' (to 'approver-1'); the relation is :Unique
```

> run: kept

The machine asks first: `What does 'request-7' have for 'approved-by'?  (why: 'request-7' needs 'approved-by')`. Then the second approver is refused! The checkpoint keeps what was attempted, `approver-2`, and the reason, in the refusal's own words: `'request-7' already bears 'approved-by' (to 'approver-1'); the relation is :Unique`.

**Authority is not permission.** The assistant of this organisation may prepare a file. It may hold the permission to release an advance and still be refused, because its authority does not cover the act's risk. A person with the authority is allowed. May the assistant release the advance, and may the approver? The verdict says why, in either case:

```haro
oGov = new stzGovernance("hub")
oGov.DeclareRisk("prepare-file", 1)
oGov.DeclareRisk("release-advance", 3)
oGov.GrantPermission("assistant", "prepare-file")
oGov.GrantPermission("assistant", "release-advance")
oGov.SetAuthority("assistant", :Advisory)
oGov.GrantPermission("approver-1", "release-advance")
oGov.SetAuthority("approver-1", :Autonomous)
? oGov.MayProceed("assistant", "prepare-file")
#--> 1
? oGov.MayProceed("assistant", "release-advance")
#--> 0
? oGov.Why()
#--> refused: 'assistant' holds 'advisory' authority (level 1) but 'release-advance' is risk tier 3 (SHOULD does not cover it)
? oGov.MayProceed("approver-1", "release-advance")
#--> 1
? oGov.Why()
#--> allowed: permission held AND 'autonomous' authority (level 3) covers risk tier 3
```

> run: kept

The assistant holds the permission for `release-advance`, and the answer is `0`! The verdict reads `refused: 'assistant' holds 'advisory' authority (level 1) but 'release-advance' is risk tier 3 (SHOULD does not cover it)`. For the approver it reads `allowed: permission held AND 'autonomous' authority (level 3) covers risk tier 3`.

The class here, `stzGovernance`, judges authority; it is not the rule governor of the dead rule above. The library knows an actor only by a name and an authority level. Whether an actor is a person or an assistant is what its level is declared to be. Binding a level to a signed-in person is the work of the authentication plane, which I have not read for this chapter.

**A decision keeps its authority at the time.** Each decision records its reason, its actor, the act, the authority and the risk of that moment. A new decision supersedes the old one without erasing it, up to a capacity (512 decisions by default), and the governance counts what it dropped. Record two decisions on the same case an hour apart: which one does the lineage answer with, and does the first survive?

```haro
nAt = 1785700000000
oGov.RecordDecisionAt("c-1", "ceiling raised for the harvest season", "approver-1", "release-advance", nAt)
oGov.RecordDecisionAt("c-1", "ceiling restored, the season is over", "approver-1", "release-advance", nAt + 3600000)
? oGov.LineageOf("c-1")[:rationale]
#--> ceiling restored, the season is over
? len(oGov.LineageHistoryOf("c-1"))
#--> 2
? oGov.LineageHistoryOf("c-1")[1][:rationale]
#--> ceiling raised for the harvest season
? oGov.LineageOf("c-1")[:authorityAtTime]
#--> autonomous
```

> run: kept

The lineage answers `ceiling restored, the season is over`. It keeps `2` decisions, and the first still reads `ceiling raised for the harvest season`. The authority at the time is `autonomous`. The instant is a fixed number, so the clock is not read. This makes the block repeatable. It is not a version store for the rule itself, and I will not pretend it is.

And the one it asked for that the library does not carry. Its default option is to work offline: bases in the field with no network, entering what they do and sending it later. The library knows offline as a declared, governed capability of a world, asked for and granted or refused like the camera or payments. Is offline granted before the world asks for it, and after? That is all the library knows of it:

```haro
oApp = StzAppQ("field-visits")
oPlat = StzPlatformQ("field")
oPlat.SetWorld(oApp)
oRegime = new stzGovernance("field-regime")
oRegime.GrantPermission("field-visits", "use-offline")
oRegime.SetAuthority("field-visits", :Advisory)
oPlat.SetGovernedBy(oRegime)
? oPlat.Granted(:offline)
#--> 0
? oPlat.Why()
#--> capability 'offline' was never requested via AddCapabilityQ().
oPlat.AddCapabilityQ(:offline).SetPurposes([ :enter_without_network ])
? oPlat.Granted(:offline)
#--> 1
? oPlat.Why()
#--> allowed: permission held AND 'advisory' authority (level 1) covers risk tier 1
```

> run: kept

Before the request, `Granted(:offline)` is `0`, and the printed reason is `capability 'offline' was never requested via AddCapabilityQ().`. After the request it is `1`, and the printed reason is `allowed: permission held AND 'advisory' authority (level 1) covers risk tier 1`.

Read this block as a limit and not as a win. Nothing in it queues anything. The library does not keep entries on a device, send them when the network comes back, or settle two people's edits.

The next section sets down the decision, and what I refused.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused a per-seat platform as the organisation's ground.** The specification preferred low-code on a vendor's suite. The retained offer proposes Softanza, and among its gains the study lists ownership first. The organisation owns its vocabulary and its rules, which it can change without programming, and the whole of the work, programs, configuration and data, is its own, with a handover to a local programmer. The vendor's suite stays where it was good, for the user accounts, the documents and the notifications. The record holds the outcome and the gains, and not the deliberation. Who chose, and what was weighed against low-code, it does not say. The chapter does not fill the gap.

**I refused to let the assistant decide.** It proposes. Its authority is advisory; a person's covers the risk. The refusal names the level, the risk tier, and the sentence that says the authority does not cover the act: `SHOULD does not cover it`.

**I refused a silent rule.** A rule that governs nothing is reported. A rule with no message is reported too. That is the price of letting a non-programmer touch a rule. The rule governor exists because the silent rule already happened in the library.

**I refused to call offline a lesson.** The Forged page, read on 9 October 2026, says: "This is a gap, not a lesson learned." A restaurant asked for the same. The study is clear that offline is the organisation's default, and the library has only a permission for it. A sister package of the estate, a browser-side one, holds a durable outbox on the device and an ordered, idempotent replay; it is outside the library. I did not run it.

## 5. The court

`graphruleset_narrated` proves a rule with a comparison and a requirement turned into an implication. `gg_rule_governance` proves that a rule that governs nothing is reported. `wisecoding_narrated`, its third scene, proves a refusal that is governed and checkpointed. `governance_narrated` proves that can is not should: permission held and authority lacking is a refusal. `governance_lineage_narrated` proves the decision kept with its authority at the time. `01_platform_narrated` proves the capability seam: the camera granted, payments refused, a capability never asked for refused with an honest reason. The guard does not itself use the capability for offline; the block above does, with the tier the platform declares for it. The refusal of an unknown rule type, of a missing file and of a duplicate name rests on scenes 4 and 5 of `rulz_roundtrip_narrated`. That is a narrated test of the library, and this book does not run it as one of its guards.

The next section sets what the field does against what the library has.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in business rules for business people. Decision tables are a standard; a person edits a cell, not a program. Here the change is a rule rebuilt in code. The file form that would carry a rule as text, the `.stzrulz` file, reads a rule's name, its type, its severity, its function, its parameters and its message. It does not read the `when` and `then` lines of its own sample.

The field leads in offline. Local-first software is a practice with synchronising databases and a decade of experience. The library has a permission.

The field leads in people. A decision table is a thing a trained analyst already knows how to read. And the organisation's own specification, which preferred the vendor's suite, had good reasons: its users already have accounts there.

The next section lists what is built, what is designed and what is absent.

## 7. The limit

What is built: a rule as an object, its number changed and the model judged again; the rule governor that names a dead rule; the checkpoint; authority against risk; the lineage; offline as a capability.

What is designed: the hub and its study, whose schedule is in the future; a rule language that a local programmer can learn; and the study's promises about rules. The language's sample in the study is written in a notation that is not the library's. The study promises that a rule is checked before it applies, that its effect is shown before it is applied, that it can be undone and that its history is kept. The library has only the pieces. They are the rule governor, the refusal of an unknown rule type or a missing file, and the refusal of a duplicate name.

What is absent: a version history of a rule with an undo. The lineage records the decision, and the rule is rebound. I found no store of rule versions, and my search was not exhaustive. The hub, built. Offline as a mechanism: no class in the library keeps entries on a device and sends them when the network returns. And a customer platform on the Softanza engine.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** A rule is an object with a comparison and a requirement, attached to a model, and a rule governor tells you when it governs nothing.

**The analyst.** The study promises that you change a rule's values, not its program. In the library, when the values change, the model is judged again with the new scope.

**The designer.** A refusal keeps its context for the person who will read it.

**The decision-maker.** Authority is not permission: ask which level covers which risk, and read the lineage of a decision.

**The educator.** An organisation's rule can be taught as a sentence with a number in it.

**The agent.** You prepare. A person decides. And you will be refused, with the level and the risk tier named.

## 9. Exercise

Pick one rule of an organisation you know that has a number in it, a ceiling, a delay, a count of approvals. Write it as a rule with a condition and a requirement, on four invented cases, two that satisfy it and two that do not. Before you run anything, write the report you expect. Then misspell one property of the condition on purpose, and say what the model reports, and what the governor reports.

Run it. The difference between those two answers is the chapter.

I left this one in your hands. The block below is the second block of section 3 followed by the last lines of the governor block, with one change: the rule has no `SetMessageQ` line, so it carries no message.

```haro exercise
oG.ClearAttachedRules()
oRule = new stzGraphRule("second-approval")
oRule.SetDomainQ("advances")
oRule.WhenQ("amount", "greaterthan", 200)
oRule.ThenQ("approvals", "greaterthan", 1)
oRule.ThenViolationQ("two approvals needed")
oG.AddRule(oRule)
oG.RulesReport()
oWatch2 = StzRuleGovernance("hub")
oWatch2.AddRule(oRule)
oWatch2.AddCase("advances", oG)
? len(oWatch2.CheckRules())
```

> run: exercise

Run it on the model of section 3. Compare what the model reports with what it reported when the rule had its message, and read what the governor returns. Under "I refused a silent rule", section 4 says what the governor does with such a rule, and why. I do not give the answer here. When you have solved it, you will know why the governor reports a rule that has no message.

The next chapter says what the projects show, and what comes next. Its measure is this: a project shows exactly what a document, a file, a run and a named person reach, and the page says where they stop.


=====

# What the projects show, and what comes next

Source: Chapter 31. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: what the three projects delivered, each with a document (a first delivery followed by a field day; a product installed under licence; a design study built with the organisation's teams); the three lessons the library carries, each with a guard (a fake never ships; a chart judged under a regulator's rules; authority against risk, with its lineage); refusals that show an absence instead of hiding it; designed: the five-row guarantee sheet; a runtime that is the language's own running the library; the declared machine on a board; absent: any customer platform on the engine; the customers' declarations written as Softanza's own, as files; a word from the bank; the record of the restaurant after 25 August 2026; the organisation's hub; an offline mechanism in the library; a live payments run against the organisation's sandbox; a named person's verdict on any of it

Run: 5 of 5 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:16 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I say, project by project, what the three projects of chapters 28 to 30 show today, and what each one makes the next step. I start with the field's three habits for saying what a piece of work has shown. Then I run five small blocks in which the library shows, by refusing, where a stage ends, and I lay out the tables: for each project, the claim, the document or file that backs it, and what comes next. I say what I refused, the author's memory as evidence first of all, and I give the court, the field and the limit in the stage words. The principle is the chapter's own sentence: a project shows exactly what a document, a file, a run and a named person reach, and the page says where they stop.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

The field has three habits for saying what a piece of work has shown.

The first is the reference: a logo on a home page, a customer story of two pages, the sentence "trusted by". It shows that someone paid or signed. It does not say what they ran, for how long, or what they said.

The second is the readiness scale. NASA's technology readiness levels, later an international standard, name how far a technology has travelled from a laboratory to the place it was built for. The highest level is a system proven in operation. It is a vocabulary of stage, and this book uses one of its own (built, designed, absent).

The third is the limits section. An empirical study must state its threats to validity. A machine-learning model ships with a card whose last part is its caveats. An artifact submitted to a research conference is badged by what someone else did with it. This is the form closest to this chapter, and the one most often skipped when the reader is a buyer.

What the three have in common is that the claim and its proof travel apart. A page says "used by", and the proof is a trust in the page. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.)

## 2. The first principle

The record gives two sentences, and they are one. The site says: "Nothing is a reference until a document proves it." And, of what the author remembers: what "no file proves is not listed."

**A project shows exactly what a document, a file, a run and a named person reach, and the page says where they stop.** This is the chapter's own sentence, drawn from the site's two. The site's words are the two quoted above, from its customers page and its forged page.

## 3. The Softanza form

This chapter is the other half of chapters 28 to 30. Those told what three projects taught the library. This one says, project by project, what each project shows today and what it makes the next step. Parts of Softanza are in production, and others are in development. I did not write this chapter after the fact, to be modest: the site's forged page already carries a section on the same subject.

The shape of the answer is a table of three columns for each project: the claim, the document or file that backs it, and what comes next. I leave a cell empty when I cannot name what fills it. And I will not fill the third column from memory.

Before the tables, five small blocks. Each is a place where the library shows, by refusing, where a stage ends, instead of passing silently. They are the chapter's sentence, written as code.

**A dependency declared and never bound is an error.** The surface of a solution is declared before anything is bound. A declared service that nothing backs is counted as a finding, not a pass, and asking for it raises where the mistake is. This is the first step the restaurant's lesson points to: binding a real payments service, which the record does not yet hold. What does the registry answer when the payments service is asked for and nothing backs it? Run it:

```haro
oReg = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oReg.DeclareMany([ :mail, :payments ])
? @@( oReg.UnboundServices() )
#--> [ "mail", "payments" ]
? oReg.Findings()[1][:invariant]
#--> unbound-service
? oReg.IsSound()
#--> 0
try
	oReg.Service(:payments)
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> stzServiceRegistry(shop): nothing is bound for service 'payments'. Bind it (or BindLive it) before use.
```

> run: kept

Two services declared, none bound, and the registry says so itself: `nothing is bound for service 'payments'`!

**What a rehearsal can claim, and what only the target can.** The platform model gives three verdicts for the same operation. *Refused*: the target forbids it. *Native*: the host itself can do it. *Rehearsed*: the target allows it and the host lacks it, so the run shows only the plan. Reading a pin on a development machine is rehearsed, not native. The word depends on the machine this book was run on. A Windows PC has no such pin, and a board that has one would print `native` for the same line. What does the board's scope answer to a pin read, and to a request to spawn a process? Run it:

```haro
oSol = new stzPlatformProfile("kiosk")
oSol.DevelopedOn(:Windows)
oSol.WithServer(:backend, :LinuxServer)
oSol.WithApp(:firmware, :ESP32)
oFw = oSol.App(:firmware).System()
? oFw.ReadPin(4)
#--> rehearsed
try
	oFw.Spawn("worker")
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> scope 'esp32' (espidf) forbids 'process' -- spawn 'worker'. This target has no such capability.
oFw.Show()
#--> Scope: esp32 (espidf)
#      [rehearsed] read pin 4
```

> run: kept (touches the system)

The pin read answers `rehearsed`, and the scope refuses the process with `forbids 'process'`!

The library's own comment, in the class that lowers a rehearsal to a real artifact, names the next step: the last one, flashing a board, "remains a PLANNED external action".

**The live payments adapter exists, and refuses to guess.** The code for the live payments system exists. Its run against the organisation's sandbox has not happened yet, and it is the next step. Given no credentials, it refuses before any network call and says what is missing. What does it say when it is asked for accounts and holds no credentials? The host in this block does not exist, and nothing reaches it:

```haro
oAd = new stzPispiHttpAdapter()
oAd.WithParticipant("shop")
oAd.WithBaseUrl("https://pispi.example")
oAd.AsLive()
? oAd.IsSandbox()
#--> 0
try
	oAd.Request("GET", "/comptes", [], [])
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> stzPispiHttpAdapter: no credentials. Give it the store and an actor: WithSecretsFrom(oStore, oActor).
? oAd.TokensFetched()
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

The adapter answers `no credentials`, names what would fill the gap, `WithSecretsFrom(oStore, oActor)`, and `TokensFetched()` stays at `0`.

The library has a script that tries the live run. I ran it once, with none of its variables set, for this chapter. It printed the word UNPERCEIVED, named the four variables that are missing, and said that nothing about the live adapter is perceived until a named person has watched a payment land. It ended without failure. The run was postponed, by me, for a reason that is not the library's: it needs the organisation's involvement. A run that could not happen is reported as unperceived. It is not reported as a pass or a failure.

**A feature that waits for a named person.** In the sound plane, the library holds as data the verdict of a person on a thing only a person can judge. While the verdict is UNPERCEIVED, the verb that would claim the result refuses and says why. Where a person has said yes, the verdict carries the date and their words. What does the formant voice answer when it is asked to sing, and what does the retuned voice's verdict hold? Run it:

```haro
oVoice = StzSoundFormantVoiceQ()
oVoice.Sing("la la la", "c4 e4 g4")
? oVoice.Refusals()
#--> 1
? oVoice.LastError()
#--> Sing is not available YET: the formant voice has not been heard by the author (sound_mu5_demo.ring plays it). SAPI was measured and does not hold a pitch (measured 2026-09-26: +-6 st asked, +-2.5 st given, up to 407 cents off, 286-575 cents of wander within a syllable -- it speaks, it cannot hold a note). Until the author calls the formant voice singing, Vowel and VowelsQ are what this voice honestly does
aVerdicts = StzSoundSingingVerdict()
? aVerdicts[:formant][:verdict]
#--> UNPERCEIVED
? aVerdicts[:retuned][:verdict]
#--> SINGING
? aVerdicts[:retuned][:date]
#--> 2026-09-27
? aVerdicts[:retuned][:said]
#--> the retuned voice is somehow singing, open Sing()
```

> run: kept

The formant voice refused to sing: `Refusals()` reads `1`, and the message begins `Sing is not available YET`! The verdict of the formant voice stays `UNPERCEIVED`. The verdict of the retuned voice reads `SINGING`, with the date `2026-09-27` and the words `the retuned voice is somehow singing, open Sing()`. This is the form of the line that chapter 33 sets as the next milestone for a customer's run: a date, a person, and a sentence. The record also carries the person's name beside it, and I do not print it here. (SAPI is the operating system's speech interface.)

**A pre-flight that says "feasible" without touching a host.** The deployment's pre-flight refuses a host that is too small. For a host whose provider is a cloud, it says feasible and "provision on aws", without any account. A feasibility is a plan, not a verification. The design file names the next step plainly: the verification against a real host or account, which waits on infrastructure. The figures below are illustrative. What does the pre-flight answer to a host that is too small, and to a cloud provider with no account? Run the block:

```haro
oBrain = new stzDelivery("shop")
oBrain.AddBackend(:api, :LinuxServer)
oBrain.RequiresIn(:api, StzResourcesQ().SetMemoryQ(2048).SetComputeQ(2).SetStorageQ(20))
oSmall = new stzDeploymentSite("tiny")
oSmall.SetKindQ(:Server).SetCapacityQ(StzResourcesQ().SetMemoryQ(512).SetComputeQ(1).SetStorageQ(5))
oTiny = new stzDeployment(oBrain)
oTiny.SetTarget(:api, oSmall)
? oTiny.Feasible()
#--> 0
? oTiny.Feasibility()[1][5]
#--> SHORTFALL -- host too small
oCloud = new stzDeploymentSite("pool")
oCloud.SetKindQ(:Server).SetProviderQ(:aws)
oPool = new stzDeployment(oBrain)
oPool.SetTarget(:api, oCloud)
? oPool.Feasible()
#--> 1
? oPool.Feasibility()[1][5]
#--> provision on aws
```

> run: kept

The tiny host gets `SHORTFALL -- host too small`, and the cloud with no account is feasible, with `provision on aws`!

Now the tables. Each one opens with what the project delivered or ran, and ends with what comes next. Every cell is from the record, read on 8 October 2026. A line count or a file date I read myself is labelled as mine.
