### What the study of the record marks for measurement

The comparative study that read the record says, in its own paragraphs headed "Not measured", what it leaves to measure next. It read the record and did not run it: its closing assessment of the record says "Nothing in it was run". Whether Haro's runtime runs the library is the first measure still to come. Whether two published speed benchmarks hold today is the second, and both were run on the former runtime. Some guards in the system folder took their totals from design documents, and one of those documents still says forty-five where its guard now holds more; the document is the next to update. The forty-one pictures of part 1 of the gallery were judged by a model, and a person looking at them is the next step. The restaurant's speed budget awaits its measure on a real 4G network. The study applies its own perception law to itself, which is why I trust the paragraph.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused the author's memory as evidence.** The site lists nothing that I remember and no file proves. The same rule bound the bank: I know the product, because I built it, and what the bank's own people do with it is not yet in a file.

**I refused to call the library the origin of what it only uses as an example.** The forged page says "example" where the library holds a project as its example and not as its origin. And I will not repeat a sentence in the study of 12.29 that says a lesson was "born of the client's need". The registry and the SLA were first committed after the reversal of 4 July, by the library's log as chapter 28 reads it. The order of the commits does not support "born of". What I can say is "needed" and "carries".

**I replaced two wordings of my own with the record's.** In chapters 1, 2 and 33 I wrote that the bank "runs" a product "built with" Softanza, and that the restaurant "runs" a platform. The record shows a Python application, installed at the bank under licence and built beside the library by the same person, and for the restaurant a first delivery and a field day, with the record ending on 25 August. The wording that the record bears out is *built by me, under the discipline the library now carries*, for the bank, and, for the restaurant, *first iteration delivered* (the site) and *a day of field-testing* (the project's article). Chapters 2 and 33 now carry wordings of that kind. Chapter 1 keeps its own, and this paragraph is where the record's more exact one stands.

**I refused a stage word the repository does not carry.** The site's estate page says it: never called available before it is. A piece of work that reaches a person through a sense is perceived by a person. Until then it is unperceived, which is a state and not a failure.

**I refused to print a percentage.** I have said aloud how far the bridge runtime has come. The study ruled that the figure should not be printed without the guard that produces it. I agree.

## 5. The court

`service_registry_narrated` proves that a declared service nobody backs is an error finding, that an unbound service raises, and that a sandbox in production is a violation. `full_scope_model_narrated`, scenes 5 to 8, proves the three verdicts: refused, rehearsed, native. `payments_live_adapter_narrated` proves the adapter against a twin served over HTTP, an adapter without credentials that refuses to guess, and the scenario that says stage (b) is unperceived. It starts real server processes, and the book does not run it; it only counts its assertions. `sound_mu5_narrated` proves, in its fifth scene, that while the verdict is unperceived `Sing` returns nothing and says why. `deployment_narrated` proves that an undersized host is not feasible, and that an assistant rehearses and commits nothing; it writes a scratch folder beside itself, so the book does not run it either. `orgrule_narrated` proves the universal rules of the chart and the separation of duties. `14_bceao_governance`, one assertion, runs the regional rules on a chart with no board and no risk function. `governance_narrated` proves authority against risk, with its lineage.

The court of this chapter's subject is not a guard. It is a line of the record, of the form the estate's law prescribes: a date, a person, and what they said. The library holds one such line in its data, in the sound plane. The estate holds one in its record, and chapter 33 tells it.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in production. Ordinary stacks run real workloads for real users. (General knowledge, not a study of the record.) The site's own page places the platforms of this part on ordinary stacks today, with the hub not built; moving their declarations onto Softanza's own engine is the next step.

The field leads in references at scale. The site prints two references and a design study, and quotes no number of countries and no number of programmers. A buyer reading a logo wall sees more.

The field leads in live infrastructure. Provisioning tools drive real providers every day. (General knowledge.) The library's deployment reaches local sites for real today, and the real host is the next step; the fifth block shows where the line runs.

The field leads in hardware in the field. The declared machine boots and judges itself in an emulator, and the vision records that no customer is on it yet. Its guarantee text says four of four promised, three kept in the emulator, and carries no slot decision; the vision adds that the fourth, surviving a cut, waits for the watchdog on the board. The board is on order.

The field leads in speed. The runtime Softanza measured itself against was faster on every row, by a factor between one and two, and both benchmarks were run on the former runtime; Haro's own speed is the measure still to come.

The field leads in pictures judged by people. Forty-one pictures and text sections of part 1 of the gallery were judged by a model: twenty right, nine wrong, twelve uncertain. The record says that no person has looked at them yet, and that look is the next step.

Where I think the record stands apart is that its limit is a section of a page, with a heading. Every stage has a word. A feature can be built and still refuse until a named person has spoken. And a live run that could not happen is reported unperceived, never a failure and never a pass.

## 7. The limit

This whole chapter is a stage report. Here is its sum in the stage words.

What is built: what the three projects delivered, each with a document (a first delivery at the restaurant, followed by a field day; a product installed at the bank under licence; a design study built with the organisation's teams), and the three lessons they gave the library. *A fake never ships*: the registry and the production gate. *An organisation is judged by its own chart under a regulator's rules*: the organisation chart and its validators. *Authority is not permission, and a decision keeps its authority at the time*: the governance object. Each has a file of the library that carries it, a guard that resolves, and a block already kept in a neighbouring chapter. The next step of each is in the tables. In the library's registry guard a sandbox is bound, and the live payment adapter is the step after it; the bank's product does not call the chart validator. The organisation's hub is not yet built, and its offline mechanism is to come.

What is designed: the five-row guarantee sheet; the runtime that is the language's own, running the library; the declared machine on a board.

What is absent: any customer platform on the engine; the customers' declarations written as Softanza's own, as files; a word from the bank; the record of the restaurant after 25 August; the organisation's hub; an offline mechanism in the library; a live payments run against the organisation's sandbox; a named person's verdict on any of it.

The sum of every next step is the line that chapter 33 sets as the next milestone: *the solution ran on Softanza's own engine, and this is what the user said.* The customers' declarations are its first piece, and they are absent today. The site says they are being written as Softanza's own. On 8 October 2026 I looked for a file of them in the restaurant's repository, in the bank's delivery and in the organisation's study folder, and found none. On 9 October 2026 I looked again, read-only, in thirteen repositories beside the library, and found no file that declares a customer's platform as Softanza's own. The chapter's word for them is *absent*, and the stage line at the top says so.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Your demo is a rehearsal until a target has run it. Ask the scope whether the line was native, rehearsed or refused, and write the same word beside your own claims.

**The analyst.** When you cite a project, write three columns: the claim, the document, and what comes next. Do not fill the third from memory.

**The designer.** A feature that no person has seen or heard ships shut, and says so. The stage is a state of the design and not an apology. I hold this as a view, not a result. What stands behind it on this page is the sound plane, where `Sing` refuses until a person has heard the voice.

**The decision-maker.** Read the stage word before the testimonial. "Built" is a statement about a guard. "Runs at a customer" is a statement about a document. You can ask for it in writing, with a name beside it.

**The educator.** Teach the difference between a project that taught the library and a project that runs on it. The first is shown here, and the second is the next milestone, in chapter 33. Unperceived is a state, not a failure.

**The agent.** You may read the tables and quote any cell. You may not turn an absent cell into a present one. If you do, the next reader of the record will find your sentence and the missing file side by side.

## 9. Exercise

Declare the surface of one project of yours: a mail service, a payments service, and one more of your own. Before you run anything, write the line you expect under each of three questions: which services are unbound; whether the registry is sound; which are unbound once the mail service has a sandbox bound.

```haro exercise
oReg = new stzServiceRegistry("my-project")
oReg.DeclareMany([ :mail, :payments, :your_service ])
? @@( oReg.UnboundServices() )
#--> what you expect
? oReg.IsSound()
#--> what you expect
oReg.Bind(:mail, new stzMailSandbox())
? @@( oReg.UnboundServices() )
#--> what you expect
```

> run: exercise

Then, on paper, make the table of three columns for the project you know best: the claim, the document or file that backs it, and what comes next. Leave empty what you cannot name. On the last line write who perceived it, on what date, and what they said, or the word "unperceived" and why.

The exercise has no answer to be checked first. The question the tutor asks is: which cell did you fill from memory?

I left this one in your hands, on purpose. Below is the first block of this chapter with one line added: it binds the payments service to the library's sandbox before the registry is asked whether it is sound.

```haro exercise
oReg = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oReg.DeclareMany([ :mail, :payments ])
oReg.Bind(:payments, new stzPaymentsSandbox())
? oReg.IsSound()
```

> run: exercise

Run it and read what `IsSound()` answers. Then ask whether a sound registry is a registry ready to ship, and what the guard of section 5 says about a fake still bound in production. I do not give the answer: section 5 names the guard that holds it, and section 7 says what the project has bound and what it has not. When you have solved it, you will know what a sound registry does not say about the service behind it.

Chapter 32 turns first to how many minds worked on this one body of work, and to the rule they keep: every law cites the incident that earned it, and a new law without a dated failure behind it is a preference. Chapter 33 then closes the book on the next milestone, a solution running on Softanza's own engine.


=====

# Many minds on one body of work

Source: Chapter 32. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: the protocol, its memos and its ledger of rulings; three hooks: one refuses wholesale staging, one refuses a register edit made on a stale view of its source system, one refuses work on a flooded machine; the instrument that measures composed stamps; the library's fourteen house rules, five of them over a code graph; designed: a watcher that wakes a session when a file arrives, in place of the clocks; a court of style for the code a programmer or an agent writes; absent: token figures in the cost record; any team outside this estate that has tried the protocol; a check of a word in the reader's own language

Run: 3 of 3 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:16 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I show how Softanza has been written for the last two years: by many sessions of a model on one machine, with no framework, only files, a version history and a few hooks. I walk through the rules the record paid for, one failure at a time, and the library's fourteen house rules that an agent can run before it commits. I say what I refused: a framework, a coordinator that grades its legislator, automation to the maximum, a ruling in my absence, a protocol sold as a product. And I say the loss I accepted: my hand thinned where the sessions wrote. The principle is one of the sessions' own sentences: every law cites the incident that earned it.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

When the field speaks of many agents working on one body of code, it usually means a framework. A program holds several agents, a supervisor routes messages between them, and the coordination is a feature of that program. Microsoft's AutoGen, CrewAI and LangChain's LangGraph are the names you will meet, all from the last three years.

When the field speaks of the rules those agents follow, it means a file the agent reads when it starts, a page of instructions in the repository. And when it speaks of many writers on one repository, it means what teams have always used: branches, review, hooks that run before a commit, a list of who owns which folder. These are good tools, and I use them every day.

What I did instead begins with one sentence.

## 2. The first principle

The one sentence this part of the record returns to was written by one of the sessions, and I keep it because it is right: **every law cites the incident that earned it. A new law without a dated failure behind it is a preference, and belongs in a style guide instead.**

## 3. The Softanza form

Softanza was not written by one hand for long. For the last two years, most of its new code has been written by sessions of a model, many at a time, on one machine. That work spans some twenty repositories that depend on each other. I rule; they propose, build, measure and report. The record calls me the Principal. In this chapter I will say I.

There is no framework here. No supervisor, no broker, no graph of agents. The arrangement is files and a version history. One repository, the central one, holds the protocol: how a session reports, how it asks, how it closes. Each repository has a mailbox. Each session writes its messages in one fixed form, eight keys in a fixed order. You have already met it if you read the memos at the end of each phase of this book. The eight keys: by whom, about what, why, what was done, the state of named things, what is waiting and on whom, what comes next, and one line of judgement. Every message is also appended to a dated file, so that the day's reasoning is never only in a chat window that closes.

What makes it a discipline and not a filing system is that each rule was paid for.

**Stage by explicit path.** Several sessions write in the same repository at the same time. Twice, a session added every changed file to its commit at once, and swept in another session's work. Once it destroyed an edit outright, once it pushed half of a colleague's move between two repositories. Nothing was lost the second time, and the record says that was luck, not method. So now a hook refuses the command before it runs. It was tested on twenty forms of the command, and eighteen behaved as written. Of the other two, `git stage -A` was repaired the same day; the other, that the hook also refuses text which only quotes the form, is an accepted cost, met in section 6.

**Read the time from the clock, never compose it.** A session writes a time on every message. Sessions, it turns out, invent times: they write a time that feels right after the last one. Nobody notices until a made-up time crosses midnight and a message is filed under the wrong day. How did the central session fare when it measured itself? Five of its twenty-five stamps had been composed! The worst was out by forty-two minutes, and four of the five were older than the law that forbade them. Across the other desks, thirty-four of a hundred and fifty-one stamps were composed, on thirteen of the seventeen desks measured. The rows behind that figure are published. The first time it was published, the rows had been thrown away, and the number could not be defended three hours later. Five weeks after the law, the central session composed a stamp again. Four written warnings had not stopped it; a peer reading a file did. The note it left is the best sentence in the protocol: a reminder is not a mechanism.

**Words name states, never people.** A session once reported my working tree as "dirty". Work that is not yet committed is not dirty, and often it contains no error at all; and a word that judges the work judges the one who made it. So the record keeps a table of words the estate does not use, with the word it uses instead.

**What no counter can see.** What did the counters say of the sound plane of the library? Six hundred and twenty-two passing assertions across nineteen guards, and yet to any listener it was broken! I heard four defects that no counter reported: a voice too quick to understand, a sentence cut before its end. The law that came out of it says that a thing nobody has perceived is in a state, not in failure: unperceived is a state, and the page must say so. It is the same instinct as the line every chapter of this book carries, "not yet read by the author", until I have read it.

**The loop is seconds, not minutes.** I stopped two sessions over the same eight-minute test run. It was measured: six seconds of the eight minutes were coverage, the rest were three sections re-rendering the world to test one pixel property. It was rewritten to twenty seconds. I had written, in my own words, that it "takes a lot of time and should be rethinked". The law was written by a session; the sentence was mine.

**Never ask without saying how.** A session once asked me to check whether a figure was right, and gave me no way to check it. I answered: please never demand a thing without telling me how to get it. Now every request addressed to a person carries the exact command, the exact path, the exact line to read.

You can see the shape. A failure, dated. A rule, written where the next session will read it. Then, when the rule is broken anyway, a mechanism. Some laws reached the last step, some are still a written rule, and the record says which.

Where the laws touch code, the library itself can hold them. Its fourteen house rules are validators, five of them over a graph of the code and the rest over text, the sixth law of the intelligence architecture made runnable. They are rules an agent can run before it commits, not folklore it must guess. One of them is about words. What does it say of a method called `KillOrder`, and is the code still clean? Run it:

```haro
cCode = "class Shop" + nl + "def KillOrder()" + nl + "  return" + nl
aFindings = StzCheckCode(cCode)
? aFindings[1][:rule]
#--> no-aggressive-verbs
? aFindings[1][:message]
#--> aggressive verb in method 'KillOrder' -- prefer Remove/Delete/Dispose/Clear/Close (Softanza tone)
? StzCodeIsClean(cCode)
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

Read the last line twice. `StzCodeIsClean` still answers `1`: the code is clean! The tone rule warns; it does not refuse. A word rule advises. The estate's doctrine says something close about its own word law: no session can test how a word lands on its reader, so that law is ruled rather than checked. The message says `aggressive verb in method 'KillOrder'` and names the remedy, `prefer Remove/Delete/Dispose/Clear/Close`. What does the rule say once the verb is `Remove`? Run it:

```haro
cCode = "class Shop" + nl + "def RemoveOrder()" + nl + "  return" + nl
? len( StzCheckCode(cCode) )
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

No finding comes back: `0`. Once the verb is `Remove`, the rule has nothing to say.

And the rules are a closed set. How many are there, and can an agent list them before it writes?

```haro
? len( StzCodeRuleNames() )
#--> 14
? StzCodeRuleNames()[3]
#--> no-aggressive-verbs
```

> run: kept

Yes: fourteen names, and the third is the rule that just spoke. The record does not link the library's tone rule to the estate's word law, ruled on 21 August 2026, so I will not either. But it is the same instinct at two altitudes: the words we use about work are part of the work.

Those are the laws. Now for what I refused, and the loss I accepted.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused a framework.** The arrangement is files, a history and a few hooks. A supervisor that schedules agents would be one more program to trust, and one more thing that decides without a record. Here, a session that is not running leaves only its files, and the files are the truth.

**I refused to let a coordinator grade its legislator.** Authority runs down from me; correction runs up, and the central session is corrected by the Observer and bound by the doctrine. A coordinator that judged its own rules would be the one part of the system nothing could fix. I hold this as a view, not a result.

**I refused to automate to the maximum.** From the middle of August 2026, sessions were opened by clocks. On 27 August I stopped them. The record keeps my reason as written then: the routines consumed quota without added value. And it keeps the part still open: that judgement was the only measurement anybody had, because the cost line every session writes at its close carries no token count. The harness does not tell a session what it spent. Of the three hundred and eighty cost lines written so far, one carries a number! What did the clocks cost? It is not in the record: it holds the reason and no figure, and I give none. Since then the ruling is that a timer may run a script, never a model, and every timer is paused for now. The direction is that a session starts when a file arrives in a watched folder, or when I start it; the watcher is designed and still to come (section 7).

**I refused to rule in my absence.** A ruling is written in the record only while I am there. On 28 September I asked the central session to settle what was pending on my behalf. The five rulings it took that day are marked as taken on my delegation. The mark is there so that nobody later mistakes them for mine. The decisions at the front of this book are marked the same way.

**I refused to let the protocol become a product.** The doctrine of the harness is open, and it is never to be sold: its value is in what it refuses. So far it has been proved in one place: one person, one machine, one filesystem, about fifteen sessions. The first adopter outside this estate is the next step.

**And I accepted a loss.** The code of the last two years was largely written by sessions, and my hand, which was the style, thinned where they wrote. A study of the record measured it. The promise under each line (the `#-->` line) is the house's way of writing an example. It stands at nearly a hundred per thousand lines in the short recipes (the quickers) and at seventy-five in my articles. In the narrated guards the sessions wrote, which assert in another form, it stands at under one. The sessions even took the name of my articles for their test files, a name that a model proposed. I accept the implementation code they wrote. I do not accept that the style a programmer or an agent writes with Softanza should be theirs rather than the house's. That is why the law of Part II exists, and why this book is written in my voice, with sessions as researchers and as the court. And it is why every chapter waits for my reading before it loses the word draft.

Next, the court, and what it cannot prove.

## 5. The court

No guard of the library proves the protocol. Its courts are outside the library, and I name them by what they do. One hook refuses wholesale staging, and one refuses work when the machine has no memory to spare. A check says whether the hooks installed are the hooks written. An instrument compares every stamp with its commit and publishes the rows. They are scripts, not Haro, and this book's reader cannot count their assertions, so they are named here and not in the court line below.

The blocks of this chapter stand on the library's guards of its house rules. `coderule_narrated` proves that a code rule is a rule over the graph, and that the house findings come out on a planted fixture in a frozen shape. It proves that the rules number fourteen, and that a definition or a comment is not taken for a call. `codegraph_narrated` proves the graph beneath them, and `coderule_project_narrated` runs the project rules over a fixture directory.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in scale and in independence. Its frameworks run on many machines for many teams; this protocol is proved so far at one person, one machine, one filesystem, and the first trial outside the estate is the next step. Its doctrine says so in its own words: these are laws that survived one estate.

The field leads in accounting. A paid interface reports the tokens of every call. This estate's cost record carries no token count yet; adding it is the next step, and until then the most important economic decision it took was taken by judgement.

The field leads in tooling for many writers. Branches, merge queues and owners of folders handle concurrency at a scale the estate has not met. Here, the answer is a discipline and one hook. The hook reads the text of a command, so it also refuses a commit message that merely quotes the forbidden form. That cost is known and accepted.

Where I think the record stands apart is in one habit: each law carries the failure that paid for it, and the record publishes its own rate of breaking its own law. The central desk broke the stamp law about as often as the estate it measured, on a different surface: 5 of 25 against 34 of 151. Four of the five came before the law. Both numbers are printed side by side.

Now the limit, in the stage words.

## 7. The limit

What is built: the protocol and its files, the dated memos, the ledger of rulings, three hooks that run in every session on this machine, and the instrument that measures stamps. The library's fourteen house rules are built, five of them over its code graph.

What is designed: a watcher that wakes a session when a file arrives, which, in my view, is the right replacement for the clocks and is still to come. And a court of style for the code that a programmer or an agent writes with the library. The house style would then be held by a machine as well as by my reading.

What is absent: a token figure in the cost record; any team outside this estate that has used the protocol; and a check that a word reads well in the reader's own language. The word law asks for that check, and no tool performs it yet; that check is the next step. The study that read the record named one more limit. I leave it here in its words, "the author" being me: the protocol that makes the record trustworthy also costs energy the product needs, and the author should watch the ratio. The record holds no reading of that ratio.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Stage by explicit path, read a file again before you write it, read the time from the clock. Each rule cost someone an edit.

**The analyst.** The record is data you can query, and it publishes its own error rates beside its rules.

**The designer.** A word names a state, never a person. A message is a closed structure a reader scans in one glance, written so that it parses for a reader whose first language is not English.

**The decision-maker.** A ruling is written only in your presence. When no figure can answer a question, decide by judgement and write down that it was judgement.

**The educator.** Teach a rule with the failure that earned it. A rule whose cost you cannot name is a rule you will talk yourself out of at two in the morning.

**The agent.** You are a worker with a record. You write a memo, a cost line and a conclusion; the hooks refuse what has hurt others; your stamps are compared with your commits. None of it assumes you are careless. All of it assumes you are many.

## 9. Exercise

Take the last ten messages, notes or log lines in which you wrote a time by hand. For each, find the moment the system recorded on its own: the commit, the time the message was sent, the time the file was saved. Before you look, write down how many of your times you expect to be later than the system's by a minute or more. Then count.

Your count is the finding. I will not give you mine.

I left this one in your hands. Take the first block of section 3, the one that asks the library to check `KillOrder`, and strip its promises: the block below is that one with no `#-->` line. Before you run it, write under each question the line you expect. Then run it and compare. Section 3 says why the last answer is what it is; whether it is the answer a function with that name should give, I leave to you.

```haro exercise
cCode = "class Shop" + nl + "def KillOrder()" + nl + "  return" + nl
aFindings = StzCheckCode(cCode)
? aFindings[1][:rule]
? aFindings[1][:message]
? StzCodeIsClean(cCode)
```

> run: exercise

When you have settled it, you will know why a word rule advises and does not refuse.

The last chapter is about the next line. Its principle says when that line can be written: the record carries a solution only when it ran on its own engine and a named person said what they saw.

# The next line: a solution on its own engine

Source: Chapter 33. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Stage: built: a declared platform built, then deployed, and refused in the other order; the twin that touches nothing real; the production gate that refuses a fake service; designed: the runtime that is the language's own, running the library; the guarantee sheet of a solution in five rows; live hosts and a real board; absent: a customer solution running on the Softanza engine, and a named person's verdict on it; the customers' declarations as files

Run: 3 of 3 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:16 UTC+01:00.

In this chapter I show you the floor the next line will stand on. A platform is declared, built and deployed in that order; a twin touches nothing real; a last door refuses a fake service. Then I say what the three projects establish today, and I read from the record the five steps from that floor to the next line: a solution on Softanza's own engine, and what a named person who used it says. I say five things I refused, among them a reference without a document, my own figure for the bridge runtime, and an instrument as proof of a perception. The record carries a solution only when it ran on its own engine and a named person said what they saw.

## 1. The concept, as the field holds it

In our industry, a platform is proven when someone else runs it for real. The home page shows the logos of companies that use it; a case study tells one of them in two pages. A team that believes in its own product uses it for its own work, and a compiler that is serious compiles itself. Engineers who need a scale have one: NASA's readiness levels, later a standard, where the highest level is a system proven in the place it was built for.

These are reasonable tests, and they all point at the same thing: production. What they do not ask is who saw it work, and what that person said.

## 2. The first principle

**The record carries a solution only when it ran on its own engine and a named person said what they saw.**

## 3. The Softanza form

This is the last chapter, and it looks ahead. Let me first show you the floor the next line will stand on, because the floor exists and runs.

A solution is declared before it is built, and the platform that carries it refuses to be deployed before it is built. What does it answer when you ask it to deploy first? Run it:

```haro
oSol = new stzPlatformProfile("bank")
oSol.DevelopedOn(:Windows)
oSol.WithServer(:backend, :LinuxServer)
oPlatform = StzPlatformQ("bank")
oPlatform.SetProfile(oSol)
try
	oPlatform.Deploy()
catch
	? cCatchError
done
#--> stzPlatform.Deploy: the platform must be Build() before it can be deployed.
? oPlatform.IsDeployed()
#--> 0
oPlatform.Build()
oPlatform.Deploy()
? oPlatform.IsDeployed()
#--> 1
```

> run: kept

The platform answers: `stzPlatform.Deploy: the platform must be Build() before it can be deployed.` Built, then deployed, `IsDeployed()` answers `1`.

Before anything is real, the solution can run on a twin, a copy of the system that holds no reference to reality. Where does a file written into the twin live? Run it:

```haro
oTwin = new stzVirtualFileSystem()
oTwin.CreateFile("orders/today.txt", "3 couscous")
? oTwin.ContentOf("orders/today.txt")
#--> 3 couscous
? oTwin.OriginOf("orders/today.txt")
#--> virtual
? StzEngineFileExists("orders/today.txt")
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

It answers `virtual`, and `StzEngineFileExists` answers `0`: the file was written, read back, and is not on the disk at that path!

And at the production gate, a solution cannot reach production with a fake service bound in it, whoever asks. In chapter 1 you saw the registry refuse; here the delivery itself refuses, with an entitled person as the actor. What does the delivery answer when that person deploys to production with a sandbox bound? Run it:

```haro
oReg = new stzServiceRegistry("shop")
oReg.BindSandbox(:payments, new stzPaymentsSandbox())
oDel = new stzDelivery("shop")
oDel.AddBackend("api", "linux")
oDel.UseServicesQ(oReg)
oDel.DeployTo(StzDeploymentSiteQ("host1"), "api")
oDel.SetActor(HumanActor("dana"))
oDep = oDel.Deploy(:Production)
? oDep.WasCommitted()
#--> 0
? oDel.Log().EntriesOfLevel(:error)[2][:message]
#--> sandbox-in-production @ shop/payments
```

> run: kept

The person was allowed to deploy. The delivery refused it! `WasCommitted()` answers `0`. Why did it refuse? The log says: `sandbox-in-production @ shop/payments`. A payments service that is a sandbox cannot be what a real customer pays through. This is not a question of authority. It is a question of what is true.

So the floor is there: declare, build, rehearse, refuse the fake, commit through a governed actor.

Now what the projects show, and the step after them.

Has Softanza been used? Yes. I wrote that in chapter 1 and I hold to it. Parts of Softanza are in production, and others are in development. The study that first read the record, a reading made by a session of a model, wrote the opposite, that no user outside my own projects had been measured. I corrected it. Three projects, and their feedback, shaped its design. A bank in Niamey has an organisation-assessment product I built beside the library, installed on its own network under licence. The installation guide delivered to the bank attests it. A restaurant in Lyon was delivered a first version of a platform for neighbourhood commerce. An amendment closed on 4 August; the notebook shows that the owner took part in the first demonstration, and on 19 August he made a day of field measurements with two phones. The last dated record of that project is 25 August 2026, and the next record is the one the next phase writes. An organisation in Niger has its hub designed. The site lists, project by project, each lesson it names and the file of the library that carries it. Part VI of this book tells their stories.

And the site says, in the same page, what those platforms run on today: ordinary stacks. The bank's is a Python application. The restaurant's is a set of web worlds, each a single file. The organisation's hub is designed, and its build is to come. The site says their declarations are being written as Softanza's own, so that moving them onto the Softanza engine is a change of engine and not of plan. The site's own words are exact: it is a step ahead, not a step taken. That step is the next one.

So the next line is this one: *the solution ran on Softanza's own engine, and this is what the user said.*

What leads from here to that line? Five steps, read from the record and not composed.

1. **A customer's declarations run on the engine instead of the ordinary stack.** The site says the declarations are being written as Softanza's own; I found no file of them yet, and chapter 31 says where I looked. The move to the engine is the next step.
2. **The language's own runtime runs the library on the customer's system.** Today the blocks of this book are run by the runtime of the former host language, version 1.27, and every page says so under its proof line. The bridge runtime that is meant to replace it was reported on 5 October 2026 to pass every assertion the old runtime passes on the library, all but one HTTP/2 request. That request failed once in the pass and passes twice when run alone. On every stable line, 3,461 of 5,112 programs agree with the old runtime. Its pass under Linux is the next step. Haro's charter was ratified on 1 October 2026, and Haro's own stages pass on its own fixtures. Whether Haro's own runtime runs the library is not yet measured.
3. **The solution writes its guarantee sheet.** The vision asks every solution for five rows, one for each system it answers to, technical, social, ecological, economic and cultural, judged as fixtures, with the machine derived from them. The sheet is designed, and the first one is yet to be written.
4. **Live hosts and a real board.** The deployment's verification against a real host or a real account is gated by infrastructure. The declared machine, a device written as a declaration, boots and judges itself in an emulator. Its hosted profile makes four promises: it is always reachable, it has a stable name, it keeps a durable log, and it survives a cut. The emulator can check three. The fourth waits for a watchdog on a real board. (General knowledge, a watchdog is a hardware timer that restarts a machine that stops answering.) The site says no production workload is on it yet.
5. **A person perceives it, and the record says who.** Not a counter. A person, named, with a date and what they said.

The last step is a law of the estate, and it was earned. The sound plane of the library once held hundreds of passing assertions while, to my ear, it did not yet sound right. Since then, a piece of work that reaches a person through a sense must ship in a form a person can perceive, and the record must say who perceived it and what they said. And when nobody has, the law says it plainly: unperceived is a state, not a failure. A report may be honest and still be unperceived.

The record already holds the form of the line. In another plane, on 2 October 2026, the record says the Principal, its name for me (chapter 32 says so), listened to a sound the library made and said, in the words written down: "yes it's real drum!!" It is not a solution, and it is not a customer. But it is a person, a date, and a sentence, and it is what the next line will look like.

The record also holds two verdicts still to come. The pictures the library draws were judged by a model, forty-one pictures or text sections, twenty right, nine wrong, twelve uncertain. The record says that no human has looked at them yet; that look is the next verdict. The live run of the payments plane with the organisation's test account was postponed, by me, and reported as unperceived. Neither is a failure. Both are next.

## 4. The decision, and what I refused

**I refused to be told the projects did not count.** The first reading of the record wrote that no user outside my own projects had been measured. It was wrong, and I said so: Softanza has been used in real projects which, while not complete, gave the feedback that shaped its design. The reading was corrected in the record, in place, with the date. What replaced it is exact: the users exist, and their feedback shaped the design, and the next step is to move one of them onto the engine.

**I refused a reference without a document.** The site counts a project as a reference only when a document proves it, and it quotes no number of countries and no number of programmers. A lesson is listed only when a file of the library carries it.

**I refused to move a platform by rewriting it.** A customer's platform moves to the engine as a change of engine, not of plan. The declarations are to be written once, as Softanza's own, and the engine beneath them changes. I hold this as a view, not a result.

**I refused my own figure.** I have said aloud how far the bridge runtime has come, as a percentage. The study that read the record ruled that the figure should not be printed without the guard that produces it. The stage word is "in construction", and the measured rows are the evidence.

**I refused an instrument as proof of a perception.** A phase whose evidence is entirely a counter is not done when the thing itself is something a person sees or hears. The counter is a result. The person is the verdict. This is my view, and the sound plane above, with its hundreds of passing assertions and the sound that was not yet right, is what stands behind it.

## 5. The court

The floor of this chapter stands on three guards. `full_scope_model_narrated`, the architect's common ground, proves that a solution is modelled first, that a deployment before a build is refused and logged as an error, and that a model cannot perform the deployment. `virtual_system_twin_narrated` proves that rehearsal touches nothing real, that the plan narrates itself, and that committing is the one door. `service_delivery_narrated` proves that a production deployment with a fake bound does not run at all, with every reason named, and that the gate opens once the service is real.

The court of this chapter's subject is not a guard. It is a line in the record: who, when, and what they said. That line is the next one to be written.

## 6. The field, and where it leads

The field leads in production. Ordinary stacks run real workloads for real users today (General knowledge, not a study of the record); the record's own page names among them the bank's Python application, installed under licence. The restaurant's first version is single-file web worlds, delivered, with a day of field measurements on 19 August, and the hub is designed.

The field leads in live infrastructure. Its tools drive real providers and real hosts every day; Softanza's deployment is proven end to end on local sites and rehearses the rest; live verification against a real host is the next step.

The field leads on hardware in the field. Its devices run in shops and clinics; the declared machine boots in an emulator, and its board is the next step.

The field leads on speed. The runtime Softanza measured itself against was faster on every row, by a factor between one and two, and the estate's note recorded the measure.

Where I think the record stands apart is in what it holds itself to. Every reference has a document. Every stage has a word. By the design document, the emulated deployment runs the same artifact that ships. A fake cannot ship and a model cannot commit. And a perception is a named, dated, quoted line, which a logo on a home page does not carry.

## 7. The limit

This chapter looks ahead, so the limit is short.

What is built: the floor. Declare, build, deploy in order; rehearse on a twin; refuse a fake at the production gate; commit only through a governed actor.

What is designed: the runtime that is the language's own, running the library; the guarantee sheet of a solution in five rows; the verification on live hosts and on a real board.

What is absent: a customer solution running on the Softanza engine, a named person's verdict on it, and the customers' declarations as files (chapter 31 says where I looked). Beside it stand the floor above and the feedback of three projects. The record does not yet name the project that moves to the engine first, nor the person who will perceive it; I leave both unwritten until the record can carry them.

## 8. Six doors

**The programmer.** Your code runs today through build, rehearsal and a governed deployment. What comes next is a customer running it on the engine, and the record will carry that claim the day it is true.

**The analyst.** Ask for two things before you count a project as proof: a document, and a named verdict.

**The designer.** What you design is judged last by a person's sense. A model's opinion of a picture is a result; a person's is the verdict.

**The decision-maker.** Read the stage word before the promise. Today the floor is built; the customer run on the engine is the next milestone, and its stage word is absent.

**The educator.** Teach that unperceived is a state and not a failure. An honest report can ship on it, if it says so.

**The agent.** You may rehearse a deployment and explain it. You will be refused when you try to perform it, (chapter 24 shows the refusal).

# The register of innovations

Source: Appendix A. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

The study of the record read four inventories the library keeps of itself: the narrations with their titles, the design documents, the engine's phase of paradigm engines, and an outside reader's list of rethought concepts. It crossed them, and found forty-six innovations in seven families. This is that register, as the study ruled it on 7 October 2026, with one column of this book's own: the chapter that tells each row. A later ruling the same day, 12.26, adds a forty-seventh row, answer by example; it is not in this table.

Read the register as the study reads it, and no better. It is a reading and not a census. A concept with no narration and no design document of its own was not counted. The study's evidence counts files that name the thing in code outside tests and archives, test files, and the engine module where one exists; it counts files that name a thing, and not tests that pass. I have not re-counted any of them. The study also counted how many pages of the site named each innovation; the site has changed since, and I leave that column out.

Of the forty-six, eight are not told in this book: the adverb engine, named variables, the unified find, object history, the intent-based file interface, natural folder navigation, the invisible and the lookalike, and statistics with the linear solver and matrices. They are in the register because the record holds them, and untold here because this book had to choose.

| 46 | Locale management and culture | software of global reach, the Softanza way | 22 (the loss: no locale layer) | a narration that runs |

### What the register says

The study's reading, which I keep: what the site names is what has a product page, a principle or an area of the Atlas. What it leaves in the dark is what cuts across the areas. Every paradigm of the first family except those the site already named (rows 4 to 8 and 10 to 12 above; the study's site column marked them, and I left it out), all twelve engines except the polyglot bridge, the pattern languages as a family, the idiom. This book is in part the answer to that: its parts are cut across the areas, and not along them.

The study also reconciled the twelve engines. The macroplan marks the phase done. Eight of the twelve have a source file under their name in the engine; four have none, which are truth, quantifiers, polyglot and polycode, the first two reported done as libraries. On the library's side three engines have no test file at all: adverb, section merging and named variables. Two of the three are rows 16 and 21 above, which this book does not tell, and the third, row 19, it tells as an absence.

### What is not measured

No code was run to make the register. The evidence column counts files that name a thing, and the engine's done marks are the macroplan's. Whether an innovation's narration runs once its stale names are repaired was not tested. A row's chapter in the fourth column is the chapter that tells it, and a row that says "named in" is a row the chapter mentions and does not tell.

# The fourteen house rules

Source: Appendix B. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

Run: 2 of 2 blocks kept their promise, on 2026-10-14 12:16 UTC+01:00.

The library has a court over its own code. It reads a class as a graph (five rules) or as text (nine) and judges it against rules, most of which were written after the library did something it should not have. This appendix lists the fourteen, and shows three of them firing.

There are two fourteens in this book, and they are different sets. The fourteen **house rules** of this appendix are rules of the library's code: they are a court, and they run. The fourteen **rules of writing**, in chapter 7, are a reading of how the style is written. They are readings of the code, stated as rules, and no machine judges them. The coincidence in the number is a coincidence.

The list of names is fourteen and the library can print it. One more text pass, `for_in_loop`, also reports, and so do two rules over a whole project, `no-dead-code` and, in the deep audit, `no-cyclic-calls`:

```haro
? len( StzCodeRuleNames() )
#--> 14
? StzCodeRuleNames()[2]
#--> q-returns-object
? StzCodeRuleNames()[14]
#--> value-called-as-function
```

> run: kept

Here are three of them, fired on code I planted. A method that writes to the console is a warning, so the code stays clean. A method that differs from another only by case is an error, since the host language does not tell the two apart and the file would not load. And a class that defines `Len()` shadows the host's own function, and is refused:

```haro
cSay = "class Door" + nl + "def Say()" + nl + "	? 'hello'" + nl + "	return 1" + nl
? StzCheckCode(cSay)[1][:rule]
#--> library_prints
? StzCheckCode(cSay)[1][:severity]
#--> warning
? StzCodeIsClean(cSay)
#--> 1
cBox = "class Box" + nl + "def Size()" + nl + "	return 1" + nl + "def size()" + nl + "	return 2" + nl
? StzCheckCode(cBox)[1][:rule]
#--> no-case-collision
? StzCodeIsClean(cBox)
#--> 0
cLen = "class Box" + nl + "def Len()" + nl + "	return 1" + nl
? StzCheckCode(cLen)[1][:rule]
#--> no-len-method
? StzCodeIsClean(cLen)
#--> 0
```

> run: kept

A note on spelling, which a program that reads findings must know. The list uses hyphens: `library-prints`. A finding of eight of the fourteen comes back with underscores: `library_prints`. The findings of the five rules over the code graph (rows 1, 3, 4, 5 and 6) keep their hyphens, and so does the finding of row 2. I print what the run printed.

### The fourteen

| # | Rule | What it refuses | Severity |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `no-len-method` | defining `Len()` on a class: it shadows the host's builtin and breaks every caller | error |
| 2 | `q-returns-object` | a method named with a closing `Q` that does not return an object to chain | error |
| 3 | `no-aggressive-verbs` | method names that begin with Kill or Destroy: the Softanza tone | warning |
| 4 | `engine-first` | new code that reaches for the host's byte-oriented string functions where the engine's forms exist | warning |
| 5 | `q-has-plain-twin` | a chaining mutator with no plain twin: `Q` chains, and plain acts | warning |
| 6 | `no-case-collision` | two methods of one class that differ only in case: the host is case-insensitive, so the file fails to load | error |
| 7 | `dead-knob` | an attribute that a setter writes and nothing reads: a setting that can change nothing | error; warning when only its own getter reads it |
| 8 | `setter-resets-on-reject` | a setter that answers a value it dislikes by overwriting what was already there | warning |
| 9 | `setter-only-moves-one-way` | `max()` or `min()` against the very attribute being set: a ratchet that swallows the other direction | error |
| 10 | `misspelled-name` | a method with a known misspelling while the correct name does not exist | warning |
| 11 | `library-prints` | a class method that writes to the console while it works; methods whose purpose is to display are exempt | warning |
| 12 | `empty-method-body` | a method declared and doing nothing; harsher for a predicate, which then answers nothing, read as false | warning; error for a predicate |
| 13 | `writes-a-mutable-constant` | assigning to `true`, `false`, `null`, `nl`, `tab` or `cr`: in the host they are ordinary globals | error |
| 14 | `value-called-as-function` | calling the result of a call, the shape a global replacement of tokens leaves | error |

I fired rows 6, 11 and 1 above. Chapter 32 fires row 3, chapter 7 fires rows 10 and 13, and chapter 5 fires row 2. For rows 7, 9 and 12 I read the severities from the source, as I did for rows 4, 5, 8 and 14; I did not fire these seven.

### What the court does not judge

None of the fourteen is a rule of writing. No machine judges, today, the style of a program written with the library. That is chapter 7's limit, and it is the reason the other fourteen are in this book as a table with their state, and not as a court.

### The other fourteen: the rules of writing, and where each stands

Read from the study's ruling on the style, with the state of each at the commit this book runs.

| # | Rule of writing | State |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | A value is lifted to its object by `Q()` or a typed lifter | runs |
| 2 | A scene is a brace block on one object | runs |
| 3 | Every printed line carries its answer under `#-->` | the promise is judged when written; a line without one is not refused |
| 4 | A parameter is named, and the name reads as English | runs, and in any order |
| 5 | A chain mirrors the order of thought, one step per line | runs |
| 6 | The verb's form says what happens | runs |
| 7 | Suffixes and prefixes are small and mean one thing each | in part |
| 8 | A function has alternatives for the way a person says it | in part |
| 9 | Precision over generality, when it costs nothing | in part |
| 10 | Code travels inside an argument as a small string | the seat runs; the mark on a parameter's name is retired |
| 11 | Small literal languages live in strings | runs in one form |
| 12 | A sentence can be near-natural | not as the deck wrote it |
| 13 | A function may be named in any human language | Chinese and French names raise; the Arabic one was not run |
| 14 | A comment speaks before the code, in the teacher's voice | runs |

# The twenty-two verbs and the constitution

Source: Appendix C. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

The interface constitution lives in its own repository, and the site says that repository is not public yet. This book calls it the interface constitution and does not name the product it belongs to. The library holds only the slot for its verbs and the finder of the elements that serve none; the list itself is the constitution's. So this appendix prints what I read from the constitution's own file on 9 October 2026: its version is 3.11, last updated on 16 August 2026, with seven articles, thirty-one sets and one hundred and twenty-two rules. The counts in this sentence are the file's own, and I counted none of them myself.

Chapter 26 shows how the library finds an element that serves no verb. Chapter 25 shows what a new medium adds. Chapter 20 shows the right to a human as the end of the loop.

### The four axioms

Its four axioms are calm over power, intent over mechanics, visible over hidden, business over technology. Its preamble says business software is not entertainment, and that its interface should not impress, and should serve.

### The seven articles

| Article | Title | What it holds |
|---|---|---|
| I | Calm | the interface must never compete with the operator's thinking; calm is a functional requirement and not an aesthetic choice |
| II | Intent Over Mechanics | interaction starts from what the human is trying to do, and widgets are consequences of intent, never primitives; selection never implies execution |
| III | Visible State | if the system knows something important, the user must see it: active filters, pending drafts, norms, background work, machine provenance |
| IV | Reversibility | every act is reversible, or explicitly and visibly irreversible: staged, named and reviewed |
| V | Predictable Authority | every consequential act names its actor, and an intelligent agent is an actor under this law, never an authority above it |
| VI | Operator Primacy and Vibes | business users are operators, not explorers; vibes, densities, dialects and surfaces are equivalent vibes of one truth |
| VII | Amendment | the law grows only from evidence; removing a protection requires proof the harm is gone |

### The twenty-two verbs

The grammar is closed: rules quantify over these verbs, and a new medium adds renderings, never verbs. Six families, each with its invariant, as the constitution writes them.

| Family | Verbs | Invariant |
|---|---|---|
| Orienting | discover, understand, locate | discovery must be satisfiable at every entry point |
| Attention | focus, filter, compare | an active filter is visible state; focus and discovery cannot both be at maximum |
| Information | read, scan, scroll, zoom | a read surface that silently acts is an intent violation |
| Selection | select, highlight, preview, mark | selection expresses interest, never commitment |
| Action | act, confirm, cancel, undo | every act has a reachable undo, or declares why it cannot |
| Continuity | pause, resume, retry, exit | pause, resume and exit are always reachable; a trapped flow is unconstitutional |

That makes three, three, four, four, four and four: twenty-two. Of them, the library's worlds (chapter 26) know five as screen intents: discover, understand, focus, select and act. The library does not check the word.

### The six rights

| Right | What it says |
|---|---|
| to understand | every element, denial and setting can explain itself in the operator's language |
| to reverse | what was done can be undone, and what cannot says so before, not after |
| to know | the state of the system, filters, drafts, progress and provenance, is always visible |
| to leave | every flow, focus and overlay has a reachable exit that returns the user to where they stood |
| to act without fear | no element creates anxiety through unpredictability, irreversibility by default or hidden consequences |
| to a human | wherever a machine serves, a person is reachable, clearly and without penalty |

The last right is enforced by a single rule, and that rule is marked as a matter of judgment: a person decides whether it is met, and no machine checks it. The library does not mention it.

### Intelligence under law

Set 31 is the constitution's answer to the agent, and it is where chapter 20 meets it. Its ten rules, with how each is enforced, as the file records it:

| Rule | Title | Enforcement |
|---|---|---|
| 97 | The Agent Is an Actor | judgment |
| 98 | Proposals, Not Commits | judgment |
| 99 | Visible Provenance | a machine, later |
| 100 | Deterministic Islands | a machine, later |
| 101 | The Dignity Contract | judgment |
| 102 | The Right to a Human | judgment |
| 103 | Grounded Proactivity | judgment |
| 104 | Machine Legibility | a machine, later |
| 121 | The Assistant Never Masks the Work | judgment |
| 124 | The Generator Ships a Working Thing | judgment |

Rule 98 is the law of chapter 20's plan, written for an interface: a proposal is not a commit. Rule 99 asks for the mark on machine-made content; chapter 14's provenance is the nearest thing the library builds. Rule 104 asks for machine legibility; chapter 26's accessibility tree is the nearest thing the library builds: what a screen reader can operate, an agent can operate. Seven of the ten are enforced by judgment, and three by a machine that is to come.

### What the constitution forbids

The constitution lists seventeen forbidden patterns, each tied to the rules it violates. Among them are modal storms, invisible active filters, hidden destructive actions, and selection that silently executes. Its retired numbers are never reused, and two numbers are burned and reserved for ever.

### What this book takes from it, and what it does not

It takes the closed grammar, which it uses in chapters 25 and 26; the six rights, of which it uses one; and the sets, of which it uses the one for agents, and Rule 118 on the five values of status. It does not take the rest. No guard of the library judges a shell against the verbs, and the library's own design text still lists twenty-four verbs from an interaction engine that I removed on 26 May 2026. The constitution's list is twenty-two.


=====

# The glossary: words name states, never people

Source: Appendix D. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

A word in this book names a state of a piece of work, or a role held in a scope. It does not name a rank, and it does not name a person. A crafter is someone who is declaring something, and a person is a crafter on the day they declare. An architect is what one holds in the scope of a solution. A user is whoever is using. When a sentence of this book is about a person, it says what the person did, on what date, and in whose words.

Each entry gives the word as the book uses it, and the chapter where it is shown. Where the record uses a word in two senses, both are given, and they are numbered.

**absent.** One of the three stage words. What the record does not hold, in code or in files, as far as I looked, and I say how far. Chapter 3.

**admission.** The act by which a candidate becomes knowledge: it is judged by the world's rules, and refused with a reason if it fails. Expression is free, and admission is governed. Chapters 17, 18.

**agent.** A program that proposes and cannot commit. In the library an agent is a file, judged at load, whose model, if it has one, holds no capability to produce effects. It names an actor and never a person. Chapters 17, 20.

**architect.** What one holds in the scope of a solution: the profile, the worlds and the governance. Not a rank. Chapter 23.

**authority.** The level at which an actor may act, compared with the risk of the act. Distinct from permission: permission is held, and authority must cover the risk. Chapter 30.

**bond.** A declared relation between two worlds of a constellation, for one named act. Nothing crosses without one. Chapter 23.

**built.** One of the three stage words. The code exists, a guard exists, and a block ran. Chapter 3.

**candidate.** What a reply, a model's answer or an example is, until the court has judged it. Chapter 18.

**checkpoint.** A refusal kept for a person, with its context and a lifetime. Chapter 18.

**commit.** The one act that reaches reality. Only a governed actor commits, under a scope, and the model cannot. Chapters 20, 24.

**commons.** The shared ground of a constellation, what every world of it stands on: an identity, a session, a store. It is a world like the others, world zero, and not a special kind of node. Chapters 4, 23.

**constellation.** A graph of worlds, with bonds between them and governance over every crossing. Chapter 23.

**court.** The judge of a thing. A court says kept or refused, and says why in the words of the declaration. A language is born with its court. Chapters 5, 11, 12, 21.

**crafter.** Whoever declares what they know about a world, and owns what they declare. Skill is not part of it. Chapter 2.

**declare.** To say what a thing is before it is built, in a text a court can judge. The act of the kernel. Chapters 3, 12.

**designed.** One of the three stage words. A document says so, and nothing runs. Chapter 3.

**door.** In this book, two senses. (1) The *doors* of a chapter: the programmer, the analyst, the designer, the decision-maker, the educator and the agent, six ways in which a chapter is useful, each a way of using the work and not a job title. (2) The *door* of the foreign runtimes: one governed way to run code in a language the platform does not speak. A door is a runtime and never a syntax. Chapters 2, 16.

**engine.** The Zig library that does the work once, beneath every surface. Chapter 27.

**facet.** In this book, three senses, which chapters 12, 16 and 26 keep apart. (1) A surface of a declared language: its text, its court, its mouth, its voice, its tongues. (2) A syntax of Haro that a programmer of another language could write; the record calls this a face, and only my statement names it. (3) A tool of an environment's ribbon, ordered by the life of the thing being made. A facet is a syntax and never a runtime.

**fake.** A double that says it is one: a sandbox, a mail sink, a recorded answer. A fake must never ship. A double that says nothing about itself is taken for live, and passes the gate. Chapter 24.

**floor.** One level of the platform's picture, from the engine at the bottom to the organisation at the top, each with its judge beside it. Not a layer of the library (core, base, max) and not a plane. Chapter 4.

**forge.** The machinery that produces a language's court, mouth, printer and grammar from its declaration alone. Built outside the library, designed for it. Chapters 11, 12.

**gap.** What a goal needs and the world does not yet hold. A conversation that asks starts from the gap. Chapter 17.

**gate.** A place where something is refused before it goes on. The registration gate, the production gate, the capability seam. Chapters 20, 24.

**goal.** A wanted state of a graph. The gap is measured, and a goal that cannot be evaluated is refused. Chapters 17, 23.

**governance.** Permission, authority and risk, compared before an act, fail-closed, with the decision kept. Chapters 20, 30.

**guard.** A file of the library's test tree that holds assertions, and that proves a chapter. This book counts a guard's assertions and does not run most of them. Chapters 5, 21.

**Haro.** The language this book writes in: the surface of the former host language, kept and renamed, with a charter and a runtime still in construction. Haro does not run the library yet. Chapters 3, 27.

**ledger.** The security plane's record of what was refused and why, written by the court and never by the thing it judged. Chapters 20, 21.

**lexicon.** The dictionary of words a sentence may use, each mapped to a verb the library documents. Chapter 13.

**lineage.** The record of a decision with the authority and the risk of its moment. Chapter 30.

**narration.** The author's article under the library's documentation folder, whose code is the old surface. Not an executable test. (The word has since been given to the executable guards named *narrated*, and chapter 21 says why that confuses.) Chapters 3, 21.

**near-natural chain.** A sentence in which every link is a real call, so that the human is the parser. Chapter 13.

**pack.** A human language as a block of data. Chapter 13.

**perception.** What a person has seen or heard of a thing, named, dated, and quoted. A comparison proves sameness and never rightness. Chapters 21, 33.

**plan.** The one artifact that crosses from a twin toward reality. It narrates, flags and validates, and is committed by a governed actor. Chapters 20, 24.

**plane.** One area of the library, named by what it governs and proved by its own guards: the system plane, the agentic plane, the planes of security and performance. Not a floor, and not a plan, which is the artefact a machine proposes. Chapters 1, 21.

**platform.** The envelope around a solution, and the ground a constellation stands on: capabilities, a commons, a registry, shells, a profile. Chapter 23.

**posture.** What the registry says a service is: sandbox, local, live, conformance. And for foreign code, what its executor says it is: trusted, external or sandboxed. Chapters 16, 24.

**promise.** The line `#-->` under an expression, which says what it prints. A claim about a stop of the program, judged kept, diverged, unreached or unjudged. Chapters 1, 21.

**proposal.** What a model, a reaction or an agent offers. It commits nothing. Chapters 17, 20, 26.

**refinement.** The write surface: a change declared with its points and judged by a four-stage gate. Chapter 10.

**register.** In this book, two senses. (1) One of the five forms a reply can take: an option, a data structure, a formula, a sentence, examples. (2) The register of innovations, Appendix A. Chapter 18.

**rehearse.** To run on what touches nothing, and to read what it would have done. Chapter 24.

**reversibility.** The class of an act: reversible, compensable or irreversible. The harder an act is to undo, the more trusted the code performing it must be. Chapter 20.

**scope.** A named frame in which a system act is written: development, deployment, runtime-current. The system acts of a program are always in a scope. Chapters 8, 23.

**spine.** The nine chapters that carry the whole argument and can be read alone. The preface.

**stage word.** One of built, designed, absent, printed at the head of each chapter's limit. Chapter 3.

**twin.** A copy of a part of reality that holds no reference to reality: a twin of files, a twin of the process environment. Chapters 20, 24.

**unperceived.** The state of a thing that reaches a person through a sense, when no person has perceived it and said so. It is a state and not a failure. Chapters 21, 33.

**wise coding.** Softanza asks and the world judges. The inversion of the loop of prompting and keeping what runs. Chapter 18.

**workbench.** Where a proposal is rehearsed. It holds a twin, a copy of the system with no reference to the real one; what the proposal would write goes into the twin, and what comes out is a plan. The one a programmer already uses, not a second one for machines. Chapters 1, 20.

**world.** One application, as a declaration with a being, a becoming and a way of being met. Chapter 23.

# The guards and fixtures cited, by chapter

Source: Appendix E. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

A guard is a file of the library's test tree that holds assertions. A chapter that says "this is proved" names the guards that prove it, in the front matter of its source, and the build reads each one: it finds the file in the library at the commit the book runs at, and counts the assertions in it. A guard that is not there fails the build.

This appendix is that reading, by chapter. Nothing in it is typed. It is regenerated at every build, so the figures are always the ones the last build read.

Two things to keep in mind. A figure is the number of assertion calls the file holds, counted in its text; it is not a run. Most of these guards are not run by the book, because they write files, open ports or spawn processes. The few I ran myself are named in the chapters that rely on them, with the date. And the only fixtures the book cites are the ones the guards and the blocks read: the course folder of the library's education program, and the data folders its guards name. The blocks of the book are themselves the other fixtures: each one runs inside the library at the commit named under every chapter's title.

The chapters cite 114 distinct guards, holding 3359 assertions between them, read from the test tree at commit 93d7a39bd on 2026-10-14 12:16 UTC+01:00. A guard cited by two chapters is counted once in this total.

=====

# The narrations the book argues from

Source: Appendix F. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

A narration is one of the author's articles, under the library's documentation folder: an essay that tells an idea with its code. There are about a hundred and thirty-five of them, and the book argues from the ones listed here. The code in them is the old surface, which Haro keeps under a new name. A narration is not an executable test: the word has since been given to the executable guards whose names end in *narrated*, and chapter 21 says why that is confusing.

Each chapter names its narrations in its source, and the build checks that each is a file of the library at the commit the book runs at. Where a name is not a file of that folder, this appendix says so. A narration's blocks are not the chapter's blocks. Several no longer run at that commit, and each chapter that cites one says which part, and runs its own.

The chapters cite 50 distinct narrations. Every name is a file of the library's narration folder at commit 93d7a39bd. A narration is the author's article; its code is the old surface, and several of its blocks no longer run at the pin, which each chapter says.

# The book as data, for the agent that reads it

Source: Appendix G. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

This appendix is for an agent that reads the book, and for the person who gives it the book to read. It says what the book offers in a form a machine can take, and what an agent may do with it.

### What there is

Files sit beside the reader, and each is regenerated at every build.

- **`book.json`** holds, for each page of the book, a chapter, the preface or an appendix: its number, identifier and slug; its kind; its part; the law it states, if it is a law chapter; the first principle it states, in one sentence; whether it is on the spine; its status; its stage words, built, designed and absent; its guards, each with its path and its assertion count; its promises, which are its blocks of code with the lines they promised, what the last run printed, and the verdict; its narrations; the study's rulings it rests on; its six doors; its sections; its open questions, which are the places the record could not answer; its word count; whether it has a French edition; and where its text is. A chapter that is a draft says so in its status.
- **`book.schema.json`** describes `book.json`, and the build validates the file against it. A build that produces a `book.json` that does not match its schema fails.
- **`llms-book.txt`** is a short text: the rules for an agent, then one line per page with its status, its guards and the count of blocks that kept their promise, and its principle.
- **`llms-full-book.txt`** is every page as text, one after the other, with the verdict of the last run written under each block of code. **`agents/<slug>.md`** is the same, one page per file; the slug is in `book.json`.
- **`status.html`** is the page for a person: every chapter with its status, its run, its guards, its words, its open questions and whether it has a French edition.

### What a promise is

A block of code that begins with the tag `haro` is run inside the library at the commit named under the chapter's title. The lines that begin with `#-->` are the block's promise: what the block printed when the author wrote it. The judge compares the promise with the run, ignoring spacing, and reading `TRUE` and `FALSE` as 1 and 0. A promise is kept, or it differs, or the block raised, or it stopped. A block that is marked as an exercise is not run, and a block that touches files, the clock or chance says so, and shows the output that was recorded on its date.

An agent that quotes a promise should quote its verdict with it.

### What an agent may do, and may not

- **You may quote any cell.** A table of this book is data. A row that says "absent" is a row you may repeat as absent.
- **You may not turn an absent cell into a present one.** The record will find your sentence and the missing file side by side, and the book says so in chapter 31.
- **You may rely on the stage words.** Built means the code, a guard and a run. Designed means a document and nothing that runs. Absent means that I looked, and say how far.
- **You may rely on a promise only with its verdict, and only at the commit it names.** A number that the book prints is a number read from a file or a run, at the date the page gives.
- **A draft is a draft.** A chapter marked as a draft is not the author's final word, and "the author read it" is a status that only the author sets.

### The chapters, as the build reads them

The table below is generated at every build from the same data as `book.json`.

# The project's history: from Ring to Haro

Source: Appendix H. Status: draft (a draft, not read by the author). Library commit 93d7a39bd.

This is the one page of the book that names the language Softanza grew up on. The chapters do not, because the platform is going where that language cannot follow. The history belongs here, told once, with thanks.

## Where it began

Softanza was born on Ring, a small scripting language. I wrote its library in Ring for years. I taught Ring, I promoted it in the field, and I spent nine months writing its book, *Beginning Ring Programming*, published by Apress in 2020. In 2021 I described the library to the Ring community for the first time. In 2022 the first presentation of Softanza called it "a best friend of existing Ring programmers". Ring's simplicity and its multiparadigm expressiveness are two things Softanza keeps, because they were never the problem.

## What real projects taught

Three things, and they are the reasons.

**The limits showed.** Real projects showed that Ring was not suited to the challenges of modern applications. The limits are in three places: performance, strong typing and ease of build. The project's own sessions measured them.

**The direction could not be influenced.** The direction of a language belongs to its owner, and ours could not be influenced. The project built Ring++ to solve those three pains without leaving Ring, and it did not meet the reception we hoped for.

**Governance is a design property.** A dependency whose direction you cannot influence, and whose behaviour you cannot fence, fails the due diligence that regulated domains require. Softanza's answer is not independence for its own sake, but independence for its users: an engine written in Zig with no third-party dependency, a virtual machine and a compiler of our own, and commitments the project already runs as tests. An open core. Charters ratified in public. Every decision in a dated record. Every claim linked to its proof.

## The path

Ring is where it began. Ring++ is the bridge, in construction. Haro is the destination. Under all three sits the engine in Zig, which does the computation once.

Today the library still runs on its first runtime, version 1.27, and the blocks of this book run on it. Haro uses the syntax of that first runtime as its first facet, so all the code already written keeps working. The library's narrated tests are the test of that promise: Haro has to run them. Chapter 27 tells where the engine stands, with numbers.

## Thanks

Softanza says thank you to the language it grew up on, and goes where its users' sovereignty requires.
